– 5 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 5 –

			
Passages 

			
Sicily to New York City

			
1910

			
Mariano and Ange were given a month to get to the docks in Palermo—however they could—where the passenger ship, Patria, would be waiting to carry them to their new lives. If they didn’t make it in time, they wouldn’t get this opportunity again and would have to walk back to their village in disgrace. 

			
So, in short order, they packed up their belongings, leaving Maria and the babies with Mariano’s parents who were constantly complaining that they’d run out of money, luck, and food. Like a period on the end of a sentence, his mother added to the end of every conversation: “We don’t even have enough for ourselves.” 

			
But it was all OK, because Maria and their three children, Giuseppe, and the twins, Pietrantonio and Calandra were going to follow Mariano in three short months along with Arcangelo’s wife, Rosaria, and their baby, Giuliana. It had all been arranged. 

			
On the morning that Mariano and Ange were set to leave, Maria cried and clung to him like he was going off to war and sure death, while he, shamefully, fought back tears himself. How could a man love a woman this much? All he wanted to do was make life beautiful for her in America. 

			
The men finally had to turn away from their wives and families and everything they’d ever known to begin the nearly seventy-mile trek to Palermo. 

			
Their leaving meant even fewer young men were left in Lucca Sicula. It was hard to believe, but by the time Mariano and Ange left, 90 percent of the young men in their town had already gone to America, almost all to Colorado. Lucca Sicula had turned into a sad village of starving women, old people, and orphans—with no prospects for the future. 

			
Odd that there hadn’t been many letters from those men who had gone to Colorado talking of their wonderful lives in that new country. Their wives would follow their men, but they didn’t write home either. However, most of the people could neither read nor write, so it wasn’t a worry. 

			
The padrones who still came recruiting would sometimes bring letters from the men who’d gone to Colorado and read them out loud. None of the letters sounded like they’d been dictated by their old cumpari because they were too flowery and used words none of them even knew. But the letters did say how wonderful life was in the new country and how they all had lots of land to call their own. Their families just figured the people who wrote the letters for them used fancier words because they knew how to read and write.

			
So, Mariano and Ange were filled with hope for the New World, yes, but they were scared and anxious too. What in God’s name were they in for? Were they on their way to paradise on earth or going to hell in a handbasket? They had heard that saying before but had no idea it had come from men being lowered into mines in baskets to plant dynamite. 

			
The La Barbera brothers walked, climbed hills and mountainsides, slept outside, and killed small game to feed themselves. Walking the beautiful hills and mountains made them worry even more that maybe they were making a big mistake, but they had no way to feed their families back in the village, no matter how beautiful even the somewhat drought stricken Italian scenery was. And besides, Saicchitano said they were going to the “Italy of America.” It would feel like home—but home without their wives and children for a while. 

			
Ange managed to get them past the hunger and thirst when there was no game to be found and no stream to drink from by asking crazy questions that made them both laugh. “Mariano, do you think Colorado cows make good Provolone?” “How do you say in American, ‘I would like to buy your mule and your wife?’” “Is there an ocean in Colorado? I was wondering because if there’s no ocean, there’s no sardines, and if there’s no sardines, how do they make Pasta con le Sarde?” 

			
“I don’t know if Colorado has an ocean, but I hear it has plenty of bears. We will have pasta con orso.” Mariano joked, pretending to shoot a bear.

			
And so, the brothers would laugh themselves silly, and for a few miles, they’d forget their troubles, forget how much they missed their wives, and focus on making it to Palermo in one piece. 

			
Once, when they were down to the last of the food without a rabbit in sight, Ange stopped dead in his tracks, put his hands on his hips, grabbed his brother, and began doing the tarantella. Then, at the top of his lungs he sang their favorite naughty ditty with verses that always ended up with something on the order of the baker holding his cannoli, or the musician holding his trumpet, and a girl’s mother warning that the man will always have “it” in his hand. 

			
Yeah, they knew it by heart, but it always made them—and all Italians—laugh like they’d never heard it before. 

			
What the brothers never lost was their determination—no matter how rough it got—to make it to a waiting ship in Naples. They’d get aboard that ship, and they’d get to the promised land—Colorado, America—by hook or by crook. They just didn’t know how true that saying was going to be. 

			
When the brothers finally reached the port city of Palermo, they found the padrone, Saicchitano, waiting on the docks, corralling the men he’d signed up. They all boarded the ferry steamer for the half day and overnight trip across the Tyrrhenian Sea. The overcrowded ferryboat hit a storm on the very first night which brought on giant waves. This put the fear of God into these landlubber smalltown mountain men who worried the ship would capsize or break apart. But after sleeping on the rocky ground during the terrible, seventy-five-mile trek from Lucca Sicula to Palermo, Ange joked that even if they drowned, at least they would drown while sitting on wooden benches. 

			
When they landed at the port of Naples, they were ushered into a long, red-brick building with more people than they’d ever seen in a single place before. Fights were breaking out, and men were screaming that they had been promised this, that, and the other thing but were being swindled. Women holding crying babies were trying to get their tickets, pickpockets were trying to pick pockets.

			
The La Barbera brothers were just two out of hundreds of Sicilian peasants in this mess who were waiting to board. After a very long day, they finally made their way to the gangplank of the passenger ship that was to take them to America. It wasn’t what you’d call a luxury liner—it was more like a floating shit box. The below-deck steerage where the padronis’ men were housed was filthy; slop buckets were always full; sea sickness and cacarella (the Tennessee trots) were as common as the rats that ran over them when they tried to sleep. Everybody’s head was so full of lice, they all looked like old men with grey hair. When rough seas hit, the ship’s sailors locked down the hatches, keeping those in steerage trapped down below. The lack of fresh air made the trots and seasickness even worse. Diseases spread. 

			
Each man had less than two square feet of space and were fed worse than dogs. All that was missing were chains to tie them down. Mariano realized right off that he probably wasn’t headed to the land of opportunity but was on a ship bound for hell. 

			
After a harrowing ten-day trip, the hundreds of Sicilians from the mountains who’d never been on the ocean before, were met at the docks in New York City. They prayed that all of their suffering was not in vain and they’d soon be kissing the beautiful, rich, black soil of Colorado. Mariano tried to believe the talk on the ship about how the number one place to go would be this Colorado, where farmland was fertile, beautiful, and free. And full of paisans—friends.

			
He tried to keep the faith even as they were shuffled through Ellis Island where they were inspected by doctors, numbered like cows going to slaughter, and registered by men who spoke no Italian. The man who registered the brothers chopped the “La” off their last names and changed the “Barbera” to “Barbarian.” “Mariano” was changed to “Marian” and “Arcangelo” to “Angela” on their official papers. Ange, looking at the nightmare that was the Ellis Island intake center, shook his head and joked, “Allura. Cristoforo Colombo havìa esseri nu pacciu.”—Columbus must have been crazy. Then he started singing, “Marian Barbarian….” 

			
The immigration inspectors set “Marian and Angela Barbarian” loose a few days later, where they were met at the exit by Saicchitano’s paid thugs—rough men who were rumored to be Black Hand. The brothers were placed in a horse-drawn wagon, with as many men as the carriage could fit, and hauled off to the rail yards. There, they were herded into boxcars guarded by the same gun-wielding thugs. It didn’t take long for somebody who still had some tobacco left to pay a guard, who told him that, no, they weren’t headed to a paradise of Colorado farmland but to the coal mines. 

			
The brothers chalked it up to rumors from men who knew nothing but spoke a lot.

			
“L’omu sta mintennu!”—The man is lying—Ange declared.

			
“For a pouch of tobacco right now,” Mariano said, “I would swear I’m the devil himself.”

			
“Plenty of tobacco where we’re going,” Ange the optimist added, pretending to smoke a pipe. “The land of plenty.”

– 6 –

			
The Actuality of Reality 

			
Trinidad, Colorado 

			
1909 

			
After hiking from Lucca Sicula to Naples, crossing the ocean to America in the belly of a boat, and finally riding in a cattle car for the last 1,700-mile trip from New York City to Denver, Mariano and Ange were anxious to put the hardship of the trip behind them. They were ready to do what they’d come here to do: work hard and farm the land.

			
Instead, “Marian” and “Angela” were dropped in Trinidad, Colorado, and taken to the Berwind Mines, which was a black and noisy mining town in the county of Las Animas. There wasn’t a farm in sight. The rumors had been true.

			
“Mama mia,” Ange cried out. 

			
It quickly became clear as the nose on Ange’s face—and nobody could miss that honker—that where they had been taken was not the paradise that the scum-bucket padrone had said it was, but a filthy, down-and-dirty shanty town. It was also a place where, in the not-too-distant future, a horrific massacre would occur—an event so shameful they kept it out of the history books. 

			
But for now, at least, the brothers were together. 

			
They were, however, having a hell of a time breathing. Ange thought it was a heart attack brought on from the stress of the trip or maybe from anger at being duped. But it turned out that because the air was so thin at an altitude of over 6,100 feet and so clogged with coal dust, it was nearly impossible for anyone to draw a breath. No wonder men there dropped faster than the mine shaft canaries. 

			
But how could this be? Why didn’t the La Barbera brothers know about this before they had left everything behind to come here? Why hadn’t any of the young fellas from their village who’d already immigrated to Colorado sent word that they were trapped in these hellholes? 

			
Worse, how would Mariano and Ange get in touch with their wives and tell them not to come, at least until they figured it all out? But it was too late for that even if they could get word back home, which they couldn’t. They had agreed in the contract that their families’ passages would be booked by the padrone and taken out of their future wages. 

			
The brothers could only hope there really were some men from their village here so they could get the straight skinny on what the hell was going on. With any luck those fellas hadn’t run off, been driven off, or killed. 

			
But how could they find them? There were so many men milling around—more men than they’d ever imagined could be in one town. There were races of men they’d never seen, speaking languages they’d never heard. By the time they arrived, there were twenty-four different ethnic, racial, and national groups speaking over three dozen languages.

			
They tried to pick out anyone speaking in their home dialect, all the while hoping they didn’t look as lost and scared as they really were. 

			
Over the racket, they heard Ange’s name called. The brothers looked at each other, and Mariano nudged him to raise his hand. They thought it might be a paisanu, from home, or at least someone who could help them. 

			
“Sono io!”—It’s me!—he called out.

			
Two men with mining badges and a little man with a book walked over to them. The badge men sized Ange up like he was a steer ready for slaughter, nodded their heads, and told him through the other man to report to the mine office. The book man, who spoke Italian, asked Ange to spell his name. Not only did Ange barely understand him (Sicilian was so different from what they called “high-class Italian” that it was itself recognized as a separate language), he couldn’t spell his name anyway. He’d never been taught. Instead, Ange said, “La Barbera, Arcangelo mi chiamu.”—My name is Arcangelo La Barbera. 

			
The man with the book went down the list and said he had an Angela Barbarian listed but no Arcangelo La Barbera. 

			
“Close enough,” one of the badge men said and showed Ange where to make his “X.” on the line. 

			
The bosses shoved Ange along to the mine shaft, and Mariano was left standing there more confused than ever. 

			
While Mariano waited to find out what he was supposed to do and where he was supposed to go, the sky opened up. He’d never seen rain like this. It was like something out of the Bible. His old fedora and threadbare jacket were as useless in the storm as a three-legged mule on a mountain. 

			
He had to find someone—anyone—he might know. There! Right there on the line. Wasn’t that Filippo Arcuri? That fella sure looked like Filippo, but it was hard to tell what with the rain and what he could see of the man’s face, which was black with coal dust and shadowed by the helmet on his head. But since Arcuri was a big man—six foot four if he was an inch—Mariano had a good feeling it was him. 

			
So, he called out, “Filippo! Filippo Arcuri!” and—dang if the man didn’t turn around. Without saying a word, the giant jumped off the line and reached Mariano in about twenty seconds, his stride being twice that of the other men. He lifted Mariano in a bear hug, kissing both sides of his face. In a second, other men skipped off the line, splashing their way through mud to greet them. 

			
They didn’t have to ask Mariano what he was doing there. They knew. 

			
Mariano, though, wasn’t so clear about what he was doing there himself, or about anything. So, he took off his fedora, slapped the puddles off it, and asked what the hell he was supposed to do besides stand there and wait. 

			
Arcuri put his massive wet arm around Mariano and patted his back, which Mariano knew was not a good sign. 

			
“Mariano,” he said, gesturing around them at the hundreds of men waiting amid the filth, mud, and splashing rain. “You do nothing,” he said in Sicilian. “Just wait to get called. And then you wait some more.” 

			
“Pi chi??”—For what? 

			
“To be picked to work your ass off in that shithole of a mine until you can’t breathe no more,” Roberto Candiano, another fella from back home chimed in, shaking his head in disgust. 

			
“This land of the free paisanu?” Arcuri added, “It ain’t so free.” The massive man, who was as tough as they come back home, didn’t look so tough anymore. He looked beaten. So did some of the other men who’d gathered around the newcomer. 

			
“What the hell did they do to you?” Mariano wanted to know. 

			
Arcuri spit on the ground. “Va Fangul Patruni!”—Fuck the padrones. Then another man who looked familiar, though Mariano couldn’t place him right away, reminded them all of the old Sicilian saying: “Si liccanu ‘I sarde e nun si li manciunu.”—[These people are so mean] they lick sardines instead of eating them. 

			
“What them son of a bitch patruni never tell you,” Arcuri added, “is that you are not free to live life like a man here in this cursed place.” 

			
Mariano didn’t know what the hell was going on. “Nun capisciu,”—I don’t understand.

			
The man he didn’t recognize earlier said, “My friend, you don’t remember me? Sono Tommaso Maniscalco.” Mariano was shocked. When Tommaso had left their town four years ago he was a kid. Now he looked forty. 

			
Tommaso continued, “At least you have a patruni, no matter how much of a rotten dog he is. Men who come here without a place to stay or a patruni get put to work as punishment with no pay at all!” 

			
Mariano realized no free farmland was coming to him anytime soon. OK, he’d work hard so he could buy it one day. “How much is the pay?” 

			
Arcuri answered, “Oh goombah. You don’t get paid right away. No, no, no. First you got to pay for these pants,” he said pointing to his legs. “Doongareece they call them.” 

			
“I saved up and bought this helmet to keep my head from getting bashed in,” Arcuri said, pointing to his headgear. “Do you have a heavy jacket for the cold and work gloves?” 

			
“No. I just have the clothes on my back and what’s left in my pouch.” He pointed to the bindle on the end of a stick. “Where do you buy such things?” 

			
“The company store,” Candiano said. “They don’t pay us in American money—they pay only in company money, which is only good at the company store.” 

			
Mariano was shocked. “Gesù Cristi Maria! You pay the bosses back with their own money to work here?” 

			
“Cettu. E chistu è nenti, cumpari.”—Sure. And this is nothing my friend. 

			
“Comu nenti?”—Really, that’s nothing?

			
Arcuri nodded his head in disgust. “The winters are freezing with snowstorms, sometimes avalanches near the mines. Then there’s diseases like the black lung that, I swear, kill off half the men.” 

			
Mariano made the sign of the cross. 

			
“Oh,” said Arcuri. “Another thing: You must also pay off what you owe your padrone before you can even leave here.” 

			
“Pay that stugots? For what?” 

			
“Your passage.”

			
“He said it was free! They want us to pay for a rotten boat and a stinking cattle car with vomit, shit, and piss all over the floor and spoiled horse meat for food?” 

			
“Si, paisan.”

			
“This is what they call the land of the free?” Mariano cried. 

			
Arcuri snorted a bitter laugh and shook his head. “It’s a lie. Just a filthy lie of the rich, white men who run things in this country.” 

			
“Let me ask you, my friend, what job do you have so you could pay for…” he pointed to Arcuri’s filthy work clothes. 

			
“I’m a coal heaver,” Arcuri said. 

			
“Che cosa?”—What is that?

			
“I shovel and carry coal. The bosses want seventy ton a day out of a mine. Me? I heave maybe three ton a day. We get paid by the ton. I do more than my share, but for that I only make twenty dollars in company money a month. Part goes to pay back the patruni, five dollars for rent to the company for a bed in a shack, and then there’s food. Nothing much left to send back to my wife. Even if there is anything left, I can’t send her the company money—it’s called ‘scrip’—so I have to sell the scrip to the money traders for American dollars. Of course, they give us a shit rate and charge to send the money. I don’t know if she ever gets it.” 

			
Mariano tried to contain his rage. If he started yelling, they’d throw him out for sure—and then what? He tried to keep his voice down. “Cu jè chiddu chi cumanna tutti ‘nta miniera ‘i carbuni?”—Who’s the big boss of the mine? Maybe he could talk to him personally. 

			
Arcuri let out a roaring laugh. “The big boss? Ha! John D. Rockefeller, Jr. He don’t live here, and even if he did, you couldn’t talk to him.”

			
“They call him ‘Rocky,’ and he’s a bastard that one,” Candiano said and spit on the ground. “He’s got boys five years old working down there. The kids say they are older, and nobody asks nothing.” 

			
“Five? How can they—” 

			
Arcuri cut him off. “Did you know there was a mine collapse last week that killed a boy?” 

			
Mariano shook his head, not sure he wanted to hear anymore. 

			
But Arcuri went right ahead anyway. “You remember Little Sal—Salvatore Amato’s boy? He was probably ten years old. The kid was working as a breaker boy.” 

			
“A what?” 

			
“The kids too young for mining work, they break coal into pieces with their hands, sometimes fourteen hours a day. Not allowed to wear gloves so they can handle the coal better. When Little Sal’s hands got too cut up and burnt from the acid in the rock, they sent him into the mine to bring down some canaries.”

			
“Birds? They send down birds?” Mariano asked, confused.

			
“Si. If the canaries die, it means the air is bad,” Candiano answered.

			
Arcuri continued, “But when the boy went down into the mine, it collapsed. Killed him.” 

			
Candiano threw his hands up, disgusted. “They handed that boy’s body back to Big Sal like he was a sack of coal.” 

			
The giant took off his helmet, bowed his head, and rubbed his eyes. “Futtini!”—Fuck them.

			
Was Arcuri crying? No, it couldn’t be. 

			
“Coal dust,” he said, “Just coal dust in my eye.” 

			
“Ma picchì nuddu mi dissi comu minchia era ‘cca?”—Why the fuck didn’t nobody tell me what this was like?

			
“Come mai?”—How come?—“I think the boss—curnutu!—threw away my letters to my family telling them the truth.” 

			
Mariano was taken by surprise—not at the shitty boss ripping up the letters but that Arcuri could read and write. “Sai leggere e scrivere?”—You know how to read and write?

			
Any man who could read could own the world. Maybe the American white men taught reading and writing instead of good pay? 

			
“No. No,” Arcuri answered. “One of the Italian wives writes them for us. We didn’t hide nothing from nobody, but we don’t think them letters ever get sent. Them mine bosses would never let the people back home know what it’s like here or nobody would come. That’s why there are so many men from Lucca here. The strong ones at least—the ones they think won’t die on them. These bastards even have a name for this kind of work. They call it ‘indentured.’” 

			
“Minchia! I thought the president of America was a good man,” Mariano said, sticking his fingers together in the pinecone hand gesture. “Indentured? Che diavolo?”—What the hell?

			
Arcuri shook his head, patted Mariano’s shoulder, and turned to go back to the work line. “Veni la rivuluzzioni cumpari.”—Come the revolution, friend. 

			
He squared his shoulders, shook off the rain like a dog who’d fallen into the river, and headed back, ready to go down into the mine and face whatever was waiting for him that day. The others followed. 

			
It didn’t take a genius to figure out that “indentured” was just a new moniker for an old game: slavery. Whatever they called it didn’t change the fact that coal turned to gold for this Rocky fella back in New York, and coal turned to shit for the miners up there in Colorado. 

			
Mariano stood in the middle of the muddy street in Rockefeller’s shanty town next to the mine and looked at his future. The sewer-less street was overflowing with mud, human and animal waste, coal wash, and rats. He had been hoodwinked, bamboozled, conned. 

			
The actuality of the reality, as they say, washed over him just like the coal wash and shit floating down the muddy road.

– 7 –

			
Big Sea, Big Lies

			
Sicily to NYC 

			
1909

			
Three months later, Maria, and Ange’s wife, Rosaria, kids in tow, boarded a ship to meet their men. Maria felt that whatever the new world would bring, it would all be fine because she’d be with the man she loved. The padrone had promised safe passage to a great new world, and Mariano had believed him, so it must be true.

			
Their accommodations on the ship were no better than their husbands had been, and as ladies with children, it was even worse in many ways. 

			
When the young mothers boarded the ship and went down, down, down into the belly of the beast—and it took a bit of time for their eyes to adjust in the dark with just a few swinging lanterns for light—they hoped what they saw wasn’t real. 

			
But it was real, and it scared them plenty. The steerage deck, which was below the waterline, was divided into three sections: single men, families, and single women. But that didn’t stop those varmints from venturing onto the ladies’ side. Along both sides were double rows of bunks with a small aisle down the middle. There was a tiny water closet at both ends—one for men, one for women. 

			
For drinking, there were barrels of “fresh” water, which had to last for the eight days of the journey. The steerage passengers said the water smelled like pickles, which was true because captains of these hell-on-water vessels would add vinegar to the barrels to disguise the stink of rotten water. 

			
Just as bad, the rowdy emigrant men started fighting, screaming, and cussing even before the ship had left the harbor. 

			
Rosaria tied her baby up in a sling and told Maria she was not going to let the infant out of the sling, except to change her dirty diaper until they landed. And she didn’t. 

			
They had been told to bring their own bedding, and Maria had proudly included the beautiful Italian linens that she and her mother had embroidered for her marriage bed. They realized right off that this wasn’t the place for fine linens. Hell, they were lucky if they could find a cot to sleep on—and those they could find were full of bed bugs and God knew what else. 

			
Their mothers raised them up to be good and proper brides and housewives—women who kept their homes, poor as they were, spotlessly clean. They had never seen filth like this. 

			
There were bunks for the adults but none for the children; not that they’d leave them to sleep alone on this dangerous deck. They decided one mother would sleep, and one would stay awake to prevent them from being attacked, raped, or robbed. Women were considered fair game for both the sailors above and the men below. 

			
On night two, a storm as fierce as any the crew had experienced blew in, and the sailors battened down all the hatches. The steerage passengers were locked in, trapped, tumbled, and terrified. The children screamed and clung to their mothers. The boat heaved all night and most of the following day like it was about to capsize. Maria, Rosaria, and the rest had no way of knowing what the hell was happening. There was no fresh air to breathe, only the vomit-filled stench of the seasick passengers. There was no means of escape should the boat sink. 

			
The terrified screams of the trapped peasants sounded like souls in purgatory begging the Lord to set them free. They managed to get through the night and half day of terror and then sometime the next afternoon, the hatches were opened. The passengers rushed the ladders, and the crew threatened to lock them in for the rest of the voyage if they didn’t get back down. They could just die down there for all the sailors cared. They then announced that it was up to the passengers to mop up the floors of vomit, diarrhea, and piss. They were to hand the buckets back up to the crew, and the fetid mess would be tossed overboard. 

			
Because of the filth, there was fear of typhoid, cholera, and pneumonia. Two infants died in the first few days, followed by below-deck funerals with no priest present. After they said prayers, sailors came down and took those poor dead babies, wrapped them in some kind of cloth, and tossed them over the side like chum.

			
What hell were their husbands bringing them to? The only hope was that a wonderful new life was waiting for them in the New World. 

			
When the ship docked more than eight days after it had left Italy, the sisters-in-law were met at Ellis Island by the middleman’s middleman, put into quarantine with thousands of other immigrants for a week, questioned by men who spoke no Sicilian let alone Italian, numbered, examined again by doctors who touched them in places no men but their husbands had ever touched, and registered them. Three Sicilian women they’d become friendly with on the boat were classified as “undesirable” and denied admittance to the United States. Le donne La Barbera never saw their new friends again. 

			
After an agonizing wait in confinement at Ellis Island, would they be found “undesirable” too? They finally were granted admittance and hustled out to where the padrone’s thugs were waiting to load them onto the standing-room-only trans-continental railroad to Denver. 

			
When the train finally arrived, they unloaded their bags and waited in the vastness of the railroad station overnight. They found benches to sleep on until the train to Trinidad arrived in the morning. They boarded with dozens of new miners and wives of miners like themselves for the rest of the 116-mile trip to the coal town. The children were so exhausted and miserable that they cried non-stop.

			
“Is this just a sign of things to come?” Rosaria worried.

			
“No!” Maria tried to comfort her sister-in-law. “Aviamu na bona vita ntô Colorado.”—We will have a good life in Colorado. 

			
“Come inizi, così finisci,” Rosaria snapped. As you start, so you finish. 

			
It wasn’t optimistic, but it was truthful. And correct. 

			
When they arrived at the mining town, Maria and Rosaria saw not farmland but black smoke belching from furnaces and streets running with mud and sewage. Bedraggled miners carrying pickaxes were coming and going as unhealthy-looking children wandered aimlessly or sat outside broken down shacks with no windows. 

			
They finally saw their husbands, standing with their hats in hand, heads down, shame written over every inch of them. 

			
Maria and Rosaria ran to them, kids in tow, and kissed them all over their dirty faces. The men fought back tears as they loaded their wives and kids onto a borrowed buckboard for the short ride to their new home. 

			
The town reminded them of the hell ship—rough-housing workers yelling, filthy conditions, and scavenging vermin. This town, as they liked to call it, didn’t have streets, just mud ruts made by coal carts and horse-drawn wagons. Their new home near the mining site was a run-down, long, narrow log house with one window. 

			
It was not paradise. It was not even Sicily in the worst of times.

– 8 –

			
Beast on Beast 

			
By the Purgatory River

			
1914–1916

			
After coming to Colorado, the fire between Maria and Mariano had gone out. He was worked like a pack animal in the mine, never bringing home enough to keep his family fed. His back was starting to bend, and his hands and face were always blackened from coal. Lovemaking became brutal, rough, and necessary to keep Mariano going. 

			
In the intervening years, things went from bad to unbearable. They had tried their best, but nothing got better. Nothing could get better—not under these conditions.

			
The more Mariano and Ange slaved in Rocky’s mines, the more money they seemed to owe Rocky’s company. Changing company scrip into cold hard cash was a losing proposition. “And we thought the strozzino back in the old country were thieves,” Ange would moan, referring to the money lenders who charged impossibly high rates to the destitute farmers. 

			
“We have to do something. We have to fight for more money and a better way of life. We mean nothing to these ’Merigaans who own us. And they do own us, make no mistake,” Mariano would reply, slamming his right fist into his left palm.

			
But Maria didn’t complain. Who could she complain to? Who could she tell that, as miserable as life had been in Sicily after the bad times had hit, nothing had been as brutal as this? 

			
And yet she continued to have babies. Of course, the babies had not been planned, but not unwanted either. She didn’t even know there was such a thing as birth control, and if she had, she would have thought it was against God’s wishes. The first time she’d ever even been kissed or touched by a man was on her wedding night. What she knew about sex was that when a man wanted you, he had you. Even after it stopped being the best part of life. If you got pregnant, you prayed for a boy. She’d been told by her sister, Cologera, before Maria and Mariano got hitched, that sex was not something to be enjoyed but something to be endured. After Maria and Mariano made love for the first time, she worried that her sister was crazy because for her, at least, sex turned out to be the most exciting and naughtiest experience of her young life. 

			
Once she got to America, though, Maria began to understand the “endured” part. The only thing, however, was that sex did bring the chance of another baby, which was God’s blessing no matter how much a family struggled to survive. During the ninth month of her twelfth pregnancy, Maria started bleeding heavily. 

			
Her friend, Francesca Lanzone, who spoke a little English, rushed Maria to the local hospital owned by Colorado Iron and Fuel, which had been set up for employees of the company. At that time, Mariano was on a company watch list of men who had been stirring up discontent among the miners. As the wife of a known malcontent, Maria could have bled to death outside that hospital for all the doctors, nurses, and volunteer ladies (many of whom were wives of the mine managers) cared. 

			
Francesca would never normally have gone to the hospital and would have called upon one of the Sicilian ladies who birthed babies to help, but there wasn’t time. It wouldn’t have mattered anyway. It was already too late. The baby was stillborn. Francesca handed the dead infant to Maria in the cold metal hospital bed. The baby’s curls circled her tiny, perfect head like a halo. 

			
The midwife who was in charge of the birthing unit hated immigrants, especially “them WOPs.” She was so repulsed by them that she kept a copy in her pocket of a famous newspaper article to justify her hatred. The article called Southern Italian immigrants, “Swarthy, kinky-haired criminals.” 

			
That midwife had seen red when Maria and Francesca—two Guinea dagos yet—first entered the hospital. It didn’t matter that the mother was about to give birth or might be bleeding to death. She wasn’t going to make Maria’s agony any easier. 

			
Looking at Maria holding her dead infant, the midwife told her to stop carrying on. Babies died. Period. Even those that didn’t come out dead, she said, wouldn’t live long. “A quarter of ’em die in this town before their first birthday anyways.” And that was a fact. 

			
She gave Maria a bunch of rags to put between her legs and said, “Move along. We’ve got babies to care for.” As Maria struggled to get up from the bed, Francesca cringed with shame when the midwife said, “Those Guinea sows breed like animals. They’re the real Guinea pigs.” And then she laughed like she was as funny as Tom Major-Ball. 

			
Her next baby, the wild child Firenze, was born after they’d lost their home, while Maria was still mourning the loss of her precious baby-doll, whom she’d named Marian after her husband. She’d lost babies before—four in fact. But the others had been lost earlier in pregnancy and were not fully formed or ready to start living. 

			
When Firenze was born one year and two months later, Maria vowed to the Lord and the newest baby girl in her arms, that even though the devil wolf had come to her birthing and she clearly had the sign of wolf upon her, she would fight to the death to protect her from anything and anyone who would try to harm her—including Mariano. 

			
She didn’t care that Firenze had plainly been born different or looked different with those bright-red baby lips. 

			
What Firenze’s papa didn’t know at the time of her birth was just how different she was from the rest. But he’d learn.

			
Firenze was clearly born an alpha. Powerful. Ferocious. Determined to live. She would not become her papa’s prey. 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 9 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 9 –

			
Strike a Blow 

			
Berwind to Ludlow, Colorado 

			
1914 

			
So, how in hell did a good man’s soul rot and die on the vine as Mariano’s had? Why did Maria’s once-loving husband turn into a snake with no soul—but plenty of venom? Call it a slow death by rich, white men. 

			
Soon after he arrived in Colorado, Mariano started drinking more than he ever had back in the old country—mostly his own homebrew—which he and Maria made out in the woods. He even sold a bit of it to his friends for extra pocket change. It was a shame how low a man with such promise had fallen. 

			
Farming and tending his vines, animals, and crops from dawn to dusk had been his way of life back home, so it wasn’t as though he was afraid of hard work. But dawn to dusk in the pit of the earth was not a job fit for man nor beast. He bucked against the yoke of being owned by this John D. Rockefeller, Jr. fella whom he’d never even seen. He felt that, in America, he was nothing more than a beast of burden with no future. 

			
Maria thought the mine’s mighty bell, which was rung each day at 4:00 a.m. to wake the miners, was as different from the sweet sounds of the church bells in Sicily as heaven was from hell. 

			
Ange, who could always find the good in the bad, and the joke in the misery would say, “In America, the church bells are played by devils, and in Sicily by the saints.” Then he’d run around covering his ears like a clown. 

			
No matter how long they’d worked there, going down into the darkened mine chambers was always a shock to the system. 

			
The tops and sides of the mine shafts were held up by logs, so being crushed to death in a mine collapse or killed in an explosion were more than a worry, they were a reality.

			
One morning, as Mariano and Ange approached the mine they heard a mighty roar and felt the ground beneath them shake. 

			
“What the hell?” Ange screamed as he and Mariano ran, without thinking towards the mine. What they found was chaos and smoke. Men were stampeding out of the shaft and screaming. Some were badly burned, others bleeding. 

			
Mariano and Ange volunteered to try to rescue trapped miners. 

			
They were taken down into the belly of the mine and worked their way through tangled debris, clearing the ground and rebuilding walls as they went. They found four bodies, but they were nearly unrecognizable from being so badly burnt and mangled. They knew the men: Juan Suarez and Liberio D’Antonio, and Juan’s sons, twelve-year-old Enrique and fifteen-year-old Manolo. 

			
Mariano and Ange found the wives and children of their dead friends, who were gathered with all the other mining families, hoping for word. Even in this land where the nearly unbearable was normal, the news was devastating. Maria and Rosaria cooked meals and tended to the widows’ children, but there was no consoling them. All told, ten men died that day.

			
Colorado Fuel and Iron paid for the funerals with great fanfare. A week later, each family was given one hundred dollars—and an eviction notice. They had nowhere to go, and they didn’t read, write, or speak the language. As they left on foot with whatever belongings they had, the tragedy was such that even those women whose husbands and sons had been spared, screamed, wept, and ripped at their clothing. 

			
Is that the fate that awaits us as well? Maria and Rosaria asked themselves.

			
When it was printed in the newspaper a few weeks later that the explosion had been caused by a mine inspector who’d tried to relight his safety lamp and had set the methane on fire, the miners gathered in rage to protest.

			
Mariano and Filippo started a word-of-mouth campaign for a work stoppage. They managed to convince about a quarter of the miners to refuse to go down. But without pay, and under the threat of losing their jobs, the protest ended in a few days. Mariano’s rage did not.

			
But this was the lot the La Barbera brothers had signed on for. They worked heaving coal from dawn to dusk, hoping they’d escape the fate that had befallen so many of their brother miners. No one could understand a word that the guy next to him was speaking; misery was their universal language. 

			
The difference between Mariano and most of the other miners, was that Mariano wasn’t beaten, he was angry. Always angry.

			
Broke as he was, most Saturday nights, Mariano would comb his hair, grab the dollar he’d made the previous week at the card tables, and make his way to Mrs. Bianchi’s saloon. Ange usually stayed home and played with his growing family, but Mariano and his friends would gamble, drink, and scheme deep into the night about how they’d get even or kill their bosses.

			
“They work us to death or kill us with unsafe mines. Is that not murder?” Mariano would rage. “An eye for an eye, I say.” 

			
Filippo, who never missed a Saturday night, would shake his head and remind Mariano of how their work stoppage had failed, saying, “Cumpari! La pinna è fatta pî ricchi, ma pâ testa dû viddanu.”—The gallows are not made for the rich, but for the head of the peasant. 

			
Mariano would counter, “A peasant now, but not for long, goombah, not for long.” No one had any idea what the hell he meant, since every last man among them knew that fighting against all the power in the world was impossible. Would he really kill a manager? Then what would happen to his family?

			
The more down in their cups they got, the more they’d talk about how the white ’Merigaan ruled this land and how the Italians, the Greeks, the Blacks, and every other minority were their prey. 

			
But then, one night in 1913, as the usual men were sitting around the card table, Enrico Anastasi, a coal heaver who had been a professor in Sicily, walked in, slammed his palm down on the card table and announced that hope was on the way. 

			
He said word was spreading like a wildfire in a drought that an outfit called the United Mine Workers of America had shown up at nearby mines and were talking strike. In fact, he said, this group was hitting mining towns all across the country. The impossible was suddenly possible, instead of just the usual drunk talk.

			
Anastasi said there was strength in numbers. He had, he whispered, even joined the union already. 

			
The union organizers, he continued, had been holding rallies where men who sounded like preachers told miners that they could—had to—strike for better wages and safer mines. In fact, there was a rally of Italian miners coming up right near town. 

			
Mariano’s reaction? He slammed his glass down and said, “Deal the cards. Promises? Promises in America are as worthless as padruni contracts.” 

			
That first union rally a few weeks later was held in a tent outside of town and was filled with miners who wanted to know how they could possibly improve their sorry lot. Arcuri dragged the brothers with him. Mariano went with no expectations, but it was better than being home with his brood of hungry kids.

			
They arrived early enough to find seats in what became a standing-room-only crowd. A Sicilian speaker from the union, Enrico Scuderi, began by talking of the most recent tragedy, which the miners were still mourning.

			
Then he held up The New York Evening Post. He jabbed his finger over and over into the story and said, “The writer of this filth calls Italian immigrants ‘childlike, dependent, ignorant, and helpless.’ That’s what these rich mine owners back in New York City think of you,” he shouted, spitting on the ground. 

			
After pausing for effect, he asked, “Do you know the names Antonio Lorenzo, Stanislaso Vittani, Francisco Ronchetto, Pietro Giacabino, and Antonio Zapetto?”

			
The crowd shook their heads, shouting, “No!”

			
So Suderi explained. “Those were five Italian miners from the coal mine in Walsenberg, not twenty-five miles from here. They were murdered in cold blood by a group of ’Merigaan vigilantes who killed them after a friend of theirs, a saloon keeper, was killed in a fight.

			
“Later that night, these same ’Merigaans stormed the jail where the last two Italian miners were held and shot them to death like rabid dogs! The killers were never charged. And who owned the mine where these Italian miners had slaved? Colorado Fuel! Another Rockefeller mine. To him, to his managers, we Italians are not even human. We are just slaves who can be replaced when they kill us.”

			
The crowd started shouting their outrage, and Suderi hit them with a big one. “You’re shocked? That was the third deliberate massacre of Italians here since 1891.”

			
The miners were shocked and also perplexed. Who had been killed and why?

			
“And do you know what The New York Times, the famous newspaper in New York run by the friends of John D. Rockefeller, Jr. said about such crimes? About our kind? They said Italian immigrants, are ‘sneaky and cowardly descendants of bandits and assassins!’” He paused and added, “They said, ‘as good as rattlesnakes!’” 

			
The crowd was on their feet yelling, “Semu frati!” “Sfacchima!” “Tu sì mortu!” “Cazzo ê capi!” (“We’re brothers!” “Fuck off!” “You’re dead!” “Fuck the bosses!”)

			
It was very loud, none of it was good, nor would any of it make much sense if translated into English. 

			
To the amazement of Mariano and his group, Anastasi was called to speak. He talked of how it wasn’t just one newspaper with a hatred for Italians. He said another newspaper had called all Italian workers, “herds of steerage swine!” Anastasi was, after all, a respected professor, a learned man who probably had more education than those rich, white bigots in New York.

			
The crowd was up on their feet, shouting. Knowing this kind of passion could degenerate very quickly into a brawl, Anastasi gestured with his hands and asked them to sit and let him finish. “We don’t need to live like this! It’s in our heritage,” he told them, slamming his fist on the podium. “We are fighters. Our ancestors in Sicily were warriors against their oppressors. That’s what they lived for. That’s what many died for! Should we be less?” 

			
The crowd roared its approval, but the truth is, few of them had any real notion of their history. So, he gave them an earful of pride. They had heard the name “Mafia” back in the old county, but only as some loose group of criminal thugs located mostly in Palermo. 

			
The strikers knew about those brigands, but they didn’t know anything, really, about the origins of the Mafia—or any of what he was about to tell them. 

			
Like a man telling a bedtime story, he told them about the Sicilian rebels of the Middle Ages. “They stood against foreign conquerors like the Saracens, the Normans, and the Spaniards,” he said. “La Mafiusu—the boldness—that’s what folks called ‘em. They fought against their oppressors like we must fight against our American oppressors! They organized so they could fight against the rich, brutal landowners just as we must fight against the rich, brutal mine owners who have oppressed us. We must be like the Mafia of old!”

			
Then, as loud as he possibly could over the din, he cried, “Megghiu muriri ‘ntra li granfi di lu liuni chi sutta la cuda di l’asin.”—Better to die in the clutches of the lion than under the tail of a jackass. 

			
The crowd’s roar could be heard all the way to the fancy homes of the mine managers in town. Like in the stages of an opera, Anastasi calmed them down again. 

			
Assuming a professorial tone, he said Sicily had survived no matter how many times it had been invaded because of strong, proud, fighting men. Men like they could become.

			
Anastasi had touched these men in a place that hadn’t been touched since they had boarded those devil ships—their pride. 

			
He also made it very clear that La Mafiusu of old was not what they now knew as “The Black Hand.” The Black Hand, he said, were the lowdown bastardi. Sure, some of them had immigrated to places like New Orleans, New York City, and Colorado and brought the name with them. They were nothing more than disorganized thugs, extortionists, and blackmailers. Mostly though, they were just dimwits. They’d leave extortion notes with black handprints on them, threatening to kill families if the marks failed to pay up.

			
When the alleged Black Handers showed up to collect, their intended victims would hide, take aim, and shoot them to death instead. 

			
“Dumb as a box of rocks,” Anastasi said to a hall full of guffaws. 

			
When all was said and done, it was a night to remember. The United Mine Workers representatives gave these men hope that there was a way out of the bondage they’d found themselves in. They could once again become men of honor.

			
It had been perfectly, operatically orchestrated—sonata, followed by a slow movement, followed by a minuet, ending in a rousing finale: “Ma-fi-a! Ma-fi-a!”

			
Of course, they were nowhere near becoming La Mafiusu of old, or even strikers, but they were on fire with hope. Hope is a powerful weapon, and even a sliver of it can be more powerful than anything the rich men in New York, with all their firepower, could destroy. 

			
Mariano was hooked. The more speeches he attended, week after week, the more convinced he was that they could be as fearless as the warriors and knights of the ancient order of Mafia.

			
Mariano and Ange attended every meeting and vowed, along with their paisanu, that they’d fight to the death for a decent living wage, safer mines, and payments to the families of men who were crippled or killed. They’d fight so their own wives and children wouldn’t have to live like beasts—ten or twenty crammed into a long shack, scrounging for food and dying young.

			
Those speeches did their job, enraging Sicilian miners into a united, if unruly, mob. They lined up by the hundreds to join the union and to strike.

			
The UMWA also rallied the descendants of former slaves and the other immigrant miners from the Mexicans to the Irish, Germans, Chinese, British, and Greeks—until thousands of miners became proud union men. 

			
And then they struck.
