– 10 –

			
How to Kill WOPs, Wogs, Women, Wetbacks, and Wee Ones

			
(And Create the Mafia by Mistake)

			
Ludlow, Colorado

			
April 20, 1914

			
Nobody outside the camps ever believed a wild mob of immigrant “wops, wogs, spicks, micks, afros, and cholos” as the mine bosses called them, could be a match for Rockefeller. 

			
But the strikers believed it. What they didn’t understand in their riled-up state, however, was that Rocky wasn’t a man to take shit from nobody, as they say. Keeping the union out of the mines would be worth it in the end whatever the cost in the present. 

			
True to his word, he hired goons from an outfit called Baldwin-Felts Detective Agency to break the strike—and if they broke the backs and heads of strikers who got in the way, well, that was just the price they’d pay for walking off the job. 

			
It wasn’t too long before an eighty-three-year-old lady, a famous reformer called “Mother Jones,” showed up in Trinidad and began encouraging the strikers to fight on. The law arrested her almost immediately “for her own protection.” They threw her in a hospital with bars on the windows and guards all around. 

			
Her lockup got the strikers so enraged that 1,000 of them met at union headquarters with Mariano, Ange, Filippo, and Gaetano among them. The strikers had been warned by a general of the Colorado militia, John Chase, that if they took to the streets, blood would be shed. Men would die. A union representative told them flat out that the US government had no problem killing men who disobeyed the rules. Not the law, mind you, but the rules. 

			
So, the strikers’ wives organized and marched in their stead. Maria was so angry at what mining had done to her once-loving husband (and what she feared would happen to her sons), she decided to join the march and bring the children with her. She asked Rosaria to come with her. “Even guards wouldn’t hurt children,” she said. She was wrong. 

			
By the time they joined the march it had grown to 400 women and children. The marchers were met by General John Chase’s cavalry regiment. The clamor and high-pitched yelling and singing of the women and children spooked his horse. That war horse had seen battle, blood, and bullets, but it had probably never seen hundreds of angry ladies before. The beast reared up and threw that general in his fancy uniform clear off his saddle and right into the mud. In front of God and everyone. 

			
The roar of women and children laughing and heckling went down the line from front to back like a wave. That general’s pride was torn like a Confederate dollar bill. He remounted with as much dignity as a mud-covered officer could muster. Furious, he did the unthinkable for a so-called “warrior gentleman.” He raised his arm and ordered the cavalry to draw their sabers and rifles with bayonets bared and charge the women and children. It was a disgrace to the uniform they wore.

			
The marchers fell back screaming, cursing, and throwing rocks at the militiamen. Both Rosaria’s and Maria’s arms got slashed, while their friend Francesca’s ear was nearly severed. The guards who stood outside the hospital would not allow the injured women and children to enter for treatment. 

			
When Maria returned home, battered and cut up, and told Mariano what had happened and how others had been “Chicagoed” (beaten—to within an inch of their lives), Mariano went mad. It was not just Maria’s injuries that drove him crazy, it was also the great injury to his manhood that could not be tolerated. The cavalry had disrespected him by treating his wife like a puttana—a whore. Whatever small reservations Mariano may once have had about trying to make peace with the mine owners disappeared like snow in the spring. UMWA would now become so important to him, and he was so sure they could win this strike that he volunteered to become a union organizer.

			
What had started out as a strike for a decent living wage turned into what would be called The Colorado Coalfield War. Union agitators—Ange among the first—got the boot. He and his family were thrown out of the company shack they shared with Mariano, Maria, and the kids. They moved to the union-sponsored tent city of Ludlow, where 1,200 other fired strikers and their families would eventually be holed up. In all, there must have been over thirty different languages spoken by these families including Italian, Greek, Mexican, Shoshoni, Irish, and English among the folks who were black, white, and brown. 

			
Rosaria cried every night after she’d put her children (there were three now) to bed on the tent’s cold, wood plank floor. She’d plead with Ange to beg for his job back and quit the damned strike. But he couldn’t go against Mariano. He’d be called a scab, and he’d be an outcast and a disgrace among the other immigrant miners, especially those from Lucca Sicula. When Mariano finally did get the big boot, the union rewarded his loyalty by moving him and his brood to a communal bunkhouse, miles from Ludlow. He was proud. Maria wasn’t. She felt that living there while her sister-in-law and children were in a tent miles away made the nightmare even worse, if possible. Mariano, enraged that she didn’t share his union pride and embarrassed because he knew she saw him as a failure, hit her and shoved her to the floor one night in a rage. It was the first time he’d been violent with her. Ashamed but prideful nonetheless, he told her she should thank that “Jesus god of hers” they had a roof over their heads. 

			
She didn’t dare fight back—he was her husband after all. She had no defenses. Yet. 

			
Even though the union organizers had thought the strikers would win their demands quickly because the mines couldn’t operate without them, the strike dragged on. And on. The La Barberas, like all the other striking families, were living on union rations. 

			
John D. Rockefeller, Jr. was not a happy man. In fact, he was so disgusted and angry that the goons he’d hired hadn’t ended the strike, he went ahead and did what rich men do: He made a deal with the government. In this case, the governor of Colorado, Elias Milton Ammons, who allowed Rocky to hire the Colorado National Guard using his own private money. The Guardsmen—even though they were part of the US military—joined up with the bums from Baldwin-Felts and became Rocky’s private army. Still the strike continued.

			
When Ange and Mariano got together from time to time, Ange told him the Guards who drove around the striker’s camp had mounted a turret machine gun on top of an armored car they called “The Death Special.” He said those goons would get into the camp in the middle of the night and fire that machine gun around the tents, paying no mind to who they could have hit. Mariano, who had grown up surrounded by corruption in Sicily, was not surprised that the army could be bought. He was devastated nonetheless to know they’d come so far for the same old shit.

			
Mariano gave Ange a six-shooter that he’d stolen from the stash in the union shack. “Be careful. But kill them if they try to kill you or hurt your family,” he warned. 

			
Ange said he had every intention of doing just that. “No one,” he declared, pointing the gun for effect, “will hurt my family. I am like Mafia!” 

			
For Rocky’s militia men, though, shooting those miners at night was like shooting fish in a barrel. So, Ange and the other strikers’ families came up with an ingenious way—they thought—to keep the wives and kids out of the line of fire. They dug trenches under their tents so families could take cover under the wooden floors when the Death Special machine gun started its nightly bombardment. They lay under the boards in the wet mud, shivering and miserable, but at least safe from bullets. 

			
Whenever Ange got discouraged—and even though the mine was hardly operating at that point—he thought about talking to other strikers about going back to work to end the “madness” as Rosaria called it. But Mariano would shame him out of it. He demanded that Ange never give in, no matter what. “Remember what you said? We are like Mafia!” Mariano’s words rang in Ange’s ears. He wasn’t a coward. He wasn’t a scab. But he was like Mafia! 

			
Finally, on the night of April 20, 1914, hundreds of striking miners of every color and creed in tent city, driven to outrage, decided to fight back. Hard. Nobody knows for sure who shot first, but for fourteen straight hours, the state militia fired their machine guns at the striking miners, and the miner’s fired back. The Greek strike leader Louis Tikas was shot in the back and killed. 

			
Ange killed an agent of the federal government and at least a few militiamen. This gun battle was only the first of many shootouts that would make the La Barbera family famous…or maybe “infamous” would be a better word.

			
The families, including Rosaria and the kids, hiding under the tent that night, were scared out of their minds. 

			
Rosaria hugged the children and tried to keep them quiet. “We can’t let the bad men know we are here, or they could shoot us,” she whispered to them to please not make any noise. “Per favore, per favore, miei bellissimi figli—Please, please, my beautiful children…shhhh.”

			
When her littlest one, Giuliana, started to whimper, Rosaria did the unthinkable and covered her mouth with her hand. It was hard enough to breathe under the tent, and this made it worse for the tot. But it was the only way, she believed, to keep them safe. She was wrong.

			
They had no way of knowing what was happening outside. Nobody seemed to know—even the ones outside doing the fighting. But sometime during the night—and Arcuri and Candiano bore witness to it—Rocky’s hired men went crazy with rage. They brought in giant cans and ran from tent to tent pouring oil on top of the canvas tents, then set them ablaze, creating an inferno of death with women and children trapped underneath.

			
Rosaria smelled the smoke, but because they were hiding under the floor of their tent, she didn’t know it had been set on fire. There was such chaos, and the sounds of people screaming in agony that it was impossible to know anything except that she had to stay under the tent to keep her children safe. It would have been impossible to even imagine that their tents were deliberately being set on fire with families trapped below. When their tent began to burn on top of them, it was too late to escape.

			
Rosaria and her three helpless children, Roberto, Giuliana, and Umberto, along with dozens of others were burnt alive like roasted pigs in a pit. The news stories called it, “the worst act of inhumanity anybody had ever seen.” 

			
That was God’s truth—and these settlers and miners had heard about and seen frontier atrocities for themselves—men who’d been scalped, children who’d been stolen, women who’d been raped by trappers or kidnapped by local tribes and had their faces covered in tattoos.

			
Some of the families who had been able to escape from under the burning tents were shot at as they fled. The stench of burning flesh, the screams of children and their mothers burning to death would never be forgotten by anyone who survived.

			
The day after the fire and massacre, the immigrant miners from Lucca Sicula were among those searching the scorched earth for the blackened and suffocated bodies of women, infants, and little kids. The official government report said only two women and eleven kids had been killed—twenty-five altogether including the men—and that’s what most newspapers reported. But the volunteers (Arcuri among them) who had gone in and removed the dead counted at least fifty-five bodies including Ange’s wife and children. Their bodies, burned down to bone, lay there like the leftovers from a cookout. 

			
One female reporter wrote that rescuers found nine kids with their fingers burnt off from holding onto the edges of the pit. The La Barbera children were among them.

			
The day after the massacre, a Greek miner, Achilles Diakos, ran around warning everyone that the militiamen had snuck back into camp. Survivors chased the bastards through the still-smoking hellfire grounds. When they caught them trying to cut off the heads and hands of the burnt corpses so they couldn’t be identified, the strikers drove them off before they hacked up too many bodies. Those whose bodies had been mutilated were never officially reported among the dead. 

			
Mariano was still at the mine, rounding up stragglers who hadn’t joined the strike, fifteen miles outside Trinidad. News of the massacre had not yet reached them. The telephone lines had been cut, and the union leaders had mostly all gone to Ludlow before the attack. Arcuri stole a horse and rode hard the next day to deliver the terrible news in person. There was no way of softening it, so when he found Mariano, he grabbed onto him as he had the first day they’d met at the mine. He had no choice but to tell him what they’d seen for themselves that night and what they’d seen and heard the next day. 

			
Yes, Arcuri said, he had seen what remained of Ange’s wife and kids. Rosaria had a body, but no head. He knew it was her because her dead, blackened children were still in her arms.

			
Mariano began screaming like a wild beast. Arcuri tried to hold onto him, but Mariano punched his giant of a friend, knocking him down. 

			
“Ma Arcangelo? Ca’ veni?”—What about Arcangelo?—“I must go to my brother!”

			
Arcuri got up off the ground and grabbed onto Mariano again to deliver the final blow: Ange had been shot in the back as he was running towards his family’s burning tent. He was dead. But he’d been a hero, a warrior, and had killed a federal agent; he’d killed armed soldiers. He’d been like the Mafia of old, fighing the oppressors. 

			
It was right then that Mariano, the man who had once loved, laughed, protected, and believed in right and wrong, died too. What remained was a monster.

			
Fierce. Brutal. Dago.

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 11 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 11 –

			
Resist. Defend. Defeat.

			
Ludlow, Colorado

			
Late May 1914

			
In the embers grey and red

			
Here we found them where they bled,

			
Here we found them stark and dead.

			
Here on Ludlow Field.

			
—Frank J. Hayes, UMWA President, 1914

			
Right after the massacre, and in the bloody ten days that followed, the United Mine Workers of America gave out free arms and ammo to any striker who wanted them. Mariano was among the first of God-knows-how-many strikers and survivors to take to the hills, armed to the teeth. One newspaper wrote, “…the hills in every direction seem suddenly to be alive with men.” Every man from Lucca Sicula joined the other oppressed miners in the hills and mountains to fight against the government in the land of the free. Even though it was an army of white, Black and brown strikers, speaking every language imaginable, they didn’t have to communicate by words. They knew what they had to do. 

			
When news got out about the massacre in Ludlow, demonstrations broke out all over the USA. Something like 5,000 citizens demonstrated in front of the US Capitol. Workers’ unions from all around the country sent members to Colorado to fight against Rockefeller and his private army.

			
Meantime, The New York Times, the so-called “paper of record” whose editors were white men in black silk hats—men who ran in the same circle as the Rockefellers—never believed they would find themselves on the wrong side of history. The editors wrote of the strikers: “With the deadliest weapons of civilization in the hands of savage-minded men, there can be no telling to what lengths the war in Colorado will go unless it is quelled by force.” 

			
Editorials like that one, as well as pressure from the rich establishment, had so scared the governor of Colorado that he caved to pressure. So did President Woodrow Wilson, a toady if there ever was one. He sent federal troops in to wage war on the miners—men who only wanted freedom and a decent living wage in the “promised land” of America. 

			
The cavalry came in big numbers, but at least one troop train of soldiers bound for the area mutinied rather than kill working men like themselves. But after all that? The miners lost the war anyway despite a great and mighty effort. Eventually, the strike just ended. 

			
But the outrage didn’t. 

			
To shut people up, the US Congress held a hearing with Rockefeller as the guest of honor in the hot seat. Many thought he should have been hung for murder just for his attitude up there. It was clear though that, to him, none of the dead miners, or their women and children were of consequence. 

			
The committee chairman asked Rocky if keeping the union out of Colorado Fuel and Iron would be worth it even “if it costs all your property and kills all your employees.” He answered, “It is a great principle.”

			
Not that it mattered that he gave such an answer because, to the great consternation of the working man, Rockefeller was never held to account nor punished for his crimes against humanity. Neither were the murdering Baldwin-Felts detectives nor the National Guardsmen. Nobody took blame and nobody got punished. 

			
The miners’ families’ deaths were, in the end, not of consequence. They weren’t rich, their daughters didn’t have shiny blond hair like the robber barons’ debutant daughters, their sons would never go to college and take over their father’s vast holdings. So really, who cared?

			
Maybe the government just plum forgot to put this whole tragic thing in the schoolbooks, because you won’t find it there. But it happened. Damn right, it happened. 

			
The great injustice of it all, the great sadness of it all, so inflamed the already-raging Mariano that he was finally brought to the point of mindless fury. Rockefeller walked away, while Mariano’s brother and family were dead. Ange with a bullet in his back, Rosaria without a head, and the children clinging to their mother as they all burned to death. The image haunted his every waking moment and nightmare-filled nights. Any shred of humanity that Mariano had left in him at that point, he cut out like a canker sore. 

			
He couldn’t stop repeating the words in his head: “Even if it… kills all your employees.” And their wives and babies. 

			
Yes, Mariano had fought with his union brothers against the militia, but that wasn’t personal, that was war against the establishment.

			
The murder of his family though? That was personal. Mariano’s honor depended on avenging those deaths, no matter what danger and what misery it might bring upon his own family as a result. The deaths of his brother and family could not go unanswered. Could not go unavenged.

			
He had to make a personal kill. An honor kill. His rage had to be satisfied. For him, a knife across the throat was the most honorable way for a Sicilian to get true vengeance. 

			
So, at some point, Mariano snuck into the Baldwin-Felts bunkhouse, his six-shooter in his pocket just in case things went bad. He snuck up to the cots and slashed the throat of one, more likely two, sleeping goons in cold blood and rubbed their spurting blood on his own face. 

			
But he’d been spotted running away and a gunfight ensued. Wily critter that he was though, he managed to escape, but his wanted poster was put up all over Colorado. He was happy to see they offered such a big reward: “Wanted Dead or Alive: Marian Barbarian for Murder of Two Agents. $500 Reward.” They even had a sketch of him, but it didn’t look anything like him. Gaetano told Mariano he was one of the FBI’s ten most wanted men. That made him proud. A top ten out of millions.

			
He was even prouder when he found out that “barbarian” meant a savage, uncivilized man. Someone to be afraid of. Yup, he liked that a lot. It suited him. 

			
Mariano was no longer like other men. He was a barbarian like the poster said. A barbarian who never looked back, or, if he ever blamed himself for forcing Ange to stay and fight—for refusing to allow his brother to go back to the mines—he never admitted it. He never regretted killing Rocky’s men, even though it would force his family to live wild and on-the-run. 

			
He dug in harder. Vengeance, not blame. Never forgive, never forget. Never become a rat no matter what. His cause had been right and just. He was angry, too, that the union didn’t have the same grit to fight to the end the way Anastasi had said the Mafiusu of olden times had done. 

			
He would never trust the American ways again. He wanted to be like the resistance warriors of old Sicily. 

			
Resistiri. Difenniri. Onori. Omertà. Mafia.—Resist. Defend. Honor. Courage. Mafia.

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 12 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 12 –

			
Purgatory/Heaven/Hell

			
By the Purgatory River

			
May 1916 

			
Firenze never wished she’d been born “normal.” Or even human. 

			
They say, right out of the womb, she felt the violence inside of her papa and smelled the fear seeping from the pores of her mama and siblings. It’s said she knew from the beginning that her father was dangerous. Maybe that’s a yarn, but help had arrived to save her that first night. And yes, it was in the form of a she-wolf. 

			
Seems likely she always knew things. Like things about the female Alpha Grey. Was that wolf her real mother, she wondered. Did she belong outside in the wilderness with her and the pack and not inside this terrible wooden place? Of course, babies can’t think, well not as far as anybody knows, but she always swore she had those thoughts even as an infant. She never much cared if people thought she was loco. Or mavara. 

			
But Firenze was clearly different from the start. She started to crawl at four months and attempted to talk at around the same time. The other kids could see the frustration on her baby face when she couldn’t form words. So, she’d howl. Bloooo….

			
Maria hid as much of Firenze’s too-fast progress as she could from Mariano, knowing he’d start in on the witch business, and when he got drunk, God knows what he’d do. But lately he had other things on his mind—like becoming the bootleg king of Las Animas County. 

			
That same year, when the snow started to melt, they left that hellish shack, carrying what few possessions they had with them, and went in search of a new place to live. They found a spot in the fertile valley near the Purgatoire River. The name couldn’t have been better suited to them because the family really was in purgatory back then. The area they found was isolated and uninhabited, so there wasn’t much chance they’d be found. 

			
They set about building another cabin and preparing the land for planting. Mariano’s friend and his first “employee,” his faithful paisanu, Gaetano, had stolen a sack of sugar beet seeds and some hand tools for Mariano. In exchange, he’d get a cut of the first batch of bootleg. 

			
Maria hoped Mariano would stay so consumed with his new enterprise that he wouldn’t give a damn about the baby. He seemed to have forgotten about Firenze altogether; he just didn’t seem to notice her anymore. 

			
By the time spring rolled around and the ground had softened up enough to prepare it for planting, Mariano’s plan to become rich and powerful began to take shape. Story goes that one fine morning, the whole family was up early and hard at work digging up the newly thawed earth using their hands, sticks, and Gaetano’s stolen tools. 

			
It was necessary to start planting the first crop of sugar beets to get a jump start on Mariano’s new business. And this was the perfect day because the moon was in a waning phase—the best time to plant root crops. It was during this phase that the moon’s energy shifted below the ground to where root crops grow. 

			
Maria fed the baby at dawn and left her fast asleep in her crude cradle (which was barely holding together after rocking half a dozen babies over the years), then she went out to join Mariano and the children. 

			
The day was warm, and the sun was a true blessing after the unbearable and unimaginable winter. They had a new cabin that wasn’t as drafty and miserable as the shack had been, and Maria felt things might finally turn around for them. After all, they had somehow made it out alive after a blizzard that had killed hundreds of people.

			
The ground around the old cabin had been covered all winter in three to four feet of snow with constant, freezing winds that could blow up to more than sixty miles an hour. That was followed by the thaw, which brought mud slides to the unforested areas and ankle-deep mud everywhere else. Bad then, good for planting now. As long as the nights didn’t dip below twenty-nine degrees, the seedlings would be safe.

			
Spring was almost here, and Mariano hadn’t even flown into a rage for a few weeks. Maybe Jesus had heard Maria’s prayers, and the miracle she had asked for was coming true. 

			
Down below, Gaetano had been working Mariano’s contacts in the union all winter long. Many men who had been fired or had gone back to slaving in the mines, were, if not as desperate as Mariano, then, at least as downtrodden and hungry for a way out. Gaetano was looking for workers for Mariano—workers who would become soldiers in Mariano’s war against the system. He couldn’t pay them at first, but Maria came up with an idea: The men who worked for him would share in the first year’s profits—once he took his cut. He was giddy with the idea of beating the system that had beaten them to a pulp with pulp—his sugar beet pulp. 

			
The Barbera’s lot hadn’t improved yet, but at least now there was a plan and some measure of hope. Maybe there was even a way to get back to Sicily. At least that was Maria’s dream. 

			
On that particular day, the sun was shining, and the air was warm and smelled of pine and spring. The virgin ground was soft, wet, and fertile. 

			
Best of all, Maria’s magical baby, her little Firenze, had survived. She hadn’t been devoured by the savage beast, hadn’t been killed by her beast of a father, and was thriving against all odds. 

			
Maria was so happy for the first time in a very long time that she started singing a Sicilian folk song, making the kids join in as they raked and prepared the earth. 

			
After five hours of hard, but good work, her breasts started aching, and she realized it was past feeding time. She’d forgotten all about it because Firenze hadn’t yet started screaming for a breast. Odd, because she was a hearty, hungry little thing. 

			
And where was Mariano? He wasn’t in the field they’d carved out. Had he gone into the cabin? But why would he? There was nothing for him in there, and she hadn’t seen him heading that way.

			
“Mariano,” she called out. “A unni si?” There was no answer.

			
Then to the kids, “Petrantoniu, Calandra a! Trova a to patri.”—Go find your father. 

			
“Mama!” Calandra teased, “Chista è America! ‘Callie m’havi a chiamare.”—This is America! Call me my American name, ‘Callie!’

			
Maria laughed, shaking her head. “Ho key, amuri mei. Cawlee.”—OK, my love. 

			
Then, to herself, scared Mariano would spoil the day, “Spero ca non è svinutu ubbriacatu sutta n’àrvuru.”—I hope he’s not passed out drunk under a tree somewhere. 

			
She told the kids she was going inside to get Firenze for her feeding, and they’d better not slack off while she wasn’t watching: “La barbabietola duci non crisci di sulu.”—The sweet beet doesn’t grow by itself. 

			
Maria put down her handmade hoe, anxious to relieve her heavy, swollen breasts and walked into the cabin humming. Then she screamed. 

			
Firenze was gone. 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 13 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 13 –

			
Blue’s Moon 

			
By the Purgatory River

			
That Same Day 

			
That little baby, she knew things. 

			
She was just four months old, but she could smell Mariano approaching. She could sense he wasn’t coming the way her family always came and went. He was coming for her from behind—from the place where there were many, many trees. Where was Mama? 

			
She sensed danger—smelled it in her papa’s sweat; she knew she was in as much danger as she’d been in that first day. She hadn’t formed enough of her power to know why he was coming for her. Just that he was. 

			
Nobody can say what that viper really had in mind. Since he’d already figured he could use her power to curse his enemies, why would he want to harm her? 

			
The story goes that he’d gone outside to pee the night before when everybody was asleep. The kids were passed out in their rag quilts on the floor. Maria was in their bed. 

			
As he started to pee against a tree, she was there. Firenze. Mariano believed she was staring at him from a few feet away—out there in the tall grass, giving him what he thought was the evil eye, ‘Il malocch.’

			
The next day, when baby Firenze sensed danger and realized her mama was not inside the cabin, she panicked. Instincts of a wolf cub. 

			
Damn! If she hadn’t managed to climb out of the cradle and creep and crawl out the open front door. She couldn’t see Mama over the tall grass—and no one could see her as she crept along. 

			
She somehow knew Mama couldn’t protect her from Papa if she wasn’t there. She lifted up her little head, her wild, black baby hair blowing in the sweet spring breeze. 

			
Like a feral animal she sniffed the air. That way. Yes, that’s the way! She picked up the pace of her crawl and made it from the grassy area to the dark area where the trees were. Even though it was where Papa had come from, instinct drove her on in that direction anyway. She stopped, lifted her nose, and sniffed again. Papa was still in the place of trees—somewhere—but she had to go through it to get to the other side where she’d be safe. 

			
She continued to crawl. The earth was no longer soft and covered with grass. Grass couldn’t grow well in the dark place. There were many rocks and things that stung; rough ground that scraped and tore into the baby skin on her knees and palms. She was bleeding, but her rag diaper was the only covering she had against the forest. 

			
Mariano was close, so close. He started calling out in sing-song. “Firenzedda Firenze! T’ha femmari! Fèmmati.”—Baby Firenze! Stop. Stop!—Then to himself, “Prima o poi ti pigghiu, mavara!”—I’ll get you sooner or later. 

			
Again, to himself: “Ti prenderò mavara!”—I will get you, witch!

			
Like a madman, he was calling to his four-month-old baby girl as though she was a dog who could understand and obey. Roll over. Sit. Stay. 

			
Mariano was so used to everyone in his world—which had become smaller and smaller since running from the law—obeying his every word that he figured…well, who the hell knows what he figured? But this was a man who didn’t make idle threats. Why bother? Every threat he made was real. 

			
He was getting closer. Too close! 

			
Baby Firenze sniffed the air again. She was getting confused. Should she go this way? That way? His footsteps were hard and loud on the ground. It was not like he was tracking an animal and had to be quiet. He was getting close. Very close. 

			
Her dirty cloth diaper got stuck on a sticker bush and ripped away, spraying her scent in the breeze. Firenze started to whimper. Then he was there. 

			
He spotted the tiny baby, naked to the elements. She looked at him. He heard the growl of an animal. A big animal. He could leave her here for the animals, but he’d come too close. She was a witch, so maybe the animals would be afraid to kill her. He had to make sure she was dead so she couldn’t curse him or his enterprise. He drew his knife and went in for the kill. 

			
She watched him, helpless. 

			
Then, even though the ground in that area wasn’t muddy at all, he somehow lost his footing, tried to regain it, and fell backwards and smashed his head—hard—on a huge rock. The first thing he heard was the crack of his skull. The last thing he heard before passing out was another growl. But the last thing he thought? That the cursed baby had done it.

			
Firenze started to crawl away from him. She must get to the safe place, back to her mama. She heard a howl and looked up. Two blue eyes were staring down at her. Her other mama.

			
Maria heard that wolf. 

			
Ooowwoooo. 

			
What she didn’t hear was the very faint baby howl that came after it. “Bloooo.” 

			
Maria, frantic to find Firenze and save her, not just from Mariano but now also from a wolf, tore through the forested mountain towards the howl like a crazed mother bear, screaming the baby’s name. 

			
The children, having lived in the wilderness, knew how to track animals and humans and saw the baby’s crawl marks in the earth. “Mama! Mama!” they screamed. “Firenze accussì jè!”—She’s this way. Maria, her long calico dress and apron ripped and muddy, ran to where the children were pointing to the ground. 

			
Maria looked at the hand and knee prints and put her head in her hands. Even though the baby was advanced for her age, she still was too little to crawl this distance. Or any distance really. What the hell? There was no time to think; everything was happening so fast…

			
Michelina, the second girl (who the kids all called “Mickey”), had run ahead of them in the forest and called out that she’d found something. When the family reached her, she was standing next to the baby’s diaper still stuck on the bush. She pointed to the ground with one hand, her other hand covering her mouth to keep from screaming. 

			
Next to the diaper were both Papa’s long footprints and the five-inch-long claw marks of a huge animal. The strides of both man and beast were far enough apart to see they both had been running. 

			
Inside the paw prints were streaks of blood. Whose blood? The animal’s, Papa’s, or the baby’s? 

			
“Firenze!” Maria yelled over and over. All she got in response was the howling of the wolf. 

			
“Marianu! Marianu!” she called. “Arrispunni pi faùri! A unni sì? Lassati sulu lu picciriddu a.”—Where are you? Please, Mariano, leave the baby alone. 

			
Then, once again, she reached down deep inside of herself and prayed to her Lord and Savior, Jesus, to save her baby. Could they survive without Mariano? She didn’t know, but she didn’t pray for him. 

			
They trekked deeper into the forest following the tracks. 

			
“Mama! Mama!” Peter got to the clearing first. He stopped dead in his tracks and cried. “Vidu papà, ddassutta. Mortu mi pari!”—I see Papa! There! I think he’s dead! 

			
Maria pushed the kids out of the way and entered the clearing. Mariano was lying still on the ground, blood gushing from his head. His arm was ripped open, his pants covered in animal hair. He was unconscious and moaning, more dead than alive. Maria looked down at him and didn’t even bother to check his breathing or injuries; she instead tore off a piece of her apron and told the twins, Callie and Peter, to wrap the rag around their papa’s arm and stay with him while she took the other kids to look for Firenze. Maria wasn’t the only one who hoped Mariano was dead and that the baby was alive. The twins made a pact as they watched over their near-dead father: They’d help Mama to make it without him. Somehow, they knew that despite his strength and guile, they’d be better off.

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 14 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 14 –

			
Wolf Bait 

			
The Forest Near the Purgatory River

			
Spring 1916 

			
Mickey, who had taken to wearing her brothers’ duds when they grew out of them, was tracking ahead of the rest of the kids when she spotted what she thought could be Firenze about one hundred feet in front of her in the forest. She wasn’t sure because the trees were so heavy with leaves, and the ground cover was so thick, it was too dark to see. But whatever the thing was that was moving on the ground, it didn’t seem to have fur, a shell, or scales. 

			
Forgetting to step quietly and hold her breath as she’d been taught to do when tracking, Mickey let out a gasp and rushed towards the form. Yes, it was her baby sister all right, and she was crawling—not crying, mind you—but crawling along, naked as a jaybird. 

			
Mickey could see that her baby sister was struggling but she was still doing an impossibly good job of creeping through forest’s deep underbrush. And she was heading straight for a clearing ahead. Mickey was confused because what she saw didn’t make any sense no way, no how. Could the infant have crawled by herself through a forest for what had to be at least a mile? Was she just seeing what she wanted to see? Was that moving form actually a ghost, a spirit, a devil? 

			
In a few seconds, Mickey got a clear look—it was Firenze all right, and her shock and fear turned to relief, relief to tears, and tears into a tear-ass run towards the baby. 

			
Mickey moved as fast as her legs could carry her through the forest, yelling, screaming, “Firenze! Firenze!” her arms out in front of her to gather up her battered little sister. The baby turned her head towards Mickey as though she knew her name when she heard it. But as Mickey got to within two feet of touching her, an animal bounded out of the clearing ahead, aiming right for them. Mickey instinctively reached for Firenze. Why hadn’t she thought to take the six-shooter from the house? She was as good a shot as any boy. 

			
Holy dear Christ. It was the wolf with the spectral blue eyes. Before Mickey could grab her sister, the wolf grabbed the baby by the scruff of her neck and ran into the forest and disappeared. “Stop! Please stop!” Mickey cried. 

			
But they were gone. 

			
Mickey knew it was the same she-wolf that had invaded their cabin the night Firenze was born. Did that devil from hell come to finish off what it had started? Or perhaps finish off what their father hadn’t? 

			
When the rest of the family caught up to Mickey, her trousers were torn, and her face was streaked with tears and blood. Shaking, she pointed her finger towards where the wolf had run away with the baby. 

			
“Mama! La Lupa!” she bellowed like a banshee

			
Maria grabbed her and shook her hard. “Lu lupu arrubbò lu picciridda?”—Did the wolf steal the baby?

			
Mickey nodded, knowing by the look on her mother’s face that she’d better stop bawling and own up to the fact that she let the baby be taken. She pointed again towards the woods. 

			
Mickey’s sniffle was the last sound they heard. No growl of a beast, no cry of a baby. There was not even a sound from where they’d left Peter and Callie with Mariano probably to die, if he hadn’t already kicked the bucket, praise Jesus. 

			
The children showed Maria the tracks. They tried to follow them, but the forest was dark with new leaves filling every tree, and the soggy ground was full of tracks. Many creatures, big and small, had passed through here. 

			
“Picciriddu meu! Unni è la me picciotta? Dio, ti pregu sarba lu me picciriddu!”—My baby! Where is my little girl? God, please save my baby!—Maria cried into the deathly quiet woods.

			
There were too many tracks from too many animals to tell which were those of the wolf that had taken Firenze. Her miracle baby had survived the night of the wolf on the evening of her birth, but she knew she could not survive this day. 

			
As they trudged along, searching without a shot of a chance, the kids began to cry and cling to Maria. Mickey was sure they were getting too deep in to find their way out. Truth be told, they all knew it. The Greenhorn is a beautiful mountain, but it is a deadly one—just like the animals that live there. 

			
After two hours of searching, the family was dehydrated, exhausted, and done in. 

			
Mickey, being the boldest in the search party, got up the gumption to stop walking and face her mother. “She’s gone, Mama. We can’t risk the lives of the other children to save just one,” she said in Sicilian. 

			
Maria looked into Mickey’s eyes, and at first Mickey thought, by some miracle, she’d gotten through to her mother. Instead, Maria slapped Mickey’s face so hard, she knocked her to the ground. 

			
“No! Tu sì na soru orribbili. Circamu finu a quannu nun truvamu lu picciridda!”—You are a horrible sister. We will search until we find the baby. 

			
But the sun was starting to set, and if they didn’t trace their steps back very soon, the woods would turn pitch black, and they wouldn’t be able to find their way home. They could perish like Firenze had—in the jaws of animals. But Maria trekked on. The sneering words of the midwife repeating over and over in her head, “Babies die, babies die, babies die….” 

			
Finally, exhausted, and without Maria’s permission, Mickey took it upon herself (and knowing she’d be in big trouble with her mother) to round up the other kids. “If we don’t go now, we won’t make it back home,” she told them out of Maria’s earshot. 

			
Maria was desperate and scared that she would fail again and lose yet another baby. She had failed at everything she’d tried in this terrible, New World. Now her husband—the man she once loved—was probably dead, and her precious, magical baby daughter had been taken by an animal. It was over. Firenze was gone. She knew it in her heart. 

			
Eventually, she became aware of the other children crying in the distance, and their cries were getting further and further away. The sounds of her other children retreating was like a smack upside Maria’s head, and it knocked her back down to earth—or maybe it was down to hell. 

			
She knew Mickey had been the one talking sense. The remaining kids were in danger, and she wasn’t even trying to protect them.

			
Meantime, the sun was setting, and it had brought the forest back to life. Growls and howls and screeches rang out from above, behind, in front, and all around, which made Maria even crazier and more frantic. 

			
Not far off, she heard a battle erupting between a mountain lion and—what?—she didn’t know. She had to get to the remaining children. She was exhausted and dehydrated, but she kept heading toward the clearing where she prayed she’d find them. 

			
But the kids were deep in the woods and scared as hell that they were lost. As they trudged on in the darkening forest, the shadow of a huge grizzly appeared on the next rise, not one hundred feet ahead of them. 

			
If they thought they knew what fear tasted like before, it was nothing compared to the terror that now crashed over them like a tidal wave. They were alone. Their father was almost certainly dead, their mother was lost in the forest looking for their baby sister, and they had no protection—not from the giant beast, tall and terrifying. 

			
They’d been taught by their father that, when confronted by a bear, the best thing to do was huddle together, keep very quiet, and wave their arms very slowly to make themselves look bigger. He had made them practice over and over in front of their cabin. It had brought on gales of laughter then. Not now. 

			
Angelina and Mickey grabbed the kids around themselves and they all waved their arms, trying really hard to keep their cries low so maybe the bear wouldn’t hear their fear. Like he couldn’t smell it. Like he couldn’t sense it. Like he wouldn’t know it. But Mickey knew he wouldn’t smell it on her. She was fearless and had a real animal sense, which is why she was the best hunter in the family.

			
The huge bear came closer, his eyes illuminated by the rising moon, and reared up on his haunches. Little Austin began to sob louder, and so did Helena, despite Angelina trying to hush them without success. 

			
The grizzly looked at these strange, furless beings, slowly waving their arms, some of them whimpering. Were these creatures a danger or not? Suddenly, the sharp smell of urine was on the wind, and the unfamiliar sound of a child’s wails pierced the night.

			
Austin, an acorn calf if ever there was one—the brother who’d always been prone to the fits—suddenly fell to the ground, shaking and moaning, his eyes rolling back in his head as he foamed at the mouth. 

			
Mickey, young as she was, forgot the danger lurking so close by, dropped to the ground, and did what she had seen her mother do many times. She felt around for a stick and forced it into Austin’s mouth to keep him from swallowing his tongue. But he was fitting something fierce. 

			
The others kept standing and waving their arms, hoping that somehow the damned bear would figure out that he was supposed to be afraid of them. 

			
Just then, Maria caught up close enough to set sight on the kids. She crept quietly, calling upon the Sicilian martyr, St. Guido, who was known to protect against animal attacks as she inched towards the kids. The huge animal lifted its head, sniffed again, let out a fierce and terrifying growl, and then, like her prayers had been answered, quickly turned tail and ran back into the forest. They could only figure that the bear smelled the sickness on Austin, knew something was bad and wrong with these creatures, and that they wouldn’t make for good eating. 

			
When Austin came to, not knowing what had just happened, Maria lifted him in her arms, and the little band quietly attempted to feel their way back through the dark, holding onto branches and tree trunks to keep from collapsing from exhaustion. 

			
Mickey tripped on a downed branch and fell into a sticker bush. Her head landed on something soft, wet, and squishy, making her cry out. At first, no one paid attention because they’d all been crying on and off for most of the trek anyway. But this cry was different. 

			
Mickey picked the wet thing up, and it smelled something awful. “Aspetta! Aspetta! È lu pannu!”—Wait! Wait! It’s the diaper! 

			
Maria took it from her and said, “Va tutto bambini. Andiamo avanti.”—It’s OK kids. Go on. She kept them moving towards what she hoped was the cabin. She would save her tears for when the children were safely asleep.

			
How she hated this America. This Colorado. She vowed that if Jesus saved what was left of her family this night, she would be a faithful, prayerful woman and do her best to keep the rest of her family safe from harm. 

			
As they trekked on in the light of the now risen moon, they came to the spot where Mariano had been left with Peter and Callie, but there was no sign of them. They’d been told to stay with their father until he died. Was this the wrong spot? If so, she and the kids were headed in the wrong direction. 

			
“Mama!” Laura called out. “La scarpa di papà!”—Mariano’s shoe. This was the right place, but the three were gone. Even if Mariano had lived, he couldn’t have walked home, especially without a shoe, and the twins couldn’t possibly have carried a grown man. 

			
What the hell? 

			
The blood from Mariano’s head was there—Maria felt it with her fingers—but the blood trail stopped or was invisible in the darkness. There was no disturbance in the area, so it didn’t look like they’d been attacked by animals, drunken trappers, or any Apache scalpers that might be roaming the area. 

			
Maybe Peter and Callie had somehow dragged their papa’s body home instead of leaving it for the vultures? 

			
If so, Maria figured she’d bury the bastard tomorrow, then get on with the business of mourning, figuring out how to raise her children, and keeping them from starving or being killed like Firenze. 

			
By the time they got to a spot where they could get a glimpse of the cabin in the distance, they wanted to scream with joy, especially because they saw a candle burning inside. 

			
They ran home with what little strength they had left, but Maria wouldn’t let them go inside. There was no way of knowing if it was the children inside or a stranger. She carefully opened the door. “Pietrantonio! Calandra!” she called. The two kids emerged from the darkness and ran into their mother’s arms. 

			
“Chi facìsti cû corpu di to patri?”—What did you do with your father’s body?—she asked.

			
They pointed to the bed. 

			
There lay Mariano Barbera, aka Marian Barbarian, his bloody head wrapped in rags. He was in bad shape. He wasn’t conscious, but he was somehow still breathing. His tremendous strength, which had once captured her heart, now just held her captive. 

			
She wished they’d just left him out there to die, but he was their father after all, and as such, he still had to be respected…whether he deserved it or not. 

			
He had tried to kill her baby but failed. Instead, he’d left the infant for the wolves. Now it was his turn to suffer. Would he live? Would he die a slow, agonizing death? Did she care? 

			
She didn’t tend to him but instead told Peter to bring in more firewood and asked Callie to fill two pails with water from the stream. Not for Mariano, but so she could feed the children. 

			
They had already lit the fire, so she filled a pot with water, threw some wild dandelions and mushrooms that had, thank God, come up in this early spring. She took two squirrels Mariano had killed that she had skinned, cleaned, and soaked in salted water for two days, and put them to the fire. 

			
She didn’t ask the kids how or why they had dragged their father home. 

			
When the food was cooked enough to eat, they sat down on the floor. Maria led the prayers, asking Jesus to make Firenze’s trip to heaven an easy one and for her baby to rest in His arms for eternity. 

			
Just then, she saw Firenze’s spirit jumping in the shadows of the fire. They all did. 

			
She told them that little Firenze was “talking” to them from heaven and that they shouldn’t be afraid. She told them it was Jesus showing her to them, so they knew Firenze was safe in his arms. 

			
Little Helena asked, “Mama, is Firenze playing in heaven with other dead babies?” 

			
Peter said, “Stop it! She ain’t dead, and I will never say she’s dead.” 

			
Callie jumped in, “No? Then why’s her spirit jumping outta the fire? Answer me that!” 

			
Before a real fight broke out, Maria put her hands together, told the children to do the same, and led them in a hymn to the Virgin, who understood a mother’s anguish better than any woman. 

			
She made no prayer or hymn for Mariano. 

			
Minutes after they’d filled their bellies, the children fell into a sad sleep on the floor. 

			
It was only then that Maria wrapped a quilt around herself, took a long swallow of what was left in the bottom of Mariano’s sugar moon jug, and cried until she could no longer feel the pain. 

			
She woke up before the sun, checked on her filthy little ones, and also on Mariano. He wasn’t dead yet. Then, she grabbed the six-shooter and went outside to fetch some fresh water. 

			
She walked down to the stream, about two hundred yards from the cabin. She dipped the bucket into the freezing water and heard a growl. She put her hand up to her eyes to block the rising sun so she could get a better look. What now? How much more could she take? 

			
On the other side of the stream, not forty feet away, stood a wolf. That wolf. The Alpha Grey with her God-awful blue eyes. She could tell it had given birth recently because her belly had been plucked of hair, and her teats were exposed. Besides which, it was spring, and she was the alpha female. But an animal mother wouldn’t leave newborn pups behind in a den, would she? 

			
The grey looked at Maria and howled as though she was mocking her. Had she come for another one of her children? Had she come to kill Maria too? Maria picked up the gun and fired. 

			
She missed, and the wolf took off. The she-wolf got far enough away to not get clipped by a bullet and planted herself in one spot—to stare Maria down. Maria was about to give chase to get a better shot when she heard a sound coming from the tall grass. It sounded like a wounded kitten. Or maybe a wolf pup. She didn’t know much about wolves, but she knew this wolf had wanted her human child and had taken her. If the wolf had abandoned a pup—maybe it was wounded or disfigured—Maria would kill it like the wolf had killed her baby. 

			
The she-wolf appeared again—but now on Maria’s side of the stream. She looked towards the weeds, then back at Maria before turning and running back into the woods, vanishing like a spirit. 

			
Maria carefully tiptoed toward the sound. If it was the bitch’s pup, she’d finish it off and cook it. She’d heard that’s what the Indians did to take on the spirit of an animal, and, hell, those folks had survived this terrible place for God knows how many thousands of years. She no longer thought of small creatures as pets like she had as a girl, but as food. She was a mother with children to feed. 

			
She crept the last few feet, fully expecting to find a wounded animal. 

			
Instead, she found Firenze. 

			
Was that milk on her lips?
