– 15 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 15 –

			
Big Boot 

			
Wet Mountains, Colorado

			
1916–1919 

			
No, Mariano didn’t die from smacking his head, gashing his shins wide open, or breaking a few ribs in the forest that day. Nor did he die from the near-deadly infection that swept over him hot and fast as a brush fire. 

			
Maria’s fears aside, Firenze didn’t die either—neither from being eaten by the wolf or another wild beast, nor from exposure after having been on the cold mountain for more than eighteen hours. Hell, she didn’t even have a touch of frostbite. 

			
But another question remained: How did Mariano get back to the cabin that night? The truth, she later found out, was that when the twins went to retrieve his body, he was somehow still alive, so they rolled him onto a sort-of sled they’d made from Callie’s petticoat and Peter’s shirt. They dragged him home, got him inside, and then waited for him to die. 

			
By the next night, Mariano was delirious, and his forehead felt as hot as the lowest rung of hell. 

			
The younger kids got to crying like nobody’s business. They were more scared than sad he might die. They were afraid they’d starve to death without him. When Maria could tell he wasn’t going kick the bucket anytime soon and would probably linger around for God knew how long, she made a decision: She’d treat the wounds rather than allow him to die the agonizing death he deserved. She did it for the kids; they had been through enough without having to live with the stench of slow death. Besides that, she worried the Lord would deem it murder if she just let him die.

			
So, she set out some watered bread she’d made from the sack of flour Gaetano had brought them up from the company store. She kept the poultice in a warm, dark place, and in a few days, it looked more green than white. 

			
She put chunks of the moldy bread on top of Mariano’s infected wounds, tied them on with clean rags, recited not very fervent prayers to Santa Maria Francesca of the Five Wounds each morning, and waited to see if he would die or somehow manage to live. Well, that ole sumnabitch, he lived. 

			
After two weeks or so, when the moldy bread and the prayers had done their work and Mariano was well enough to get outside with help, he immediately started criticizing their work, saying the sugar beets weren’t planted right. He refused to admit anything could go right without him. He didn’t even thank the kids for dragging his ass all the way home or Maria for saving his life. 

			
It took all of Maria’s willpower to keep from picking up the six-shooter and letting him have it between the eyes. 

			
About the beets, he was, of course, wrong as sin on Sunday. Those plants took to the rich Colorado earth like, well, like prayers on Sunday. Damn! The soil near the stream was so fertile and untouched since the day of creation, you could stick a shoe in the ground, and it would grow a boot! 

			
Like the plants, Firenze grew healthier, heartier, and heftier—even better, in fact, than she’d been before her night in the woods. The other kids always kept the story to themselves, even as they got older because the idea of Firenze crawling that distance when she was four-months-old was even crazier than what had happened on the night she was born. The night of the wolf. They knew if word got out, she’d be marked “strega.”

			
***

			
In the three years since Firenze had gone missing and was somehow returned to them, Mariano had not only fully recovered, he had become a fulltime bootlegger, introducing himself to strangers as Mario Barbera. (“Mario” and “Barbera” were the only names the law didn’t have on wanted posters or in their files). Besides, he thought chopping off the “La” as they had done at Ellis Island sounded more ’Merigaan, and although he liked “Barbarian,” it just had to go. But from then on, “Barbera” would be the family name.

			
Mariano the tough guy liked to brag that he was too mean to die, and he said it like it was a good thing. Maybe it wasn’t a good thing, but it sure as hell was a true thing—maybe the truest thing he ever said. 

			
He liked to show off his wolf bite scars. They added to his reputation. Even those who called him “friend” said he was cold enough to steal a coin off a dead man’s eyes. He was alive but dead inside.

			
While he might have become mean, he wasn’t dumb by any means, and he had been right as rain about booze: By 1917, bootlegging was very big business in Colorado, and it was about to get much bigger. Nationwide Prohibition wasn’t declared until 1920, giving these Siggie moonshiners four long years to perfect their craft. 

			
A year and a half after prohibition had been declared in Colorado, the US entered World War I. Every miner who was an American citizen or any immigrant male who had even applied for citizenship could—and would—be conscripted to fight in the war. 

			
The funny part is that the Sicilians who didn’t apply for citizenship (because all they wanted to do was go back to Sicily after finding out the “land of the free” was really the land of indentured servitude) could not even get drafted to fight in that rich man’s war. Even when Italy joined up with the allies, there was no way they’d risk their lives—again—to help the ’Merigaan oppressors. 

			
These men couldn’t be called up to fuel the rich man’s war (they were considered non-declarant aliens), or even help them get richer with the coal and steel needed for the war effort. For the first time since stepping foot on American soil, the Barbera family had caught a break—via bootleg. Better still, thanks to prohibition in Colorado, Sicilian workers who made moonshine were able to quit the mines and pay off their padroni by joining with the only armies they cared about—the armies of moonshining Italian families. And these men were joining the families in such numbers that the US Army could have learned a trick to two about recruitment and loyalty from them. 

			
That was such a kick-in-Rocky’s-ass, it was like getting a Christmas bonus every day of the year. 

			
The men who left the mines took with them the guns that the union had given out during the strike, so they came fully loaded to Mariano’s and the other families’ bootleg enterprises. 

			
It wasn’t a smooth ride though, make no mistake. Mariano had personally killed and ordered hits on more than a few Sicilians who tried to muscle in on his boot. And he’d kill more if he had to, and he’d do it without flinching. That’s why he loved the Sicilian saying, U santu di marmuru nun suda—A marble saint doesn’t sweat. 

			
No matter what or who got in his way, Mariano forged ahead, believing only that might make it right.

			
His only regret was that his oldest boy, Giuseppe, was not there to see what he’d accomplished. The family had accepted the fact that Giuseppe, who had signed on to the cattle drive back in 1915, was never going to return. It was the way of the West—brutal and deadly. Mariano, though, never wrote him off as dead.

			
And he was right. One day in 1919, Giuseppe, riding a dapple-grey stallion, returned like a ghost from the past.

			
He had left as a skinny, half-starved, wiry boy, but came back a muscled and mighty strong man. He’d been driving cattle for all those years and had made friends of men who were White, Black, Mexican, Greek, Chinese, Comanche, Ute, and yeah, even Italians from Calabria, Bergamo, and Roma. In other words, he was no longer the unshucked boy who had left almost four years before. 

			
He’d grown way up and now called himself Joe. 

			
He had a stained, ten-gallon hat on his head, spurs on his boots, and cash in his pocket. He could speak some Spanish, dance an Irish jig, and play the guitar. God knows what else he learned on the range. 

			
He had expected—with good reason—to return as the savior of the family, so he was shocked to find out from one of Mariano’s spies back at the mine that the family was living on a decent-size plot of land a few miles north of Pueblo. When he left, they’d all been hiding out in a shack in the wilderness on the mountain. What the hell? It took him more than a week to even find them. 

			
He saw his father first—out in a field inspecting a crop of—what?—he didn’t know. But he was out there bold as brass. Giuseppe jumped from his horse, ran to his father, and hugged him like there was no tomorrow. Mariano was so overcome that he hugged him back and wouldn’t let go—a rare sign of affection from the grizzled bastard. They had all given the boy up for dead—but he hadn’t. 

			
Giuseppe was confused as hell. Here was his father, working the land like a free man. Wasn’t he a wanted man with a price on his head? 

			
Sure, Mariano told him, but the law had their hands so full with all the Sicilian gunslinger-bootleggers warring out in the open—right in the streets—that they seemed to have forgotten about him. He let out a hardy laugh when he told his son about one bounty hunter who had come looking for him. He said he’d shot that bounty man full of more lead than a butcher’s pencil and then hauled his body up the mountain for the animals to feast on. 

			
Well, he added, the bounty hunter hadn’t exactly been a corpse when he left him up there because he wasn’t all the way dead. Didn’t matter. Nobody’d ever find what was left of him anyway. 

			
He didn’t wait for Giuseppe to ask, because Mariano was thrilled to tell him about his new enterprise. It was so successful, he said, that he’d been able to even buy the piece of land they were standing on—legitimately! 

			
Those crazy ruminations he’d had back on January 1, 1916, about becoming a big man and how one day Maria would be able to go into town to buy bolts of cloth, some provisions, and even that giant provolone hanging in the window of the local Italian shop were becoming truer by the minute. 

			
What the mean ole bastard would never tell Joe, though, was that if it weren’t for Maria, his business would never have succeeded the way it did. She was the brains; he was the muscle. She was the one who’d come up with the idea of putting the land in her name, seeing as how he was a wanted man and all.

			
Of course, she got none of the credit—just the blame whenever things went wrong. But since she was smarter than him, she kept all the bad times in her head the way a bookkeeper keeps books: red and black.

			
He had made enough, he said, with that first illegal batch of Sugar Moon, that he bought these few acres, built a small house and a corral. Then he bought a herd of goats and two pigs.

			
“Joe” was so shocked by this unlikely turn of events he was practically speechless—not that he was a big talker to begin with. 

			
Joe said he’d heard about this new no-liquor law in Colorado, but after bringing another herd to the slaughterhouses on his way back, he didn’t stop at a saloon to find out. He just wanted to get home. He thought he was going to save his family with the money he’d made. Well, they sure as shootin’ didn’t need it anymore. But Mariano took it anyway. Enough was never enough for him. 

			
Joe told of how the men on the cattle drive all had their own hooch, so they figured what they’d heard about booze being against the law in Colorado was just another rumor. 

			
“No rumor,” Mariano laughed, putting his arm around his son. 

			
Wine, and especially bootleg moonshine, he said, had become more valuable than coal. Or gold. “And you don’t have to dig for it—just maybe die for it.” 

			
But it was the best thing that had happened to Sicilians since coming to this country. He showed Joe the stills and his stash of weapons—which included machine guns like the ones that had been mounted on top of “The Death Special.” He showed him the sugar beets and told him they mixed 120 pounds of sugar with the local spring water and yeast to make the “Sugar Moon.” He said his “company” also made wine, which he sold at a big profit with grapes he imported. From where he didn’t say. 

			
Before even letting his mother, brothers, and sisters lay eyes on Joe, Mariano introduced him to some of his “soldiers.” These men patrolled at the edges of his land, toting machine guns. He said these fellas were their family now. Their Società Onorata family. The Society of Honor. 

			
“La me quali famigghia??”—My what family?—Joe asked. 

			
Mariano didn’t answer except to say there was a new order. He said he wasn’t the only one who’d figured out how to turn a profit by turning sugar beets into moonshine. Other families were also in the moonshine business and out of the Goddamned mines. 

			
These Italian immigrant moonshiners, though, he warned, had got to brewing up more than just booze. As fast as their stills were built, they got to springing their paisani from the mines and arming them up. These men went from working underground to working in the underworld. 

			
He explained that the Barberas—without the “La”—were their own bootlegging family, and Joe would have to learn who the other families were because some were allies and some were upstart rivals. It never crossed his mind that his son would want a different life from his. 

			
The Lombardos, the Marinos, and the Amatos were the upstarts, he said. 

			
The La Donas, whom they knew from Lucca Sicula, and the Carlinos, who were blood relatives of Maria’s, were Barbera allies. He then said, “We are together like Mafia.” 

			
Joe would later learn what that meant, exactly…and the tragedy it would bring. But right then, all Joe said was, “Mama Mia!” He could hardly believe what he was hearing. 

			
A bigger problem than the law, Mariano explained, was the deadly feud going on between the La Donas and the Carlinos, but he added, “Noi di Barbera arristamu niutrali, propriu comu ‘n Italia quannu accuminciò la guerra!”—We Barberas remain neutral, just like Italy was when the war began. 

			
Joe couldn’t figure out what this all meant and why his father was calling the family, “Barbera,” and not their actual name, La Barbera, but knew it was better not to ask, and better still not to ask how in the hell they were supposed to stay neutral in what amounted to a range war, not over land, but over booze.

			
Joe learned who their customers were that day too: the miners, the bosses, and yes, even the governor who’d passed the law banning booze in the first place. Everybody wanted booze—more now than before it was banned. Mariano charged the big bugs twice what he charged the miners and regular folks. That, he said, was the honorable thing to do.

			
“Botta ri sali.” Mariano laughed bitterly, turning the expression about miners bumping their heads against salt mine walls against the coal mine bosses. Now the bosses were the ones pounding their heads against the walls, he said, because they were dependent on their former wop/dago/Guinea slaves for what they wanted and couldn’t get legally: booze. 

			
Mariano told Joe that as the oldest son, he would learn the business and become his number one man. Joe’s head was spinning. 

			
Mariano figured by the next year he might make enough money to buy more property and get himself a herd of cattle. Joe explained to his father that to keep a herd, you had to have horses and cowhands. 

			
“OK! I will be a big American cowboy,” Mariano declared in his newly acquired broken English. 

			
Joe had to tread through the next part as carefully as walking on new eggs, explaining to his bad-tempered papa that he should never use the word, “cowboy” about himself—especially around white ranchers. Or even bootlegging, amateur ranchers, he thought.

			
Furious that his son knew something he didn’t, he grabbed Joe’s shirt and demanded, “Perchè no?”—Why not?

			
Joe, now an actual, real-life American cowpoke, explained to him that he, Mariano, would be a “cowboss” not a “cowboy,” because a “cowboy,” was the nasty slang that the white men used for colored cowpokes and ranch hands. 

			
White ranch owners considered the Black ranch hands not men but boys, he explained, even though one in four cowhands—a full twenty-five percent—were Black men. Mariano didn’t believe it at first, but Joe said, “I believe it to be a true fact.” Whatever it was, Mariano took it as God’s truth. 

			
Joe said when his father learned this, it made him mad enough to swallow a horn-toad backwards. He saw his father’s fury boil to the surface and instinctively ducked. 

			
But Mariano wasn’t mad at Joe, and he wasn’t mad because he might have been compared to Negroes. Just the opposite. 

			
Mariano slammed his right fist into his left hand and declared in his best broken English: “Then I will call myself ‘cowboy’ because Rockefeller and the white men call us Italians same name as they call Black men anyway.” Then he added for good measure, “Vaffanculu Rockefeller! Vaffanculu the mines! Vaffanculu all rich, white ’Merigaans.”—Fuck them all.

			
From that moment on, to all his men, and later to any new ranch hands—no matter the color—Mariano Barbera was only called, “Signor Cowboy.” 

			
His fury cooled as quickly as it had started, and he went right back to bragging about his achievements. But before Mariano would allow his son to go find his mother, brothers, and sisters—who Joe missed like life itself—he gave him one last warning.

			
“I have what they call a ‘left-hand wife,’ in town,” he said. “And if you ever run into her or if your mama ever asks about her, you keep your mouth shut, or I swear I’ll kill you.” 

			
Well, that didn’t take long, Joe thought. Was that just his father’s way of saying he better keep his trap shut, or did he really mean he’d pump him full of lead? Joe didn’t know. 

			
What he did know was that, for Mariano anyway, America really was the land of opportunity—if a man was smart enough and tough enough to find those opportunities. And if he had to kill for those opportunities, well, that was just the way it was. 

			
Isn’t that what men like Rockefeller had always done, Mariano asked his son. Kill to get what they wanted? The only difference between Rockefeller and Barbera, he said, was the color of their skin, the country they came from, and the accent that came outta their mouths. He said that joke always got a big laugh from the regulars down at Mrs. Bianchi’s. Some of those regulars even worked for him now. 

			
Joe got it right off that his papa had become even meaner, ornerier, and more dangerous than he’d been, even during the strike. The strike had been about all the men. This new business was all about Mariano Barbera. He was the big bug now. 

			
Making people fear him was his way of dealing with others. It was how he’d dealt with his family, and now it was how he dealt with rivals in these mean mountains of moonshine and machine guns. Thing of it is? Mariano was a man without a conscience, and that in itself could scare the shit out of anybody with a brain. He stunk of mean, and the badness clung to him like slime on a slug. 

			
Mariano, it was clear, had no fear. None. Except for one little thing he didn’t talk about. And she scared him shitless. 

			
 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 16 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 16 –

			
Season of the Witch 

			
Pueblo, Colorado 

			
1919 

			
Yeah, he had tried to kill that baby, but she lived. Instead, he was the one who almost died. He knew as well as he knew the fingers on his own hand, that the wild child was definitely una mavara. He also knew that any shot he had of bending her to his will to do his bidding against his enemies was over. 

			
He knew that she knew what he had tried to do to her. He also learned that the day of the wolf attack, Firenze would have killed him first if she could have. Of course, she couldn’t. Yet. She still needed help.

			
Maria knew it too. They all kinda knew it, but nobody said it out loud or even whispered it to one another. This badness between the baby and Papa wasn’t done. 

			
And as soon as baby Firenze (who they all now called Flo) added words to her mad howling, she named the wolf—her wolf—“Blue.” Her siblings first thought the wolf was a demon, what with the blue eyes and all, but Flo knew different. Blue was her guardian angel in the cruel world she’d been born into. 

			
So, when Mariano and Joe walked in the door that day, Mariano was glad to see there was no sign of little Flo. Maria near-fainted at the sight of her son, and Joe near-fainted at the sight of their nicely appointed cabin. She reached up high to grab her tall son’s face and kiss it all over. “Peppineddu!” she cried using the Sicilian nickname for “Giuseppe.” The other kids, some nearly grown, stopped their racket, and stared, stock still. 

			
Who was this man? 

			
Peter and Callie though, recognized their older brother right off and almost knocked Maria out of the way rushing to hug him. Then Helena (who now called herself Helen) as well as Angelina, Clara, and Austin, realizing this was their brother, joined in the hugging. 

			
“And who’s that fella there?” Joe asked of the kid who remained standing in the corner wearing banged-up boots, a wide-brimmed hat, and work clothes that had seen some hard riding. Mariano snorted, but Maria put her arm around the nearly fourteen-year-old. When Joe got a close-up look, he found out that him was a her. 

			
“Dici salutu a to frati, amuri meu,”—Say hello to your brother, my love—Maria told Mickey.

			
Mickey stuck out her hand to shake his. “I’m Mickey. I ’member you. You went offin’ the cattle drive, and we thought you was dead. You ain’t skinny no more, and now you got skin like Silas. Do you remember me?” 

			
Joe nodded but didn’t bother to ask who Silas was because he was too busy trying to figure out the whole thing. 

			
He was worldly enough now to know that a girl could be a boy. Why, the cattle drivers loved to tell the tale of One-Eye Charley Parkhurst, back in the last century. Ole Charley, he was the greatest, bravest, toughest stagecoach driver who ever lived. But Charley was a lady who decided to be a man. 

			
But the surprises just kept on coming. If that wasn’t one too many shockaroos—and it was—he then heard a weird howl coming from the front steps. Then another. 

			
But dang! No one inside was paying any attention to the howls right outside. He looked around wild-eyed and put his hand on his gun pocket. Since the cabin door had been left open, he quickly went to shut it, but before he could, he saw a little child on the grass holding onto the fur of a big wolf.

			
Fearing the wolf would kill the child, Joe immediately drew his pistol, but quick as lightening, Maria grabbed the gun out of his hand. 

			
“No! Peppineddu! Lassalu stari. Chiḍḍu è lu cani di Firenze!”—Leave it alone. That’s Firenze’s dog. Then, the tot and the beast proceeded to climb the front steps and come inside, where they plopped down on the floor like they belonged. 

			
What the hell is happening here? 

			
That a girl could be a boy, well, that was possible, but that a baby could have a wolf as a pet? No. Not possible. 

			
He’d been gone not quite four years, he figured. How could there be a planted plot of land, a nice house, farm animals, a bootlegging business, ranch hands, a sister/brother, and a baby with a blue-eyed pet wolf that his mother called a dog? No, not possible. 

			
He turned to the family for an answer, but they didn’t seem to think anything was strange, so he turned back around for an answer from his father, but Mariano was gone. “Wait a cotton-pickin’ minute,” Joe said using an American phrase he’d learned on the trail. “Where did Papa go?” 

			
Mariano, as usual, had high-tailed it out the door faster’n a pronghorn antelope as soon as he saw the wolf. So much for Papa having no fear. Joe didn’t know it wasn’t the wolf that scared his father so much—it was the wolf’s little companion. Even so, he was shocked at his father’s cowardice. 

			
As Joe took his hat off and scratched his head, little Flo stood up and scooted over to Joe and hugged his legs like she knew who he was. He scooped her up in his arms, keeping his eyes trained on the wolf. He wasn’t buying any of it. Yet. 

			
“Blooo,” Flo cried out happily, pointing to her “dog” laying on the floor. “Blooo! She Blooo!” she giggled over and over. 

			
And anyway, who was this kid? Was she a new sister, he asked in Sicilian. 

			
When he left, he didn’t know his mother was pregnant, though she seemed to be pregnant all the time. 

			
Callie laughed and explained the little girl had been born on New Year’s Eve 1916, at midnight, and she was one of his new baby sisters. “She’s Firenze but we call her Flo, like an American,” she said in perfect English. “Clara’s asleep in the cradle.”

			
She said the wolf had sort of adopted Flo, and that the little girl named her Blue because of the animal’s blue eyes. How a toddler knew the color blue to begin with was anybody’s guess.

			
Joe told Callie to take a breath and start over and slow it down. He was trying to get it all straight in his head—which, by the way, he never did. He’d seen some crazy things on the trail and had even smoked a pipe with the company’s Comanche scout which gave him weird dreams while he was awake—but nothing beat this for strange. 

			
“Blue has stayed close to Flo from the night of her birth,” his sister Helen added. “After a while, we all got so used to the big wolf being around that we didn’t fear her no more.” 

			
But Mariano? He was a whole different story. Nobody had the gumption to tell Joe that their papa was afraid to kill the wolf because he was more afraid of what baby Flo would do to get revenge than he was of the savage beast in their midst. So, Papa tried to stay out of their way. Mariano had even made Maria nurse Flo outside. Once she got big enough, he made her take her meals outside or all alone by the hearth during storms. 

			
By the age of three, Flo didn’t want to be separated from her brothers and sisters anymore, and she sure didn’t want to eat outside. Crying didn’t change things, so one night when the family sat at the table and she was put outside, she started to howl. Loud. This made her nasty ol’ papa so angry that he went outside with a board. He’d had it. Scared or no, he was going to end this once and for all. “Vaffanculu a mavara! Vaffanculu a lupa!”—Fuck the witch! Fuck the wolf! 

			
As she sat alone howling on the ground, her lips grew redder as her papa approached and raised the board. Without warning, Blue emerged from the woods and raced towards him. Mariano turned and high tailed it back inside. Both the child and the wolf howled until he slammed the door behind him. 

			
Maria ran out and saw no wolf—just her little one eating her pastina with her fingers. Maria grabbed her up, brought her inside, and did something she’d never done before. She put Flo at the table and told Mariano that, from now on, he could eat outside, or he could sit with the family and leave the child be. Maria, too, seemed to be gaining courage from the wolf. Or maybe from her own little daughter. 

			
She then said something to little Flo that she’d say again many times in the coming years. “Nobody is better than you. Nobody.”

			
Mariano started to get up to strike his wife when Firenze turned towards her papa and howled. Loud and scary. He swore she had also made the sign of il malocch’ at him because when he stood up, he peed all over himself. He believed this shame was caused by the wild child; she had for sure given him the evil eye. He even wanted to ask Maria to do the malocch’ test by putting water, salt, and olive oil in a bowl. Only older women could perform this test, and the reaction would tell her if he had the curse or not. But he didn’t trust his wife anymore. Maybe she would say he wasn’t cursed when he really was or say he was cursed when he wasn’t. 

			
One thing, though, he never challenged Firenze’s place in the Barbera pack again. He feared she’d hit him with the evil eye again and that the next time, he might even lose his strength. So, he saved the hitting and the punching for the others and only when little Flo was out playing in the far field and couldn’t see the beatings.

			
Everyone made excuses to Flo when she inquired about their “boo-boos.” They didn’t want to unleash Blue on Papa—no matter how hard he struck them. They could live with a broken nose or black eyes. If Mariano was dead, though, they would have no protection from the other bootlegging families who could kill their brothers, take their land and their stills. 

			
Flo had earned her place at the table, yes, but that didn’t mean that Mariano had to share a meal with her. He was afraid to look at her, at those lips he swore got even redder when he was near her, so he ordered Maria to serve him, Peter, and Joe before the rest. He refused to allow the youngest son, Austin, who had the fits, to sit with “the men.” Austin was made to eat with the women. The girls didn’t care because they loved little Austin and didn’t want him around Mariano anyway. 

			
As for Blue, she would disappear for long stretches but always came back to Flo when she wasn’t nursing pups. They played together in the field and seemed to have their own language. Flo’d even let her baby sister, Clara, a year younger, tag along. 

			
Firenze learned the way of the woods, and folks swore that Blue was as domesticated as a house dog when she was around the little girl at the homestead. 

			
A year later, Blue limped onto the Barbera’s porch and collapsed, bleeding. She’d been attacked—probably by a female in her own pack who wanted to take over as the alpha female. Blue had become too attached to this human girl to protect and defend her pack properly in those savage woods. 

			
She had become the thing wolves fear and dread most: an outcast, a lone wolf. 

			
Flo, nearly hysterical, dragged Maria outside and, throwing herself atop the wolf, begged her mother to save Blue. Maria and the boys carefully lifted the wolf and brought her into the tool shed. It was safe because Mariano rarely went in there now that he was a “cowboss” and didn’t have to repair fences or anything else himself. 

			
Maria tended to Blue as though she were one of her own human babies. She cleaned the wounds, applied moldy bread to them, and wrapped them in cotton rags while Firenze made her a bed of straw. Weeks went by, and Flo fed her bits of meat out of her hand and every few days Maria changed the dressing. Even so, it looked like the wolf might not make it, so Flo took to sleeping out in the shed with her until Blue began to improve. 

			
Her pet had gone from the brutally tough female mate of the alpha male in the pack to a wounded outcast. Flo promised her that now she’d have a new pack, even though it was a human one. 

			
They just had to keep Mariano from finding out Blue had become vulnerable and was living among them.

– 17 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 17 –

			
Carried. Nation. 

			
Trinidad, Colorado 

			
1920 

			
On January 17, 1920, the Volstead Act went into effect, making alcohol illegal in the whole US of A. 

			
Crazy as it sounds, and after all the trouble the booze ban had caused in Colorado, the do-gooder ladies of the Temperance Movement had still convinced their lily-livered politician-husbands to pass the law. As Mariano had always known, these men had no interest in giving up liquor—just in getting their nagging wives to leave them alone. 

			
But these men also figured, and rightly so, that they’d still be able to get their booze and wine. It would just be harder to come by—unless you knew the right people, that is. And for the first time in their lives, the Barberas were the right people—even if it was for all the wrong reasons.

			
Although many called illegal booze “moonshine,” the term “bootleg” took hold because it’s what the cowpokes called the hooch they hid in their boots when they traded with the Indians. So, the meaning of “bootleg” went from hiding booze to hiding booze. 

			
What not one of them could have guessed though, was how wild not just the West would get but also how wild the whole country would get. All the government had to do for hell to break loose was to keep one single thing from folks: alcohol. It made everybody go crazy wanting it. 

			
Mariano had a saying: “Keep anything from a man, and he’ll want it even if he didn’t want it before.” The sweet beet and the grape had become the forbidden fruits of the modern age. He just couldn’t understand how these Bible-thumpers didn’t liken this to Genesis. 

			
To imbibe of the forbidden fruits, the husbands of the do-gooder ladies started frequenting what they called “speakeasies,” where the booze was bad and the ladies badder. And lots of it was made and supplied by the likes of the Sicilian Colorado gangs. Mariano had been right way back when he declared that to live men needed cooch and hooch. 

			
Now that all bets were off, the stiffs in their tuxedos kicked up their heels every chance they got even though they were scared to death the law would catch them, the gangsters would ban them, or their wives would kill them if they ever found out. 

			
If things were good for Mariano and his “enterprise” when Colorado had gone dry, it got so much better—and a helluva lot more dangerous—when the whole damned country dried out. Shootouts became as common in Las Animas County as bocce. 

			
In fact, prohibition had been such a good thing for Mariano he actually—briefly—considered going to church one Sunday to thank Maria’s God for his good luck. Then he went to Mrs. Bianchi’s on Saturday night, got stinking drunk, went upstairs with his left-hand wife, Cora-Lee Jones, and had a time of it before passing out until way past church-going time the next morning. 

			
With the whole damned country begging for alcohol, the demand became too much for small operators like Mariano to handle. He was, so to speak, small grapes compared to some of his paisani bootleggers. 

			
A few families had bought trucks and were distributing booze to Chicago and New York City. Yeah, the so-called Sugar Moon Wars between the rival Sicilian families in Colorado was heating up to boiling. 

			
The time had come for the Barberas to choose sides—they needed the protection of a bigger “family,” or they risked being taken out by one of them. The La Donas and the Carlinos were going national, making deals with the newly formed mobs in Chicago, New York, Kansas City, and New Orleans. Each family had relatives in every city. Joe and Peter, who were now grown up, had a talk with Mariano. “We gotta choose a side,” Joe said. “We don’t have enough ’moon to distribute to the big cities.” 

			
“We risk everything if we’re alone,” Peter added. “It’s time to choose or lose.” 

			
“Carlino is family,” Joe said. “What with Mama’s sister, Zia Cologera married to Ziu Vito. I feel that we are safer with them.” That side of the Carlinos had come to Colorado but lived a distance away. They saw each other occasionally, more so now that they were both in the business. The Carlinos were smart and had become serious, big-time bootleggers.

			
“They are sly foxes those Carlinos,” Mariano snapped. “I wouldn’t trust them—unless we have something to offer.” 

			
What they had to offer was a thriving business, loyal workers, loyal customers, and a ranch through which to launder money. 

			
Because blood is thicker than water, even if the blood connection was through Maria’s side, they finally settled on siding with the Carlinos. 

			
Although he would never admit it to anybody, Mariano had to first approach Sam Carlino, and then if Sam was agreeable, he’d move onto the big cheese, his father, Don Vito Carlino. A deal was struck, and the Barbera’s family enterprise became part of the Carlinos’ growing national operation. 

			
Don Vito would never break his word, but to make sure the Carlino brothers—Sam, Pete, and Carlo—kept their end of the deal, Mariano made a double blood pact: His not-even seventeen-year-old daughter, Callie, would be betrothed to her twenty-one-year-old cousin, Carlo, who was called “Charlie.” Mariano had really wanted to give Charlie his thirteen-year-old daughter, Laura, with the black eyes, big dimples, beautiful face, curly brown hair, and grown-woman’s figure. That would have been a good deal for the Carlinos. 

			
But Maria threatened to have little Flo sic Blue on him if he offered up young Laura, so he backed off. Maria had learned that brains, a magical child, and a wolf on your side were great levelers against a fists-first spouse. 

			
Callie was the girl Maria worried about the most anyway. She was seventeen and not what you’d exactly call marriage material. 

			
The business deal they made was Callie’s dowry: The Carlinos got partial control of Mariano’s business with half the profits going back to the Barberas. The embroidered linens Callie had made with Maria just as Maria’s mother had made with her, were just a little extra that nobody but Callie cared about. 

			
Charlie Carlino, was what they called a “dandy.” He liked his booze, he liked his poker, he liked the ladies, and he liked to take baths. He went around sporting cowpoke duds that you would never see on other stockmen or gunslingers. You’d likely see him riding into town looking like a real buscadero wearing white fur chaps made from sheep or goat or some Goddamned hide, and below that, carved leather boots with honest-to-God silver spurs. He always had his long gun slung over his back the way some musicians carry their guitars. On his head, he likely as not wore a spotless, white ten-gallon hat with a leather band and silver stars around the brim. He even wore a carved leather holster that held his six-shooter. Back then, almost every man carried his gun in his pocket or tucked into his belt, so Charlie looked, well…like a sideshow cowboy in a Wild West show. 

			
“Oobatz!”—crazy—the Sicilians would laugh—but not to his face.

			
The cowpokes in town used to say. “The boy don’t have nothin’ under his hat but hair,” but that wasn’t true. That cowboy had plenty of notches on his gun and brains in his head, no matter how fancy his clothes were. 

			
He just liked to dress up. 

			
Not that a fella dressing up like that didn’t cause talk. Most other men looked like they’d been sleeping outside too long or mining too hard. 

			
Not Charlie.

			
Looking like a dandy man was his game. It kept everybody off guard. He’d show up to a poker game all dressed up so the marks would take him for a mark, and he’d usually wipe them out. Sure, he had to kill a few gamblers along the way, but who didn’t? He liked to say, “If you don’t know who the sucker at the table is, then it’s you.”

			
On the first day Charlie came courting, Callie had washed up good, tied her hair in ribbons, and put on a dress trimmed in lace that she had made herself. She was waiting for her intended with her Mama and Papa out on the front porch when Charlie rode up on his big black horse with those silver spurs and the stars on his hat sparkling like fire in the sunlight. By God, he dazzled like a prince. 

			
Callie, who was much shyer than her mother had ever been, turned so red at the site of him approaching that Charlie thought maybe she was all burnt up from working out in the field like a farmhand. When he tipped his hat, Callie got to shaking so hard Maria worried that she might fall off the steps. 

			
To make it all worse, Callie wasn’t anybody’s idea of a beauty queen—nice gal but plain in the face, a broken nose, skinny as store-bought thread and flat as the side of a barn. So, all that shaking and shying that could have looked charming on a pretty woman, well, on poor Callie, it looked like a case of the palsy. 

			
Charlie wasn’t what you’d call taken with the young lady. But since the families had worked it out and it was all decided, he did his duty and made a little small talk with Callie for a few minutes. When he got up to go talk to Mariano about future product distribution, that poor girl jumped up so fast she spilled her mama’s homemade red wine-and-peaches all over Charlie’s pretty, white-fur chaps. Those furry pants ended up looking like they’d been attacked by a boar in a peach orchard. He said it was alright, but he didn’t mean it. To a dandy like Charlie, wrecking his duds was worse than shooting his horse. 

			
Maria knew Charlie wouldn’t be a good or faithful husband to Callie, but who was? At least she and Mariano had fallen hard in love as soon as they laid eyes on each other, so he had been faithful. At first. 

			
She had taught Callie, like all her daughters, to cook, sew, and keep a clean house. She tried to talk about sex with the girl the night before the wedding, but Callie looked bored. Maria was worried. 

			
The wedding between Callie Barbera and Charlie Carlino was planned to take place at The Sacred Heart Cathedral in Pueblo. The family’s own priest, who had also relocated from Lucca Sicula—Father Angeletto—was on tap to perform the service. 

			
Callie was done up in a beautiful white dress, white stockings, and button top shoes with high heels. Her sisters had braided her hair with flowers, and her mother had embroidered a veil. Together they had pulled Callie’s corset strings so tight they almost broke her ribs, but the poor girl was so skinny they could have pulled ‘till the cows came home, and she still wouldn’t have any curves. 

			
Charlie, on the other hand, in a silk coattail suit with his long, black, curly hair trailing out the bottom of his silk top hat was so pretty, he put even Laura and Angelina to shame. He looked like a God. 

			
One of the women in the church, whispered too loud, “Oooowee! That boy’s as fine as dollar cotton,” which set up embarrassed giggles.

			
Taking no chances, Mariano did not lift Callie’s veil for the handoff, but when Charlie did, the murmuring from some of the meaner lady guests could have been heard all the way to Denver. 

			
Everybody felt sorry for the bride except for one Carlino cousin, Donatella, who was crazy, madly, in love with Charlie. She whispered to another disappointed cousin, Felicia, “Don’t feel bad for her, she got the best-lookin’ man in the state. Feel bad for me because he should have been mine.” 

			
Felicia elbowed Donatella in the ribs and said, “Calm down! He wudda cheated on you, just like he’s gonna cheat on poor, poor Callie.” 

			
Donatella laughed almost out loud. “Yeah, but it wudda taken him a whole lot longer to start!” 

			
Callie knew they were laughing about her less-than-beauty-queen face and stick figure, and she burned with shame. But she had a little secret of her own. 

			
What they didn’t know was that Callie had snuck into town more than once to pay a visit to Mariano’s left-hand wife, Cora-Lee Jones. Callie wanted to learn a thing or three about pleasing a man. 

			
What Callie had known about sex up until that point, she’d learned from watching livestock. What she learned from Cora-Lee no cow ever did to no bull. Sugar Moon and vino flowed like spring water at the wedding party which was held in the Carlino’s meadow. The Carlinos supplied bunkhouse-big pots of Sunday gravy—tomato sauce with braciole, pork ribs, meatballs, and homemade sausage—which was served with the pasta. 

			
The Barberas supplied the American BBQ. They’d slaughtered a goat, a pig, and ten chickens. The women had made every kind of Italian pastry. When the dancing started, everyone headed into the spotless barn that had been decorated with flowers and paper streamers. Peter had hired a bunch of the locals to play music with them: Joe on guitar, Peter and Mickey on fiddles. 

			
Much to the annoyance of the new couple’s fathers, the local musicians were Greek and Black fellas, so the closest they got to the tarantella were square dance tunes. Joe, Mickey, and Peter had to tell them to take a break so they could play the Italian songs. The crowd made them play until their fingers practically fell off, with every old man in the crowd getting up to sing. 

			
Charlie was so graceful on his feet when he waltzed with Donatella and kicked up his heels with the Barbera sisters in a tarantella, it was like his patent leather shoes with spats didn’t touch the ground. Even when he was square dancing, he was smooth as silk with his coattails flying out behind him like he was a bird. Callie watched him, her love overflowing like water over a falls. He knew she didn’t dance, so he occupied himself with the willing ladies. Callie was OK with it—as long as he didn’t make her dance. 

			
Joe, Peter, and even Austin were all good dancers, and they had all tried to teach Callie dance steps before the wedding, but it was about as hopeless as trying to put socks on a rooster. 

			
So, at the wedding shindig, each brother took a turn sitting and talking with Callie while the other brothers whirled their sisters and all the other pretty young ladies on the dance floor. 

			
They were trying to make Callie look occupied so she could somehow get away without dancing. But it wasn’t to be. When the crowd demanded it, Charlie finally did the traditional thing and took his new wife’s hand to lead her to the dance floor. Callie, bless her heart, turned purple with embarrassment. So did her mama, while her brothers and papa ran to the booze barrel. They knew what was coming: The Wedding Dance. 

			
The women tied colorful streamers onto the hands of the bride and groom, as the guests surrounded them, and each took hold of a streamer. The guests were supposed to wrap the streamers around the couple as they swirled around in a waltz. 

			
Callie tried following her new husband, but she was so clumsy she nearly choked herself on the streamers. Worse, she stomped all over Charlie’s beautiful white spats with each step. The crowd stood there with their mouths open, and even the meanest of them was embarrassed for her. Peter, Austin, Joe, and Mariano belted back the booze like it was banned, which it actually was. 

			
As soon as the hellish dance display was over, Charlie headed right to his family’s house and changed into his boots. 

			
“What the hell did Papa get this poor girl into?” Peter whispered to his brothers. 

			
“Yeah,” Austin whispered back. “Charlie looks mad as a sheep-killin’ dog. I hope he don’t take it out on Callie once they get alone.”

			
They belted back more booze. 

			
Papa didn’t care if Charlie whipped the girl bloody once they got alone—or even every day of her life. Mariano Barbera was a happy man. He had pulled off the deal of a lifetime. 

			
When the time finally came for the bride and groom to leave, Charlie unhappily lifted Callie onto the buckboard that had been decorated with flowers and cowbells, lest anyone miss the hooting and hollering as the couple rode off to make history. Or to make misery as Charlie’s cousin Luigi joked. 

			
Charlie and his brothers had built the couple a nice little house close to the Carlino’s main home so that Charlie could hang out with his brothers until he was forced to go home to his new bride. 

			
When the couple got to their new house, Charlie, as was his nature, did the right thing and carried his new bride over the threshold. She nearly passed out with nervous excitement as this was the first time a man ever touched her. 

			
I am the luckiest girl in the whole world, Callie thought when he lifted her up even though the bones in her corset nearly pierced a lung. All Charlie was thinking was that she was so skinny he could feel her bones through her corset. 

			
He had been hard drinking all day, so he knew he was too drunk for loving her up that night. With any luck, he thought, he’d just pass out. And for that he thanked the Good Lord. Maybe he’d have to stay drunk the rest of his life. 

			
How was he supposed to ever do right by his bride? How was he going to give her babies? He just did not have desire for her. 

			
It got worse. Charlie was so drunk it took a while to wobble back to the front door to kick it closed. But when he turned around, he was so shocked by what he saw, he sobered up in about half a second. In the time it took to shut the door, Callie had managed to take off every bit of her bridal getup except the veil covering her face and stood before him, naked as the day God made her. 

			
He was about to run out and try to figure out what the hell to do, when Callie grabbed onto him, unbuttoned his pants, pulled them to the floor, took his member in her hand and began to do what Cora-Lee had shown her on a wooden doohickey she kept on her dresser. He was so shocked that he jumped back in horror. 

			
“How did you learn that? You’re not just unsightly, girl, but you’re actin’ like a whore to boot!” he screamed. 

			
Callie, that usual nervous Nellie, got very calm and easily recited exactly what Cora-Lee had taught her to say: “I know you could take any woman to bride, but our families made a pact. I am your wife now, and since I am not as comely as some, I learned how to please my future husband in other ways. From a woman who is an expert in these things.” 

			
Charlie was so shocked he tripped and fell on the floor while trying to pull up his pants and get out the door at the same time. 

			
“Who?” was all he could get out from this position on the floor. 

			
“Papa’s left-hand wife, that’s who.” 

			
“Cora-Lee?” he cried out like he’d been shot. 

			
As Charlie lay there unable to move like a man who’d been shot for real, butt-naked Callie slid on top of him, wiggled her way down, removed her veil, smiled up at him, and put him in her mouth. 

			
Cora-Lee was right, Callie thought. This is a whole helluva lot of fun! 

			
They definitely made history—not misery—that night. All night. And every night. 

			
Never was there a more faithful Carlino man than Charlie. Not that he gave up the card table, the booze, or walking around like a popinjay, but he left the tables earlier, stayed home longer, and arrived at the stills every morning looking happy as any man who’d been out whoring all night. Nobody ever imagined that Charlie’s “whore” was his own wife. 

			
Mariano himself couldn’t figure it out. He knew the Carlino brothers were not happy that the handsomest of the Carlino men had ended up with a girl no man would look at twice. The family’s oldest brother and next in line, Sam Carlino, couldn’t say anything since he had agreed to the matchup. That girl, he’d tell his wife, would never produce children worthy of the Carlino name. But good ole Charlie never said a word to anybody, leaving them to speculate about what the hell he was up to. 

			
Everybody saw it and everybody said it: Since the wedding, Charlie always had a smile on his face. He’d sometimes lose himself in thought in the middle of business and start grinning like a loon. And Callie? She wasn’t just happy she was happifying her man, he was happifying her every single night too. 

			
The girls down at Mrs. Bianchi’s even started complaining about how much they missed Charlie. They thought he’d be back the night after the wedding looking for some good lovin’, but he never showed up for them again. 

			
Back when he’d been out whoring every night, those ladies would fight over who’d get him upstairs first. No more. He had stopped looking at other women altogether. 

			
Charlie was such a happy man that he would take Callie all the way to Denver to buy her fancy bonnets and silk dresses with ruffles and ribbons. One time he even bought her a new-fangled, short, and very racy flapper dress to show off her great legs, which had grown fatter from living the good life. Nobody in Pueblo had ever seen a lady’s legs on display like that. Hell, not even a lady’s ankles. Well, not any lady who didn’t work over at Mrs. Bianchi’s anyway. They’d drive around in his new Model-T Ford, and when they’d stop, she’d swing her legs out, the tops of her silk stockings on display bold as brass. 

			
Yup, her gams became as much the talk of the town as her hubby’s duds. 

			
And Callie was always happy to help her dapper dandy pick out even fancier duds for himself. He took to wearing a white fedora with a feather in the hat band on special occasions. The couple dressed in such finery that folks stopped to stare at them. Nobody could ever figure him out before he got married, and after he was hitched, he became more of a curiosity than ever. What kinda man not just dressed like that himself but dressed his wife up like a doll? It wasn’t normal.

			
“Maybe Charlie is a two-spirit,” Yellow Fly, a Ute cowhand at the Barbera ranch said to his brother Water Man, who everybody called Walter. 

			
“Could be,” said Walter. In two seconds flat, the other hands joined in the speculating. 

			
“You mean he’s a feller who likes men, not the ladies?” asked Jimmy John, a drifter cowhand who roamed from ranch to ranch. 

			
“No,” Yellow Fly explained. “We believe there are lady-ladies, man-ladies, lady-men, man-men, and some who aren’t neither or maybe both.”

			
The bunkhouse grew quiet as the fellas tried to make sense of what Yellow Fly had said.

			
Finally, somebody piped up with, “But that’s more’n two spirits or whatever you call it, that’s four.” 

			
“Five, not four,” said Lawrence “Bones” T. Bone, the cook who showed up for cattle drives and poker games.

			
One of the Negro cowhands, Bill Black aka Black Bill thought about it and finally said that even though Charlie was sure pretty enough to be a lady-man, he and Callie sounded like they couldn’t get enough of each other. 

			
“Those two are louder than howlin’ wolves,” his cousin Silas pointed out. “That don’t sound like no lady-man to me.” 

			
No, none of that talk was to make fun of Charlie, no sirree. Nobody back then cared very much who a man had sex with. There weren’t any ladies on cattle drives, so well, a man found his loving where he could. 

			
But cowhands have leaky mouths as everybody knows and love to yak it up just for the sake of it. 

			
Truth be told, Charlie was a favorite among the men because he was tough, he was fair, he was funny, and he was so beautiful to look at that even the roughest of the men couldn’t help but stare at him. 

			
Mariano, of course, had heard talk about Charlie’s possible “proclivities.” He just ignored it. 

			
Not that he didn’t think it himself. One time, he told Cora-Lee that any man who walked around town in white fur pants must be finocchio. She just smiled. You didn’t have to be Sicilian to figure out what finocchio meant. 

			
One thing was sure: If Charlie had ever been a two-spirit fella, he was strictly a one-woman man now.

– 18 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 18 –

			
The Great Escape 

			
Pueblo, Colorado

			
June 3, 1921 

			
Flo managed to reach the age of five without Mariano doing her in. That was a good thing. It meant she could start school. 

			
School? No way was she going to school, she cried, her lips even more ruby-red than usual. Every day? No way! Not without Blue, and not without Clara she screamed. Flo wasn’t the kind of child you could punish, reason with, or smack into submission. Not even Maria could give her a good whack on her coulo when she deserved it for fear of Blue jumping into the scene. 

			
Flo stomped her feet and ran off with that wolf of hers and wouldn’t come back, so Maria went searching and found the two of them sleeping in the woods. Since she couldn’t make her do what she wanted her to do, Maria had figured out long before this incident that she had to make deals with her stubborn daughter. 

			
Maria promised Flo that she’d lie about Clara’s age to the school marm and tell her the girls were twins. In exchange, Blue would have to wait for them outside—no wolves allowed in school. 

			
Maria figured she’d get away with it because it wasn’t like anybody had been there to record their births. Flo and Clara had been born in the wild in the shack on the mountain. And they had no birth certificates, so who cared what the teacher might suspect. 

			
Even though Clara was tiny for her age and Flo was tall for hers, Miss Trainer, the school marm, allowed them both into the school but warned Maria that she’d be checking with the town hall to be sure of Clara’s age. With Flo interpreting, Miss Trainer warned Maria, “Either she’s a midget, or you aren’t being honest with me, Mrs. Bearbary,” deliberately mispronouncing their last name. 

			
Maria didn’t like the teacher lady’s tone, but she believed Miss Trainer would never be able to find out Clara’s real age anyway. She didn’t realize that since the family had gone legit (sort of), and bought property, they had participated in the government census. Peter told Maria that the kids’ ages were written down in black and white for the government to see. Worse, he said, the town clerk who collected taxes had probably made birth certificates once the children were registered on the census. Clara was eleven months younger than Flo and not old enough to go to school, so she would never be allowed back once the teacher found out. Flo cried her eyes out to Joe and Peter as though the world was coming to an end, and it broke their hearts. These tough guys were soft as creamed butter when it came to their little sisters, and there wasn’t much they wouldn’t do for them. 

			
That very night, the town hall just happened to burn to the ground, taking all the records with it. The Barbera family was never questioned about the fire or the ages of the children by anybody ever again. 

			
Clara lived the rest of her life believing she was Flo’s twin. 

			
The “twins” continued to ride the family donkey—they called her Bella Ragazza—to the one-room schoolhouse and back every day with only Blue as their guide and protector. They only missed school when the snows came. But now it was June, and they had finished nearly a whole school term with nothing going wrong. 

			
As for Blue, she had recovered, though she had lost the wild animal savagery she needed to lead her old pack. But she was still strong and wild enough to tear a man or most beasts limb from limb, and Mariano knew he was number one on Blue’s kill list. He didn’t know why the wolf was no longer with her pack, but he knew enough to stay out of her way. 

			
She was now as domesticated a wolf as anyone had ever heard of and was Flo’s closest companion. She waited outside the schoolhouse every day until the girls were dismissed to guide them back home. Dismissal often meant finding the bloody carcass of a cougar, beaver, or other animal near the schoolhouse. Blue’s fangs and fur would be smeared with blood—but probably never her own. It was enough to keep Miss Trainer in her place. 

			
Now that the Barberas had a bigger house and the kids even had beds, Maria and Flo would wash the blood off Blue in the stream before the beast curled up on Flo and Clara’s bed. 

			
School meant that, for the first time, the girls met kids who weren’t in their family or even in their family’s tiny circle of other Sicilian families. Some kids weren’t their color, and most of them either didn’t know much, if any, English or had accents so thick it was hard to understand what they were saying. They all were afraid of Blue but understood she was there to protect Flo and Clara. Miss Trainer would make Flo send Blue off into the woods during recess for fear that the wolf would drag one of the kids away. 

			
Flo and Clara learned the alphabet and had begun to write out sentences. They had little chalkboards and would proudly show Mama how they wrote their own names and words like “house” and “horse” in English. The first word Flo learned to spell was “Blue.” Maria was so thrilled her girls could write she nearly cried, even though she could not read the words they wrote. Mariano only cared that his sons had learned to read and write in English, could add and subtract numbers, and could shoot a man’s eye out before he got his gun cocked. 

			
Maria knew the danger her family faced in the bootlegging business, but all in all, America had gone—in her mind—from a nightmare to a mixed blessing. 

			
Life was hard wherever you lived, but where else would her “unmarriageable” daughter, Callie, be so happily married to a rich, handsome man who worshipped her? Charlie didn’t care that Callie wasn’t shapely or that she had a big, broken nose. Charlie was so wild for Callie that if she had ever told him the truth—that it was her father who broke her nose when she was twelve—Charlie would for sure have gone and killed Mariano as dead as Caesar. She never did tell him because it was important to keep the peace between her new family and old. 

			
Maria was proud of all her children. Back in Sicily, her daughters—or even her sons for that matter—would never have gone to school. They would never have learned to read and write. And she herself would never have lived on her own husband’s ranch with milking cows, goats, and chickens. And recently, Joe and the ranch hands had driven one hundred head of yearling steer from Ogallala, Nebraska, to the Barbera’s spread. They were true American ranchers. 

			
Mariano—who could ride a horse with the best of them—didn’t go to pick up the cattle with them because there was the more profitable bootleg operation to tend to. 

			
He did, however, design a cattle brand and had the local blacksmith make the branding irons and a sign which he hung at the front edge of their fenced-off property. The “Circle Double Bar A” ranch it was called because the brand looked like two bars with a circle underneath and a bottomless triangle on top. It spelled out in symbols the family’s name with a circle for “unity.”

			
For the first time since Maria and Mariano married, there was food aplenty, fresh water from their own spring, homegrown vegetables to eat, and sugar beets to turn into Sugar Moon—and, of course, there was plenty of homemade wine. The fact that her husband was violent was less important to her now that she had Blue and Flo to keep the beast at bay. 

			
Sure, it was a hard life, having to make three meals a day for the family and the ranch hands. But her daughters did their part too. 

			
Maria was actually glad that Mariano had taken a left-hand wife to keep him occupied, though she never let on that she knew about her. Better her than me, she’d say to herself when Mariano would stay away for days. There was talk that Cora-Lee had a child by Mariano, but that was none of her business either. 

			
When he was home, Mariano would start drinking hard before the girls got home from school or supper was on the table. Then he’d drag Maria to their bed, hold her down, and take her over and over again. Then she’d go back out—usually with fresh bruises—and finish cooking. 

			
She didn’t think to call what happened to her “rape” because he was her husband and had his rights, but it was rape no matter which way you called it. 

			
What was Maria’s business was an understanding that bootlegging had brought them a good life. Make no mistake, she kept an eye on every barrel and every cent, even after the Carlinos took over most of the business. That little Sicilian lady was proving every day, and in every way, that she was very much the brains behind the bootleg. She also began keeping a strict eye on the product and the livestock. 

			
In fact, when the steers arrived, she, along with Mickey and Joe, took control of the branding and bookkeeping as each calf was set out to graze. It was a beautiful thing to behold. 

			
No one from the old country would ever have imagined that this could have been possible. Barbera Bootleg Booze had bought them honest-to-God American respectability. Well, almost. Mariano was a real Colorado rancher living the American dream. 

			
But the dream meant he would always be looking over his shoulder for a prohi, a fed, a sheriff, a bounty hunter, or worse, “soldiers” from a rival family armed to the teeth looking to do them in. Mariano had killed Rocky’s men for revenge, but since becoming a bootlegger, he had also killed two rival bootleggers for the hell of it and a bounty hunter to warn anybody else who might come looking for him. Those stories, the rumors around them, and his wanted poster helped build Mariano’s reputation as a dago to be feared. 

			
But anything worth having was worth risking it all to keep it. After a terrible, tragic start, life in this America had turned out to be good for the Barberas. Until it wasn’t. 

			
June 3, 1921, started out like every other day—except for one little thing. Flo had caused a ruckus in the classroom the day before when she smacked another girl—very hard—for making fun of Blue. As punishment, the teacher told Flo she’d have to stay after school and clean the chalkboard erasers, wash down the chalkboard, and sweep the schoolhouse. And Clara had to stay after school, too, because she couldn’t go home by herself. Maria wasn’t happy with Flo’s wild ways (or the schoolmarm’s strict ways, either, for that matter) but figured it wouldn’t do Flo any harm to get some discipline.

			
In the late afternoon, Blue began an unholy howling outside the schoolhouse. Miss Trainer threw her hands up. That wolf was always creating a commotion. When she wouldn’t allow Flo to go outside to see what was bothering Blue, the wolf started scratching at the schoolhouse door. Miss Trainer had had it up to here with the beast and finally screamed at Flo to climb out the window, afraid to open the door for fear the wolf would charge inside. Clara scrambled after her sister and got halfway out the window before the teacher grabbed her feet to keep her from escaping. Didn’t work. Clara kicked the teacher’s hands away and wiggled her way out. 

			
Once both were outside, Blue, acting crazy, bit the hem of Flo’s calico dress—the one she loved best with the flowers on it—and dragged her back to the tied-up donkey like a mother grabbing a bad kid by the ear. Little Clara was close behind. 

			
Miss Trainer yelled at them to come back. “If you dare leave, you’ll face the strap tomorrow. I warn you!” When they didn’t turn around, she leaned all the way out the window. “That’s it! Don’t come back! You’re expelled!” 

			
Little Clara, tears streaming down her face, begged, “Please, Miss. Please don’t. My papa will be so mad.” 

			
“Too bad. And don’t send your Guinea gunslinger father here either.” 

			
And that was the last thing Miss Trainer said. Ever. 

			
Clara turned to Flo. “A signorina ni jetta fora se nun tunnamu ‘intra!”—Miss will expel us if we don’t go back inside!

			
Flo, whose lips were now redder than a red-hot chili pepper, said she didn’t care about the stupid teacher anyway. Flo and Blue had their own way of communicating, and she knew that if Blue needed them to go home, they better get a move on. Blue felt something. 

			
Is Mama sick? Did the house burn down? Did Austin take a fit and die? 

			
“Andemu!”—Let’s go!—Flo screamed, even though she knew they were going to get a helluva good paddling from Mama if they didn’t go to school the next day…and a worse paddling from Miss Trainer if they did. 

			
Clara was afraid, so Flo leaned down and grabbed her up like a rag doll and pulled her onto the donkey, as tears ran down little Clara’s face. 

			
Blue ran in front of the donkey. She wasn’t headed towards home, but to higher ground, while constantly looking back, trying to get the donkey to get a move on. Flo was trying to do the same. But donkeys are donkeys, so Bella kept wanting to head home as she usually did. 

			
Blue bared her teeth, which scared the bejesus out of Bella, and she snapped to and reluctantly followed. Blue was leading them to the high ground way above the schoolhouse. “Mama’s gonna give us a lickin’ iffin we get home late,” Clara cried. 

			
Flo shushed Clara and told her Blue must know something they didn’t. “Capish?” 

			
“Si,” Clara answered, hanging her head. “I unnerstan’.” 

			
She didn’t, but she would.
