– 19 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 19 –

			
Rivers Over Flo

			
Pueblo, Colorado 

			
June 3, 1921 

			
Dark clouds hung low along the lofty range, 

			
When broke the storm, Hell’s fury seemed unleashed 

			
To wreck and ruin all the universe… 

			
—Walt Drummond (published in The Pueblo Chieftain June 17, 1921) 

			
As the little band was climbing up that rise, without warning, the sky got very dark, and they could see a cloudburst forming over the city. The clouds opened up, and the rains started falling like nobody’s business. Clara started crying again, griping, and blaming Blue for getting them stuck in this terrible storm, when they could have stayed safe and dry inside the schoolhouse. The winds got to whipping, so the girls found a flat piece of ground where they could tie the little donkey to a tree. Then they did as best they could in the wind and rain to unpack the saddle bag. They managed to get the rain tarp out and covered themselves and Blue, too, praying they wouldn’t die. 

			
Next, they heard an ear-splitting roar down below where the mighty Arkansas River met Fountain creek. Clara screamed and Flo grabbed Blue to keep from screaming. Blue had her fur up as though she could fight the storm. They peeked out from underneath the tarp and saw that the rivers had swelled up higher than heaven. The water’s rise wasn’t just from the ungodly amount of rain but also from the runoff of snow rushing down the mountain like it was its own river, flowing into both the swollen Arkansas River and Fountain Creek. 

			
Even though the girls couldn’t see it as it happened, the rising water had breached the eighteen-foot levee and smashed a one-hundred-foot hole in it. Water surged over the city like the biblical flood. The girls once again peeked out of their makeshift tent and saw horses, cattle and, yes, human beings, being washed away. Then they heard the sounds of buildings collapsing. 

			
Clara screamed, “Bella! Bella!” as the poor little donkey was getting battered by the storm. Bella was braying in terror, but there was just no room for her under the tarp.

			
They lifted the rain tarp up some more—enough to see if Bella was still alive (she was). Unbeknownst to them, the city of Pueblo was, for the most part, getting swept off the face of the earth. 

			
The storm raged for hours, the two little girls not knowing if they’d live or die out there. Blue had forced them to higher ground, saving their lives, and stayed by their side. As the night and the storm wore on, the girls huddled together, their arms wrapped around each other. Blue lay straddled over their laps, keeping them safe and as warm as she could. 

			
After God knows how long, the torrential rains finally took a break—although it was still raining on and off. When dawn broke, the girls, soaking wet and covered in mud, threw the tarp aside. 

			
Bella, battered and covered in branches, mud, and leaves, had broken loose but was still standing nearby. She had survived. The girls ran to her, hugged and kissed her, told her they were sorry she’d had to stay alone during the storm, and promised they’d never do that to her again. 

			
They dried her off with their bare hands, tried to get the mud from her eyes, and walked her down the rise a bit, holding her tight. 

			
When they got to within viewing distance of Pueblo itself, they beheld what couldn’t be possible. Pueblo looked like it was half underwater and mostly destroyed; shattered buildings floated atop the water. 

			
With Blue guiding the way, they began to make their way home, their feet sinking up to their ankles in the mud. The high ground was indeed a slippery slope even for two little girls who’d spent most of their lives outdoors riding horses, planting, digging crops, and learning the ways of the natural world. They kept slipping and sliding as the winds blew and the rains came and went. 

			
They had to get home. But was there a home to go home to anymore? 

			
They didn’t get far because they couldn’t get to the town. Everything was either gone, blocked by debris, flooded, floating away, or on fire. 

			
They got close enough to behold a sight so terrifying, they had no way to make sense of what they were seeing. Giant blocks of fire, floating on top of the river like hell’s own monsters, rushed by. How could the river be on fire? 

			
These fire monsters floated at top speed right into the heart of Pueblo, burning whatever buildings in their paths hadn’t already been swept away. The terrible crashing of buildings collapsing went on and on. 

			
The screams of the dying and injured survivors were worse to the ears of these two young ones—only four and five years old—than were the sounds of the city being destroyed. They sat down on the slippery, sloping ground and stared at what they could see of what remained of the bustling, modern city of Pueblo. Just the day before, the city had hotels, an opera house, stores, businesses, paved streets, trolley cars, sewers, horseless trucks and carriages, plus one of the most modern railroad stations in Colorado, which had seven railroads running in and out of there. 

			
But now everything was uprooted, wrecked, overturned, or gone. 

			
They heard terrible high-pitched sounds and watched a herd of distressed cattle floating down river to their deaths. It sounded like the screams from hell Mama had told them about—the screams of the sinners who would spend eternity burning. 

			
Drowned horses were swept away, whole families huddled on the roofs of houses because the rest was under water. Two railroad passenger cars had capsized, and ladies—wearing dresses cut so low that their breasts were out—were standing on balconies until the river rose up to sweep them away, screaming and waving for help. Cottonwood trees rushed down river, the roots which once held them to the ground pitching skyward like the devil’s own pitchforks. 

			
Worst of all, they saw the twisted bodies of infants and children, all alone, being swept along the mighty river like toys in a carnival game. 

			
How could they ever find home again? 

			
They thought they could make out the schoolhouse—or what was left of it. If they had stayed there, like Miss Trainer had ordered, they would probably be dead. They had gone to higher ground, but they didn’t see any other people up there. Were they the only ones who had gone into the hills? 

			
Pretty much. The good citizens of Pueblo had thought the rising waters would be a grand sight to behold, so hundreds of them made their way to the levee to watch the spectacle. They were having a high old time of it—until the water breached the eighteen-foot-high wall and came crashing through. 

			
But, by God, the little Barbera girls, their wolf, and their donkey were up, up, and away from the flood, though they were a long and dangerous way from home and their mama. 

			
Flo and Clara, bedraggled, scared, wet, cold, and so tired that they needed a rest, moved to a big rock, sat down, and hugged Blue. “You will be our mama until we find our mama. Right, Blue?” 

			
Blue started howling. 

			
That’s when the high, sloping, muddy ground began to slide away. First, it took little Bella. 

			
 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 20 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 20 –

			
The Missing Whore 

			
Pueblo, Colorado

			
June 1921 

			
The Barbera Ranch was far enough from the raging rivers to remain mostly OK except for a section of crops that got damaged from the torrential rain that had fallen. 

			
Word spread that Pueblo was destroyed. Many, many dead. The schoolhouse? Who knew? 

			
Maria cried, screamed, and pulled at her hair like a wild woman, repeating over and over, “Unni sunnu li me picciriddi? Unni sunnu Flo e la picciridda Clara?”—Where are my babies? Where are Flo and little Clara?

			
She got on her knees, begging her go-to saint, St. Anthony, finder of lost things, to help them not just find Flo and Clara, but to find them alive and safe. Meantime, Mariano—who months earlier had set up his left-hand wife, Cora-Lee, in her own bordello downtown—was more concerned about his investment than his missing children. His name was not on the property deed or any legal papers because he couldn’t risk the law raiding the joint and remembering he was still a wanted man. So, he and Cora-Lee just shook on it—that would be the bed, not their hands.

			
He’d financed his gal’s sporting house to make money, yes, but also to keep Cora-Lee from catting around with other men at Mrs. Bianchi’s. As a madam and owner of her newly named Miss Cora-Lee’s Tea Parlour, she got to run her own girls, and Mariano got to run her. Or so he liked to believe.

			
He felt that she was now his property and his alone. He never compared his “ownership” of her to his once being owned by Rockefeller in the mine. Since Cora-Lee held the deed, she let him think what he wanted. She knew otherwise. 

			
For over a year, Mariano had it made in the shade like lemonade stirred with a spade, because he not only skimmed off the top of the whoring business, he was also able to run Barbera booze through the joint to boot. 

			
So, as soon as the heaviest rains let up, Mariano rustled up his sons and some ranch hands. They saddled up and rode towards Pueblo. They were supposed to be looking for his missing daughters, but Mariano was only interested in finding Cora-Lee. 

			
They watched as the Arkansas River receded. Floating on top and rushing by them were human bodies and dead animals, big and small, in the still-raging waters. 

			
They were stopped before they reached the actual town by a sheriff’s posse. The Barberas thought they were after them for bootlegging, but the last thing these lawmen were interested in at that point was the Barbera gang. Mariano and his crew immediately saw why their “enterprise” was of no concern to the law. 

			
And the sight nearly knocked those tough-as-nails cowpokes off their saddles. 

			
The town of Pueblo was no more. Stores, houses, and places of business were destroyed. Mud covered the horseless carriages, so that they looked like clay sculptures. 

			
The once-elegant hotels, boarding houses, and businesses that survived were still knee-deep in water. Trains were derailed, miles of track were still underwater, and two passenger train cars had rolled over on their sides. 

			
Even Mariano was nearly brought to tears by the massive destruction. His tears dried very quickly however, when the sheriff’s deputy asked, his brogue thick as an Irish bog, “Did ye come to volunteer?” When they shook their heads, the deputy told them that by order of Governor Oliver H. Shoup, all men entering town had to aid in the rescue and cleanup. “Go to work or go to jail.” 

			
Joe tried to explain to the deputized lawmen that their little sisters—just four and five years old—were missing. He promised that they’d work their asses off in town after they found the girls. 

			
“Go to work or go to jail,” the lawman repeated. Mariano was about to draw his pistol when Joe stopped him and asked for a compromise: The whole group would stay and work if he alone would be allowed to go search for the girls. 

			
“They never came home from the schoolhouse,” Peter explained. The deputy, who was just a kid himself, said, “I’m real sorry to tell you this, mister, but the schoolhouse was hit hard. Water reached way high,” he said raising his hand above his head. 

			
Then he removed his ten-gallon, looked away, and said, “They found the body of the schoolmarm, Miss Trainer, floating two mile downriver.” 

			
“I know my little sisters escaped,” Joe said without any reason to believe it other than he refused to believe otherwise.

			
Mariano had other concerns. “E li burdelli?” he asked. You didn’t have to speak Italian to know he was asking about the bordellos. 

			
His question shamed his sons and shocked the deputies. How could he be asking about whores in the midst of all the horror and destruction and possible loss of his daughters, the lawmen wondered. 

			
His sons and the ranch hands weren’t surprised; they were just disgusted.

			
Joe dismounted, hat in hand, and quietly apologized for his father’s crudeness, and begged to be allowed to search for his little sisters. 

			
The sheriff nodded his head and told Joe he could go look for the girls, but the rest of the crew had to stay and clean up. Especially that father of his. 

			
The men were put to work cleaning, moving logs, breaking down what was left of destroyed buildings and houses. Even Mariano had no way to get out of it. He really just wanted to go look for Cora-Lee. He shamelessly approached a few of his old compari who had been put to work righting an overturned truck and asked if they knew where Cora-Lee had got to.

			
They all shook their heads. The last thing on their minds was worrying about the ladies in the casa delle puttane—brothel. The girls there weren’t human beings to these men who had used and abused them whenever they had enough coin to do so.

			
As the days went by, filled with the backbreaking work of cleaning up a destroyed city, no word came about anyone they knew who had gone missing since the flood hit. 

			
The Barbera crew worked and waited for what seemed like forever for Joe to return. With each day that passed, the men were losing hope that he’d found the missing sisters. If Joe were anybody but steadfast, reliable Joe, they might have thought he had given up and gone home to tell his mama the bad news.

			
But he hadn’t. He had scoured the whole city, he’d ridden up and down the banks of the still-swollen river, praying he wouldn’t see their corpses floating amid the wreckage, dead animals, logs, and burning timber. He hadn’t seen their corpses, no, but then again, he hadn’t seen their living selves either. He asked every man, woman, and child survivor if they’d seen them, but nobody had. It was as though the girls had vanished off the face of the earth, swallowed up whole by the raging waters. Would they never be found? There wasn’t even a sign of little Flo’s wolf, who would probably have run off and instinctively gone to higher ground. Joe just didn’t know whether it would be worse to go home to his mama without the girls or with, God forbid, their little bodies. 

			
On the fifth day, Joe returned to Pueblo alone. The Barbera crew, who was busy knocking down what remained of the post office, saw him riding into town alone. Peter put down his shovel and, in front of all the men, burst into tears. 

			
Joe hung his head in sorrow and shame that he had come back without them. Mariano was relieved. He’d never have to deal with that devil child and her Goddamned wolf again. As for little Clara? He couldn’t have cared less. One less skinny daughter to marry off. 

			
Meanwhile, Maria was losing what she had left of her mind. Her daughters were missing, and now she had no idea where the hell her boys, the ranch hands, or her husband were. 

			
After a few days of cleaning up, Mariano and the crew were allowed to return home. Peter and the ranch hands went first. They had all agreed on a plan. They’d tell Maria that all the children at the school had been sent to another town temporarily until it was safe to return. They’d deal with the truth later—when Maria had figured out for herself that the girls were not going to return. 

			
Joe stayed to search for them, hoping at least to find their bodies. 

			
Mariano? He didn’t return to the ranch either. He went to look for Cora-Lee. He mounted up and went straight to her place. What he found was a shattered shell of the once-popular Miss Cora-Lee’s Tea Parlour. Hers had been the very girls that Flo and Clara had seen screaming for their lives.

			
The back wall of the building was gone, though the staircase still stood, and chandeliers were on the ground in the mud. The furniture and naked-lady paintings had washed away along with the piano and all the bootleg booze. 

			
No sign of Cora-Lee or any of her specially picked, beautiful painted ladies. Crazy as it sounds, Mariano wasn’t as upset that he’d lost one of his thriving businesses or his stash of Barbera bordello bootleg as he was that Cora-Lee was gone. 

			
It never occurred to him that maybe she’d been killed along with the three hundred or more other poor souls who died horrible deaths that day. No sirree. He was beside himself with the unbearable thought that maybe she had just up and left him. 

			
He imagined she’d hightailed it outta there with the booze and set up in another city. How she and her girls would have done that in the middle of the flood and destruction, he didn’t bother to think about. He was just stricken by the belief that she’d left him. Even the massive flood was only about him. 

			
She must be on her back or on her ass, pleasing other men, he fumed to himself. She was no longer his alone. 

			
“Puttana! Puttana!” he yelled. 

			
In his twisted way, he loved her. 

			
He vowed that when he found her, he’d screw her blue…and then he’d kill her. Nobody left him and lived to tell about it. Nobody. 

			
Then Marian the Barbarian sat down on what remained of the bordello staircase and cried. 

			
 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 21 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 21 –

			
Little Girls Lost 

			
Somewhere in Los Animas County 

			
June 1921 

			
Joe continued his search even though he despaired of ever finding his little sisters. He rode for days up and down the Arkansas River and Fountain Creek, pulling little bodies out where he found them, whether they were still floating or putrefying on the riverbanks. He’d bury them with crosses he’d make from sticks. 

			
Each time he found a body that looked like Flo or Clara, he’d be both relieved that he could bring them home and heartsick that he’d have to bring them home dead. 

			
But after a week, there was no sign of the girls, their donkey, or even Flo’s wolf. One morning, he tracked a wolf up on a high ridge. It looked to be a lone wolf, which was a good sign. But there weren’t human footprints, which wasn’t good. When he finally spotted the wolf—and it was a grey alright—it didn’t have those spooky blue eyes. He was relieved because if it had been Blue, it would mean Flo and Clara weren’t with her. He knew it might sound crazy to anybody else that a man could be relieved that his little sisters weren’t with a wolf, but nothing about his family—especially since little, beloved firecracker Flo was born—was what you could call normal. 

			
So, on he rode. On a Tuesday, with light rain falling, Joe spotted the flower dress his mama had made for Flo. He was sure it was hers because Flo wanted to wear it every single darned day. It was hanging, ripped and ruined, on a high branch of a cottonwood tree. Should he take it back to Mama, so she’d have something of her daughter? He climbed up and managed to get the dress—but he couldn’t decide whether he should give it to Maria or not. 

			
But then he got to wondering whether finding the dress was a good sign or a bad one. Was it there because the girls had made it that far and were alive, or did it mean they’d been drowned and carried further along the river, their bodies smashing into the trees—maybe for miles and miles? 

			
They couldn’t have made it. This had to be ten-miles from Pueblo. Children their age couldn’t have made it this far. There were also no tracks from donkey hooves, but then again, it was so muddy that any tracks close to the riverbank would have been washed away. 

			
Joe tried tracking for two more weeks—asking everybody he could find if they’d seen two little girls—one of ’em wearing just her undergarments. He was a strong man, but his heart would be torn when he allowed himself to imagine the fate that may have befallen his baby sisters and how he might have to break his mama’s heart if he never found them. 

			
Finally, one homesteader lady down by the river told him that a bachelor man—a trapper—had a little girl, or maybe it was two little girls, in his shack up on the mountainside. 

			
That was all she knew. 

			
Joe raced up the mountain, his six-gun in his pocket and his long gun on his shoulder, ready in case he had to kill the man. His mind was racing. Is the man a kidnapper, a slaver, a sexual deviant? he wondered. The thought made him retch. Maybe he’s just a decent, church-going man who kindly took them in until their family came to claim them. Probably not.

			
As Joe got close to the bachelor man’s cabin, he heard what sounded like little girls’ voices. They were crying something fierce, and the sound pierced Joe’s heart like an arrow. 

			
The man had heard Joe’s horse approaching and came out with a shotgun. “Get offin’ my land!” he warned. The man’s lone pig in a small enclosure bellowed.

			
“The mountain ain’t nobody’s land,” Joe reminded him in a not-so-friendly way. The filthy man had a still on his property, but it was a small thing, probably just for his own ‘shine. As he was taking stock of the situation, one little girl came outside, wearing nothing at all. Joe’s heart sank, and his blood boiled. He couldn’t see the girl clearly because she scurried away with her back to him. 

			
“Firenze!” Joe yelled, but the child scampered back inside. “Firenze! Clara! È Peppineddu!”—It’s me, Joe.

			
The man turned his shotgun on the house. “One more word, dago shithead, and I’ll kill those girls sure as I’m standing here.” 

			
“I came for my sisters,” he said. “If you try to keep ’em you’ll have to kill me first, and that ain’t happening. Either which way, if you don’t turn ’em over, the whole Carlino Gang will be up on this mountain faster than a herd of scalded cats.” 

			
“Get the fuck offin’ my land,” the man repeated, aiming his rifle right at Joe. 

			
Joe didn’t flinch but just said, “They will find you. They will slowly kill you, cut you into pieces, and feed what remains of your sorry ass to that pig of yours. Now you turn those girls over to me and nobody will be the wiser.” 

			
“Who the fuck is the Carlino Gang? One of them Guinea bootlegger gangs?” He laughed. “I ain’t afeared of no fuckin’ WOPs.” 

			
As the sobs of the girls got louder, Joe tried to keep himself from committing murder. He wasn’t his father, even though he would have liked nothing better than turning this swine into pig feed. 

			
The man aimed his shotgun at Joe and pulled off a shot. Joe ducked, and before that filthy deviant had a chance to get off another one, Joe shot him between the eyes, causing the varmint’s brains to blow out the back of his head.

			
He heard the little girls screaming inside the house. He dismounted and approached the front door. He cracked it open. The shack had no windows, so he couldn’t make out anything in the dark, but two shapes huddled in the corner. 

			
“Firenze, Clara? É Peppineddu,” he said as softly as he could. All he got in response was sobs

			
As his eyes got used to the dark interior, which smelled of piss and shit and some kind of animal or maybe human remains, he made out the backs of two naked girls with their heads buried in each other’s arms. 

			
As he approached, the thump of his boots hitting the wooden floor sounded louder and more threatening than he meant them to be. He reached down to touch them. They screamed and tried to run away, but he put his arms around them and carried them over his shoulders outside. He had to get them away from the filth—even if it was to see the corpse of their torturer. 

			
He put them down and saw their faces for the first time. Wrong age, wrong height, wrong girls. They weren’t his baby sisters. These girls were tiny, and crooked—probably from not eating—but they weren’t all that young. They could have been twelve or thirteen even. Hard to know. 

			
“Papa!” they screamed, looking at the dead man, whose head was half blown off. 

			
“Oh shit!” Joe moaned. He had killed their father. 

			
But there was something wrong with this picture. What kind of man keeps his daughters naked? As Joe tried to get them to stop their screaming, he saw the bruises on their faces, arms, and legs. Then he saw the rope burns on their wrists and ankles. 

			
As they stood shivering and afraid, he took two shirts out of his saddlebags and covered them up. “It’s gonna be OK,” he said. “I’ma take you home to my mama. She’ll take good care of you.” 

			
Then, even though they were cowering and crying and scared out of their heads, Joe managed to lift the two girls onto his horse, and he walked beside them. 

			
How in the hell was he going to explain this to his mama? He didn’t think any further into the future than that. 

			
“What’s your names?” 

			
“Girl,” said one. “Girl,” said the other one. They didn’t even have names. 

			
For two days they rode and walked, stopping to sleep under the stars. On the first night, they shared some salted meat and hardtack that Joe had in his saddle bag. Nobody in the right mind gets excited over dried food, but these girls? They ate like they’d just been served a T-bone steak. 

			
Mama would not be happy to see their terrible manners. Even when they were starving, she’d never let the kids grab whatever food they could find and run away with it. But these poor kids never learned manners because nobody had ever cared enough to teach them…well, anything.

			
After they ate, Joe gave them a trail blanket, and they lay down under a tree and hugged each other to sleep. He heard them whimpering and sobbing, but since he was just a man, he was helpless to know what to do under such unusual circumstances. He thought of Flo and Clara. Were they alone and scared too? Or dead? Had someone shown them kindness?

			
On the second day, Joe killed a rabbit that the girls knew how to skin and make ready for cooking. Joe learned right off that the girls also knew which wild plants were safe for eating such as onion, rice grass, and dandelion, and they knew how to make the wild greens into a soup over a campfire. He hoped his little sisters, wherever they were, remembered how their mama had taught them which plants were safe for eating. 

			
On night two, Joe and the two orphan girls had a real prairie feast: roasted rabbit and plant soup. 

			
He was glad that they had some skills, hoping these small, broken girls with no names might be a help to his mama and that he wasn’t bringing her home more of a burden. Maybe the girls could lend a hand in cooking for the cowpokes in the bunkhouse, which was a big chore three times a day when the boys weren’t out tending or driving herd. 

			
There wasn’t much talking during the next day’s journey either. Maybe just a “watch for the snake,” or “I seen some dandy-lines over there,” until they finally approached the Barbera property, where no words were spoken at all. They passed under the crude arch with the Barbera brand burned into the wood; the girls were all wide-eyed because they had never seen a proper homestead before. 

			
Joe heard the ranch hands screaming first. “It’s Joe! He done brought Flo and Clara home,” Silas Smith screamed. Everybody rushed towards them, Maria tearing out of the front door at top speed. 

			
“Firenze! Chiara! I miei piccoli bambini.”—My little babies!

			
Then, like the ranch hands, she stopped dead in her tracks and went mad, pulling at her hair and screaming like a wild animal. 

			
Somehow it had not occurred to Joe, who was the most level-headed of the whole bunch, that when he came riding home with two girls, folks would think they were Flo and Clara. Instead, he was bringing home a couple of orphans. 

			
Callie, who had come to stay with her inconsolable mother, burst into tears, which set off the rest. Now everybody was crying.

			
Joe took the girls off the horse, hung his head, and said in Sicilian, “Mama. I’m sorry. I’m so sorry, I didn’t find Firenze and Clara. These two girls are orphans.” 

			
The girls, more scared now what with everyone carrying on at once, just sat down on the ground and joined them in wailing. 

			
Angelina and Callie picked the girls up from the ground and took them by the hand to the creek to get them scrubbed up. When they returned, wearing blankets, they took them inside to get them some dresses to wear. Maria went loco. She refused to let anyone wear her lost daughters’ clothes, though they would have fit. These orphans were that small.

			
So, Callie took off her silk petticoats, ripping the bottoms off like they weren’t worth a fortune, and went back outside and put one on each girl. Peter gave them his only two work shirts. 

			
“Mama,” Callie tried to reason, her arms around both girls. “Wouldn’t you want someone to take care of Flo and Clara like this?” 

			
Maria couldn’t be consoled. Callie, not using her head, told her that maybe the two girls were a gift from God. 

			
Maria put her face right up to Callie’s and screamed at her in Sicilian, “What? To replace my babies?” Then she slapped her daughter. 

			
It was a good thing Charlie wasn’t around. He was so smitten with his wife that he wouldn’t cotton to her being slapped around—even by her own mama. 

			
Joe tried to explain to his mother that the girls had been living with a man, maybe their father, who tied them up and did God-knows-what to them. He had rescued them. Surely there was always room for two more at the table. 

			
Walter, never one to keep his mouth shut when he should, said, “But Joe, you said they was orphans.” 

			
Joe answered, “They are now.” 

			
Maria, a kind woman after all, after a while, couldn’t help but take pity on these two wretches. It was what, as Callie had said, she would wish someone had done for her missing babies. So, despite her heartache, she found herself walking the battered girls towards the house. 

			
“Nothin’ that Mama’s Eye-tie pasta cain’t cure,” Walter called out, eyeing the strange little creatures and crying like a girl himself. 

			
A few more days passed, and Maria called Peter and Austin into the house. As they walked up to the house from the corral, Peter said, “I hope she don’t want us to go out lookin’ for Papa.” 

			
“Who’s himself probably out lookin’ for the whore,” Austin answered, as he opened the door and saw the orphan girls all cleaned up and making meatballs. 

			
The girls were little and bent from malnourishment but seemed older than their sizes. They were cleaned up and had some food in their bellies, but their crooked legs and spines made them as sad a sight as you’d want to see. Maria had taken them in, tried her best through her own pain and sorrow to heal them, and had even given them names: Lucia and Antonella. 

			
Maria, fighting back a mountain of tears, wiped her hands on her apron and said as firmly as she could to her family, “It’s time to bury your sisters.” 

			
“But Ma! There ain’t no bodies,” said Austin. 

			
If looks could kill, Maria’s might have killed Austin right on the spot. “I don’t need bodies,” she said, her lips so tight it scared everybody. “Build two coffins. Paint them pink.” Her English was good enough now to at least make her wishes clear in two languages. 

			
Two days later, two pink coffins were put onto the buckboard, and the whole family (well, the whole Barbera family except, of course, for the missing Mariano), the entire Carlino clan, friends, and ranch hands gathered out in the meadow. They all stood around one big, empty grave like Maria had ordered. She wanted Flo and Clara to spend eternity next to each other, even though there was no “each other” in the boxes. 

			
Father Angeletto came out to the graveside riding on his mule like Jesus himself. Joe, Peter, and Mickey played the Ava Maria on their fiddles as the mourners gathered around, weeping and waiting for the priest to begin the funeral ceremony in Latin. 

			
Everyone sobbed harder when Maria opened the coffins and put Blue’s ball and Flo’s tattered flower dress in one coffin and Clara’s rag doll and her favorite dress in the other. She closed the lids and threw herself on top of the closed coffins wailing their names. The sight of that poor lady so overcome with grief made even the toughest cowhands and the usually-calm Carlinos cry like babies as the brothers made ready to lower the coffins on ropes into the ground. The men had to keep Maria from throwing herself into the grave. She had once made an oath to never lose another child and now she had lost two. 

			
Although sadness and grief overcame the group, the Carlinos were also furious as rattlers that Mariano hadn’t had the decency to come home for his own daughters’ funeral—even though he had no way of even knowing a funeral was going on. 

			
“No rispetto!” Charlie whispered to his brothers about Mariano’s absence. “No rispetto!” Then, even lower and in English, God forbid the family would hear, “He must be out chasing that sciaquadell’.” He didn’t add that it was the very same sciaquadell’ who had also made him the happiest man in the whole country. 

			
As they lowered the empty, bright-pink caskets into the ground, Maria began such a loud moaning and rocking that it broke everybody’s hearts. Austin caught her just before she hit the ground in a faint. Callie and Angie let out great sobs, reaching out for their mother. Charlie watched, his heart breaking for his beloved wife. Everyone crowded around Maria trying to do something—anything—for this good woman whose heart was being ripped out in front of them. 

			
Maria was not carrying on in an unusual manner or making what they call a spectacle of herself in front of everyone. What she did and how she was behaving was very normal for any Sicilian mother who couldn’t bear the grief. 

			
Austin, who himself couldn’t bear to see his mama coming undone like that turned away. He knew she loved him best even though he sometimes had fits and visions because he wasn’t right in the head. So, when he had his back to the mourners and was facing away from the grave, he saw something that he just knew couldn’t be there: a grey wolf standing on top of the hill.

– 22 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 22 –

			
A Time to Mourn, A Time to Dance 

			
Barbera Ranch, Colorado 

			
June 25, 1921 

			
Austin was so upset by the vision of the wolf that he fell down and got to shaking, jerking, foaming at the mouth, and staring up into space. He knew he was having one of his visions when he saw the wolf—he’d had them many times before, especially when he was about to have a seizure. He thought the vision of the wolf meant his sisters were watching over their own funeral. It didn’t stop him from beginning to feel the seizure coming on, however. 

			
Helen and Mickey rushed to Austin’s side, lifted his head off the ground, and, like they’d done so many times before, stuck a stick in his mouth to keep him from swallowing his tongue. 

			
Callie got so scared that the Carlinos would think the fits could be passed on to her own children (God willing, she would get pregnant soon) that she nearly fainted herself. 

			
When Austin’s seizure finally passed, he managed to get himself up, and the first thing he did was to look up the hill. The grey was still there. 

			
He started crying and rubbing at his eyes so hard, it looked like he would poke them out. Finally, he opened his eyes again, and the wolf was gone. It had been just another one of his visions. Sometimes his mother, Clara, and Flo, too, had visions, although they weren’t broke in the head like he was. 

			
After the Austin scare, the family got back to praying over the empty coffins with Father Angeletto. Without saying a word, Austin broke away from the group and walked towards the hill alone. He was so ashamed of fitting in front of everyone that he needed to get away to save his mama even more shame than he’d brought on her already. He wondered if she or any of his sisters had wished it was him that had died and not the girls. He had seen Callie’s face when he came out of the fit—he thought she for sure wished it was him in one of those coffins.

			
Callie’s father-in-law, Don Vito, had seen Austin’s seizure out of the corner of his eye. He shook his head slightly and looked at Callie, who blushed. Yes, Don Vito now knew her family’s secret shame. Would he tell his son to do what men do to keep his wife from getting pregnant? 

			
She had to get pregnant—and soon. Having Charlie’s son would ensure he never left her, or, at least he’d always be tied to her. 

			
Was Cora-Lee still in town to give her advice about getting pregnant, she wondered? But Cora-Lee probably only knew the secret to not getting pregnant, instead of the other way around.

			
Don Vito’s wife, Cologera, who had never cottoned much to her niece/daughter-in-law Callie in the first place, did that thing she did with her mouth, making her lips so small they practically disappeared. It wasn’t bad enough that Callie was no looker, but now Cologera also knew the rumors were true: a Barbera boy had the fits to boot. Since Cologera was sister to Maria, she knew the fits were not something that ran in the Ragusa family; that curse had to have come from that nasty Mariano Barbera’s side of the family. 

			
Cologera had been angry since the start that her husband had agreed to marry her beautiful son Carlo (Charlie) to that, skinny fìmmina sfacciata. 

			
The service was rightfully long with everyone heartbroken for the lost little girls and for Maria. When Father Angeletto finally finished the service, the mourners each threw a wildflower on top of the coffins then turned to go back to the homestead for the funeral meal. As they walked, some noticed that Austin was no longer among them. Since he liked to roam the hills talking to the animals after one of his fits, nobody paid his absence any mind. 

			
The group kept walking towards the hill, some still wailing, all wiping their eyes. 

			
When they rounded the top, they beheld a sight that could not be real. Austin was sitting on the grass with one arm around Flo and the other around Clara, with Blue standing behind them. Ghosts! They were seeing the spirits of the dead girls and that infernal wolf from hell. Maria started to run towards them and was stopped by Peter. “Mama! No. Nun sunnu veri!”—They ain’t real. 

			
As strong as Peter had become, Maria broke free of him and raced towards the girls. Some in the funeral group tried without luck to catch up to Maria to stop her from reaching the ghosts, while others stayed back, yelling, and crying. Most got right down on their knees and made the sign of the cross to protect against the malocch’. 

			
Maria got to the little girls and leaned down, sobbing and trying to hug and kiss them. But they wouldn’t let her touch them. If they were alive, they would have welcomed her, wouldn’t they? But they looked so real. 

			
Even though she couldn’t touch them, she still believed they were her living, flesh-and-blood children. Blue, she saw, was all banged up, missing fur and bleeding from many wounds. 

			
The mourners, meantime, got up off their knees and ran like a wolf pack themselves towards the ghosts. They saw Maria on the rise of the hill with her rosary beads in her hands, giving thanks to Jesus and St. Anthony for performing this great miracle. But some, seeing how filthy the girls were—muddy, scratched, cut, battered, bruised, and only wearing bits of underwear—decided that maybe they had never died in the first place, because as every fool knows, when children died, they became clean and shining in Jesus’s arms. 

			
Some of the rest of them thought the girls must still be dead because the living would never act like this, only allowing that hellish wolf and Austin—who they all now knew was off-his-rocker—to touch them. 

			
So, were they alive or spiriti? Father Angeletto decided they had definitely returned from the dead, so he needed to write to Rome immediately to tell of the miracle.

			
Then Yellow Fly said, “Maybe the ‘twins’ really were dead and came back for their own funeral.” His people believed the soul lived on after death, and they were, after all, on Indian land. Or what once was Indian territory. 

			
Charlie Carlino had another thought, which he voiced out loud. “Maybe that dang wolf is like that she-wolf, Lupa, in the Romulus and Remus tale.” The crowd got to whispering and speculating that maybe these “twins,” too, were like the twins in that tale. Back in ancient times, everyone believed those twins had drowned, but they had really escaped death, were saved by a she-wolf, and finally returned to their true home. “Thing is,” one of the Carlino uncles pointed out, “when Romulus and Remus were old enough, the king, who had tried to kill them, was killed himself—probably by them twins.”

			
Nobody would say out loud that maybe Flo and Clara had come back to kill their “king”—the ornery, missing Mariano. But everyone thought it.

			
Maria understood the truth though, and she always had, since the minute the she-wolf entered the shack when Firenze was born. These weren’t ghosts—these were her own flesh-and-blood children. And as she had known somehow on the day Firenze was born, she knew there had to be a proper hand-off, mother-to-mother. Blue had been the girls’ mother through their mysterious journey home. So, Maria hugged the wolf, thanked her, and gently examined her terrible wounds. Flo pointed to the fur on Blue’s back. It looked like a recent bullet wound, and it was a bad one.

			
“Pi favuri, Mama! Fix Blue!” Flo cried and allowed her mama to touch her face and even her lips, which weren’t very bright anymore. The crowd roared. Half of them with fear, half of them with joy. 

			
Maria declared that the living girls had indeed returned home. They were not spiriti. Anyone who didn’t believe it should leave and never speak to her again. Nobody knew what the hell to do, so they all stood there like posts. 

			
Then Maria ripped her black dress right off right in front of everyone and ordered the men to use it to lift Blue. 

			
When the girls were convinced Blue was being cared for—and, for that matter that they were really home—they allowed their mama to hug them, which set the mourners all to wailing again. What started out as tears of sorrow for the dead became tears of joy for the living. 

			
Everyone wanted to hug the girls, but they wouldn’t let anyone other than Maria and Austin touch them, and they wouldn’t be separated from Blue. So, the wounded wolf was carefully lifted onto the wagon along with the girls—the same wagon that had carried their pink coffins—and they headed towards home. 

			
At the gravesite, Peter, Joe, and Mickey, all dressed alike in men’s Sunday suits, picked up their fiddles and broke out playing “Buffalo Gals,” and the mourners started dancing with joy. Had a stranger passed by and seen the fresh-dug graves and dancing, he’d have thought for sure the crowd was possessed by the devil. 

			
Once in the wagon and safely on their way home, Flo squinted her eyes, scary-like, and said loud enough for everyone to hear, “Dov’è papà?”—Where’s Papa? No one had an answer, but it was more than likely most of them secretly prayed he was dead.

			
 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 23 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 23 –

			
Don’t Read All About It

			
Somewhere Near Pueblo, Colorado

			
1921 

			
They had all managed to get along without Papa just fine now that the boys were grown. As the days wore on, Flo’s lips regained their unnaturally bright ruby color and both she and Clara, under Maria’s steady hand, began to heal and regain their health. And so did the orphans, Antonella and Lucia. But the wounds to the minds of those four girls would last forever.

			
Austin had removed the bullet from Blue’s back the first day, and Flo applied moldy bread to it every day after, wrapping it with a big piece of muslin to keep it in place. Even though her wounds had been very bad, she also began to heal. Peter liked to say that the wolf would have been safer defending herself in the wild than watching out for his wild little sisters. Probably true too. 

			
After a few weeks, Clara really wanted to talk to her mama about how she saw her beloved Bella get swept up in the mudslide and disappear. But when she tried to talk, Flo shushed her. It was not to be spoken of, not yet. Flo needed to keep it all inside as though she was ashamed of what had happened to them. Clara remained heartbroken and would mourn that donkey until the day she died. 

			
Right then, though, the most important thing on Flo’s mind was to nurse Blue back to full health. She insisted a mattress of straw be sewn for the big wolf so she could be comfortable and sleep next to Flo as her wounds healed. 

			
It was more than a month before the girls slowly began to share the story with their mama, bit by bit, of how they had survived. They told her of how Blue had taken them to higher ground just before the flood. How somehow with God’s blessing, they sat down to rest on a big rock and, unlike Bella, had not been in the direct path of the mudslide. They told of other flood survivors they’d met on the way who were searching for kin. Those who had it to spare, gave them what scraps they could to eat. And how some lowdown varmint had tried to steal them one night where they were sleeping on the prairie, but Blue had attacked the man, and he shot at them as they escaped. 

			
Could he have been the father of Antonella and Lucia?

			
Weeks passed, and one day, a reporter from the Pueblo Times by the name of Robert Roberts Jr. showed up. He’d heard the impossible tale of the brave little girls whose pet wolf had taken them to higher ground to escape the flood. He wanted to be the first reporter to get the scoop. What a story! Talk about your heartwarming, human-interest article. It was just the kind of tale the beaten-down folks of Pueblo needed. 

			
So, Robert Roberts Jr. drove to the ranch in his Model T, took his camera out to take their picture, and planned to write it up from the girls’ own lips. 

			
Maria heard his noisy car pull up and stood peering out from behind the screen door. 

			
Roberts Jr. got out of his car, tipped his hat, and said, “Good day to you ma’am. I’m here to talk to your daughters about their incredible story of survival. But you can’t be their mama because you look so young!” 

			
Of course, Maria had no idea what bull this man was slinging, but she could tell a smooth talker in any language. Flo, who had followed her to the door, translated what the man had said for her. Maria had already sized him up for a no-good, but when she found out he was a reporter, she knew for sure he was. Besides which, if the girls photographs, God forbid, showed up in a newspaper, it would bring nosy strangers—not to mention the law—to their home. She swung open the screen door with the force of a hurricane wind.

			
“Ehi, signuri! Vatinni fora cu tutta a machina fotografica!”—Get out of here with your camera.

			
“Ma says you gotta leave,” said Clara, who was never more than a foot away from Flo. 

			
But the man ignored them and kept setting up his camera on the front porch. After all, who listened to little girls or a woman, let alone a WOP? He just grinned his big, toothy smile as though he was an actor in one of those moving pictures. 

			
“Aww come on girls! Move a little to the right,” he ordered, moving them into position for a good shot. “You’ll be famous like Annie Oakley and Calamity Jane. And where’s that wolf of yours?” 

			
He laughed under his breath, figuring the little mother would scare easier than a rabbit. He had an assignment, and he wasn’t about to leave until he got the story and the pictures to go along with it. He told the girls to tell their mama he was going to make them famous, which was exactly the wrong thing to say. 

			
Once again, Maria asked him to leave, and once again, he ignored her and kept talking up the girls. Maria finally threw her hands up and went back inside, wiping her hands on her apron. 

			
“Well, that was easy,” the reporter chuckled, then said under his breath (which they heard anyway), “That one’s so timid she wouldn’t bite a piece of bread.” 

			
The girls laughed. How dumb could one grown man be? 

			
The front door opened—as they knew it would—and out came Maria with a long gun—as they knew she would. 

			
“No u capisti? Italiano? Oh-kay Doookey,” Maria said, aiming the rifle between his eyes. “I tell Englay-say at you. Get offa my ’ouse or I shoot you dead.” 

			
Flo shook her head and said, “Mister? I’d listen to her if I was you. Mama’s got pretty good aim.” Maria stepped closer to him, shoving the girls behind her and kicking over Robert Roberts Jr.’s camera stand. 

			
“Va’ e fatti’na passiata!” which sort of means “Take a walk.” 

			
You didn’t need to understand Italian to understand a rifle at close range aimed your way. That reporter grabbed his equipment and backed right the hell off the porch without taking his eyes off Maria or the rifle while trying not to trip and fall. 

			
Still, he yelled, “I’ll be back! You ain’t seen the last of me!” as he turned around and ran to his Model T. He was loading his camera into the back of his open-top sedan when he felt a tug on his pant leg. He hadn’t heard anything, so he looked down. “Oh shit!” A giant grey wolf was tearing at his brand-new brown-striped pants. Good thing he was wearing suspenders to hold them up, or he would have been, as the poker players like to say, “All mouth and no trousers.” 

			
Austin showed up behind him out of nowhere. “Blue! Stop!” he yelled. The wolf let go of Roberts Jr.’s pant leg and snarled. Roberts Jr. said, “Thanks, Mister,” surely thinking there was at least one sane person in the group. 

			
Austin smiled and made what the reporter wrongly took as an apologetic shoulder shrug. Roberts Jr. nodded his head and winked. 

			
Austin was confused, and said, “What? Since you don’t capisti Italiano, Mister, let me explain. If Mama says no newspaper story, it’s no newspaper story.” 

			
Now it was Roberts Jr.’s turn to look, if not confused, then exasperated, rolling his eyes like he was trying to communicate with a bunch of monkeys. 

			
Austin gave him the bad eye. “Capisti, pardner?” Then he shoved Roberts Jr. with all his might backwards over the car’s open top. The reporter fell in with his feet sticking up. Austin leaned in and smiled, proud to show off his new gold front tooth. “Nice to make your acquaintance, Robert Roberts Junior. That’s what they call you, right?” he said, smacking the side of the car. “Safe travels, Cumpare.” 

			
Roberts Jr. righted himself, got behind the wheel, and began pulling out, leaving a trail of dust in his wake and all over Maria. Having just been humiliated by a man who didn’t look like he had all his marbles, he called out, “I’ll be back! Don’t you worry about that!” 

			
Maria understood part of what he’d threatened and threw a stone at the car, aiming for his head, and since she had an arm like a real Dead Eye Dick, she beaned that reporter good. 

			
“Butta gazz!” she yelled. It was her way of saying “Goodbye.” 

			
But “goodbye” was not in his dictionary. “I’ll be back” sure was though. 

			
The ranch hands who’d heard the kerfuffle had run out of the corral ready for a fight, but they instead stood back and let Austin have his minute. By the time the car finally pulled away, the hands were applauding Austin like he’d just won the bronc-riding contest at the rodeo. They clapped him on the back, saying things like “Good job, kid!” and “That was tellin’ him!” None of the men had ever said kind things to him before, so he blushed and said the most American thing he could think of: “Aw shucks, fellers.”

			
Walter, never one to miss a chance to let loose with one of the crazy sayings he’d learned on cattle drives, shook Austin’s hand, and declared, “Iffin that feller’s brains was dynamite, he wouldn’t have enough to blow his nose.” Nobody knew what the hell he was talking about, but they all laughed anyway.

			
So, no, the girls’ story never made it to the papers, even though they sure did like the notion of being like Annie Oakley and Calamity Jane, so they took up the roles for themselves. Black Bill even carved toy guns for them out of wood so they could pretend to be those rough-and-tumble cowgals. 

			
Even though she hoped she’d seen the end of it, the tale of Senora Barbera’s actions that day caught on like wildfire across a dry prairie. Maybe it was spread by the leaky-mouthed ranch hands, or maybe it was spread by Robert Roberts Jr. himself. 

			
Either way, people talking was exactly what she did not want. The less people knew about her family, the stills that were their lifeblood, and her husband’s whereabouts, the better. Not that she had any idea of her husband’s whereabouts anyway, but still. 

			
And the story about Blue? The rumors after the incident made the beast sound like one of those werewolves in the novels all the cowpokes read—those who could read anyway. If guns and crazy “Eyeties” weren’t enough to keep strangers away, talk of werewolves surely would be. Or so Maria thought. 

			
And more importantly, whether the townsfolk knew it or not, the Barberas also had the protection of the bigger “family” now—the Carlinos.

			
Even though Maria felt safer as part of the Carlino organization (especially with hair-trigger Mariano gone), she was still afraid that the law would find their stills, or that her sons would be ambushed by the rival La Dona family, shot by a rogue bootlegger, cattle rustler, or arrested by the prohibition agents who loved nothing better than finding and killing Italian bootleggers. The West was never as wild as it became after Colorado went dry. Kansas, North Dakota, and Iowa had been dry since the 1880s, but they weren’t full of indentured Italians who knew how to brew and how to stew. 

			
Maria liked to comfort herself with the thought that bandits had roamed the hills of Sicily too. Wasn’t everybody always scared of being killed, squeezed by the Black Hand, or robbed by the outlaws in the old country? Wasn’t the law itself to be feared everywhere?

– 24 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 24 –

			
Return of the Range Rover 

			
Barbera Ranch, Colorado 

			
September 1921 

			
The marriage of Callie and Charlie and the business arrangement it brought had solidified the pact with the Carlinos, sure, but despite warnings from Don Vito, the war between the La Donas, the Barberas, and the Carlinos kept heating up. It put them all in as much danger as any raid by the feds. Maria worried every day until her boys came home. Well, her boys and Mickey, who rode the range and also rode shotgun on the trucks carrying the booze when necessary. The young woman had her own Tommy gun and wasn’t afraid to use it. 

			
They all knew Mariano, wherever he was, would have to be blind or dead not to see that wars were raging and threatening everything he’d built. 

			
One day, five months after the flood, Mariano came riding back to the ranch like nothing had happened. He had lost Cora-Lee, but he’d be Goddamned if he was going to lose his property. He imagined the whole dang place had gone to rack-and-ruin without him. It hadn’t.

			
Instead, he found that, during the time he had gone missing, Joe and Mickey had taken charge of the cattle, and Peter was managing the ranch hands. Maria? She was running the bootleg in partnership with the Carlino men. Damn. In fact, she was overseeing every single thing. 

			
It never occurred to him that the Carlinos would think that Zia Maria was a whole helluva lot more capable than Ziu Mariano. When left to her own devices, Maria had kept a steady hand and watched every penny. Truly, they all just wished the bastard had stayed gone. 

			
“Your mama’s got more guts than you can hang on a fence post,” Charlie told his wife. If it was possible for Callie to love Charlie more than she already did, that remark would have done it. Not only was he complimenting her mother, but for a man to believe a woman could run things, well, “You’re the bee’s knees, Charlie,” she told him, using the jazzy American expression he had taught her. He then said—and she wasn’t to tell anyone—that his father had told him he preferred dealing with Maria in all matters of business. 

			
In fact, the whole damn lot of them were doing just fine without Marian the Barbarian, a nickname that Don Vito had always found disgusting and offensive. But Mariano had left his family and his responsibilities to go looking, as everyone suspected, for a puttana. Maybe the name did fit him after all. 

			
Back before he went missing, Vito would tell him, “Mariano, we are men of honor—civilized men, not barbarians.” 

			
But Mariano didn’t know the difference anymore. He had killed those Baldwin-Felts detectives out of honor. He had earned honor the hard way—on the field of burned women and children. 

			
Even though they all knew that was true, Maria also knew it was the day he’d lost his soul forever. 

			
Mariano had expected that, upon his return, he’d be greeted like a long-gone king arriving home after a battle to restore order to his kingdom. Instead, he found that the ranch, the cattle, the bootleg, the distribution, and the deal with the Carlino family were running smooth as a baby’s behind. The kingdom was just fine.

			
Nobody needed him, and the Carlinos did not even come to pay respects when he returned. Mariano was in a rage. 

			
Not that it took much to get him riled up to begin with. One night, he’d got to drinking his own bootleg and grabbed Maria, shoving her down on the bed, ready to take what was rightfully his. 

			
“Mariano,” she cried, managing to squeeze out some tears. “Va’ e fatti’na passiata! I have the cancer. Down below!” He was so shocked that he stopped attempting to rape her. She got up, told him it was “catchy” and turned her back to him. He thought it was out of shame; it was actually to keep him from seeing her laughing. 

			
“Chi voi diri?”—What do you mean? Then, “If you die, who will do the bookkeeping and make my food?” 

			
The more he went on about his own needs, in spite of believing she had cancer and would suffer and die, the more of a kick she got out of pushing the lie. 

			
“Non è il tipo di cancro di cui muori, marcisce solo il pene dell’uomo.”—No, she didn’t think she had the kind of cancer that would kill her, but it would rot his penis if they had marital relations…unless he already had it. Then she said that, of course, she’d still perform her marital duties with him because he was her husband, after all. 

			
The whole thing made Mariano crazy as an unwound horse. Not only was her disease depriving him of sex, but he also then got to wondering who she had gotten the cancer from. So, he did what he always did: He shook her, but then stopped a second later, afraid that even touching her could give him the cancer. 

			
Towering over her, he demanded to know who she had been loving up while he was off heroically trying to find—

			
“Who, dear?” she cut in. “Cui?”

			
He stopped there, and after a lot of hemming and hawing, came up with: “Quannu ciccava i nostri figghi Firenze e Clara.”—When I was looking for Flo and Clara. 

			
Maria had to keep from smacking him, because that lie was as clear as the nose on his face. The fact that she was ready to smack him in the face even surprised her.

			
Mariano knew she knew he was lying, so instead of trying to sell it, he changed course, puffed up his chest, and went back to threatening—the skill he was best at. 

			
“Prima ammazzu a tia, e poi ammazzu a iddu!”—First, I’ll kill you. Then I’ll kill him! 

			
“U facisti,” she said, wiping a fake tear from her eye. She went on in Italian: “The doctor said I got it from picking up and washing your underwear after you had sex with your prostitute in town.” 

			
Mariano immediately grabbed his man part as though it was about to fall off, checking it the way men check their hair when they know they’re going bald. Did it have the cancer too? “Am I going to die from rotted cugghiuna? Mincha!”

			
Maria just shrugged and let him stew, pretending to look sad for him. 

			
Then Mariano remembered Cora-Lee. Maybe she didn’t leave him and run away after all. Maybe she caught the down-belows cancer, and it had killed her. What a relief. 

			
But wait. Fangul! No. Cora-Lee could never have given him the dirty disease. She was clean. She took baths every week. What a cruel fate Maria’s God had cursed him with. 

			
Joe and Peter heard the argument behind the bedroom door. They knew what it was about, although they couldn’t hear the specifics. They loved their mama but were stuck. “Isn’t it a man’s right to take what is his when he wants to?” Peter asked without expecting an answer.

			
“Not if the woman is our mama,” Joe answered. “She has her own way of doing things.”

			
“Truer words were never said, brother,” Peter laughed. “I’ll bet she don’t even have no cancer.”

			
“That’s a sure bet, definitely a sure bet,” Joe said, and they walked away from the door knowing their mother was safe, not just from cancer but also from her poisonous husband.

– 25 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 25 –

			
The Donna-Uomo in the Rancho Uomo

			
Barbera Ranch, Colorado 

			
The Following Day

			
Mickey, who’d taken to sleeping in the bunkhouse as one of the boys, had heard only Peter’s first remark about a man’s right, so she punched him in the gut. He gave her a shiner; she gave him a bigger one, and that’s how he got that split eyebrow. 

			
Right after the dust-up, they got up off the floor and tended to each other’s wounds.

			
Peter had grown up and had become a very handsome young man—he looked like that movie star, Clark Gable, whose picture his sisters had cut out of The Pueblo Times. The scar didn’t do anything but make him look more rugged. As for Mickey, it didn’t matter how tightly she wound that cloth around her bosom to make herself flat as a boy or how much she dressed in men’s clothes. It didn’t matter either that she wore a derby or sombrero hat or hid her hair under them. Nope. No matter what she did to ugly herself up, she was still a looker. 

			
The ranch hands knew to stay away from her. They weren’t afraid her brothers would shoot their eyes out—they were afraid Mickey would. 

			
Mickey did not want to, but she had no choice but to move back into the house. Her mother had told her that her father would kill her if he knew she was sleeping in the bunkhouse. He didn’t scare her any longer, but there was no sense in turning up the heat under an already simmering pot.

			
Mariano was so afraid of cugghiuna cancer that he often just popped into the bunkhouse to sleep or take his meals. The last thing anybody wanted was for him to find out Mickey was sleeping there, not with men, but as a man. 

			
Maria knew—they all knew—that Mariano would have to believe he had taken back control of the ranch, or he’d go on a bender and ruin everything they’d built in his absence. 

			
Respect was paid to him, and they all acted like he was boss, but they went about keeping the crops, the livestock, and the bootlegging humming along same as it had without him. 

			
With not much to do at the ranch, plus with Mrs. Bianchi’s gone, and Maria having the cancer, Mariano got to cogitating about how he needed sex. There were no decent whores around since the flood, and those difurmatu—the orphans—Maria had taken in weren’t up to his standards. They were old enough for sex—fourteen or so—but they disgusted him, what with their knock-kneed, rickets legs. 

			
So, one night, Mariano walked into the bedroom that Mickey shared with her little sisters. He’d teach Mickey what a real man was like. She woke to see him trying to climb on top of her. She grabbed her gun and fired a warning shot into the ceiling that just missed Mariano’s head. 

			
“Puttana del cazzo!” he screamed, trying to wrestle the gun away from her.

			
He was drunk and she was strong, so she shoved it right against his temple and cocked the hammer. “Mi? Si jò sugnu puttana, tu purpu sì.”—If I’m a whore, then you are homosexual. She was letting him know that, for all intents and purposes, she was a man. 

			
“Get out!” she spat. “And never come near me again!” 

			
She kept the gun aimed at his head as he jumped off her. The other girls sat up in their beds, shaking with fear, the covers pulled up to their eyes.

			
When Mariano turned to leave, Maria was standing in the doorway, her own long gun locked and loaded. He mumbled something about how Mickey had tried to seduce him or some dumb lie. With her rifle at his back, she walked him out to the barn to sleep it off. Before she left, she said, calm as can be, in her deep Sicilian: “You touch one of my daughters again and I won’t be merciful.”

			
Then Maria closed the padlock on the outside of the barn door. The final humiliation. After that, Mariano couldn’t say anything about it, but it simmered in his brain.

			
Mickey moved back to the bunkhouse the next day, and the ranch hands were as happy as a bunch of hogs in mud. Mickey was better than most of them at roping, riding, and wrestling, although if her papa knew she was wrestling with any of the bunkhouse boys, he would have shot them deader than Old Henry Clay.

			
She also was the best poker player of any woman they’d ever seen. Hell, she was better than any man they’d ever seen—even if she’d already drunk them under the table. 

			
Maria didn’t care whether Mickey liked boys, girls, or frogs, as long as she didn’t do the worst thing possible—move away from home like Angelina had done when she married that city slicker, fat Arthur Mauro. Panzut! 

			
Thing is, Mickey didn’t know what the hell to do about the feelings she had. She was most comfortable with the bunkhouse boys and could spend every day and every night riding the range, roping steer, and sleeping under the stars next to Black Bill and Yellow Fly. But she didn’t have any yearnings for them in that way. They didn’t seem to have any feelings in that way for her either.

			
Problem was, she was so good looking that every time a wedding, baptism, or confirmation came up among the families and she had to put on a lace dress and wear ribbons in her hair, the boys in the county would go crazy and come around to try to see her.

			
Even though she never paid them any mind, she was about to become the living proof of the term, “fatal beauty” anyway.

			
It all began when Billy La Dona saw her in church one Sunday. She was miserable wearing a dress for a christening after mass. Her scowling face didn’t hide the fact that she looked like a beauty queen. The fact that Billy was from the hated enemy La Dona family didn’t stop him from falling straight away in love with her. 

			
He began staring at her across the pews, thinking Romeo and Juliet. She felt his stare and glared back, thinking, You’re barking up the wrong tree, feller.

			
Risking hell from his own father for daring to set foot on enemy land, a few days later, Billy rode all the way to the Barbera ranch carrying presents for her: a mirror wrapped in paper and tied up with string and a bag of coffee. These showed his serious intent. 

			
Cranky Helen happened to be outside and thought he might be there for her and actually graced him with one of her very rare smiles. But Billy looked right past her and said, “Pardon, ma’am,” which stopped her cold. 

			
“Ma’am?” she growled, right back to her old self, “You’re older than me. Gidrul! What do you want?” He explained he was there to call on Mickey and asked if she was at home.

			
She laughed in his face, sneering. “Just because a chicken has wings don’t mean it can fly, mister.” 

			
He looked confused. “You mean another feller’s courtin’ her?” 

			
“Yeah, that’s what I mean,” snickered Helen. 

			
Billy, who was the deadliest if not the brightest of the La Donas—which is why the Barberas all called him Billy Imbecillé—had no idea what she meant. But not wanting to waste that long ride, the fancy mirror, or coffee, he offered them to Helen instead, asking, “Are you bein’ courted too?” 

			
 

			
Helen took the packages and said, “No.”

			
He got excited until she said, “I’d rather eat my own feet than let you court me. Now ride on outta here.” 

			
As he rode out, he spotted Peter on his horse. Peter’s first instinct was to draw his gun. Billy’s first instinct was to get the hell out of there as fast as he could. 

			
Highly insulted, humiliated, and shamed to his core by “that Helen girl” and then by Peter, the whole thing added fuel to the fire of the feud between the bootlegging families. Not that Billy could tell his family he had tried to court a Barbera gal. They’d have shot him themselves. Instead, he held it in and planned his revenge—his vendetta—in secret. One that would destroy everything.

			
When Helen told Mickey what happened, they laughed like they were about to die. Mickey took the coffee; Helen kept the mirror.

			
Mickey didn’t like boys, and she didn’t like boys to like her, but boys just didn’t get it. So, she took a pair of shears and cut her beautiful, long, dark-brown hair to just above her ears. Maria slapped her own head when she saw it and told her to wear a hat when she was around her father, or he’d whip her for sure. 

			
“Let ’im try,” Mickey said, harkening back to when he had last pulled a gun on him. 

			
Thing is, though, Mickey being Mickey was becoming a problem. It was not uncommon for men to “be” with other men because there were over a hundred men to every woman in Colorado. But for women to be with women? That wasn’t so common, so it was not as accepted. 

			
Most men couldn’t understand why a lady would choose another lady when there were so many fellas around to choose from. 

			
For Mariano in particular, who had seen plenty of that sort of thing down at Mrs. Bianchi’s among her fancy ladies, the idea that one of his daughters could behave like that was driving him mad. He even managed to rationalize it in his head that her “proclivity” was the real reason he had tried to climb on her. He was just trying to “fix” her. 

			
He would not have a man for a daughter. He would not. He’d marry her off, that’s what he’d do. But who’d take her? Then he got an idea.

			
 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com
