– 26 –

			
One-Eye Walnut Jack

			
Barbera Ranch, Colorado 

			
October 1921

			
You didn’t have to be smart to see things weren’t going Mariano’s way. It was all the fault of that puttana, that Cora-Lee, he’d think, and then, in the blink of an eye, he’d change his mind and practically elevate her to sainthood. 

			
Whatever she was, he understood that if he hadn’t been so hooked on her, he wouldn’t have taken all that time to go looking for her. She caused him to lose respect not only in his own family but among the Mafia families too. Cora-Lee had ruined everything. He loved her madly and hated her passionately. 

			
Before the flood, he had always thought of her as just a whore who wasn’t important to him—except for business and sex.

			
He’d even joke to his poker pals at Miss Cora-Lee’s Tea Parlour, “Once you’ve eaten rice, you get up from the table right away!” In other words, once he had his fill of her in bed, he’d not think about her again. How wrong he was. 

			
Worse, while he’d been gone searching like a wild man for the woman he thought was so forgettable, his wife had become the boss of both the ranch and the bootlegging operation. She had even earned the respect of Don Vito himself.

			
Worse, Maria had allowed his daughter, eighteen-year-old Michelina, to turn into a masculu-fimmina—man-woman. Everything had gone crazy without his heavy hand. He demanded that Mickey stop dressing like a man, but even she—his own daughter—would brush him aside and tell him it was just part of her job.

			
“If you want the bootlegging and cattle business to run smooth, I have to look the part,” she’d say and ride off to meet the other ranch hands.

			
Rispondere in malo modo—disrespectful answers—he thought. Always disrespectful answers. As he’d heard the cowboys say, it was time to take the reins.

			
“Michelina Mascula!” he called out one morning, using the worst word he could think of, to summon his daughter into the barn where she was ordering the cowboys to either get saddled up or hop in the car to get out to the stills. 

			
While Mariano waited for Mickey to come, he took what you’d call a survey of the cowpokes going about their business. He was there to pick a husband for Mickey. Not like he could try to marry off another one of his daughters to a Carlino. His sister-in-law, Cologera, hated that handsome Charlie had been made to marry stick-figure Callie. But at least Callie was female.

			
He knew that most of the ranch hands would bolt before getting tied to one woman for life. He thought maybe Black Bill would agree to it if he offered him a patch of land, but then he remembered that he’d heard talk that Bill had a wife somewhere—just like Maria’s father had once heard about him. Besides, Bill was too valuable a ranch hand to risk aggravating. The very thought of marrying Mickey might well drive him off for good. That was enough to scratch that cowpoke off the list of potential husbands.

			
Then there was Giovanni Arcuri, Filippo’s nephew, who called himself Jack in the American way. He might make a good match. Filippo owed Mariano for taking his dumb lug relative on. Who else would put up with Jack? Mariano knew Jack didn’t have a wife, so that was good. Mickey was a pain in the ass, and Jack was a big, ugly guy who didn’t much get along with anybody. He was always starting fights in the bunkhouse. It was a perfect solution.

			
Jack was strong as an ox and not afraid of nothing and nobody. Hell, Mariano thought, he was just the right man to make a woman out of his manly daughter. Plus, he’d get Jack and Mickey out of the bunkhouse by building them a cabin on the property. Jack wouldn’t be able to start fights with the other ranch hands every night, whom he always accused of cheating at cards, and Mickey would stop embarrassing him in front of the other families. She’d stop being manly. All she needed, he knew, was to stop riding the range and start riding a cowboy. 

			
Or as he heard his son Joe say to the unshucked cowpokes, “Hey kid—time to get your cannoli in a holey.”

			
He yelled for the men in the barn to hurry up and get the hell out. “Arcuri—you stay.” So, Jack hung around, figuring Mariano was either gonna give him the boot or make him do something he didn’t want to do. 

			
It turned out to be both.

			
Mariano told him straight out that he had to marry Mickey or go work for the La Donas, which was not really a choice because they’d kill him if he ever showed his face at their place.

			
Not being the most subtle of men, Jack took his hat off, scratched his head, and answered that while he sure would like to get married, Mickey wasn’t the marrying kind. Then added, “If you know what I mean.”

			
Mariano grabbed him by the collar and said, “No, I don’t know what you mean.”

			
Jack laughed. “Well sir, then you are the only one in this whole damned ranch who don’t know what I mean.” 

			
They were scuffling with one another when Mickey walked in. “What the hell is goin’ on here?” she wanted to know.

			
Jack picked up his hat off the ground, rolled it around, and answered, “Your father wants us to get hitched, but I tol’ him you ain’t the marrying kind—and you know what I mean.”

			
“You are right about that. I am not the marrying kind, and iffin’ I was, it sure wouldn’t be with you!”

			
Mariano knew it wouldn’t be easy to get these two hitched, but the way he figured it, they had no choice. He’d throw them both out, and they’d never survive the winter.

			
Mickey yelled and stomped her feet. Jack said he’d rather marry a rattler. And so it was settled. They’d get married. 

			
Mickey ran like a madwoman to her mama who was picking vegetables in the garden. “I will never marry Jack or any man!” she declared. 

			
Maria took her by the shoulders and said, “You have no choice. You can’t stay unmarried. I was already married three years with babies at your age. But you can do what you want when no one’s looking. Understand?”

			
“No!” Mickey said. “I’ll never let that beast mount me. Ever!” Maria licked her fingers, wiped the dirt off Mickey’s face and said, “Michelina, amuri meu. You will do your wifely duties and then do what you want when he’s away. Capisti?”

			
“I won’t marry him. I won’t!” 

			
Maria just shook her head. The girl would have to learn that she couldn’t always have her way. Yes, she allowed her to dress and act like a man, but she would not allow her to stay unmarried. Maria handed Mickey a burlap sack of vegetables. 

			
Mickey followed her inside and stomped her mud-caked work boots on the wooden floor. Maria took her by the shoulders. “Me’ figghia, Giovanni is not the smartest man on the ranch and, well,” she covered her mouth to keep from showing her smile, “you are.” 

			
For the first time, Mickey knew for sure that her mother knew who she really was. And she didn’t judge her for it. She only wanted to help her get through it. 

			
So, the women of the ranch began to make wedding preparations while Mickey kept to her duties as a ranch hand manager. Everybody thought she’d accepted her fate like the good girl she probably was under all that hell raising she did. Then, a few days before the wedding, Mickey proved them wrong and shaved off her hair in a fit of fury. 

			
“Dang,” said Walter when her hat fell off while she was roping a steer. “Did you take your head for a shave at the barber shop?” 

			
Mickey, who loved all the hands, thought that was about the funniest thing she’d ever heard and punched him in the arm for good measure.

			
On the big day, when her mother tried to put the veil on her head to cover the stubble, she had no hair to pin it on. Mickey said, “I told you I wouldn’t wear it.” And then to put a fine point on it, added, “I ain’t some virgin goin’ to slaughter in that thing,” and tossed it on the ground. 

			
Maria, trying to lighten the terrible situation joked, “So you are not a virgin?” Mickey laughed but still refused to put it on.

			
Angelina, who’d come back from New York for the wedding and was so tall she towered over all the others, came up with an idea.

			
She told Callie to fetch the bright, white cowboy hat that her husband Charlie had donned for the big doings. Charlie wasn’t eager to part with it, so Angelina had to step in and beg him.

			
Mickey allowed her sister to put the ten-gallon on her head, but it sunk below her eyes, so Angelina had to stuff it with newspaper to hold it up. She then tucked the top of the veil under the hat so that the rest trailed down her back. 

			
Somehow, they got her to the church, where Mickey refused to take her father’s arm as she stomped down the aisle to the altar where her not-beloved was waiting. Jack had cleaned up pretty good, she thought, but she wouldn’t have cared if he’d bathed in a tub of lavender soap. 

			
Father Angeletto, who had seen pretty much everything in that Wild West town, was so disgusted when he saw the bride in a man’s cowboy hat that he made a face.

			
A couple of folks in the pews started snickering, but Mickey turned around and looked at every single person, giving each one the bad eye. They stopped laughing when she took the hat right off and handed it to her father. The veil slipped off her stubble and floated down to the altar like a dead spirit at Mickey’s feet. Mickey tore off the paper ring that was supposed to hold the hat in place. The folks in the church nearly fell off their pews at the sight of the bald bride.

			
And Jack? He was a man in shock. What the hell? His bride was bald as Old Chance the stagecoach driver. Balder. At least Old Chance had sideburns. The church grew quiet as a church mouse—and even the church mouse was probably struck dumb at the sight.

			
When it came time for the priest to ask if she’d take this man in holy matrimony she said, “Hell no!” and everybody started laughing. 

			
Mariano, who was standing at the altar with them, answered for her. “She do!” he said. 

			
Jack wasn’t as brave and said, “I do too.”

			
Father Angeletto wasn’t sure they were really married, but considering half the folks were carrying pistòli, he pronounced them man and wife anyway.

			
“You may kiss the bride,” he uttered. 

			
Mickey pushed Jack’s face away and said, “No you may not, mister.”

			
Before the bride had a chance to stomp back ahead of her groom on her way out, Charlie scrambled up to the altar to pick up his white hat so it wouldn’t get stepped on.

			
The barn had been cleaned out, and paper streamers were hanging, and there were wildflower bouquets in jars on every table. The Carlinos and friends and relatives from town and beyond were invited with food spread out over many tables. The wine and booze flowed like…well, like it was legal. 

			
Peter and some of the boys, whom they’d finally taught to play Sicilian tunes, provided music—from the tarantella to the waltz—and an Italian version of a square dance. Mickey sat on a bench and sulked. Jack, who was no dancer, got to dancing up a storm, excited by the prospect of the honeymoon night ahead. 

			
“Your husband’s dancin’ like there’s no tomorrow,” said Flo, who was done up in ribbons and lace. 

			
“If he keeps it up,” Mickey answered, “that dumb ass critter will have no tomorrow because I’ll be obliged to kill him.” 

			
Little Flo laughed, which made her sidekick Clara laugh, but little as they were, they knew their sister meant it. “He ain’t the best dancer in the world,” Clara added.

			
“He ain’t good at nothing,” Mickey added, shooing the girls away.

			
Antonella saw how miserable Mickey looked and went over and sat beside her on the bench to try to comfort her. 

			
When the band struck up a dreamy waltz, Mickey turned to her and asked, “Wanna dance?” 

			
Antonella blinked and said, “We shouldn’t do that.” Mickey looked annoyed and then tried to take her hand. Antonella gently pulled it away. “I’ll get you some food,” she suggested. 

			
“Get me some booze,” Mickey answered. 

			
When it came time to leave, the newly-hitched couple headed outside, Mickey’s bald head shining in the moonlight. The Model T had been decorated with old cans hanging off the back bumper like these two were a normal, happy pair.

			
When Jack tried to help Mickey get into the Model T, she looked at him in confusion. “Do you think I cain’t get into a car by myself? What the hell is wrong with you?”

			
Mickey got in the open-top car, slammed the door, and spotted Antonella among the well-wishers. She waved her over and when Antonella got to the car, Mickey squeezed her hand. The tiny young woman pulled it away but before doing so, Mickey felt her give it the smallest squeeze back.

			
When Mickey and Jack got to their new cabin, Mickey proceeded to light the oil lamps and then she went ahead and stripped down naked—shameless in front of her new hubby. Jack was thrilled…until she pulled on her work pants, shirt, and boots. Jack stood there shocked. What the hell was going on, he wondered.

			
“There’s work to be done,” Mickey announced heading for the door. When he pointed out that it was dark outside, she said, “Work on a ranch never stops.” 

			
So Jack decided to take matters into his own hands. He grabbed her and with his arms around her, puckered up and made the giant mistake of saying, “How ’bout givin’ your honey some sugar?”

			
Mickey tried shoving him away, but he was such a big lug that even she, with her big muscles couldn’t much budge him. When she finally managed to wiggle free, she said, “I’m goin’ to the outhouse. Don’t follow me.”

			
After twenty minutes or so, Jack, worried that she’d run away, trudged over to the outhouse. It had started raining something fierce, so when he knocked and there was no answer, he opened the door.

			
There she was, sitting on the commode where she did her best thinking, with her pants down around her boots.

			
“Go away! Can’t you see I’m doin’ my business?” She slammed the door with her foot. 

			
After standing there in the rain, Jack finally opened the door again and dragged her out and back to the house kicking and screaming. When they got back inside, soaking wet, he tried kissing her again. She picked up a knife and cut him a small scratch. Jack jumped to the other side of the room. “Dammit, darlin’!” he said. “It’s time you stopped all this fussin’ and give me what a man wants.”

			
“What I want is for you to keep your damned hands to yourself.”

			
“Oh honey, why don’t you just behave yourself?” he said, grinning like he was enjoying sparring with his wildcat wife.

			
“Did you just tell me to behave myself?” 

			
Jack (stupidly) nodded and rubbed his hands together in happy anticipation of what he figured was about to happen.

			
Instead, his new bald-headed bride grabbed a walnut off the table and flung it at him like she was Red Faber, taking his left eye damn near out.

			
Jack fell to the floor, screaming in pain. Mickey grabbed one of her newly embroidered moppinas, told him to hold it over his eye, hopped into the car, and drove as fast as she could back to the homestead.

			
She woke her mother, told her what happened, and said she needed help. Maria—furious that her daughter wounded her new husband on their first night together—told Lucia to grab the medical supplies she had in the kitchen and told Joe to fetch Doc Dawson, who was probably still drunk from the wedding.

			
Maria tended to the eye as best as she could, but by the time Doc Dawson arrived, wobbly as a newborn calf, he pronounced it too far gone to be fixed. 

			
The bridegroom was immediately nicknamed One-Eye Walnut Jack by the crew.

			
Mickey felt really bad about blinding Jack and all, so she came to an agreement with him. They could have sex once a week on Sunday, or, if she was unavailable, then on Thursday. But he was never to bother her on Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Friday, or Saturday, or she might be inclined to take his other eye out.

			
While it wasn’t the best arrangement in the world, Jack was happy that he was at least getting some once a week, whereas before he was married, he got it none a week. He couldn’t drive cattle the way he used to—too many calves ran off on the side of his bad eye—so he learned to be a great one-eyed shooter. Some of the men took to calling him “Dead-Eye Jack,” which he figured sounded better than One-Eye Walnut Jack.

			
He was banned from poker though, because he had become more convinced than ever that everyone was cheating him now that he couldn’t see who was doing what on his bad-eye side.

			
As luck would have it, and crazy as it was, Mickey found herself pregnant. And in spite of her mother and sisters begging her to quit riding, roping, and wrestling while in her delicate condition, she kept it up, and nine months later gave birth to a ten-and-a-half pound, healthy baby boy. They called him Filippo in honor of Jack’s uncle. Mickey immediately nicknamed him “Flip.” The name stuck to him like skeeters on flypaper.

			
Jack was thrilled with his new son, and so was Mickey. Not because she was ready to settle down, nurse a baby, and start knitting. No way. And it wasn’t only because she could get right back to work either, although that did make her happy. Mostly she was happy because she was able to convince her mother to let Antonella come and live with them. 

			
“That way, she can take care of Flip, do the cookin’ and cleanin’ while I’m out with the cattle.” Maria knew exactly what her daughter was up to, but as long as Antonella was OK with it, and only if Antonella was OK with it, she wouldn’t refuse.

			
So, the tiny, bent young woman moved in, cared for the baby, did the cooking, the cleaning, and the mending. But that’s not all she did.

– 27 –

			
KKK Meet M.A.F.I.A.

			
Trinidad, Colorado 

			
1921–1923 

			
In 1920, when the 18th Amendment passed, making booze illegal in the whole of the United States, the Sicilians had to up the game they’d already been playing. While most of the country had been sitting on their asses, the Sicilians had been putting a fine point on their bootleg booze. By the time the rest of the country went dry (except for Connecticut and Rhode Island), the Sicilian bootleggers knew how to brew it, sell it, hide it, distribute it, and keep it all in the family. The government had inadvertently given them four long years to perfect their craft.

			
Still, when the 18th Amendment actually passed, no one—from the winos to the regular joes to the swells—could believe the bad news. The Sicilian bootleggers meantime—from the Carlinos to the Barberas to the La Donas—couldn’t believe the good news. They’d been brewing up a storm and praying for that day to come. It was vindication for the years they’d slaved in the mines, even though it would never make up for the family and friends who’d been slaughtered like swine. 

			
The rich Anglos wanted booze, and now they had to rely on the Italians, whom they despised, to get it. 

			
Supplying the whole country with illegal booze became much bigger than most families could handle, so Don Vito put out the word that it was time for them all to cooperate with one another and to organize. It was the only way to give their families the kind of protection that folks outside their world would never understand. 

			
As for the bootlegging? They had provided the people of Colorado what they wanted and, as Charlie liked to say, “We give all of the people what they want all of the time.” Callie thought he sounded very Lincoln-like. 

			
Colorado’s Sicilian bootlegging families did their best to keep the feds in the dark about what the hell they were dealing with. The feds were so sure the Italian bootleggers were Black Handers that they put up wanted notices of men who weren’t even involved in their operation at all. 

			
The families loved that because the self-styled “Black Hand” thugs were usually nothing more in those parts than unorganized teppisti—hoodlums—who tried to scare other Italians out of their hard-earned dough. They weren’t bootleggers or Mafia. They were just idiots. 

			
If the feds found one of these clowns first and cut him down, so much the better. They were saving the families the trouble of knocking off the bad seeds. And they didn’t even need to put out contracts. It was so easy it was almost embarrassing. The more they confused the feds the better. 

			
As bootleg booze became mother’s milk for millions of people, the Barberas and the rest of the Sicilian bootleggers moved up in the underworld. They got into national booze distribution with a new division: smuggling primo liquor out of Canada. 

			
How in hell did those ignorant, Guinea bootleggers keep growing right under their noses, the feds wanted to know. It had to be stopped. But catching these moonshiners was like trying to catch ghosts in a bag. 

			
The solution to several problems came like the miracle Maria had been praying for. Don Vito had found a way to make use of Mariano and get him out of the way so his wife and family could better run the business. He called Mariano to his home one day and explained that he wanted to send him to Denver to do a secret investigation. Carlino knew that before the once-respectable Mariano had gone to hell with drink and whoring, he’d had a good sense of things. He could always tell when things were about to go bad.

			
Before he had disappeared like a barrel of his own confiscated booze, Mariano had preached over and over to anyone who would listen that American law was not there to protect Italians but to exploit them, hang ’em, or shoot ’em. He was righter than rain. 

			
Don Vito told Mariano that he had found out the mayor of Denver, a man by the name of Benjamin Stapleton, was interfering in their business. “He’s more of a criminal than any of the bootleggers. He has no honor, no soul. You ever heard of the Ku Klux Klan? It’s a big, organized group of white Protestant men who are out to kill anyone who isn’t a white Protestant. They are the ones who got Stapleton elected. He belongs to them.” 

			
To pay the Klan back, he explained, Stapleton hired a Klansman as Chief of Police and then he deputized 125 Klansmen as cops and prohibition agents. “Soon, all of Colorado’s prohibition agents will be KKK, as they are called,” he warned. “I need you to find out everything so we can fight back.” 

			
Mariano was not surprised that a group of Protestant men wanted to kill them. “But how can I get their information when they would never trust one of us?” Mariano wanted to know. 

			
“This is how,” the Don said, and handed him a big wad of cash. “Go there, play poker, drink in the speakeasies, listen, learn. Find out what they are doing.” In other words, Mariano had to disappear. Again.

			
Which he did, sneaking out in the middle of the night. Maria and the family assumed he was off on a booze bender. Which he was, but this time someone else was paying for this cowboss to pony up to the bar.

			
It was the perfect job for him: boozing, whoring, gambling, bullshitting. 

			
The information Mariano sent back was so premium, though, Don Vito decided to call the heads of the various families to his home. Maria attended with Peter, who was supposed to do most of the talking for the family, even though she was the one calling the shots. It was unheard of for a woman to sit in among the men, but Maria excused it away. She said her missing husband was ill—which everyone took to mean he was on a bender—and Peter was learning the business, and she was just there to protect her family’s interests. 

			
Charlie, who loved his mother-in-law, still shook his head in amazement and said, “Chi paisi! Fimmini a tavula!”—What a country! Women at the table! 

			
When the group settled down, Charlie’s father explained why he had called them to his home: He had recently come into information. He never mentioned Mariano for fear there might be a traitor in their midst regarding the federal crackdown on their businesses.

			
The government had a new way to deal with—or more specifically kill—Sicilian bootleggers and had deputized Ku Klux Klansmen as prohibition agents. 

			
“La K cosa?”—the K thing?—asked one of the assembled men. 

			
Peter asked permission to answer the question. “The KKK are those men who hide behind white hoods and burn crosses in front of the houses of immigrants and colored folks.” 

			
Anthony Alonzo, who had been an attorney in the old country, added, “They hate us and say Italians should be deported because we brought what they call the ‘whore of Rome’ to America. That’s what they call our Catholic Church, ‘the whore!’ These men have no training, no honor, no soul. They just want to kill the colored, the immigrant, and the Catholic.” 

			
The local Klan, he explained, was led by a certain man called The High Exalted Cyclops of the Trinidad Klan. 

			
“He must have one eye in the middle of head,” one of them joked. “Maybe that’s why he wears a hood over his face!” Everybody started laughing—except for Don Vito. 

			
“You think this is funny?” the Don asked with that look in his eye that scared the hell out of most of those tough guys. “The time has come to be extra vigilant. These Ku Klux Klansmen now have a license to hunt us like wild game.” He warned that they could claim everyone they killed just happened to have an illegal still. 

			
He shook his head in disgust and put them on alert: “If these fake polizzia find your operation, they will wreck your stills, smash half your barrels, steal the other half, and rob you of all the cash you have on hand.” 

			
They stopped laughing. 

			
“And probably burn your house down. The government is paying them to murder us, our families, our businesses. Just like they did to the strikers. It is just that dangerous. And they aren’t even real G-men.”

			
Little Boy Frankie Parisi stood and asked, “With all due respect Don Vito, we all know that money talks. No?”

			
The Don shook his head. “You will be sorry if you try to bribe them. They’re scum. They’ll take the bribes and steal your goods anyway. They think we are pissuni ‘gnuranti—ignorant fish. Do not prove them right! But first, we need to stop killing each other if we ever hope to defeat them. You understand?” 

			
He meant that they themselves—the other Sicilian bootleggers—were creating as big a threat to their businesses as the feds and the Klan were. He warned them that this situation, now commonly called The Sugar Moon War, had to end. 

			
The Sugar Moon War was as real as South vs. North back in 1861, except this time, it was South vs. South. Sicilians vs. Sicilians.

			
Already shot-up Sicilian bodies were littering the fields and roads in Pueblo, Chicosa, and Trinidad like fallen, rotted apples. Some were killed in family feuds. Some were shot dead by deputized Klan prohis—who claimed the kills were caused by family feuds.

			
Now that so much money was at stake, even the slightest slight or the poaching of clients could—and had—become deadly. 

			
“We have the government to fight so we can’t be fighting each other,” the Don said banging his open palm on the table, which ricocheted around the room like a shotgun blast.

			
Then, to every man’s shock, Maria stood up. She was so tiny she could barely be seen behind all those derbies, ten-gallon hats, fedoras, and even a few top hats. The men all turned when she called for their attention. Even though they stared at her downright audacity, she said in her best English, which was colored over by her thick Sicilian accent, “We Barberas pledge our loyalty, Signor. We stand with you if you choose to make peace with the La Donas.” 

			
Don Vito nodded his head in approval, so she continued, but in Sicilian, speaking to all the assembled men. 

			
“Signori! We came to this America looking for a home and found ourselves without one, so you men fought your way out of the pit. The ’Merigaan say we are ignorant immigrants for fighting for our place here like they once did. We did not choose this life in America. America chose this life for us! 

			
“We are ’Merigaan too. We fought for a home, and we have one now. We cannot let them take it from us.” 

			
Then she paused and turned to look at the men around the room. “And we must stop trying to take it from each other. It makes us as bad as these—what do you call them?—Klanaman.” 

			
The men all nodded as Peter looked on as though his mother had lost her last marble. 

			
Moved by her talk, the men, one by one, stood up and clapped. She was so shocked that she flopped back down in her seat and saw that her son was among those standing and clapping. For her. 

			
“Grazzi, Signora Barbera. Grazzi,” Don Vito said. 

			
It wasn’t, he went on, just about the bootleg brew and the premium booze they smuggled out of Canada anymore. It was about the distribution of it to New York, Chicago, Kansas City, Boston, St. Louis, Miami, Seattle, San Francisco, and Detroit that was causing the latest problems. Wars were fought and people died securing and protecting routes and customers from poachers. The Carlino/La Dona feud that had started back in Sicily over something no one even remembered was blowing all the way the hell up here. 

			
Don Vito waved his arm around the room. “We all knew Pellegrino Scaglia. We all know he was assassinated while out making a delivery with his children!” he cried out, slamming his hand on the table again. 

			
He mentioned no names, but everyone believed it was a hit ordered by the La Dona family.

			
“Those cowards attacked a man with his own family in the cart. That can never be tolerated. The bullets lifted the boy out of the cart, and he fell under the wheels, crushed to death. Scaglia, my old friend, was a brave man, not a coward. He threw himself on top of his little daughter to protect her as he was being filled with lead. The child was found alive under her father’s corpse, drenched in his blood. 

			
“Because of those murders and our war against each other,” he continued, “the heads of the O’Banion outfit, Alphonse Capone, and that crooked lawyer George Remus are meeting this very night in Pittsburgh to try to settle our war! We could lose everything because we are killing each other.” 

			
Maria stood again and said, “Stamu facennu a nuiautri a stissa cosa chi ni fici cummattiri cu Rockefeller picchì a facia a nui.”—We now do to each other what we fought against Rockefeller for doing to us.—Money turns even our good men into thieves and killers.”

			
Before the meeting, Vito had told his sons privately that the La Donas would have to pay but not yet. “I like best the old French expression,” he said. “Avinnitta è nu piattu chi s’havi a sebbiri friddu.”—Revenge is a dish best served up cold. 

			
“How cold, Papa?” his son Sam had demanded. “How cold?”

			
“As cold as a corpse,” he had answered.

			
Even so, at that night’s meeting, Don Vito told the crowd he would call for a sit-down with La Donas. 

			
“What if they don’t agree?” asked Gaetano. 

			
“Si ‘voi a paci, priparati a guerra.”—If you wish for peace, be ready for war.

			
When the meeting ended and the men walked out into the Colorado night filled with so many stars there was hardly any space between them, Maria and Peter overheard Charlie joke to his father that Maria was a better “man” than her husband. No one knew Mariano was doing the Don’s work.

			
Don Vito stopped short. He wouldn’t have his son talk ill of his father-in-law. “Figgiu mei,” his father said, “Mariano was once a great man. Remember that. He lost much during the strike trying to get the mine workers a fair deal. That Goddamned strike…brought Italians nothing but death and misery. So, please, respect his memory.” 

			
Vito never made a mistake when talking, so Charlie figured his father had just admitted that he believed—or maybe hoped—that Mariano was dead. 

			
He was very wrong.

– 28 –

			
Dancin’ in the Moonlight

			
Pueblo and Back 

			
December 1922

			
All the women in the Barbera family, except for Mickey and Maria, had been kept in the dark about the war and the dangers it brought, so Callie went about her life as sex queen/wealthy man’s wife as usual. Charlie was her all, but her happiness was tainted by the fact that she hadn’t given him a baby yet. 

			
Meantime, baby Flip was doted on by not just his grandmother but by his aunts and all the ranch hands too. Callie tried to pretend Flip didn’t really exist. She resented that Mickey wasn’t one for mothering, yet she was the one with a baby. 

			
The whole crew knew Mickey and Antonella shared more than a bed each night and that One-Eye Walnut Jack was either too dumb or too scared to say anything. The arrangement somehow worked for them. 

			
That didn’t mean there wasn’t a whole hell of a lot of speculating about it. But eventually, even the gossiping bunkhouse boys got tired of talking about it and decided it was just a happy case of a “Boston Marriage,”—casa lesbica—a housewife who has a girlfriend on the side. 

			
Mickey had ordered one of those new-fangled breast pumps, so she didn’t have to stop herding cattle or, more importantly, take time off from her new job of guarding the acreage around their homes. More men, too, had been hired on so the entire perimeter of their property was guarded from a La Dona attack at all times.

			
Not that Callie gave a hoot about any of it. She wasn’t curious about why there were guards around the property or why everyone was suddenly carrying guns even when they went to check on the damned goats. 

			
All she cared about—besides Charlie, of course—was how Mickey, who must have hated being intimate with Jack, got pregnant faster than, well, a jack rabbit, while she and Charlie who had sex more often every night than most humans do in a month, still came up empty.

			
“Mama!” she’d cry. “Why does Michelina who didn’t even want a baby, have a baby, and I can’t get one? It’s not fair! She’s like a man, but she got pregnant right quick. My darling Charlie and me, well, you know, Ma.”

			
“I know what?” asked Maria, knowing full well what she meant.

			
Those two could be heard moaning and groaning every morning and every night. When they actually slept was the big question everybody was always asking.

			
Hell, Charlie had even taken to riding home for lunch instead of chowing down at the chuck wagon with the boys whenever he could. Why? So, they could get to more moaning and groaning, that’s why. 

			
“I made a novena to Saint Gianna who helps the helpless get pregnant,” Callie wailed to her mother. “Charlie will surely go find another lady who can give him babies iffin’ I don’t get a bun in this oven soon,” she said, laying her hands on her caved-in stomach. Her hipbones stuck out like she was about to die of starvation. 

			
When she went to see Doc Dawson, he told her there wasn’t a darn thing wrong with her that he could tell. “Maybe it’s Charlie who got the, ah, problem?” Callie was so offended that anyone could think Charlie wasn’t superior in every way that she said, “Well, Doctor, that goes to show what you know. My Charlie is a perfect man. The problem is with me.” 

			
Dawson knew when to back off. The woman was blind with love and would never see reason. So, he said, “That’s probably true,” meaning she was crazy. She didn’t get it.

			
After more days of wailing, Maria told Callie there was only one thing to do: They’d travel to visit Chepeta, the Ute shaman, or as the settlers called her, the medicine woman. Chepeta was so wise that, while tribal members would often change their names as many as five times to match transitions in life, she only had two name changes. Here, as her birth name, and another when she began to walk at an unusually early age. An older relative changed her name to honor her Aunt Chepeta, the most famous Ute wise woman who ever lived. And nobody ever thought it should be changed to anything else. Wise is wise. Spirit is spirit. 

			
Yellow Fly had fetched Chepeta many times when calves were sickly or when fever spread around the hands or the livestock. But it wouldn’t do for her to come to them this time, though. Callie and Maria had to go to her. 

			
“Chepeta can make the miracle that even our Lord hasn’t seen fit to grant you,” Maria said. The medicine woman was her daughter’s last hope, and they both knew it. 

			
To keep the trip’s purpose as secret as possible, Callie told Charlie that she and her mother were going to tend to a sickly relation for a few days. She was vague about just which relative it was. Charlie figured it was lady stuff, so he accepted it but insisted that Gaetano accompany them as a guard. 

			
Even if she was doing a good deed, he wasn’t happy about his Callie leaving him—ever—no matter for how short a period of time. 

			
“But darlin’!” Charlie cried, pointing down to his man parts. “Who’s gonna take care of Big Man while you’re gone?”

			
“Well, don’t you go sharin’ Big Man with no other woman. You’ll just have to make do.”

			
Charlie made a jerking off gesture with his hand, causing Callie to pretend to blush. “I guess this will have to do until you get back,” he whined.

			
“Or better still,” she offered, “this will do you ‘til I return.” She opened her mouth wide, kissed him, and rolled her tongue around. Then she pushed Charlie right onto the bed, got on top of him and put Big Man inside her where he liked it best. Everyone on the ranch heard them yelling, moaning, sighing, and carrying on, but by then their noises were common as cows mooing. 

			
Charlie loved Callie so much that he never said anything about why she hadn’t given him a son yet, but it did play on his mind. Maybe they did it too much and her lady parts was worn out? Maybe she just wasn’t born to breed? Or maybe God was cursing them for being so happy together?

			
Callie had her own thoughts. She was definitely what the medical doctors called a “nymphomaniac,” so maybe God didn’t want women who wanted sex all the time to have babies. Her mother told her that wasn’t true, and that the more sex a woman had the better chance she had of getting pregnant, which only caused Callie to become hysterical. “Iffin’ we had more sexshal relations, we’d never have time for anything else!”

			
Indeed. It was a problem.

			
Maria and Callie took the buckboard and rode out to where the Mouache band of the Ute tribe was camped out south of Trinidad. Gaetano followed a respectable distance behind. 

			
Charlie was a man every ranch hand wanted on their side, yes, but it was Maria who put the real fear of God into all the men. So, Gaetano did as she asked when she told him to make camp about half a mile from the Ute’s encampment. This trip was about women’s business, not anything a man could know about.

			
Maria had brought Chepeta a silver comb and hairbrush and one of Callie’s fancy lace petticoats with ribbons. Chepeta, who was dressed in her traditional long, deerskin dress and leggings thought they were pretty enough but probably best suited to the ladies who sold sex in town.

			
Maria also bought jars of her own canned tomato sauce, a big jar of Italian spices, which was her own secret bland, and homemade pasta. 

			
Chepeta didn’t know what to do with the sauce or the strange looking little bread things (the pasta), but Maria showed her how to cook it up. There was no common language among the women, but with hand gestures and drawings in the dirt, Callie made clear the reason that she was there.

			
“Ah,” Chepeta said, smiling, “no’apükkangkün!” 

			
Callie hoped that meant “get pregnant.”

			
The next morning, Chepeta took Callie into her wickiup where she sang and danced around her, applying herbs to her body and making up a tea with leaves, berries, and twigs that Callie had never seen before. 

			
As Callie tried her best to look unafraid, Chepeta took her leather rattle, which was filled with quartz crystals, and shook it until flashes of light crackled all around them. Powerful magic for sure. But for Callie it was also scarier than hell itself. She was too paralyzed with fear to run out of the tent.

			
After that ceremony was finished, Chepeta put Callie inside a sweat lodge for a day. Callie thought she was going to die and then swore that her heart actually stopped when she was finally taken out of the steaming hot tent and plunged into the freezing cold stream. Maria knew her daughter, now the very spoiled wife of a very rich man, could stand with some roughing up, so she didn’t pay any mind to Callie’s crying and carrying on. Meantime, she slept on the ground outside and prayed on her rosary. There was no such thing as too much praying when it came to this situation, she figured. After having survived and even thrived on this frontier, Maria knew miracles came from more than one God and bad times from more than one devil.

			
After two days, the rituals and cleansings were done. Chepeta gave Callie a sack of herbs that she was to boil into a tea and drink every day. Somehow, they understood.

			
There was one more thing, she said. But Callie and Maria didn’t understand what she was trying to say, so Chepeta had to call over the male scout, who spoke English. 

			
He said, “Chepeta says you must do yoko two times at the full moon.”

			
“Yoko?” Callie asked. “How do you do yoko?”

			
The scout looked embarrassed, but Chepeta nudged him and said, “Sumaikku!”—Go ahead.

			
The scout made a circle with his thumb and forefinger and shoved his finger on his other hand in and out of it.

			
“Oh,” said Maria. Callie didn’t blink.

			
Then Chepeta said the one phrase she knew in perfect English, “I guarantee!”

			
Callie bowed down to thank her and then popped back up again, suddenly distraught. “You mean we can only do yoko two times in one night?”

			
Even the scout laughed when he interpreted it, and Maria and the medicine woman exchanged looks. This girl had no shame. 

			
Chepeta raised two fingers and shook her head. “Gaidease!” 

			
“She said only two times yoko. No more,” the scout explained. 

			
Callie attempted to thank Chepeta in the same Shoshoni language muttering, “huuwihyu,” which came out more like “who are you?”

			
By the time the ladies and Gaetano got a move on and got home after supper, Ute magic was already at work. There was a full moon, and Charlie was in bed.

			
Callie poured lavender water all over herself to hide the stink of the ride, put on a lacy, racy nightgown and climbed into bed. Charlie was quick to respond, and they made love—twice. 

			
Callie was nervous he’d want to go a few more times like usual, but when he tried, nothing happened. 

			
“The heart is willin’, darlin’, but the flesh is weak tonight.” He flopped back down on the pillow and pretended to be fast asleep.

			
She took this as a good sign from heaven that the blessed event she had so yearned for had already begun.

			
Charlie took his lack of going all night as a very bad sign from hell that the horror he so feared—the war between the families—was about to boil over.

– 29 –

			
The Big Bang

			
Pueblo, Colorado 

			
September 1, 1923

			
Two weeks after they got back from the Ute camp, Callie missed her first moon cycle. She told only her mother, fearing that her bloods might start flowing, and the bad news might break Charlie’s heart. She knew how much he wanted a son. 

			
He never noticed that she’d missed her bloods, which was funny seeing how he was so obsessed with her skin-and-bones body. Crazier still, he didn’t even remark on how her flat-as-a-barnside breasts had started to pop.

			
Charlie hadn’t taken note of it, or if he had, he never mentioned it to Callie. Charlie had changed alright, but he held the reason as close to the vest as his gun. The meeting of the families had brought home the reality of the dangerous situation they were in. 

			
The American vigilante group, the KKK, had been given a hunting license to kill immigrants as long as they claimed they were bootleggers. Proof not needed. That was a fight the families were willing to take on, but it would only work if they all banded together. But was that even possible?

			
Despite what his father had said, Charlie feared the murder of Pellegrino Scaglia and his young son, and the attempted murder of his daughter, made peace seemingly impossible. The La Donas had declared war not just on the men, but they had done the unthinkable and gone after their rivals’ children. How could the families ever move on from such a thing? Could his father have misjudged the situation?

			
While his father was always right, in some ways Charlie was secretly relieved Callie hadn’t produced a child. Imagine if they had a baby and he couldn’t protect it? The very thought made him sick with rage.

			
No one was safe. He didn’t share any of these fears with his wife. It was men’s business, but it was weighing him down to the point that he could think of nothing else. 

			
Callie was confused and upset. Maybe he was disappointed in her because she hadn’t produced a child yet. Maybe he just didn’t love her like he did before, because, for one thing, he wasn’t as fussy about looking perfect for her. 

			
She didn’t worry too much that he had another lady because, hell, he’d even stopped dressing up like a dandy when he went into town. He had stopped dousing himself with that fancy Caswell No. 6 Cologne; the one the ranch hands said was so strong that Charlie couldn’t sneak up on a blind man.

			
Callie was worried for sure, but she tried keeping faith that her man was keeping faithful to her even when he started coming home later than usual since most of the time she knew where he was.

			
He and his brothers had taken to spending the evenings at their father’s house. But why was he staying later and longer each night? Callie didn’t know but decided to believe it was just him needing time for man talk. Business and all that. Whatever it was, it was a secret not for women to know.

			
But she had a secret of her own—one that would make him happy again no matter what was troubling him. She just had to wait to make sure before announcing it. So, by the time Callie had missed two cycles and was starting to finally put some meat on those bones, she decided to announce the big news to Charlie over a dinner of chicken cacciatore and a bottle of her family’s best Barbera rosso. 

			
Even though he didn’t walk in the door until well past eight, she had kept not only the dinner warm but also kept herself looking hotter than a fried chili pepper. All she wore was a silk slip that left nothing to the imagination. 

			
Charlie tossed his hat on the rack and sat down at the table without even washing up as he usually did. 

			
“Charlie,” she said rubbing her stomach and breasts, which he took to mean dinner would have to wait until they hit the sheets—or maybe even right there on the table.

			
“I have news,” she announced stopping all the rubbing around, her nipples bulging out of the thin silk like fresh popped corn. Since it didn’t seem to involve more sex, Charlie stopped being all that interested, so she moved behind him and put her hands on his shoulders.

			
It kind of annoyed him because he liked food almost as much as he liked Callie’s ways with her mouth—and that didn’t mean her sparkling conversation. 

			
“I said I have news, Charlie.”

			
“OK. What’s your big news?” 

			
What the hell was wrong with him? He sure didn’t seem to care what her news could even be.

			
“I’m pregnant!” she yelled, dropping her hands, clapping, and dancing around the table. “We’re having a baby!”

			
Charlie just looked startled. 

			
It wasn’t the reaction she had been expecting, so she started to cry. “Charlie, I said we’re having a baby!”

			
Charlie snapped out of it long enough to say, “Why darlin’, that’s great news!” He got up and hugged her, but his heart wasn’t in it. 

			
“Charlie, what’s wrong?” Callie cried. “I thought you’d be the happiest man in the world now that our family is starting.”

			
“I am happy, sweetheart.”

			
Callie had a thought. “Oh wait—are you worried you won’t be getting as much lovin’ everyday? Well, don’t you worry about that. Even when I am too big for you to mount, I promise my hands and mouth will be working overtime!”

			
Charlie laughed and gave her a smooch, but for reasons she didn’t understand, it didn’t end in bed. Instead, he dove into his grub, and as she was cleaning up the dishes, he went to bed instead of taking her to bed.

Some nights he didn’t come home at all and stayed over at the Carlino house while she sat alone with her big news and new big titties.

They’d never be safe from the deputized Klansmen either. To his mind, it was almost better to make her think there was another woman than to make her feel unsafe while she was carrying his baby.

It was so plain, heavy, and ugly, it looked like a punishment, not a garment. “Charlie!” Callie asked, “What in the world are you doin’ wearin’ a heavy vest like this in the summer?”

			
He told her it was because he was doing more roping and would fall off his horse more often. The vest, he said, kept him from breaking his ribs. It didn’t make a whole helluva lot of sense, but she didn’t question it.

Maria snipped the cord, checked the infant from top to toe and declared, “Cincu ‘irita di mani, cincu irita di pedi!”—Five fingers, five toes!

			
Callie struggled to sit up, “Is it a boy?” was all she wanted to know.

			
Her sister Laura, who was helping with the birthing, blurted out without Maria’s permission, “Callie, it’s a beautiful baby girl.”

That’s what she thought.

He believed her because he believed everything she said. Then she said she was sorry it wasn’t a boy.

			
Charlie laughed, kissed the baby’s head, and said, “Ma chi bedda!”—But she’s beautiful! 

			
Dark skin, curly black hair, perfect little feet. Everyone agreed she was a beautiful baby; everyone except for Callie, that is.

			
Maria shooed Charlie out of the room—the birthing room was no place for men—and told him to go pass out cigars. But when he looked for the cigars in the bunkhouse, he found empty boxes. Those card-playing varmints had gone and smoked every last one.

			
“No problem, brother,” Peter said to the new father. “You stay here, and me and Gaetano will take the Ford and go pick up some good ones down at Bratton’s.” 

			
W.R. Bratton Cigar Emporium was the place for the best Pueblo had to offer. It sold cigars in the front, bootleg in the back, and had a speakeasy in the basement. What could be bad?

			
Peter and Gaetano took the open-top Ford into town, and as Gaetano was parking the car, they saw a sedan with the windows blacked out speeding up to pass them. 

			
“Stunad!” Gaetano yelled, jumping up from his seat and slapping his right hand on his left arm in the gesto dell’ombrello gesture, yelling, “Va Fangul!”—Go to Hell!—for good measure.

			
The shotgun blasts that followed hurled Gaetano halfway out of the car. What used to be Gaetano was splattered everywhere. Peter tried climbing over to him and saw that the top of Gaetano’s head was mostly gone. 

			
Another shot rang out, just missing Peter, who reached for his gun. That’s when he remembered they’d all put their guns down that day, and he’d forgotten to take it back for the drive to town. More shots. The boy got hit. The force of the bullets lifted him up and threw him back down on top of Gaetano’s bloody corpse. 

			
Several minutes passed before townsfolk felt safe enough to come out and run to the bloody Ford. The sedan with the shooters had sped off, so no one saw anything even if they had seen something. Peter was still alive, but there was so much blood nobody could tell which man it had come from.

			
Doc Dawson who had been knocking back a few downstairs at Bratton’s ran up two steps at a time when he had heard shooting and the yelling. He reached the ground floor just as some men were carrying Peter into the store. Robert Roberts Jr., who just happened to have been downstairs imbibing with the doc, followed him up and grabbed his camera and stand where he’d left them leaning against the wall.

			
The men laid Peter on the counter right on top of the displayed goods. Doc listened for a heartbeat and began cutting away his clothes. “He’s still breathing!” he declared.

			
Roberts Jr. and his camera moved in closer for a good picture. Doc noticed what he was doing and yelled, “Get that sumnabitch the hell outta here!” But Roberts Jr. kept on taking photographs, not budging. By then, Bratton’s was so packed with onlookers that even if the President of the United States himself had shown up, nobody would have moved. 

			
Leave? thought Roberts Jr. Hell, this could be the revenge story even I couldn’t make up.

			
And that no-good reporter could make up a tall tale out of spit and string. Especially when he was supposed to be writing news and truth. 

			
Roberts Jr.’s Protestant God had just handed him the story of a lifetime. He grinned to himself, thinking how sweet it would be to get even with Peter’s illiterate Guinea bitch mother who had refused to let him talk to the little flood girls or even take pictures of that devil wolf of theirs. 

			
Screw the flood girl story. This? This was one hundred times better. He’d publish pictures of Maria’s son as he lay dying in a store. She would be sorry she drove him off. She’d be so damned sorry.

Deader than Kelsey’s Nuts

			
Pueblo, Colorado

			
September 2, 1923

			
The Pueblo Times

			
Black Hander Hit Point Blank

			
By Robert Roberts Jr.

			
Special to The Pueblo Times

			
The twenty year-old Sicilian gunslinger and murderer, Pietrantonio La Barbera, alias Peter Barbera, alias Pete Barbarian, son of the wanted Black Hand bootlegger and assassin, “Marian the Barbarian,” got his just desserts on Saturday when he was gunned down by his own kind.

			
The young killer and his mobster friend, Gaetano Passentino, forty-two, were riding in Barbera’s convertible car when rival Sicilian mobsters overtook them in a black sedan and opened fire. The assassins’ bullets blew half of Passentino’s head clear off and fatally wounded Barbera. 

			
The sedan carrying the killers sped off as soon as the deed was done. No one saw their faces, but it is assumed they were rival gangsters because violence among the Italians has been increasing daily since the murder of mob boss Pellegrino Scaglia, while he was with his children last year. 

			
In an attempt to save Barbera’s life, local townsfolk put aside their revulsion of the filthy immigrants and rushed to their aid, as God-fearing Americans will do, regardless of the danger to themselves.

			
Passentino was beyond saving, but Barbera was still alive. They carried him into W.R. Bratton’s Cigar Emporium and laid him on the display counter where the victim proceeded to bleed all over the imported cigars. 

			
Dr. Dawson, who happened to be in the shop at the time, was Johnny-on-the-spot, cutting away Barbera’s clothing in an attempt to see where he had been shot. As Doc was removing the bullets, young Barbera, who had lost much blood, passed away.

			
This will no doubt bring upon the good people of Pueblo more violence at the hands of Italians. The New York Times even published a highly praised editorial warning Americans about the particular danger of the Southern Italian immigrant. 

			
The editors wrote: “There has never been since New York was founded, so low and ignorant a class among the immigrants who poured in here as the Southern Italians.” The editors further warned that Sicilian children were “utterly unfit—ragged, filthy, and verminous” and should not be educated in public schools “among the decent children of American mechanics.” Sadly, that sage warning has gone totally unheeded in Colorado.

			
The Barbera family, who are as bad as they come, have been in the news before. The late Peter Barbera happened to be the older brother of the infamous so-called “Flood Girls.” These were the five-year-old twins who, along with their pet wolf, somehow “magically” survived the 1921 Pueblo flood even as others in the schoolhouse perished.

			
The Barbera family had yet to be informed of Peter Barbera’s death at the time this newspaper went to press. 

			
***

			
Robert Roberts Jr.’s front-page story ran along with a half-page photo of young Peter sprawled out without clothing, covered in blood on the cigar counter at Bratton’s. 

			
What that yellow journalist forgot to mention was that Peter didn’t die by the time he had filed his story with the newspaper. As it turned out, Peter did not die at all. 

			
In reality, local hotel keeper, Bad Boyce, had raced to the Barbera Ranch to tell the family about the shooting, not knowing if Peter was alive or dead. Everyone from the family plus the ranch hands rode, drove, and ran to the scene. Doc had managed to save Peter’s life, but just barely. That boy was so full of holes, he looked like a cowboy’s boots, and he had lost more blood than you’d imagine a person had in them.

			
Peter stayed on the counter that night, watched over by his mama and the Barbera men, while outside, the ranch hands surrounded the store armed to the teeth.

			
The next day, weak as he was, they managed to bring Peter home, where Maria and the sisters tended to him while the men stood watch at the property borders and around the house.

			
Maria called in Father Angeletto, and the two of them prayed over Peter, the priest anointing the wounded young man with olive oil and salt. His mama applied moldy bread to his bullet wounds and kept watch with her daughters twenty-four hours a day.

			
After three days of Peter going in and out of consciousness, Maria called upon Chepeta once again, this time to do a healing. Father Angeletto, outraged that she would put a heathen medicine woman ahead of Jesus in the healing arts, left in a huff. Of course, he’d return. He was too afraid of offending any of the Mafia families, and as importantly, he didn’t want to lose out on their generous donations. 

			
As everyone did what they could, from praying to rituals, Peter’s sister Mickey wasn’t among what she called her “sisters of mercy” who were tending to him. Mickey had other plans. 

			
Once Chepeta had finished her ritual and left, Father Angeletto returned to the ranch on his donkey. 

			
“He thinks he’s Jesus,” Austin said to the men, which gave them the first laugh since Gaetano’s murder and Peter’s shooting.

			
As the priest entered under the Double A sign, he ran into Mickey who was cleaning a gun. Furious that she wasn’t doing the proper “woman thing” by ministering to Peter with the other ladies, he took to preaching to her about the power of prayer and how she seemed to be ignoring her brother in his time of need and solace.

			
“Solace my ass,” Mickey shot back, shocking the priest into a near faint off his own ass. She then whistled and looked through gun sight and said, “Vengeance is mine sayeth the Lord.” The priest made a quick sign of the cross, which annoyed the hell out of her, so she aimed the gun his way, causing him to take off on his mounted ass like his own ass was on fire.

			
On Wednesday night, Mickey rode out on horseback with Black Bill, Yellow Fly, and One-Eye Walnut Jack towards the La Dona home. They waited under cover of darkness in an unguarded spot on the property until they saw a car pulling up. Mickey and the boys walked their horses as quietly as they could as close as they could get to the house. The car stopped in front of the house, and four men jumped out. They were all laughing and joking. The screen door opened, and the two men came out holding a bottle of booze. One of the men who’d been in the car waved a copy of The Pueblo Times and read Roberts Jr.’s headline. They all cheered like he had just read that The Denver White Elephants had won the American League baseball pennant. 

			
“We kilt ’em good.” One of the men added for good measure, “Them Carlinos and Barberas won’t be messin’ around with the La Donas no more!”

			
Mickey aimed her shotgun and signaled for Yellow Fly, Black Bill, and One-Eye Walnut Jack to do the same.

– 31 –

			
Love and Death and Love

			
Pueblo, Colorado 

			
September 4, 1923

			
The La Dona gang had already been drinking pretty good by the time Mickey, Yellow Fly, Black Bill, and Mickey’s half-a-husband, One-Eye Walnut Jack, snuck onto the La Dona farm in Vineland. 

			
They were all great shots, which is why Mickey took them with her. They had settled in a wooded area where they couldn’t be spotted but were close enough to hit their targets.

			
Still on horseback, Mickey aimed and nodded her head to let the others know to do the same. When everyone had a man in their sights, Mickey whispered, “Go!” and what seemed like an endless barrage of bullets flew. The La Dona boys screamed in pain and fell like dominoes right where they had been celebrating half a second earlier. Some of the La Donas managed to get a few shots off, but they were shooting blind into the dark while they had been silhouetted by the oil and electric lamps inside the house like ducks in a carnival game.

			
Seconds after the shooting started, the screen door slammed open, and two other men with rifles came flying out. Mickey and One-Eye Walnut Jack shot them too. She raised her arm for the group to move out, turned her horse, and they all took off as fast as their very fast horses would carry them.

			
They heard the sound of women and children screaming as they put distance between themselves and the La Dona gang. They rode like the wind, careful to avoid any honeypots—mud, sand, tall grass, and soft dirt—that would leave tracks. But it was impossible to avoid them all, so Walter, who was the best tracker they had (which made him the best man for covering tracks) had been waiting at the edge of the La Dona property. He had a big piece of carpet tied to his saddle, and he dragged it behind him, brushing away tracks and righting the bent grasses.

			
Mickey was so happy that her plan had gone off without a snag and even happier that One-Eye had made two kills, that she went down on him when they got home, as Antonella sat sulking outside the room she normally shared with Mickey.

			
Antonella was still sitting on the floor and feeling sorry for herself outside the bedroom when Mickey opened the door at sunrise. “Get over it,” she snapped at Antonella. “The man deserved a bonus.” 

			
Then, “Hey, Jack! Get your ass outta my bed.” 

			
When he came stumbling out, pulling his pants up over his long johns, Mickey said, “That was a once-in-a-lifetime treat for doin’ so good. Don’t expect it’ll ever happen again because it won’t.” Then she thought about it. “Unless you bag old man La Dona.”

			
That gave One-Eye Walnut Jack a whole new lease on life. For the first time, he had a goal, something to shoot for so to speak: Blow old man La Dona away and get a blow job in return. 

			
Meanwhile, a day later, when word reached the Barbera Ranch that there’d been a slaughter at the La Dona Farm, Maria put two and two together. 

			
She stood on the porch and screamed like a wild woman, “Michelina! Ntrasi subbito!”—Get in here right now! 

			
When Mickey walked into Maria’s house, she removed her ten-gallon. “Jesu Christo Maria!” her mother said to her. Mickey had completely shaved her head not even leaving her usual stubble. She was bald as a cue ball.

			
“What did you do?” Maria demanded, her Sicilian so thick that only her family could ever catch every word. “What the hell did you do?”

			
“I shaved the rest of my hair off,” said Mickey. “It would get all sweaty and stuck when I was out on the range.”

			
Maria swatted Mickey’s head so hard it left a welt right in the center of the bald. “No! You stupid girl. What did you do when you rode out with the men the other night? Did you kill La Donas without an order? Without a contract? Without my say so?”

			
“Oh, that,” Mickey answered, like she just remembered she’d assassinated a shitload of men a couple of nights earlier. She had thought for a second about denying it, but her mother was way too smart for that. Not only was Maria clever and sly, she knew her children better than they knew themselves. 

			
But why, Maria wondered, had she been so thoughtless as to forget to warn Mickey not to do anything? Why hadn’t she told her the family would take care of the La Donas in its own time? 

			
Why? Because the usually strict Maria had been working so hard to keep Peter alive, she hadn’t talked about her plans to take down the rotten bunch of what they called, “La Dona Belladonna.”

			
Now it was too late.

			
“You’ve put us all in jeopardy!” she screamed at Mickey. 

			
“We were already in jeopardy,” Mickey reminded her mother like she needed reminding. “Those fuckers, pardon my language, Ma, but those fuckers killed Gaetano, and Peter is hanging by a thread.”

			
“It wasn’t your place! I would have gotten vengeance in my own way, and I would have broken them and made them surrender.”

			
“Well, Ma,” Mickey said, not the least bit sorry and in fact proud of herself. “Thing is? You shudda seen those bastards go down.” She pointed her hand like a gun and swept it around the room. “Pow! Pow! Pow!” 

			
Mickey saw her mother’s enraged face and tried to pretend she was sorry that she’d done it, but she knew her mother knew it was a bunch of bull. 

			
“Get out!” Maria snapped. “If any more harm comes to this family, it will be on your soul.” 

			
That reached Mickey, and she bowed her head for real and said, “I’m so sorry, Ma.” Then, “But they deserved it.” 

			
Maria picked up a plate and threw it to deliberately smash into the wall an inch above Mickey’s head.

			
When she left, Maria went to the room where Peter was laying on top of the covers, sweating and groaning. Maria took out her rosary, knelt, and prayed, “Arcangelo Michele, proteggi nuiatri ‘nta battagghia…”—Saint Michael the Archangel, defend us in battle. Be our protection against the wickedness and snares of the devil; God rebuke him, we humbly pray; to hell with Satan and all the evil spirits who roam the world for the ruin of souls…

			
The story was everywhere, and accusations flew around Pueblo like dust in a storm. Robert Roberts Jr. took particular delight, of course, in continuing to write about the “ambush and slaughter” of members of the La Dona gang by “coward swarthy Sicilians under cover of darkness.” Like he knew, which he didn’t.

			
Neither John nor Antonio La Dona had been killed, although like Peter Barbera, John La Dona had a fifty-fifty chance of survival. Pete La Dona, escaped without a scratch. Several of the henchmen, probably the very ones who had ambushed Peter and Gaetano, were killed. Roberts Jr. went so far as to accuse the Barberas and Carlinos of the “cold blooded murders.” It was true, of course, but he was such a slanted reporter, he printed it with no proof.

			
Little Flo, who could read and write with the best of ’em, walked into the kitchen and picked up The Pueblo Times from the table. Maria and Helen were busy rolling out the dough for the night’s pasta.

			
“This is that man who came here and you threw him out, right Mama?” Flo asked. Maria nodded her head. 

			
“Why does he hate us?” the little girl asked. “He wrote that terrible article about Peter and Gaetano too! He called them terrible names. I hate him.” 

			
Maria tried to explain to her that many white ’Merigaans—the Protestants—in this new land hated Italians even though their families had come here as immigrants just like the Italians had. Flo nodded her head.

			
“Where does he live?” Flo wanted to know. Maria shooed her away saying it didn’t matter where he lived, and she better not try to find out because he was a bad man.

			
Flo asked, “Mama? Why wasn’t nobody arrested for shooting Peter?” Peter had always been her favorite because he had taught her useful things like how, if you held a buttercup under somebody’s chin, you could tell if they liked butter. “And,” she added, showing Helen the newspaper and stomping her feet, “why ain’t they arrested nobody for shooting Gaetano dead?”

			
Cranky Helen grunted and, looking up in great annoyance, said, “Why? Why you ask, you pest? Resistiri. Difenniri. Onori. Omertà. Mafia. That’s why. You know nothing.”

			
Flo put her hands on her hips. “That’s not an answer, and I do know stuff, but how can I know more stuff if nobody tells me nothing?” Then she added, “Helen, you are such a grouchy old maid, you never teach me anything.” 

			
Helen picked up the rolling pin and mock-threatened to hit Flo on the head with it.

			
Maria tucked the little girl under her arm and kissed her on the head. “Nun lu diri a nuddu.”—Do not tell anyone. 

			
Flo wasn’t sure what she wasn’t supposed to tell anyone, but she ignored it because it was a thing her mama seemed to say all the time about everything.

			
What she meant, that Flo didn’t yet understand, was that Sicilians would never talk to the law, even if talking would bring the killers of their own family members to justice. The law? They were worse than your worst enemy.

			
“Now skedaddle,” Helen told Flo. “Go outside and play. And mind your own business!”

			
Flo stomped out to the front porch and called Blue, who came running out of the woods. The wolf hopped up on the porch, stuck her head in the front door, and growled at Helen, which made her scoot for protection behind Maria. Flo thought that was about the funniest thing she ever saw, which just made Helen even grouchier than usual. 

			
Laughing out loud, Flo ran off with Blue, with little Clara trailing along. God knows where they went, and they never told a soul where they had gone off to that day either. 

			
At first no one paid them any mind, but later there were suspicions and plenty of speculation about it. The girls wouldn’t say…like their mother had taught them Nun lu diri a nuddu. Meantime, since the ambush of Peter and Gaetano and the retaliation by Mickey and her boys, to the untrained eye at least, it looked like everything had quieted down. But truth to tell, Maria was still preparing—as she had hinted to Mickey—a lot more than pasta dough. She was preparing a full course of retaliation. But first Gaetano had to be given a funeral with all the honors he had earned. 

			
Gaetano’s body had been taken to the T.J. Maggiore Funeral Parlor, but two days had gone by, and they still had no word on when they could hold the wake. So, Maria and Joe went into town to find out what the holdup was all about.

			
Signor Maggiore explained that he had been trying very hard to put together what was left of Gaetano, but it was a nearly impossible task. He tried to suggest that perhaps a closed coffin would be best. Maria and Joe were shocked.

			
“Closed coffin?” Maria gasped. “No, that would be disrespectful. And,” she added making sure that the undertaker understood her meaning, “everybody should see what the La Donas did to one of us.” There was no further talk nor consideration about what her own family had done in retaliation. One thing was clear without it being spoken—the murdered men at the La Dona home would not be waked at the T.J. Maggiore Funeral Parlor.

			
Callie, meantime, was not interested in what she called “men’s business” even if the men’s business she was talking about was run by her mama.

			
Yes, the new mother did have a lot on her mind: Would her brother ever recover? Was her new little family safe? What would happen when her sister Laura, who was helping out the with baby, went back home in a few days and she was alone with the infant she called “Screaming Mimi?”

			
She was not happy that the baptism had to be a private affair instead of the big blowout she had imagined because of the dangers surrounding the family. Would there be another blood bath when they had the funeral, which, unlike the baptism, would be open to all friends and family?

			
Callie was mostly interested in getting her marital relations back on track. To keep Charlie coming home every night, they must get back to the love making that had taken up every free minute before the baby was born.

			
She was worried. What if she and Charlie resumed their wild sex life and she ended up pregnant right away? 

			
I wonder if Chepeta has a spell to keep me from getting pregnant right away? she thought and then dismissed it as bad luck.

			
She fully intended to give Charlie a son. Just not right away. Babies were a lot of work, and besides, the baby (whom they had named Carlotta) took time away from Charlie. If pregnancy was hard, her own infant, she discovered, was harder to deal with. She told Laura, much to her sister’s surprise that, “This cryin’ nasty little thing wasn’t what I hoped for.” 

			
“It ain’t like you didn’t know what was in store for you. Mama popped ’em out like rabbits.” 

			
“But we were all good babies, not like ‘Screamin’ Mimi’ over there,” she whined as Laura went to pick up the crying baby from the cradle. 

			
“You forget. We all wailed and carried on plenty as babies. Somebody was always bawlin’ and if we weren’t, it was because we were too starved to scream.”

			
Callie just shrugged her shoulders.

			
Charlie was still out a lot and at his father’s house until late, but Callie made sure that between feedings, she was all over him like paint on a wall. Now that she had big nursing breasts, their sex life had entered a whole new dimension.

			
Being a new mother hadn’t improved her looks, nor had she ever developed that “new mother glow” they talk about. For reasons nobody could figure out, though, the already too-handsome Charlie did seem to get better looking. 

			
So that gal had to make sure she kept him hot as a stolen tamale.

			
She did have those new, big breasts, which was good. Her biggest thrill was not feeding the baby but offering them up to Charlie. That was a new sex trick that she figured even Cora-Lee probably never learned. 

			
 

			
When Carlotta wailed to be fed and then would attach herself to Callie like a starving pup on a teat, it just annoyed her. Those breasts were for her man. She worried that Carlotta would ruin them and make them sag.

			
And even though they couldn’t have what you’d call full marital relations yet, Callie was all about pleasing Charlie as many times a day as he wanted in every way she could think of. The more he would get to loving on those breasts, the louder Callie’d cry out, “Oh, Carlo!” using his given Christian name instead of “Charlie” like usual. 

			
She did it because it drove him wild, especially as he was coming. He just didn’t know what else was coming.
