Baxter Bridge Over Troubled Waters

			
Baxter Bridge, Near Pueblo

			
September 10, 1923

			
After a morning of mighty satisfying sex, Charlie set out for the bottling and distribution site like always. Well, not like always because, since the ambush and Mickey’s retaliation, the Barberas and the Carlinos had bodyguards riding shotgun with them. Nobody rode, worked, played, or even went to the outhouse alone. When the men were gone from their homes, their houses were guarded by sharpshooters who had pledged their lives to protect their “family.”

			
The Sugar Moon War was full on, and nobody was safe. It was like Vito Carlino had never called that meeting with the other families or issued the warning for them to stop warring with each other or they’d risk losing everything. 

			
That morning, Callie blew her man a kiss from the front porch with her free hand as she held the baby who suckled at her breast with the other. She couldn’t help if the top of her dress fell down and her other breast was exposed, could she? 

			
Charlie’s bodyguard, Dominic Ingo, tried like hell not to stare, but he’d never seen such a display. Now he knew why the other fellers said in private that for an ugly-ass woman, Callie showed off more of what she had (and didn’t have) than any other good, Catholic woman they’d ever seen. Dominic would never put up with such shamelessness from his wife. But that Charlie? He seemed to love it. Good thing Callie’s bodyguard was a woman—her sister Mickey. 

			
What Charlie didn’t tell anybody was that, since the Peter and Mickey incidents, his father told him a meeting had been arranged between the families. 

			
“They want to negotiate a truce—or at least a cease-fire. So do I. You will be my representative, and one of the La Dona boys will represent his family at the first meeting.”

			
Don Vito told Charlie to make it clear the Carlino’s would accept nothing less than a piece of the La Dona bootleg action in exchange for lasting peace. Charlie was to keep the meeting to himself and report back to his father immediately thereafter. 

			
“Do you trust them, Papa?” Charlie wanted to know. 

			
“I trust no one who isn’t blood, but I trust that they want peace as much as we do.” 

			
So, on that fine morning, Charlie took a turn Dominic didn’t expect and headed towards the Baxter Bridge, which spanned the Arkansas River. It was not the way to the plant. Dominic didn’t dare ask or question it. If Charlie was of a mind to tell him, he would. Not that it didn’t make Dominic nervous.

			
What Charlie hadn’t told his bodyguard, of course, was that he was to meet Pete La Dona.

			
Charlie was feeling very confident that a truce was in the offing. But since he had sworn omerta—silence—to his father, he hadn’t told Dominic about the meeting. 

			
As they drove onto the bridge, Charlie said, “You know, Dom, I might be the luckiest buckaroo north of the Mississippi.” His grin was as wide as the brim of his spotless, white hat. “My wife is one hunka woman. Isn’t she?”

			
Dominic nodded his head in agreement. What was he supposed to do—tell him the truth? If the guy was blind with lust, he was blind with lust. 

			
“Did you see her on the porch this mornin’? What woman could nurse and be a gorgeous hot tomata’ at the same time?”

			
Dominic almost said under his breath, well not that one, but instead said, “Yes, a rare talent in a woman.” He silently prayed to Jesus that those wouldn’t be his last words because then for sure, he’d go straight to hell.

			
Charlie nodded and got that look in his eye. “I think I’ll go home for lunch today.” 

			
“OK,” Dominic answered without making a face. He was thinking, though, Jesu Crista Maria! With the war raging, the last thing a man should be thinking about is going home and getting laid in the middle of the day. Everybody else was worried about being ambushed, and he was worrying about a lunchtime are una cosina velo?—a little quickie.

			
As their car traveled the north side of the bridge, another car was heading towards them on the south side. Dominic sat up straighter and cocked his rifle. “Are those the La Donas?”

			
Charlie squinted in the sun. “I think so.”

			
Dominic shot him a look. 

			
“OK, between us. My father has been speaking to them about a truce. It’s good. They want to make peace, we want to make peace. We’re on our way to meet them. But I don’t know why they’re traveling in the opposite direction though.” Not concerned about it, he patted Dom’s knee. “Don’t worry. I’m in control.”

			
“Don Vito approved this?”

			
“Si. He wants the bloodshed to end.”

			
“You trust them?” 

			
“I trust them as far as I can throw a grenade,” he joked, “but we believe they will keep their word on this, yes.”

			
Dominic looked at him like he’d lost his mind. As the cars were getting closer to one another, Dominic said, “Charlie, I don’t know about this.”

			
“I do,” Charlie laughed, stopping the car. Then he reached into the back seat for his Tommy Gun. He patted it and said, “I don’t have a grenade, but John T. Thompson here is just as good.” He paused. Then, “I mean if things go south.” 

			
Charlie saw the look on Dominic’s mug and said, “Don’t worry, paisan. You worry too much.”

			
What happened next—what started it—is up for argument. The way it finished isn’t. The La Donas’ idea of a peace offering was for them to jump out of the car, guns blazing. Or at least that was one recounting of the story by witnesses. The La Donas on the other hand, claimed that Charlie and Dominic were hiding on the far side of the bridge on the banks of Arkansas River and had ambushed them. There was never a deal pending.

			
The whole thing smelled like week-old fish. The only one who would—and did—buy that story was Robert Roberts Jr. 

			
Whichever way it went down, it’s known that Charlie and Dominic did take refuge at some point behind their Ford, shooting the La Dona car so full of holes it looked like the La Donas’ story sounded. 

			
Charlie and Dominic, guns blazing, made it to the riverbank on the north side of the Arkansas river. The gunfight between the Carlinos and the La Donas lasted so long—some say it might even be the longest gunfight in Colorado history—that Pete La Dona was able to hop back in his car, tear back to his farm in Vineland, pick up what reinforcements he had on hand, and call a La Dona soldier, Frank Bacino. He ordered Bacino and his brothers to tear ass to the battle site loaded with ammo and guns. 

			
Hours after the gunfight started, the La Donas, freshly locked and loaded with the Bacino stash, hit Dominic. Hard. He fell back on a stump and bled out. Outnumbered, outgunned, and probably out of bullets, Charlie retreated. He slid down the riverbank and crawled until he found a section of the Arkansas River where it was shallow enough to enter. 

			
Just as he got his footing, a barrage of bullets hit him square in the back, throwing him facedown into the raging river. That beautiful boy, that new father, that Charlie, the dandiest fella to ever walk the streets of Pueblo, was dead. 

			
His shot-up body floated away downriver, graceful as a reed.

What Do We Do About Carlotta?

			
Pueblo, Colorado

			
September 10, 1923

			
Callie swore that as Charlie and Dominic pulled away that morning, she heard a hen crow.

			
Maria had taught her kids that when a hen crows, someone in the family is going to die, so you had to kill it immediately to destroy the bad omen. But that morning, Callie didn’t feel like running after the blasted bird and wringing its neck, so she figured she’d ask Charlie to do it when he came home. Charlie would for sure love to have roasted hen, so maybe they’d have it tomorrow.

			
That night though, she baked thin prosciutto slices in the oven until they were crisp as Sunday morning, which she’d sprinkle over pasta when they sat down to supper. Everything was set and ready to go—water was boiling, table was set with wine and bread. So where the hell was Charlie? She was annoyed—or as annoyed as she could get at her man—because it was already past six. After that morning’s loving, he stretched those perfect, muscled arms of his, leaned down, and kissed Callie, telling her he couldn’t wait to get home for supper and more of the same. 

			
But now he was late. She just hoped Charlie hadn’t gone and stopped off at Bratton’s for a snootful and a round or ten of poker. 

			
At around 6:30, when she heard a car pull up, she ran to the front porch to greet him, leaving baby Carlotta screaming in the cradle.

			
But what the hell? That wasn’t Charlie’s car, and neither were any of the ones behind it, so she ducked back inside, locked the door, and grabbed a six-shooter from the drawer in the kitchen table. 

			
The La Donas! Or maybe the frigging prohibition agents? Damn!

			
Hiding in the bedroom with the baby, she couldn’t see who the men were, but she could hear them walking up the front steps. 

			
Charlie—where the hell was Charlie? Why wasn’t he home protecting her? Where the hell was Mickey? Wasn’t it her damned job to keep intruders away and keep her and the baby safe?

			
When she heard the knocks on the door, she thought maybe she could climb out the little bedroom window and run with baby Carlotta to safety. But the window was too small. 

			
From the porch she heard, “Callie, it’s Joe. Please open the door.” Why was her brother here? Had he, God forbid, been captured by the La Donas who were going to kidnap her too?

			
Mickey called out, “Callie. You need to open the door. The whole family is here.”

			
“What? Why is the family here?”

			
Then Maria spoke, “Figghia mia, pi favuri…”—We have something to tell you…

			
Callie’s heart actually stopped beating for a second. She couldn’t breathe. If she opened the door, she knew she would never be able to close it again. Her beautiful life would be ruined. So, she didn’t. She cowered in the bedroom, holding the baby in the crook of her left arm with the six-shooter shaking in her right hand.

			
Maybe the La Donas had captured the whole family, and they wanted to take her and the baby too? Even she knew her thinking was ridiculous, but she couldn’t, just couldn’t, think about the other possibility.

			
“Charlie—are you with them?” she called out.

			
At that point, someone put a key in the door, and people walked solemnly into the tiny house. 

			
Maria and Laura knocked on the bedroom door and then walked in. As soon as Callie saw them, she began to wail, rocking back and forth, still holding the baby and the gun. 

			
It couldn’t be! No. It wasn’t. It couldn’t. But she knew it was. Charlie was dead.

			
Laura took the baby, and Maria tried to take the gun, but Callie put it up against her temple. 

			
“Ti pregu Calandra, a picciridda avi bisognu di so matri…”—Please Calandra, the baby girl needs her mother—Maria begged. “I need you. Please.”

			
“And I need Charlie,” she screamed. “Where is Charlie?” Then she passed out, dropping the gun on the floor. 

			
When she revived, everyone was surrounding her. “Where is my Charlie?” she cried. 

			
“We haven’t found him yet,” said Joe. 

			
“Well, why are you all actin’ like he’s dead then?” she screamed.

			
“Because we know that for sure. I’m sorry, Callie, but it’s the doggone truth.” 

			
The family gathered ’round her and tried to explain what they knew, with everyone filling in bits of the story as Callie became more and more hysterical. 

			
Charlie’s body hadn’t been found yet, they said, but they’d found Dominic’s remains. What they knew about Charlie was that he’d been shot and killed, and his body was somewhere in the river. 

			
There was no hiding the truth from her the next day when the ranch hands found Charlie’s body a few miles downstream from where he’d fallen. 

			
When the men first saw his corpse, they were thrilled. It looked like Charlie was alive and bobbing up and down to stay afloat. But when they got close to him, they saw instead that his fancy jacket had got snagged on a branch. It was the rough water that was causing him to move about. Damn! Poor, dead Charlie looked like a dancing zombie. 

			
The men brought his bloated body over to T.J. Maggiore Funeral Parlor where Signor Maggiore drained him and fixed him up as best as he could. He didn’t tell the family that the once-beautiful Charlie been shot more than twenty times. In the back. A cable was sent to Mariano in Denver by Don Vito, so he made it home in time for the wake. Not because he cared one way or the other about Callie or even Charlie but because he wanted to show Don Vito he was more than just a loyal soldier. And no one dared ask how he even knew about the murder of his son-in-law. 

			
But to Mariano’s annoyance, Don Vito seemed to be paying more respect to Maria than to him at the funeral parlor.

			
How can that be? She’s just a woman, Mariano thought as he knelt at the coffin.

			
Hundreds and hundreds of folks packed into the funeral home to wake Charlie. Even Peter got up from his bed at his mama’s house where he was recovering to attend and take his place with his family. 

			
If you didn’t know better, it looked like a wedding for the dead with the Carlinos sitting on one side and the Barberas on the other with Charlie laid out in front. Callie was dressed head-to-toe in a black lace dress with a black embroidered veil covering her face. The veil looked just like the white one she wore the day she and Charlie married. 

			
She sat in the front row with Charlie’s mother and father, Cologera and Vito. It is hard to say who was more hysterical, mother or wife. Callie kept going up to the open coffin to brush imaginary dust off of Charlie’s white tuxedo or to kiss his cold, cold lips. Every time she did that, though, she got hysterical all over again.

			
Vito remained calm and accepted the hands and kisses of all the men who came to pay their respects to him, offering their condolences and pledging their loyalty.

Never had anyone seen grief like that, even among these folks who’d already suffered so much loss. Callie went so far as to dress baby Carlotta, who was just ten days old, in her christening dress, which she had dyed black.

“I won’t raise her,” she said calmly.

“Do you not understand? I don’t want to be her mother.”

			
“But what do you mean?” Maria demanded and tried to hand the poor little baby back to its mother. 

			
Callie dropped her arms so she wouldn’t have to hold her. “I will go to Skenekeedy in New York and live with Angelina and Arthur. I can’t be here.” 

			
“You think you can just go to this Skeeneky or whatever it’s called and live with them like you don’t have a child?” Maria shouted.

			
“Yes, I can, and that’s what I’m gonna do.”

			
“Does Angelina know this?” Maria demanded. 

			
Callie tidied her bun and said, “I sent her a cable.”

Maria had tears starting up out of love for Mickey’s kindness, but at the same time, her mouth was scowling in disgust for Callie’s selfishness. 

			
Maria looked at Mickey and said, “She is my granddaughter, and I will raise her up as my own.”

			
Then, turning as cold as she’d only been when dealing with rival mobsters, Maria held the swaddled baby closer and said, “Go to New York, Calandra. Never come back. You are not welcome here, and you are no longer my daughter. Joe will take you to the train station in the morning. Get packed.” 

			
With that, she took baby Carlotta and walked out with her other daughters stalking after her. Carlotta began to cry. Maybe that infant girl knew right off that she was not wanted and would never really belong anywhere with anyone.

			
 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 34 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 34 –

			
Oh No Little Flo!

			
Pueblo, Colorado

			
September 12, 1923 

			
The Pueblo Times 

			
Bootlegging Black Hand Murders

			
By Robert Roberts Jr. 

			
Bodies of immigrant Italian mobsters are falling like grapes from their illegal bootleg vines.

			
On Monday, a two-hour gunfight between the Black Hand Carlino family and the La Dona family, who are not known to be Black Hand or in the bootlegging business, took place on the Baxter Bridge.

			
Carlo “Charlie” Carlino, who was married to a daughter of bootlegging assassin Mariano “Marian the Barbarian” Barbera, was killed, and his body was taken by the current downriver, where it was found hanging on a tree. The body of his armed bodyguard, Dominic Ingo, was found slumped behind a tree stump.

			
This reporter has it from a witness to the event that both the Carlino car and the car carrying Pete and John La Dona, and friend, Carlo Valenti, were passing on the bridge in opposite directions at the same time. For no apparent reason, the cars both stopped and Carlo Carlino and Ingo, and possibly another armed assailant, jumped out of their sedan and started firing their Tommy guns. 

			
Another account has Carlino and Ingo hiding in the marshes waiting to attack the La Donas.

			
Because the Carlino mobsters were not decent shots, only the La Dona car got hit, and the La Donas walked away with nary a scratch.

			
Carlo Carlino, who fancied himself an all-American cowboy even though he was just an immigrant son of a bootlegger, was known for his feminine attire such as fur chaps, white hats, and pure-silver spurs. 

			
His union with Calandra “Callie” Carlino née Barbera, was said to be the result of an arranged marriage uniting two of the most vicious Black Hand families in order to form one larger criminal enterprise.

			
The La Dona brothers have made it known that Charlie Carlino had recently made several visits to their house trying to convince them to join him in extorting money from poor Sicilian immigrants—their own countrymen—via Black Hand threatening letters.

			
Pete La Dona told this newspaper, “I would rather beg than do that.”

			
It is not known at this time whether the La Dona brothers and Valenti will stand trial. 

			
When this reporter interviewed townsfolk about the murders, the sentiment was unanimous: “Two less filthy immigrants to worry about.” 

			
***

			
The article had a photo of Charlie’s body being carried into the funeral home, covered by a bloody blanket but with his face exposed. His eyes were open, and so was his mouth in what sure looked like a silent scream. 

			
The morning the article ran in The Pueblo Times, baby Carlotta’s wails woke little Flo at 4:00 a.m. She noticed that Blue wasn’t at the foot of her bed—night hunting, she figured. So, Flo walked into her mother’s room and saw Blue at the foot of the cradle instead. Not night hunting but protecting the baby. Flo watched Maria open a can of condensed milk, which was how the motherless infants got their milk when a wet nurse couldn’t be found. 

			
“Good girl, Blue,” Flo said, kissing baby Carlotta and her mother. Then she smacked the side of her leg for Blue to get up and follow. They wandered out to the front porch to see what adventures they could get up to.

			
She saw someone had tossed what looked like an old, rolled-up newspaper onto the front steps. Flo was forbidden by Maria to look at newspapers since Robert Roberts Jr. had written the awful story with the photo of shot-up Peter, so of course, Flo being Flo, she picked it right up. She wanted to get a sneak peek before Maria caught her. It was barely light yet, so she lit the porch lantern. 

			
When she saw the photo and read the story about Charlie, she kept herself from screaming and waking everyone up. She thought this was even worse than the one Roberts Jr. had written about Peter. In fact, this was the worst thing ever to be in a newspaper. Flo was enraged. 

			
It was, she said to herself, time to take things into her own hands. Well, her hands and Blue’s paws that is. That child, she was the only hell her mama ever raised.

			
She pulled on her button-top shoes and headed into the woods. 

			
“Blue, it’s time to do what I said I’d do. Whaddaya think?”

			
Blue just looked at her. Since they had their own secret way of communicating, Flo took that as a yes.

			
“Blue, find what we hid.” Her wolf led her right to the exact spot where she’d hidden the thing she was forbidden to touch. It was what she’d snuck out of the barn that day Helen had shooed her out of the house.

			
Flo then went to the corral looking for Black Bill’s cousin, Silas, before the men headed out.

			
Silas always had a soft spot for Flo and her wolf. Figured she knew more about the natural world than most folks twice her age. He’d even sneak her penny candy he bought whenever he went to town. She loved him. She could trust him.

			
So, when she asked him for Roberts Jr.’s address, saying she wanted to write him a letter telling him that he was being mean to her family and it wasn’t nice, he thought that was about the sweetest thing he’d ever heard.

			
But Silas told her he had a better idea. “Well, Little Flo,” he said, “I think it would be a might bit better if you write that letter to the editor of the newspaper, instead of that no-account reporter man.”

			
“But why,” she wanted to know.

			
“That way they’ll print it.”

			
“Oh no, I can’t do that. Mama said I’m not allowed to do anything that will get our name in the newspaper.”

			
He thought it was damned sweet of the child to want to write to that sumnabitch and tell him to leave her family alone. It nearly brought tears to his eyes.

			
“I promise I won’t tell nobody, Silas.”

			
“OK, then—it’s our secret. You know that log house? The one half a mile from here with the cottonwood out front?” She nodded her head. “That’s where he lives.”

			
“Thanks, Silas,” she said innocent as can be. Then, “Oh, he doesn’t have a dog or a wife, does he?”

			
Silas laughed. “Little lady, even a starving dawg wouldn’t live with that sidewinder if he was offerin’ it a side of beef.”

			
Good, she thought. 

			
Later that day, when Robert Roberts Jr. didn’t report into work, his editor, Franklin Moss, rode over to his house. He was mad as a hornet that Roberts Jr. had missed his deadline.

			
He was ready to give him hell, but when he got to the house, Roberts Jr. was already in hell. Somehow, he’d got his leg caught in a small animal trap that somebody must have dropped by mistake right in front of his gate. 

			
Moss managed to get Roberts Jr. into his car, with the reporter still attached to the trap, screaming and crying, and drove him to Doc Dawson’s. The doctor had to ask for help getting the damned thing off Roberts Jr.’s leg, not only because it was on tight, but also because the patient was carrying on like a little girl, so it was hard to hold him still.

			
Roberts Jr. should have just held still. All that carrying on caused the rusty metal trap to cut further into his leg, which caused a terrible infection. It took a month to heal, but that reporter with the gift of gab was left with a mouth that didn’t work anymore. Lockjaw, they call it. 

			
Too bad he hated the Barberas, or he could have called on Maria and her famous moldy bread cure. That would have fixed him up right quick so he could have kept on asking questions that were none of his business.

			
Nobody ever did find out who was so stupid and careless enough to drop an animal trap right in front of somebody’s gate. Those things cost good money.

– 35 –

			
You Pay the Hit with a Hit

			
(Aeschylus, 535–456 BC)

			
Trinidad, Colorado

			
September–October 1923

			
Mariano felt that, even though he’d delivered the goods for Don Vito on rival families—quick like the Pony Express—the Don hadn’t shown him the respect and gratitude due him in front of everyone at the wake. 

			
It never occurred to him that no one knew, or more importantly could know, of their secret arrangement. Or that, if the head of the Carlinos had shown any kind of public gratitude to Mariano, it would have made all the mourners wonder why he was suddenly grateful to a man who had shown himself unworthy of respect.

			
Mariano gave no thought to the fact that the man was grieving the loss of his golden son. He just knew he’d done such a great job, he should be treated like a trusted ally. 

			
Marian the Barbarian would have to show his loyalty in a bigger, more obvious way. He had to do what others, including his manly daughter, had failed to do—he would kill a La Dona son.

			
An eye for an eye, a son for a son, a hit for a hit.

			
Mariano found out before he left Denver that the La Donas had a new truck depot in Trinidad. So, after the funeral, he had his son Joe drive him to Trinidad.

			
“You be sure,” he told Joe, “to let it be known only to Don Vito that I was the one who revenged the death of Carlo.”

			
“Don’t do this, Papa. It will be bad for us. You will make things worse. Please.”

			
“Go home,” he answered, dark as coffin air. Joe’s words had fallen on deaf ears. Mariano ran from the car like a man on fire.

			
Mariano crept up to a spot near the truck depot behind a thick grove of pines. As soon as Billy La Dona came out laughing and joking, Mariano took one clean shot and young La Dona went down. He died on the spot.

			
Mariano ran all the way to the train station and hopped the 10:15 morning train to Denver as it was pulling out of the station.

			
He felt very good about himself. Even after all that time gambling and whoring in Denver, he still was the crack shot he always had been. Now if he could only keep his mouth shut about what he’d done.

– 36 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 36 –

			
The Get Out Get Together

			
Barbera Ranch, Colorado 

			
October 1923

			
One fine, crisp morning Blue started howling aggressively, and Flo and Clara stopped riding their ponies and watched as big, black cars headed their way. This was something that had never happened at the ranch before, so they rode as fast as the wind back up to the house to tell Mama. 

			
They hadn’t known why their mama had kept them home from school that day, but now they figured something big—or something bad—was happening.

			
“Mama! Mama!” Flo yelled as they approached the porch, her eyes glued to the dirt drive that led up to their house. “The biggest, blackest, longest car I’ve ever seen, and two other black cars, just turned into our property right under the Double Bar A sign! Hurry Mama! Hurry,” she hollered, running up the steps and pulling open the screen door to stick her head in.

			
Instead of asking what all the fuss was about, they saw their mama frantically managing the older girls at the stove. She was wearing her best calico dress and had her hair done up in a high bun with sparkly hair pins. Flo and Clara’s older sisters had their hair piled high, too, and even though they were dressed to the nines, they were busy cooking up a storm. 

			
Huh?

			
And their brothers—Joe, Austin, and Peter too? Why were they dressed in their Sunday best and running around like headless hens? Peter was still hobbling on a crutch, but even he was trying his best to look normal.

			
They heard the commotion in the barn as the ranch hands rushed out, dusting off their pants with their hats and slicking down their hair with spit. They lined up shoulder-to-shoulder in front of the house. None of them were even packing guns. 

			
Clara poked Flo and tilted her head towards the back pasture. “Holy Juan cannoli,” Flo said. The table for twenty had been set out there with Mama’s good, embroidered tablecloth, linen napkins, and the best dishes—the ones she bought down at Handle’s Dry Goods in town. The table was so loaded down with bottles of Barbera rosso and bianco wine, baskets of fresh-made bread, whole cheeses, giant platters filled with dried meats like prosciutto and pepperoni, and little dishes at every place setting filled with garlicky olive oil, that if that table hadn’t been made of good, sturdy barn board it would have been sagging under the weight.

			
All Maria would tell the little girls is, “We’re having special company. You go wash your faces, and change into your good dresses. And tie Blue up. Right now!”

			
Tie up Blue? Flo had never tied her up. She thought about it for a second just because her mama had said to do it, but then decided that no, she wasn’t about to tie Blue to a post. No way, no how.

			
They went inside to wash up and change as the cars pulled up. Maria and the grown-up children hurried outside to greet the passengers with such obvious awe, it was like King Victor Emanuel III of Italy himself had come to pay the Barberas a visit.

			
Flo and Clara, peeking out the window, watched as the first two men stepped out of the long, black car holding Tommy guns. Then another man rushed over to hold the door open even though it was already open, and Don Vito stepped out. Finally, a tall man, probably over six feet, with blubber lips and eyes as cold and black as a dog’s nose stepped out. 

			
“He’s wearing his coat over his shoulders like it’s a cape or something,” Flo remarked. 

			
They saw as Maria nearly curtseyed. “Benvenuto a casa nostra, Signor Capone,” she said quietly, adding, “E a te, come sempre Don Vito.” 

			
Not even the big sisters knew who the tall man was, so cranky Helen whispered to Joe, “Why is Mama making such a big fuss over the man with Zio Vito.” The other sisters leaned in to hear the gossip.

			
“Why? That’s Scarface! Signor Al Capone, that’s why. Head of the Chicago Outfit.” He answered as though that meant something to a bunch of his cowgirl sisters in dresses.

			
As they stood there not moving a muscle, more men with machine guns came out of the other cars, and finally, the last man came out, his head down like he was ashamed.

			
Mariano!

			
They all moved to the table out in the pasture, Maria and the older girls poured wine as the guests enjoyed the first dish—cheeses and dried meats with bread.

			
Next, they served up the pasta course with Sunday gravy and passed around bowls of braciole and tripe that had been cooked in the gravy.

			
Signor Capone was the first to finish. He patted his stomach, turned to address Maria, and asked her to sit next to him. She sat down beside him, and he said, “From what I hear of you, Signora Barbera, you deserve a seat at the table with the men.” She looked at her husband, who kept his head down.

			
Capone rapped his knuckles for quiet, and the men at the table instantly fell silent. “Your family has been a good producer, and you have been a good soldier for Don Vito. The only woman to have such an honor.

			
“But I came here to tell you that the two unsanctioned hits against the La Donas—deserved or not—cannot go unanswered. I will tell the La Donas the same thing.” 

			
He nodded to Don Vito who continued, saying, “I ordered that this war between the families had to stop. Your family refused to honor my command on this. I told you it would be bad for business—and it has been.”

			
Capone added, “Mariano Barbera and others we know disrespected those wishes,” he said, deliberately disrespecting Mariano by not addressing him directly.

			
“Your husband killed Billy La Dona in an unsanctioned hit. Now the war we didn’t want is officially on, even though we can’t afford one. We cannot have the law shutting down suppliers as a result of the hotheaded actions of one man and one of your reckless sons.”

			
Apparently, even Capone didn’t know Mickey was a woman. “Now the La Donas will seek revenge again on you,” Capone warned. “I don’t want this to happen. Don Vito doesn’t want this to happen.”

			
Then, pausing for impact, he said in a loud bellowing voice, “You need to leave Colorado.”

			
Everyone sat stunned. Finally, Maria spoke, “Signori, with all the respect due both of you, please! My family worked like slaves in the mines, then we were starving without a home, and then we worked day and night to make this ranch. We employ many Italians, and we produce fine wine, and the Sugar Beet drink that you yourself buy. This is our home.”

			
“I know how hard you’ve worked, Signora, but your family refused to follow orders and have brought this on yourselves.”

			
“Can we work out a peace with the La Donas? That’s what my son-in-law Charlie—Carlo—was on his way to do. Make peace. Then he was ambushed by the La Dona sons and shot like a dog, and his body thrown in the river!”

			
“There are different versions of what happened that day, Signora Barbera,” Capone answered waving his hand in the air dismissively. “It’s no matter now. His death was one of many—too many—murders between your families. You are more of a danger to each other than the Klansmen prohibition agents.”

			
“I give you my solemn word—” Maria began but was cut off.

			
“I’m sorry, Signora Barbera, but that’s not good enough. You will locate in Brooklyn, New York, in a Sicilian neighborhood called Red Hook where your family will run a fruit and vegetable store. You will handle the movement of money from our New York operations through your business.”

			
“But the La Donas…?”

			
“You can’t be here anymore. Not now, maybe not ever. I don’t know. I do know that it’s not safe for the women and children.”

			
Mariano gave a sigh of relief. “So, I can stay and run things here?”

			
Capone jumped up from his seat in a rage. “Va Fangul!” he yelled, his fists balled up on the table. “You would let your wife and children live in Brooklyn, New York, alone? Unchaperoned by the head of the family?” 

			
Mariano sputtered. “I just meant—”

			
Capone lifted his hand and slammed it on the table. “It is those kinda reckless, disrespectful ideas that have caused all the problems in our businesses. Capisti?”

			
It was right about then that the back door swung open, and Clara, Flo, and Blue came running out to the table. Blue wasn’t tied up, but she did have a long rope around her neck which Flo was sort of holding. They bounded to the table, making the already-nervous guests jump ten feet in the air. 

			
Maria shouted, “Firenze! I told you to tie Blue up!”

			
“She is tied up,” Flo answered, leading Blue right to them. Capone’s thugs did what came naturally and pulled their guns. Even Capone, who tried to show no fear, reached for his gun.

			
“Whatchya all doin’?” Flo asked Capone, bending down, and hugging her wolf like a mama. “She’s friendly. Her name is Blue, and you can pet her. My name is Flo, and this here’s my little sister. Her name is Clara. What’s your name Mister, and why do you have such a big car?”

			
Capone didn’t pet Blue but, without taking his eyes off the wolf, reached into the big bowl of meat, picked up a chunk of braciole that was dripping red gravy, and threw it to Blue. He was hoping the wolf would chase after it and eat it instead of eating him. Blue didn’t go for it and instead moved closer to Capone to get a sniff of him. Capone jumped up, knocking his coat off his shoulders and onto the ground.

			
“Jesus, Mary, and Joseph,” he screeched. “Don’t your ah, ah, dog like meat?” 

			
This caused Flo to laugh out loud. “Of course she likes meat. She’s a wolf! A Sicilian wolf, so she loves braciole best of all.”

			
Scared but calming down and almost charmed, Capone, who was more clever than most, could see that the females in this family were fearless, smart, and even funny.

			
He took a breath and, with one eye on Blue, continued what he was saying before, but now, wanting to get the hell away from the wolf, he wrapped it up quicker than everyone expected. 

			
“Anyways, as I was saying, Signora Barbera, you can decide who will run this spread. The Carlinos will take full control of your liquor business and all the money from it. You are out of the bootleg.” 

			
“So, we can keep the ranch?” Maria asked hopefully. Neither Capone nor Don Vito said anything. She took that to mean they still owned it, but they just couldn’t live on it. 

			
One of Capone’s men picked up his boss’s topcoat, dusted the Colorado dirt off of it, and draped it back on his boss. Capone squared his shoulders and got back in his big, black car. 

			
Flo, who wasn’t done talking to the stranger, knocked on the window. Capone rolled it down. “Hey Mister, why do you wear your coat on your shoulders instead of inside your arms?”

			
He rolled the window back up, but she could hear laughing and talking inside. She heard him say, “That kid with the lips and the wolf is a pain in the ass! Too bad she’s a girl. Smart as hell.” 

			
They sped off, leaving everyone—except Flo—scared and scratching their heads. 

			
“Nice feller,” little Flo said with her hands on her hips. “Scaredy cat, but a nice feller anyways.”

– 37 –
		

	
	
		

			
 – 37 –

			
The Double Bar A Double Take

			
Pueblo, Colorado 

			
October 11, 1923

			
Every time Maria thought about never coming back to her homestead in Colorado, she burst into tears. Even though Don Vito had told her not to worry and that he had friends of all kinds in New York who would always watch out for her, she was worried.

			
If anybody had told her back in 1910 that the place she once cursed, the place she had hated with its black holes of coal, the place where she’d birthed and buried babies, the place where they’d nearly starved and frozen to death, would turn out to be the place she never wanted to leave, she would have said they were crazy.

			
She and her family had built not just a ranch from nothing, they’d built a life—a life where she became who she never imagined she could be. She was a boss, a businesswoman, a ruthless protector, the mother of a strange band of children, and a woman without the strings of a husband holding her down. She wasn’t “just a wife.”

			
As she had grown in stature, she came to understand that being “just a wife” was maybe not even what she had wanted all those years ago when she first saw Mariano back in Lucca Sicula. Maybe “freedom”—a word not in a Sicilian woman’s book back then—was what she had always really wanted.

			
Whether that was true or not, it’s what she got in this wild new country, and she loved it. She had married Mariano when she was a young girl in love. Now, she was a woman in charge. She feared that in Brooklyn, New York, she’d be forced to go back to her old life as the wife of a mean bastard—but even worse this time. She no longer had any love for her husband, and yet, she and their kids would all be living together with him in a big, filthy, strange city. Signor Capone had told her she’d be handling the money, yes, but he was in Chicago, and she would be in New York. 

			
Worse, what she remembered about this New York City was the horror of Ellis Island. The screaming, dirty people, the brown air, the disrespect she was shown because she was what one officer there called “another filthy wop immigrant.” She didn’t know what it meant back then, but she kept the words in her head so when she learned to speak English, she’d learn the meaning of them. She did learn, and she’d shown them all just what a filthy wop immigrant could become.

			
But now, after all they had built in the Wild West, she would go back to where she started in the new world: New York City. Worse: Brooklyn, a village inside New York City. Would she be just another filthy WOP immigrant in that terrible place?

			
It was Mariano who had brought them so low. She refused to blame Mickey in any way, even though she had been the one to attack the La Donas’ home. She knew Mickey did it out of love for her brother, but Mariano did it only to prove to the bosses that he was a big man. It was the final straw.

			
She cried over every item she packed and every item she had to leave behind. Mariano had been ordered to go on ahead to Brooklyn to find them a place to live. She imagined a house; he found a walkup apartment. He sent her a cable with the address: 151 Union Street in Brooklyn, leaving the packing and travel part for her to figure out. 

			
More important to figure out was who would be left in charge of the ranch.

			
A week before they left, she gathered the family and the ranch hands together out by the corral. A storm was brewing, so everybody—men, women, and children—had bandanas covering their faces. Even the hands who had been out securing fences and putting equipment away rode back in. 

			
They all knew there was a storm brewing in their lives too. 

			
As always, the cowboys were leaky mouths, and the stupid talk had been flying like sage brush in the wind. Who would stay, who would go to Brooklyn, who would decide to join a cattle drive and make their own way in the world? 

			
Maria had thought long and hard about who was most fit for the job of running the ranch and which family members were targets that the La Donas would aim for. No doubt, her boys, who were now men, would be shot and killed if they stayed. She removed her bandana to make her announcements. 

			
They were all expecting big changes, sure, but when she said Mickey would be left in charge with the help of One-Eye Walnut Jack and Antonella, everybody was stunned nonetheless.

			
Cranky Helen stepped up and pulled down her bandana to talk. “Mama! I’m gonna stay.”

			
“No, you will not,” Maria said, shaking her head. “You don’t know how to herd, you never learned your way around a gun, and you are without protection. Michelina will be too busy to be your bodyguard.”

			
“I won’t be without protection,” Helen shot back.

			
The whole bunch looked at her. Who would be her protector?

			
“What do you mean?” Maria asked, losing patience with her most disagreeable daughter.

			
“Silas,” Helen answered. “We want to stay and raise a family together.”

			
If eyes could pop out of heads, the ground around that corral would have looked like an eyeball floor. Everybody was dead quiet for a second, and then the whole bunch started talking at once.

			
“Silence!” Maria shouted, putting her hand up. “You can’t marry Silas, and that’s final.”

			
Cranky Helen stuck her face right up in Maria’s and said, “Why? Because he’s a Negro? You’re worse than the white mine owners who hate Italians.” 

			
Maria shook her finger in Helen’s face and said, “No, you stupid girl. It’s not because he’s a Black man. It’s because he’s thirty years older than you! You’d end up a nursemaid, not a wife.”

			
“Too late to worry about that,” Helen said, wiggling her ring finger which had a gold ring on it. “We got married yesterday.”

			
Peter pulled his bandana down, dust be damned, and spit out, practically choking on his words, “You got married without Ma’s permission? You ain’t even old enough to get legally hitched.”

			
“This isn’t the big city, brother,” Helen said as Silas came up and put his arm around her waist. “This here’s the Wild West, remember? The judge down to the courthouse married us for one dollar.”

			
As the others looked on, speechless, Silas got down on one knee in front of Maria. “Please Missus, we’d like your blessing. We want to stay on and help run the ranch and keep it safe. You know I’m the best cow herder on this whole spread. I mean ’ceptin’ Mickey of course.”

			
Maria smacked her head with her hand. “What next? Is somebody going to say they robbed a bank?”

			
That’s when Yellow Fly, who had let his braids grow down past his belt these past few years, stepped up. “Missus, I also have something to say.”

			
Maria moaned, “I need to sit down.” Walter, Yellow Fly’s brother, ran a chair over from the porch. She flopped right down in it.

			
“You know I will stay, and so will my brother,” Yellow Fly said, not moving a muscle, “but there’s something else you should know, Missus.”

			
“Jesu Cristo Maria! Is it bad news?” she asked, even though she didn’t want to know.

			
“No, it’s good news! Lucia is carrying my son.”

			
“What?”

			
“We became man and wife in the tradition of my people.” Lucia lowered her bandana, and proud as a peacock, showed everybody the four lines that were tattooed from her mouth down her chin.

			
Joe, always slow and easy, kinda shook his head and laughed low. “Well, this will be one helluva ranch family.”

			
Everybody knew what he meant. They’d made marriages of every kind—and none of them what you’d call “normal.” Woman/woman. Black/white. Ute/and whatever the hell Lucia the orphan girl was. Imagine it: a tattooed four-foot-high woman, a bald woman in man’s clothes, a man with hair longer than any woman’s, and a Negro boss, name of Smith, all running a ranch in Colorado handed over to them by “filthy WOP immigrants”.

			
“I need to think about all this,” Maria said. “I need to think.” She had figured the ranch hands would want to stay, and maybe one of her sons would beg to stay because they loved the cattle drives, loved the horses, and the outdoor-hard-work way of life. She knew she’d have to force them to leave no matter what. She knew all that. 

			
She thought she had carefully planned for the future of the Circle Double Bar A Ranch. But she hadn’t planned on any of this.

			
Maybe it is God’s will, and it will all work out. Or not.

			
 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 38 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 38 –

			
The Blue Blues

			
Pueblo, Colorado 

			
October 28, 1923 

			
The wagon carrying their goods for the train trip to New York was overloaded, but it was underloaded considering the rancher’s lives they had left behind and the city life they were facing.

			
Maria had carefully packed the good dishes she’d bought at Handle’s Emporium. She treasured them. They weren’t fine china or anything, but they were the first purchase she’d made when they finally had a little money to spare. These dishes, painted with birds and flowers, represented the good life to her and symbolized where the family had come from and what they had built.

			
She also took the embroidered linens that she and her own mother had sewn. She even packed her wine glasses. 

			
She ordered the family to leave behind whatever fancy duds they’d acquired—there just wasn’t room for everything. 

			
She thought—they all thought—they’d be back; that this Brooklyn town was just what you’d call a temporary hideout. When the families made peace, they’d be back, she kept saying over and over. 

			
If Mama said it, it must be true—right? She’d led them through the worst and the best because she was stronger than any man. She’d kept most of them alive when nature, enemies, and God Himself had tried to kill them off.

			
One-Eye Walnut Jack and Antonella stood with cranky Helen and Silas Smith, Yellow Fly, and Lucia in front of the house. They were all weeping.

			
Mickey, who was driving the wagon, and Walter, who would be driving the sedan with the family, were near hysterical as the final bundles were loaded onto the wagon. Flo and Blue squeezed in next to Mickey, and little Clara climbed on top of the bundles. 

			
“We’re going to a new adventure,” Maria said in her best broken English, holding back a mountain of tears big as the Greenhorn itself. Then, in Sicilian, “God willing, it will be good. I was very unhappy when I first came to this place Colorado. And look at how we prospered. The city could turn out good too.”

			
As the wagon lurched forward and the sedan moved out, the screams and cries from those remaining behind could probably be heard all the way to Brooklyn.

			
For the first time in maybe forever, the family began to doubt that Maria really believed what she had just said. What could be better than the big outdoors, the mountains, the woods, their horses, their cattle, their goats?

			
Laura was already mourning the beautiful dresses she’d had to leave behind. There’d never be a place to wear such finery in this Brooklyn. She’d read about it. It was crowded, dirty, and poor.

			
Joe worried that he’d have to find work as a laborer. No call for cattle drovers in New York.

			
Peter, fully healed now except for a bunch of scars on his body, figured he’d survived the worst, and he’d make the best of it in the city. Everybody loved Peter. He just assumed they’d love him in the big city too.

			
Austin wondered and worried. What would become of a man who had the fits and wasn’t able to read and write much? Would Mama always be there to protect him?

			
Little Clara was excited. New school, new friends, and new dances to learn!

			
But Flo? She was not happy. Would she and Blue have a prairie to roam in a place like a city? Would Blue have to be—God forbid—tied up so she wouldn’t run away or be run down by a sedan car? Would she be allowed to bring Blue to school with her every day? Would she have to tie her up in back of the schoolhouse? No, none of that was good. A city couldn’t be good for her and Blue.

			
The train station was crowded and full of more people than most of them had ever seen in one place before. 

			
The fellas loaded their goods onto the train, and the ladies began to board. As Flo and Blue started climbing the high steps into the train car, both Maria and the conductor, who was wearing what looked to Flo like a policeman’s uniform—and they sure didn’t trust policemen—stopped them.

			
Flo looked to her mama. What the heck is going on? Does he want Blue to go in the train car with the baggage? No way!

			
Maria bent down on the platform and hugged Flo. Why was Mama crying? Was the policeman going to arrest them?

			
“Gioia du me cori, Blue non pò veniri cu nui. Nun si ponnu puttari i lupi ‘nto trenu e nun si ponnu puttari i lupi a Brook-a-land, New York.”—My darling baby girl, Blue cannot go with us. They don’t allow wolves on the train, and they don’t allow wolves in Brooklyn, New York.

			
What is Mama saying? It is not possible that Blue couldn’t go with her. No. Not possible. Flo hugged Blue tighter like she’d never let her go. 

			
It started to dawn on her that they thought she could leave her other mother—her wolf mother—behind. 

			
Flo tried to bite the conductor’s leg, but Maria grabbed her away before the conductor knew what she was trying to do. Nor did he understand when mother and child started carrying on with each other in Sicilian.

			
“Mama!” Flo cried, reverting to Sicilian. “Would you stay behind and send your children to this Brooklyn place? Would you? Well, Blue is my mama, too, and she won’t let you take me away from her.” 

			
She paused and gave them all a positively feral look. “And I won’t let you take me from my mama or abandon her. She can’t go back to her pack. She’ll die out there. She’ll be a lone wolf!” 

			
Flo grabbed Blue’s fur and tried to run. 

			
Maria stopped her by pulling her back by the collar of her dress. She knew she couldn’t try to reason with the child because what Flo had said was the God’s honest truth. Blue had declared herself Flo’s mother on the night of her birth, which was only the first time she saved the child from death. Blue had saved both Flo and Clara from the flood and had taken a bullet for them, probably shot by a child-slaver, as they tried to make their way home. That wolf had done as much for Flo, more even, than a human mother could have done. 

			
Maria knew she’d never be able to get through to her beloved daughter, so tears poured down her face as well. Pulling Flo and Blue apart would be, as the wise child had said, like abandoning your own mother to the wild. 

			
Determined, kicking, and fighting to get her mother’s hand off of her, Flo screamed, “I’m going home then! I’ll live with Mickey and Antonella and Walnut Jack. They love me, and they love Blue, and you don’t!” 

			
Then, she broke free as the collar of her dress ripped away, and she and Blue took off running on the tracks as fast as the wind. Joe and Peter dropped what they were doing and ran after them. A train heading in the opposite direction was coming their way. Flo would be torn in half.

			
Peter caught up to them and grabbed Flo up just as the train came barreling through.

			
“Blue! Blue!” Flo screamed, but she couldn’t see her. The train was now covering the track where she and Blue had been running.

			
Flo kicked and screamed and punched Joe. “Blue is my mama and my guardian angel, and you killed her. I hate you. I want my Blue!” 

			
She was not to be comforted. Not by Joe, not by Peter. When they carried her, fighting like a wild beast, onto the train and Maria tried to hold her and comfort her as the train lurched forward, Flo kicked her mother. “I hate you! I hate Brooklyn. I hate everybody. Blue! I want my Blue. You killed Blue.”

			
Clara looked shocked and started wailing too. “Blue. You killed Blue?” she cried, echoing her sister’s words and heartbreak.

			
Despite the tight hold Maria had on her, Flo broke free and ran to the back of the train car where the couplers connected. Peter ran after her and grabbed her just before she either jumped off or had her legs crushed between the cars. 

			
Flo scrambled to the open window, but Joe took hold of her as she tried to climb out. She screamed, “Blue!” As she struggled, they saw, through the gap between the cars, the absolute impossible. Blue was not dead but was running at top speed next to the train. The wolf ran and ran until the train gathered up enough speed that even she couldn’t keep up and collapsed on the ground.

			
“Blooooo!” Flo screamed. It had been the first sound she made when Blue had come to her rescue on the night of her birth. It was the last sound she made when she abandoned her beloved Blue to what she knew, if she had lived, would be the worst fate possible: that of a lone wolf. 

			
Flo’s red lips lost all their color as she opened them to howl.

			
“Blooooooo….”

– 39 –

			
Trains, Plains, and Automobiles

			
Colorado to New York

			
October 31, 1923

			
There was no comforting that wild child. And when there was no comforting that wild child, there was no comforting Clara either. What Flo felt, Clara felt. 

			
Clara loved Blue and owed her life to her, but it was clear that Blue was Flo’s alone—like little Bella Ragazza the donkey had been Clara’s alone. Clara put her head on Flo’s shoulder and said, “I lost my own other mama in the flood, and now I lost my aunt, too, ’cause that’s what I always figgered Blue was to me. Just like Zia Cologera but with blue eyes and fur.” 

			
That got them to crying and screaming even worse than before. Flo refused to speak to Maria or eat a bite of the food her mama had brought aboard. Peter and Joe snuck the conductor money to buy food for Flo and asked him to give it to her outside of their mother’s sight. 

			
Flo was happy to eat the conductor’s food, just not her mama’s, who she had decided was the worst mother in the world.

			
They all saw it, but no one told Flo that she no longer had ruby-red lips. She was behaving wild enough without adding a new weird thing that might make her crazier. Only once did Clara touch her sister’s lips and make mention of it. “Hmmm. One day you and me will have to buy some of that lipstick we seen in Helen’s magazine,” she said.

			
Flo didn’t bother to question it. She was in mourning and didn’t give two hoots what she—or anybody else—looked like. 

			
The train trip was tough enough on everyone without the sisters crying, screaming, and carrying on all day long every day. Only baby Carlotta was quiet. That child was so good, it was like she was afraid that if she kicked up a fuss, her grandmother, like her mother before her, would abandon her. 

			
Maria felt the baby’s pain, so she’d just kiss her over and over again and whisper, “Bedda, dolce bedda.” 

			
The other two little girls were miserable, thinking their world had come to an end, and they hated being confined in the train. They had no idea that the tin lizard (as they’d heard their brothers call the train) taking them east was pure luxury compared to the human cattle car Maria and Rosaria had ridden in coming west. 

			
To distract herself from how she’d broken Flo’s heart, Maria would think about how terrible the trip from New York to Colorado had been and how their lives had changed on this trip back. Was it only fourteen years ago? 

			
Instead of fighting for a seat and sleeping on the floor with their children when a seat couldn’t be found, this train had leather seats, polished brass handrails, and wood tables. Heck, it even had a flush toilet in a little room at the back instead of a slop bucket in a slop closet. 

			
But what really bowled her over was the notion that a train could have sleeping compartments and a dining car. A whole room just for eating? Not that Maria used it. She had brought their food, because who would trust food made by strangers? She slept in her seat with a money belt stuffed under her corset and a gun in her carpet bag. There was no way on God’s green earth that she’d let some desperado sneak up on her as she snoozed in a sleeper car. Besides as every damned fool knew, it was easier to shoot a thief when you were sitting up than lying down.

			
But neither desperados trying to hold them up inside the train nor bandits trying to hold them up from the outside, turned out to be the problem. The problem was the lethal combination of her own little disperati and all the well-off American city slickers who were traveling on the same train. 

			
The men looked like they worked as managers for the mine owners back home, and the women looked like they’d spent too much time in school. Even though they looked very proper and polite, they weren’t. And they made it clear that they didn’t want to be traveling with “loud, uncivilized Eye-ties,” which was another slang that the upper classes seemed to think was hilarious. And so, they complained. About everything. 

			
Those men in their fancy suits griped to the overworked Negro conductors about how “those immigrants” (that would be the Barberas) had two filthy, kinky-haired little brats who were screaming and crying. The other group (their do-gooder wives) complained that “those little WOP girls are being walloped to within an inch of their lives by the mother.” They told the conductors to “do something to stop the beatings!” 

			
Negro conductors were not about to put white folks in their place—even if they were dark-skinned white folk. And besides, the children weren’t getting beaten. They were just upset.

			
They knew why the little sisters were carrying on. Flo had told the conductor—his name was Samuel—why they’d been crying and fussing: She was mourning her Blue. But her pet wolf wasn’t something the other passengers would have cared about, or believed, anyway. 

			
When they’d left Colorado, frost was already on the ground, but the further east they travelled, the steamier it got. Dispositions were heating up like the air around them. 

			
Peter joked to his brothers, “No wonder they call it ‘temper-ature.’”

			
Their clothes didn’t help. Men were in suits and the ladies—who had squeezed themselves into “modern” rubber girdles—were so hot they felt like they were trapped in a Turkish bath house. By the time it was nearing 100 degrees inside, tempers were shorter than Princess Wee-Wee at the carnival. Something was bound to give. And that something was Maria’s home-brought food. There were whole cheeses, salamis, and prosciutto loaves that were so big they were the size of small kids. 

			
The hotter it got, the smellier the grub got—especially the Taleggio cheese. Truthfully, yes, it does smell like cowboy socks, but to the Italians, the over-ripe cheese smelled as fine as French perfume. To the Americans, it smelled like stink on a stick.

			
After a day of the lady passengers complaining about his mama’s food, Peter decided to take matters into his own hands. He got up—his leg was all healed up by then—and walked with his long, slow stride to the back of the carriage where two of those gossiping, griping, women were seated. One looked as sour as a pickle and the other as sweet and luscious as a bottle of store-bought honey. 

			
“Which of you fine ladies been complainin’ ’bout my mother,” he asked, somehow making it sound like he was flirting. 

			
The pretty one blushed and said, “Well, don’t you look like Clark Gable!”

			
“I appreciate that ma’am, and you sure are pretty as Clara Bow the movie star yourself. Now I must say—and don’t take this wrongly—I just can’t have anybody bad-talkin’ my kin, most particularly my mama.”

			
“Well, sir,” the pretty one started in, but Peter put a long, elegant finger up to his lips to stop her from going further. 

			
“Dear ladies, I sure am so sorry about the noise my little sisters is makin’ too. But I’d have more success hollerin’ down a well than trying to keep those girls quiet. See, they ain’t bein’ beat or bein’ bad. Those poor little girls, they just lost their dog on the railroad tracks. That pup was so upset about us leaving that she chased the train until she collapsed on the track. And those poor young ‘uns are mighty, mighty sad.” 

			
The pretty one said, “Oh my. I’m so sorry about that.” And then she batted her eyelashes so fast it looked like she had dirt in her eye.

			
Peter gave her that sideways grin of his—the one he knew was a lady killer. Then he leaned in and said really low, “As for Mama, well ladies, she’s off-limits. I just don’t cotton to nobody—even two beauties like yourselves—saying anything about her that ain’t pleasin’.” 

			
He winked, and those ladies got so flustered they looked like they were about to faint. In fact, Sour Pickle did, in fact, pass out for a few seconds. He knew it wasn’t the boiling hot temperature in the carriage that brought on the fainting spell; it was his smile and bad-boy eyes. 

			
Only Peter could get away with telling two women he disapproved of their attitude while making them feel like he was making love to them.

			
It was right about then that the pretty one’s husband, who was fat as a whorehouse cat, walked over.

			
“Is there a problem young man?”

			
“No, not unless our conversation broke up your poker game,” Peter said, a twinkle in his eye as he tossed his head towards the game tables in the next car. 

			
“I’m sorry ’bout that,” he added, and everybody knew he didn’t mean one word of it.

			
Pretty Lady tried to hide her smirk behind her lace fan.

			
“I beg your pardon,” the portly must-be-mine manager said, particularly annoyed that an ignorant young man, who he assumed was fresh out of one of his company’s own mines, dared speak like that to a gentleman. 

			
Peter stuck out his hand, which Mine Manager shook like it was covered in slug slime. “Name’s Peter. Peter Barbera out of the Double Bar A Ranch in Trinidad.” He tipped his hat, “Ladies.”

			
“You don’t work the mines? You’re a cow hand?” Mine Manager asked, eyeing him suspiciously. “Yet your name is familiar—” 

			
“No. Never have worked them mines, thank the good Lord. We’re a cattle family. Sixteen hunnert head last time I looked.” 

			
Mine Manager was surprised and spit out, “But you’re Eye-talians right?” 

			
“You bet. You should join us one day for some of that real Eye-talian grub that my mama has brought.” The sarcasm was lost on those folks.

			
Even so, Peter could tell Pretty Lady was getting all smeary eyed. Without Mine Manager noticing, he gave her a sly look. 

			
Pretty Lady stood up, her silk dress rustling, setting Peter himself aflame.

			
“Darlin’, we’re just fine here,” she said to her husband, touching his sweaty face. “Young Peter was just explaining the situation with his little sisters—the ones who were making such a fuss.” She paused and moved her wet hand to her husband’s arm, sneakily drying her palm off, and said, “You go enjoy your game my love, and I’ll join you for supper later in the dining car. Six thirty?”

			
“I sure am truly sorry about all the noise, Mister, ah, I didn’t get your name,” Peter said, eyeing Pretty Lady and refusing to call her husband “sir.” 

			
Mine Manager didn’t give his name, shaking off the encounter as one of the inconveniences of travel——and headed back to his game.

			
No one knows what happened next, but Pretty Lady and Peter seemed to be missing for one whole hour. By suppertime, all the other ladies who were headed to the dining car swept by his seat, giggled, and said, “Good evening, Clark.”

			
Maria just shook her head. “You’re gonna get your coglioni shot off one of these days,” she warned in Sicilian. 

			
Peter nearly spit out his coffee and let out a guffaw that could be heard all the way back to where Pretty Lady was sitting with the ones Austin called, “The Peter Barbera Fan Club.” 

			
“Mama! Comu si dici cosi accussì?” Peter gasped.

			
She told him she didn’t learn how to neuter just farm animals in Colorado, so he’d better stop behaving like a bull or she’d do the job herself. His mother never failed to amaze and even, yes, scare him. 

			
Joe, standing nearby, tipped his ten-gallon to his brother and said, “You better mind Mama ’cause she means what she says. Who do you think was the one who decided old Wild Bull Hickock should become a steer?” He shot a look their mama’s way.

			
Peter smacked his head. “Veru, frati miu?”—Really, my brother?

			
Joe smirked and Peter instinctively grabbed his man parts. Austin came running up from his trip to the toilet, jumped in front of Peter, and whispered, but not nearly low enough, “Them ladies can see what you is doin’!” He continued in Sicilian: “Babbu! Apri l’occhi!”—Idiot! Open your eyes.

			
Maybe the ladies saw what Peter did and heard what Austin said and maybe they didn’t, but they weren’t complaining, at least not about that. Maria shook her head, raised her eyebrows, and tilted her head back towards the gaggle of married women. 

			
Despite what had gone on between Peter and Pretty Lady, not to mention all the complaining from other passengers (but not the ladies any longer), the train trip would remain an adventure the family would never forget.

			
Austin liked sitting next to his mama because, for one thing, without Blue, who’d been able to warn him a fit was coming on, he’d never know when he’d have an attack, so he wanted his mama nearby. 

			
For another, he also enjoyed looking out the window and talking with her about what they saw. He’d never been more than a few miles from home before, and the only “big” town he’d ever seen was Pueblo—which still hadn’t been fully rebuilt to its former glory by the time they’d left. 

			
They saw prairies, fields upon fields of harvested corn stalks, majestic snowcapped mountains, Indian settlements, and all that endless wildness. Animal herds roamed free, rivers ran wild, and wildflowers were still in bloom. The great swaths of undeveloped territory truly made this the land of plenty and, yet, Maria thought, they were leaving it all behind to go live in a crowded, noisy city—a place where they’d have to buy food instead of growing it. It was important to her for Austin to see, love, and appreciate it all—even from the train window—while he still could. She didn’t think her poor son would ever see these great, open spaces again.

			
Joe had seen a good part of the West already from his saddle on the cattle drives, but even to him, the scenery out the train window was sight after sight of wonder to behold. He felt that America was about the most beautiful land in the world. 

			
None of the big cities the train stopped in, like bustling Kansas City and St. Louis, Missouri, could compare in Joe’s mind to the wide-open spaces.

			
He’d seen Chicago when he was driving herds to the stockyards and was even less impressed with that city when the family changed trains there for the final leg to Grand Central Terminal in New York City. 

			
“Yeah, I’ve seen a city or two before,” he boasted, adding to his brothers, “They ain’t nothin’ but brick and noise and no sky.”

			
But to Laura, every city they passed looked like a golden paradise from the window of a train. As a bonus for her, the lady passengers often left the fashion magazines they’d already read in the dining car, and Laura read them cover to cover like they were the Bible. And to her they were—fashion bibles.

			
Flo and Clara didn’t care about magazines or the beauty of the landscapes. They only cared what they were leaving behind: their life with their beloved animals. The countryside out the windows looked to them like what they’d left, and the cities looked like places with too many people and probably too few wolves.

			
As the train got closer to New York, the conductor walked thru the cars as the sun was rising, crying, “Next stop Newark! Next stop Newark!” 

			
Maria stood up. “We’re in New York—everybody get your bags.” Before scrambling for them, the girls all looked out the window. This sure didn’t look like the New York City that Laura had shown them in a book. It looked ugly.

			
The conductor saw them pulling their suitcases down and stopped them. “No ma’am this isn’t New York, it’s Newark,” he said to Maria. It still sounded like he said, “New York” to them. 

			
“Are there two towns with the same name?” Flo asked him. 

			
“No, little girl,” he said, “We’re in New Jersey. We still have one final stop to go. This is the city of Newark, New Jersey, and next stop will be Grand Central Terminal in New York City, New York.” 

			
When the conductor heard the mine managers snickering at the family’s confusion, he, who hadn’t been treated like a human by those same men, added louder than necessary, “But I have to agree with you, ma’am. Newark…New York. They sure do sound the same—unless you been riding this route as long as I have.” 

			
The doors closed, the train rolled on, and it seemed in no time flat, that same conductor walked through the cars announcing, “Welcome to Grand Central Terminal, New York City, the grandest train station in the grandest city in the world!”
