– 40 –

			
GRAND TO NOT SO GREAT

			
New York City, New York 

			
November 1923

			
And grand that terminal was indeed, with its soaring ceilings and what seemed like millions of people rushing here and there. Maria was terrified of losing the girls in the madness, and her grip on their hands was so tight they complained she’d break their bones. The sons who were carrying the bags worried that they didn’t have easy access to their guns should trouble erupt. 

			
And out of the crowd, sure enough, trouble approached: a long, lanky man in a wide-brimmed fedora came rushing towards them. His pants and jacket were rumpled and dirty, the brim of his stained hat pulled so low it cast a shadow over his whole face. Maria had been through enough bad behavior by traveling men back on the boat and at Ellis Island to know that this skinny man with a slight limp not only looked like trouble as he pushed through the crowd causing people to back away from him, he looked downright dangerous. How right she was. 

			
She signaled for her sons to drop the bags and reach into their pockets where they each had a gun. At first no one recognized the man. All they knew was that the dirty-looking fella didn’t belong among these fancy city slickers. 

			
The man stopped, pulled his hat off, and set his mouth into a grim attempt at a grin. Mariano!

			
He no longer looked like the robust, tough, dapper cowboy that had been ordered to leave Colorado. Now he just looked tired, drawn, skinny, and probably drunk. He was at least twenty-five pounds thinner. 

			
Without women to wash, iron, and tend to his duds, he looked like half a bum.

			
But I gave him money to buy as much food as he could ever eat. Even enough to eat in risturanti every night. Why does he look like he hasn’t eaten in a month? Maria wondered. It couldn’t be good, whatever the reason. 

			
Austin, who didn’t have much of a filter, whispered to his brothers loud enough for the sisters to hear, “Jesus and Joseph. Pa looks like he’s been chewed up, spit out, and stepped on.” Flo, Clara, and Laura got to laughing out loud, but the second Maria shot them a look, they shut up tighter than Charlie Chaplin’s wallet.

			
Austin was right, of course, but it just wasn’t funny—not to Maria, anyway.

			
She had gotten a telegram from her derelict husband while she was still back in Colorado saying he’d be there at the station to meet them, but there was a wide river between what Mariano said and what he did these days. She hadn’t put a whole helluva lotta stock that he’d be there when they reached the big city. The only thing that made her feel better about it was the assurance by Don Vito, who was ten thousand percent more reliable than her husband, that Mariano would be at the station. 

			
Thank God, at least my sister married a man who is true to his word, Maria thought. 

			
Peter, Joe, and Austin took their hands out of their pockets and picked the bags back up. The girls gave weak hugs. Maria just stood stone-faced. There just wasn’t a whole lot of love between that man and his kin.

			
The family followed Mariano through the crowd and then through the big doors that opened up into the heart of New York City. 

			
“Them buildings reach into the sky,” Flo said looking up in wonder. 

			
Clara answered, shielding her eyes from the glare, “There’s so many windows. Are the buildings made of glass? How come they don’t fall down?” It was dizzying.

			
Back down on earth, Mariano led them to his pickup truck, which he’d driven from Brooklyn to Manhattan. The brothers climbed in the back to clear away the bags of potatoes, crates of gagootz, and broccoli rabe. 

			
“Where is the grass? Where are the fields?” Clara wanted to know. Mariano told her they were in a place called Central Park.

			
“Chi è chistu Central Park-a??”—What is this Central Park?—Maria asked. Mariano explained that the president of New York City put all the grass and fields in places called parks.

			
Flo piped up, “You mean like grass museums? Is the grass allowed to leave? Do they have wolves?”

			
Joe scooped her up in his arms, pulled her into the back of the pickup, flopped her down in his lap, and planted a fat kiss on her cheek. “If you ain’t the cutest thing this side of the Rockies, I don’t know who is!”

			
“Joe, is our new house in Central Park?” Clara wanted to know. 

			
“No, no it ain’t sweetie pie. No, it ain’t.”

			
Peter helped Maria, who was holding baby Carlotta, into the front seat, while the rest piled into the open bed in back. 

			
They drove along for well over an hour, until they reached Red Hook, Brooklyn.

			
It was clear to anybody with a brain that this Red Hook place was about the furthest thing you could imagine from the pastures, meadows, and fields of the Double Bar A.

			
Here were tough streets, not valleys. Instead of cows and horses in the fields, there was everything from horseless carriages to horse-drawn wagons traveling on cobblestone streets, carrying everything from food to horse manure. But this manure didn’t even have the same smell as the manure up in Colorado, which they always found to be what the perfume advertisements called “fragrant.” Clara sniffed and made a face. “This here don’t smell like horse cocky. It smells like dead people.”

			
From the mouths of babes as they say. It was easy to see—and feel—that Red Hook was a treacherous and deadly place. It was, as Maria had been told, filled with Mafiosi “families.” Don Vito had also warned her that in New York City, of which Brooklyn was only one area, Sicilians weren’t the only ones in the bootlegging business like they had been in Las Animas County, Colorado. In fact, he estimated the Italians controlled only about a quarter of the business here. Their biggest rivals were the Jewish gangs that controlled fifty percent, while the Irish had ten percent. 

			
Sometimes they cooperated with one another, and sometimes they just killed each other.

			
Every block Mariano turned onto brought fresh, new shocks. People begging, children without shoes, women and their children in rags huddled together on stoops seemingly without anywhere to go. 

			
On the bigger streets, men pushing wooden carts filled with vegetables, rags, pig carcasses, fruits, and smaller loads of manure fought for space with the horse-drawn buggies, motorized trucks, and cars. A few feral dogs were sniffing around, trying to snatch meat off the carts, but they were beaten back with sticks, which caused Flo to start screaming at the dog-beaters before bursting into tears. Wherever they could carve out space, there were kids playing Ringolevio, Kick the Can, and Potsy—games the Barberas had never seen before. 

			
Even the sidewalks were crowded. Vegetable stands, ladies pushing baby carriages, and vendors hawking pots and pans, as well as every kind of household item, and medicines guaranteed to cure everything from tuberculosis to whooping cough to rheumatism. 

			
Mariano turned onto Union Street and slowed down; everyone was hoping that this wasn’t where their new house would be, but they all felt in their guts that it most likely was. Union Street was noisy, it was crowded, and it had a murderous feel to it. 

			
Clara and Flo started to cry again. No grass, no mountains, no wolves. No open spaces, no mooing cattle, just the clomp of beaten-down horses, folks yelling to buy their wares, babies crying, and angry drivers cursing in every language at each other while blowing their horns. 

			
“Why is everybody so mad?” Flo wanted to know. 

			
“They ain’t all mad—well some of ’em are,” Peter said. “They’re just in a hurry to get someplace fast. Where? Who knows?” 

			
Laura only saw beauty. The fashions worn by the goomahs—the mistresses of local Mafiosi—dazzled her. Short silk dresses, flapper hats pulled down low, shoes with rhinestone buckles and heels so high you could touch the sky, and yes, silk stockings. 

			
“Where can I buy me some of those dresses?” Laura wanted to know and managed to duck the swift backhand of her mother. 

			
“They puttani,” Maria scolded. 

			
“Like you would know,” Laura whispered, sulking to no one in particular.

			
“I hear you say!” Maria said. “I am know puttani when I seen puttani!” She then glared at her husband who kept looking straight ahead at the road in front of him.

			
“You better be taking us to a nicer place than this,” Maria warned Mariano who said nothing in return but looked cowed. 

			
Joe whistled low, Austin looked the other way, and Peter smirked and whispered to his brothers, “Fellas, if a lady could be a sheriff in this town, her name would be Mama.”

			
 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 41 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 41 –

			
Brook-a-Land

			
Red Hook, Brooklyn, New York 

			
1923

			
Mariano stopped the truck in front of 151 Union Street. “Welcome to Red ’ook, Brook-a-land!” he said, waving his arms around in front of the tenement building that had a panini shop on the bottom floor. The sign painted in gold on the window read, “Ferdinando’s Focacceria.” Then underneath it, “Founded 1904.”

			
“Since when do we eat in a taverna?” Maria wanted to know. “I want to see our house. We have work to do. No time for sitting with your goombahs drinking wine.” 

			
Mariano looked confused. “Goombahs? No! Chista è lu novu appartamentu.”—This is our new apartment.

			
“We’re going to live in a taverna?” Flo asked, looking as confused as the rest. Even she knew that the only people who lived above taverns were what they called “fancy ladies.”

			
Clara asked, “Will those gals with painted faces who walk around in their corsettu live with us?”

			
Flo, wanting to show off that she knew all about it, piped up and said, “Clara, you mean, prustituti! That’s the real name of fancy ladies with painted faces who only wear underwear and no dresses.”

			
Maria smacked her own head. True, in Pueblo, the only taverna she knew of did have a brothel upstairs. Well, until the flood swept the place away, along with all the puttani, including her husband’s mistress. 

			
Mariano explained that Ferdinando’s Focacceria was not a taverna but a respectable ristorante serving paninis to the dock workers in the day and then early supper to families. He said their new home was upstairs. Everyone looked at each other, stunned. 

			
Furious, Maria said in Sicilian, “You took our money—the money I saved and gave you to buy us a house. Instead, you bought a building with a taverna?”

			
Mariano, now equal parts angry that he was being questioned and ashamed that he’d done what he’d done with the money, shouted back, “Brook-a-land é caru!”—Brooklyn is expensive! 

			
“Caru? It’s so filthy that they should pay us to live here!” Maria shouted back in Sicilian.

			
Mariano countered. “That’s what you said about the mines, and look how rich I made you.”

			
“You made us?” Sensing Maria’s anger, Carlotta started wailing as Maria fought back her own tears. 

			
She was reminded of the words that her beloved, murdered sister-in-law, Rosaria, had said to her when they first arrived in the filthy Colorado mining town: “Come inizi, così finisci.”—As you start, so you finish. 

			
Rosaria’s prediction back then had, in fact, turned out to be worse than any of them could have ever imagined, but in time, it became better than any of them could have hoped for. It had become a good life. A very good life.

			
That was mostly because Maria had fought to keep her children from languishing in the land where the streets were paved not with gold but with coal and blood.

			
Would she be able to do the same thing here where the streets were paved with cobblestones and also with blood? 

			
As they all stared down at the unfamiliar stones embedded in the street, Joe explained that the stones had once been what they called “ballast” that kept empty cargo ships from capsizing as they crossed the oceans en route to New York. 

			
“They came in empty and went back full to the brim with American goods,” Joe explained.

			
Like us, Maria thought. We came here empty, hoping to become full of the good things America could provide. 

			
The blood? That came from the same place as it had in Colorado: greed.

			
Time will tell, she thought.

			
The family grabbed as many of their belongings as they could each handle and climbed out of the truck. Maria took the gun from her carpet bag, laid it on the seat, and told Laura to stay with the truck and shoot anyone who tried to steal anything.

			
“But Ma!” Laura cried. “I didn’t come here to kill people.” 

			
Maria gave her the look, and Laura picked up the gun. “Right. Shoot anybody who tries to steal from us.”

			
They carried their things up the dark, rickety stairs, with the boys carrying the heaviest loads. When they reached the second-floor landing, Mariano stopped in front of a brown door that had been painted over many times. It had “2A” scrawled in pencil in the middle. They all held their breath as he inserted the key into the lock and turned it several times until the bolt gave way. 

			
The same thought was running through everyone’s mind: What is behind this door?

			
When he shoved the warped door open with all his might, the whole family was struck dumb at what little they could see of the inside.

			
“What the hell?” Peter whispered under his breath, Joe showed no emotion, and Austin started shaking. He dropped between the stair landing and the open apartment door, foaming at the mouth.

			
Maria handed baby Carlotta to little Flo, knelt next to her boy, reached into her bag, grabbed the spoon she kept in there for these incidents, and stuck it in his mouth to keep him from biting off his tongue. As Austin was shaking and foaming, another tenant on the floor came out of her apartment to gawk. She was wearing only a thin, pink silk slip, had peroxide-blonde, finger-waved hair, red painted lips, and eyebrows that were plucked thin as blades of grass.

			
Mariano started screaming in Sicilian. “Take this circus freak inside. He disgraces me. No one can know we have un figlio idiota in famiglia!”

			
Even in her frenzied state, Maria managed to say, “Who—the puttana?”

			
Maria turned to the woman and snapped, “You! Stop looking at us. Go inside before I shoot you.” 

			
The woman scurried back inside but called out from behind her door in a southern drawl, “Mario, honey. Do something!”

			
Different place, same old shit, Maria realized. It was a theme that would be repeated many times in this new Brook-a-Land place. 

			
As she tended to her son, Maria managed to say, “I’ll deal with her in a minute,” then to Mariano, “Tu sì la disgrazia, non lu nostru pòviru figghiu.”—You are the disgrace, not our poor son.

			
When Austin eventually stopped seizing, Maria got him to his feet. The brothers helped him inside, but then almost wished they hadn’t. They tried hard not to gasp as they surveyed their new home. Gasping would have only made their mama feel worse. Four rooms, nine people, including the infant, the only sink was in the tiny kitchen that had a coal stove and a small broken table with two chairs. The filthy windows had a view of endless lines of wash strung from broken-down building to broken-down building so they couldn’t even see the ground below. The toilet, Mariano explained, was down the hall, and all the tenants shared it. As for the bedrooms, he was happy to announce that there were two. He didn’t have to explain the mattresses covering the floor. They spoke for themselves.

			
Going from acres of prairie, herds of cattle, and a big ranch house to a four- room walk-up apartment in a crowded, dirty city was, for the family (and most especially Flo and Clara), like being sent to prison. Maria, who had thought she couldn’t be more disgusted with her husband after they were forced to leave Colorado, looked around that tenement and realized that disgust was a well without a bottom.

			
She had turned from a bride who couldn’t bear to have her man out of her sight to a wife who couldn’t bear the sight of him. 

			
She also knew she would have to make the best of it, just as she always had…for her children’s sake. 

			
At the same time, she feared “the best” might well be behind them. Even though she had fought against Helen staying in Colorado and marrying a man three times her age, she was now relieved that she had stayed and escaped this horror. This Brook-a-land. This New York. 

			
At least we own the building, shitty as it is, she thought. Maybe Helen and Lucia and their husbands will come to live with us once I fix the building up and get rid of the no-good tenants. 

			
She then put her hands on her hips and announced to her no-account husband that the first thing she was going to do was throw out the prostitute and take over that apartment so they’d have more room. As she headed to the door to confront the woman, Mariano stopped her. “No, you can’t throw her out,” he said, hemming and hawing as Maria ripped his arm away. 

			
“No? Just watch me,” she spit out and walked out into the hallway.

			
It took Mariano only two long strides to grab his tiny wife and spin her around, holding tighter this time so she couldn’t move. He looked first at his sons who’d come out to stop the fight, then back at her, as she struggled without success to break away.

			
Marian the Barbarian put his head down and uttered a phrase no one had ever heard come out of his mouth before:

			
“I’m sorry.” 

			
 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 42 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 42 –

			
Double Or Nuthin’ Leaves Nuthin’

			
Red Hook, Brooklyn, New York

			
Same Day

			
“The safe way to double your money is to fold it over once and put it in your pocket.”

			
—Will Rogers, Humorist/Citizen of the Cherokee Nation 

			
Maria felt her stomach drop and her heart speed up. We’ve lost it all. Again.

			
She stopped struggling, and Mariano loosened his grip, giving Maria the chance to spin around and get right up into his face. 

			
“I’m sorry?” she seethed, her eyes squinting shut, her fists at her sides, balled up in anger. She’d lived with this man long enough to know what Mariano’s first and only apology had to mean.

			
“You are sorry for what, exactly?” she spit out anyway, wanting him to admit what he’d done in front of his children. Mariano just looked down, rolling his hat around in his hands, so she demanded, “You didn’t even buy this building—did you?” 

			
He said nothing.

			
“And you lost the money I gave you?” Again, he didn’t answer as the rest of the family stood still, sickened at this possible, newest betrayal by their father. 

			
“I had a business opportunity,” he said in Sicilian, trying to excuse himself with an air of confidence, but failing miserably. “It was a sure thing,” he continued, “I could have doubled our money.”

			
“A sure thing?” she seethed waving her hands. “It broke my heart to leave my daughters, my only grandson, and everything behind. All because of you. Now you bring us to this dirty building with no space to breathe? Over a taverna with puttana next door? Our cattle lived better than this. And you didn’t even buy it. Make no mistake. I will telegram Don Vito. Let’s see what he says.”

			
Mariano stopped pretending he had any remorse. He was still her husband, her boss. How dare she think she could disgrace him in front of his sons. “You wouldn’t do that,” he said, ready to strike but fearful of her words at the same time. “You wouldn’t dare to do that.” 

			
“I don’t make threats,” she said, flicking her fingers under her chin at him.

			
He raised his fist to hit her, but Joe and Peter jumped between them like men separating brawlers in a bar fight—Peter facing Maria, Joe facing Mariano.

			
Clara had experienced her father’s fists firsthand, but Flo had always had Blue to protect her—now she, too, was gone. The little girls ran, terrified, out of the apartment, down the stairs, and out into the street. Never having had to watch out for traffic before, they sprinted at top speed out of the building’s front door and into the street.

			
Trucks, cars, and horse-drawn wagons barreled towards them. Once they realized what they’d done, they froze in fear in the middle of the road. A sedan swerved, just missing them, a horse reared up and galloped around them, a truck, too high for the driver to see little girls standing in the road, was still heading in their direction. The sisters felt themselves being lifted up off the ground as a loud male voice commanded, “Ferma!”

			
God’s voice, thought Flo, and recited the best prayer she knew, hoping God’s mother would pass the message along to her son so he could save them. “Hail Mary, full of grapes, the lord is a tree.” 

			
The man who’d grabbed them wasn’t God, but he was as close as you could find to Him on that wretched street.

			
It was a giant of a man in a white apron, and he had risked his own life by running into the road, scooping up both girls with one massive arm, and rushing them out of harm’s way. Realizing it wasn’t God, but a human man running away with them, Flo did what Blue had taught her to do when a predator threatened. She bit at him and growled like a feral beast as little Clara screamed, “Help! We’re being kidnapped!” 

			
The man’s free hand reached around and covered Clara’s mouth to shut her up, which made Flo kick him in the shins. Still, he didn’t let go as he tried to maneuver them out of the street and onto the sidewalk.

			
The brothers heard the shouting from inside the tenement building and came running down the rickety stairs three at a time. Joe pulled the girls out of the big man’s grip, as Peter and Austin wrestled him to the ground and began pummeling him. 

			
The man’s head started bleeding, and his long white apron was spattered with blood, but he was still struggling. 

			
A woman, her apron splattered not with blood but with red sauce, her hands white with flour, came running out of the restaurant screaming, attempting without luck to pull the brothers off the man. 

			
“They was a’tryin’ to run in-a street with-a the cars,” the beaten man tried to explain as the woman kicked Austin in the seat of his new pants. A crowd had formed, everyone yelling at once. Several men jumped in, cursing the brothers and trying to pull them off the man. 

			
Over the din, came another cry. “Cosa sta succedendo qui?”—What happened here? The crowd looked up as a tiny woman and a tall, gaunt man burst out of the building’s front door—Maria and Mariano. 

			
“Cosa sta succedendo qui?” Maria repeated while trying to keep Mariano from jumping in with her sons, who were still holding down the bleeding man.

			
The lady in the apron called for someone to bring rags to stop the bleeding, “Questo è mio marito,”—This is my husband—she said. “The bambini was be killed!” she cried in her best English. “Mio marito grab-ba ragazzine for the runover.” 

			
The boys loosened their grip as everyone—including Flo and Clara—stopped moving, ashamed of the mess they’d created. The man had only tried to save their lives. 

			
“Caro Dio! Per favore perdonaci, Signora,” Joe begged the lady, apologizing for what they’d mistakenly done. 

			
“Voi animali!” she spat back at him as she tried to lift her husband off the sidewalk. 

			
They begged her to let them lift him for her. People in the raucous crowd were becoming more unruly; the men who’d tried to break up the fight yelling and shaking their fists at the Barberas.

			
“I saw Ferdinando risk his own life to rescue those girls,” one shouted. Others agreed but said they had also seen the brothers rush out after hearing the little girls’ cries that they were being abducted. Others spoke in whispers that one of the children had growled like a wolf, another swore she heard her howl. 

			
Mavara? Strega?

			
Seeing that another situation was developing among the crowd itself, the woman in the apron nodded her head at the brothers but never changed her expression. Joe, Austin, and Peter lifted the man carefully and carried him back inside as the lady held a rag to his bleeding head. They laid the poor guy on the serving bar near the front door, which was a terrible reminder of how Peter had, several months back, been laid atop the counter at Bratton’s. 

			
Maria touched the woman’s hand and told her how sorry she was and tried to thank her for what her husband had done. The woman shrugged her off and pushed her away, white flour handprints remaining on Maria’s black dress.

			
The woman told a terrified young boy, whose school papers were scattered on the floor, to hurry and fetch the doctor.

			
The doctor arrived in what seemed like seconds. He took control of the situation immediately, and, in Sicilian, ordered the crowd of onlookers to disperse. This man was no Doc Dawson. For one thing, he wasn’t drunk, and for another, he was Italian. Dr. Alessio Polito, a distinguished sort of gentleman in a crisp white shirt, bow tie, and good suit, examined the injured man and declared, “Just a busted lip, a bad cut on his head, and he’ll have a black eye, but he’ll live.” 

			
He sent someone to chip pieces of ice from the giant block in the restaurant’s ice box while he cleaned and stitched up the battered man’s lip and wrapped gauze around his head. Dr. Polito told Flo and Clara to stand on chairs so they could hold the ice packs on the man. The girls climbed up on the chairs, each praying that the chore would last all day because they knew that as soon as it was done, their mama would smack their backsides but good for running into the street. 

			
Not what you’d call a good start in a bad neighborhood.

– 43 –

			
A Panelle for Your Thoughts

			
Red Hook, Brooklyn, New York

			
Same Day

			
Clara saw—but did not say—that just before the truck was about to run them down, Flo’s lips had turned back to ruby red. But when she looked at her sister now, her mouth was once again as pale as it had been since losing Blue. 

			
Clara thought, Must have been my ermaginator.

			
Maria thought, We have caused all this trouble on our first day in New York City.

			
Dr. Polito thought, Just another typical day in Red Hook. 

			
As Dr. Polito was packing up his medical bag to leave, the beaten man’s wife moved to the back of the counter and brought up a basket covered in a red and white checkered cloth. The aromas of fresh-cooked pasta con sarde mingled with still-warm bread. Her husband had prepared it for the social club across the street where the neighborhood mobsters hung out, but instead she gave it to the doctor. 

			
They’ll understand, the woman thought as she followed the doctor to the door, thanking him again. Everybody from the mobsters to the bishop loved Dr. Polito.

			
The doctor stepped out, the sweet tinkling of the little bell above the door mingling with the chaotic noise of the street outside, and he was gone. 

			
Maria, whose whole life was waiting to be unpacked, had had nothing in hand to offer the doctor but swore to herself that she’d never forget this kindness.

			
And she never did. 

			
The man lying on the bar, banged up and bandaged as he was, managed to wink at the little girls holding the ice. “No worry,” he said. “Yous-a bambini did right. Who know what means if uomo strano grab-ba you?” He was trying to tell them it was good that they knew to fight back when a stranger grabbed them.

			
Signora Buffa, his wife, was not as conciliatory and stared daggers at the violent family who had created such a mess before they had even officially moved in.

			
“You come here, first day you fight. No good. You fight again, and I make sure the landlord throw you in-a the street.”

			
Maria responded in Sicilian, “Dear Lady, we are good people. Country people, farmers. My children don’t know the ways of the city yet, and the little ones thought they were being stolen.” 

			
Maria shook her head at her family and vowed, “I promise, Signora, no more violence.” 

			
At that, the door swung open and Laura, who had been hiding in the truck, burst through, gun drawn, hands shaking. 

			
The lady screamed and Maria grabbed the gun out of Laura’s hand, which went off, shooting a bullet into the floor. 

			
“No more violence?” the woman scolded in Sicilian. “You are violent, uncouth people.”

			
“Mi dispiace, Signora,” Maria apologized as she lay a reassuring hand on the lady’s shoulder.

			
“Go,” the woman bristled at them, shaking off Maria’s hand. “Don’na touch me, don’na touch mio marito, and don’na come back ‘ere. Never no mo’.”

			
They understood and turned towards the door.

			
Normally Mariano would have beaten the three girls for the turmoil they’d created—and Austin just for the hell of it—but now all he wanted was to get away from them all. 

			
He stuck his hat back on his head and sulked out the door before the rest of the family, as though he was just respecting the restaurant lady’s orders. Which he would never consider doing. 

			
I had a whore every day, a game every night, and I was free here like in Denver, he thought. 

			
He stomped back up the stairs, and they heard an apartment door slam shut above the restaurant’s tin ceiling. 

			
It wasn’t the door with “2A” penciled on it.

			
The rest of them hadn’t moved yet, so the woman again ordered them to leave and not come back, repeating under her breath, “Voi animali.” 

			
The man lying on the counter—the opposite of Mariano in every way—was still groggy and aching, but he managed to wave his hand and say to his wife, “No, amore mia. Solo un malinteso.”—No, my love. It was just a mistake.

			
He turned to Flo and Clara who were taking their job of ministering to his wounds very seriously. “My name-a eese Signor Ferdinando. This-a is my ristorante.” 

			
“Oh,” little Flo said, shaking his limp hand. “It is very nice to meet you Signor Ferdinando,” hoping that was the polite way to respond to a man who’d just saved their lives but was now bleeding after being beaten by her brothers. 

			
Clara grabbed his other hand, and copying her sister, said, “It is nice to meet you, Signor Ferdinando.” 

			
Signor Ferdinando managed a smile, looked at his furious wife, and asked her anyway, “Per favore, date delle panelle alle bambine.”—Please give the kids a panelle. 

			
Ferdinando believed there wasn’t anything that couldn’t be solved with a snack—especially panelle, the Sicilian chickpea fritters that were one of the specialties of Ferdinando’s Focacceria. When the girls bit into his delicious chickpea panelle, they thought exactly the same thing. 

			
It was the start of a lifelong friendship, and one that would strangely reemerge again many generations later.

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 44 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 44 –

			
No School Like Old School

			
Red Hook, Brooklyn, New York 

			
November 1923

			
The family managed to settle in as best as they could in the overcrowded rooms. Maria, who’d had enough of public schools thanks to the very dead Miss Trainor, enrolled Flo and Clara in the local church school—Saint Mary Star of the Sea elementary school, which was free to the children of parishioners. 

			
In exchange for the free schooling, the mothers of the children in the parish cleaned, cooked for the priests, and kowtowed to the nuns who ran the school like it was a juvenile detention center. 

			
On their first day as the new girls in school (Clara was enrolled again as Flo’s twin), Flo made the mistake of asking their teacher, a nun named Sister Joseph-Mildred, “Why do you have the same name as my brother if you wear a gown? Are you a man or a lady?” 

			
The children in the classroom laughed out loud, the loudest being Louis LeRoux, who upon returning from the water fountain, squirted water out of his nose, which made the kids laugh louder and the nun even madder than she already was. 

			
Clearly, she didn’t find any of it funny. 

			
Not one to take remarks from wise-ass brats, especially immigrant brats, the nun cracked the ruler on Flo’s desk. She’d been aiming for her hands, but Flo, being fast and limber from living with Blue, pulled her hands out a second before disaster struck.

			
The nun’s savagery so startled Flo that she followed that by doing what came naturally. She hunched her back and growled low. The nun was not sure whether she actually heard a growl or not, but she was sure that whatever it was, it was not good. And it was probably unholy. 

			
Sister Joseph-Mildred lifted Flo out of her seat by her armpits and slapped her face. Hard. “You little dago guttersnipe!” she screeched. “Did you growl at me? You better learn you aren’t in the Wild West anymore. This is the big city, and I’ll beat you silly if you ever disrespect me again!”

			
Flo remembered what her mama had taught her before they left Colorado, which was very specific: “Don’t act like a wolf there. They don’t know about those things in Brook-a-land. They might think you are lupa mannaro.”—a werewolf.

			
Flo took a breath, quit growling, and sniffled, “I wasn’t bein’ disresect, teacher, I just wanted to know why—”

			
With that, Sister Joseph-Mildred grabbed her up by the collar, slammed the dunce cap on her head, and dragged her over to the corner where she had to face the wall for the rest of her first day in her new school. Tears ran down Clara’s face all day, but she tried not to make a sound even as the snot ran down her face, making the nun even angrier.

			
When Maria arrived to pick the kids up and saw the big red mark still visible on Flo’s face, she knelt down and asked her what had happened.

			
“The teacher slapped me because I asked why she had my brother’s name, and if she was a lady or a man,” Flo cried, falling into her mother’s arms. “She called me ‘dago guttersnipe.’” 

			
“Where is the teacher?” Maria asked in Sicilian. Flo pointed to her classroom. She didn’t know what guttersnipe, meant, but she sure knew what dago meant. Maria put her face down next to Flo’s and told her, “You listen to me. Nobody is better than you—nobody!” 

			
With that, she told the girls to wait for her on the playground and walked to the door of the classroom where the nun was erasing the blackboard. 

			
“Sorella Giuseppe?” Maria called from the doorway. The nun didn’t turn around and said, “I don’t speak Eye-talian, so speak English or go find one of your own kind to interpret for you.”

			
Maria understood her well enough to answer, “I speak-a small da’ Inglesay.” 

			
“I don’t understand you,” Sister Joseph-Mildred said, still not bothering to turn around. Maria walked into the classroom, up to the blackboard, tapped the nun on her shoulder, put her mouth right up to where the sister’s ear was hiding under the veil, and whispered, “You toucha mia figlia, Sorella Teacher? Maybe I send somebody a’teach you.”

			
With that, she turned around and walked out, leaving the nun stunned—and hell bent on revenge. By tomorrow, that filthy howling little dog and that midget sister of hers will be expelled, Sister Joseph-Mildred promised herself.

			
So, keeping her word, right after supper, the nun walked over to Monsignor Preston’s palatial residence a few blocks over, lifted the enormous lion-head door knocker, and knocked three times. When a young priest answered the door, she told him she needed to speak with the monsignor. “It’s of the utmost importance,” she snapped.

			
In a few minutes she was escorted into the monsignor’s study as he was finishing up his brandy. She told him the story, embellishing the part about the growl and adding that she thought she saw fangs on the child. She asked that both the feral child and her sister be expelled immediately. 

			
“I hate Southern Italians,” she added for emphasis, smoothing her already smooth habit. “They think they’re God’s gift, but are just filthy darkies pretending to be white. Or maybe they are just hairy and smelly because they are part dog or wolf or something.”

			
The monsignor shook his head in agreement. “Those Guineas are nothing but trouble. Always have been, always will be. Coming here, invading our neighborhood. The sooner they find somewhere else to ruin and get outta here, the better.” 

			
Rising up, holding out his hand for the nun to kiss his ring—which was a gesture well above his rank—he assured her, “Don’t you worry, Sister. The brats will be expelled tomorrow. So help me God.”

			
Feeling powerful and happy, Sister Joseph-Mildred walked out of there with a spring in her step, which was about as unusual an occurrence as spotting a wolf girl in Brooklyn.

			
The next morning, Maria took Flo and Clara to school bright and early. The schoolyard was full of kids playing ball, potsy, and marbles, jumping rope, and having fun. The bell rang at 8:00 a.m., Potsy and the children lined up according to grade and classroom. Sister Joseph-Mildred stood, guarding the door, salivating at the thought of turning the Barbera brats away in front of everyone. When the girls reached the door and gave their names to the novice checking the kids in, Sister Joseph-Mildred stepped out and took the attendance sheet out of the young nun’s hands. 

			
In her haughtiest voice, she chirped, “That won’t be necessary, Sister Rose. These two are not—”

			
Before she could finish her pronouncement, the monsignor, red faced and out of breath, came rushing through the schoolyard, accompanied by a man in an overcoat and fedora. “No, no, Sister, let them in,” cried the monsignor, sweat pouring down his face despite the cold weather.

			
“But—” she started to say, and the monsignor put his hand right over her mouth. “Do not disobey me, Sister! These girls are very welcome here.” He then tipped his hat to Maria, patted Flo and Clara on the head, and said, “Such good girls.”

			
The nun looked from the monsignor to the man in the fedora, whose face was as blank as a schoolroom slate. 

			
When the nun, shamed and cowed, walked back inside holding the hands of both girls, Flo told her, “Sister, my mama said nobody is better than me. Nobody.” This made the nun stiffen, furious that she could no longer slap the child or even squeeze her hand until it hurt. 

			
She turned around to see if the man in the fedora was still there. He was and he smiled at her—if that’s what you’d call such a thing.

			
He then took a fat envelope out of his coat pocket and handed it to the monsignor. “For the poor, fathah. From Mister Lansky,” he said. 

			
It was clear to everyone that the Carlino’s hand reached far, just as the Don had promised. 

			
The monsignor gave the man a blessing and said, “Thank you, my son. It will go to good use,” then stuck the envelope right in his own pocket.

			
 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

Author’s Note
		

	
	
		

			
Author’s Note

			
The Sicilian language used throughout is as authentic to the period as possible. In many cases Italian, English, and Sicilian are intermixed conversationally. The misspellings are deliberate when used as the spoken word. The natural disasters in Italy and in Colorado such as the Messina Earthquake of 1908 and the Pueblo Flood of 1921, actually happened and were the springboard for much of the characters actual and fictional lives. Some of the unnatural events—such as the Ludlow Massacre—are real, while others have been fictionalized. Some characters such as Michelina, Callie, and Vito Carlino are an amalgam of several different people I’ve known or have heard stories about.

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 45 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 45 –

			
Cash In, Cashed Out

			
Red Hook, Brooklyn, New York 

			
1923–1928

			
The story of how the monsignor and the man in the fedora had come to the little parish school spread around the neighborhood faster than one-half of no time. If Calvin Coolidge himself had shown up, it wouldn’t have been as big news. The only time that the monsignor ever showed up at the school was for the Christmas pageant. The fedora man? He was never seen there before and never again after that.

			
That visit made it very clear to the whole neighborhood that sweet-faced little Maria was someone who had influence with folks of influence, both in the neighborhood and beyond. The thinking was, if you had the monsignor, you had the mobsters.

			
Therefore, by week’s end, business had picked up to overflowing at “Mariano’s Deluxe Fruits and Vegetables Store.” When Maria was manning the place, she noticed that the produce that had seen better days went flying off the shelves. 

			
Even so, the Barberas were still mostly broke, thanks to Mariano. The money she made from washing the Carlino money didn’t cover expenses because Mariano was either giving everybody credit so he’d look like a big shot, or he was paying women back in groceries for what he’d lost to their husbands at the tables. 

			
But no matter how short they were every month, Maria wouldn’t let customers buy what they didn’t need to gain favor, so she put a stop to it. Instead, each evening, she, Laura, Flo, and Clara would gather up the fruits, vegetables, and loaves of bread that weren’t at their peak (and many times, at their peak) and put them in sacks. When they finished making up the bundles, Maria and the girls would walk from building to building discretely hanging anonymous packages (or as discretely and anonymously as they could in the crowded tenements), on the doorknobs of families who were struggling. 

			
Within a year, Maria’s reputation as a good woman with powerful friends had grown, and she began to take little jobs washing money for the Carlino’s allies too. 

			
Meyer Lansky, of the Bugs and Meyer Mob—the first person known to launder money through Swiss banks—sent some business her way just to show good faith with his Italian friends. But she’d already experienced his “good faith” when he had sent fedora man to have a talk with the monsignor.

			
The connection between the Italian and Jewish bootleg mobsters was “Lucky” Luciano, a friend of the Carlinos and La Donas. He and Lansky had been great friends since they were kids growing up together. Birds of different feathers flocking together.

			
As long as everybody took their piece and only their piece, and everybody stayed out of everybody else’s affairs, the alliance would hold. 

			
A year after they moved in, Flo and Clara were playing Potsy on the sidewalk on a Saturday morning when they saw Maria rushing towards them from the store. She hustled them inside, up the stairs, and pulled down all the shades. “Don’t talk,” she ordered.

			
“What’s going on, Mama?” Flo cried. “We was playin’ Potsy!”

			
“I say ‘don’t talk,’” Maria scolded, putting her hand over Flo’s mouth. Mama knew something they didn’t, and that was good enough for them. 

			
Seconds later, shots rang out, followed by screams and the sound of people running, doors opening and slamming shut. When it grew quiet, Clara snuck a peek under the shade and let out a scream. “I tol’ you no look!” Maria whispered, taking the child into her arms.

			
Clara had seen a man, full of bullets, bleeding out right inside the potsy grid they’d chalked on the sidewalk.

			
Word on the street was that Lansky had done his buddy Luciano a solid by sending four Jewish mobsters to take out the dead man, one Salvatore Maranzano. He had been an Italian mob boss who’d put a contract out on rival Luciano. 

			
Different town, different players, same old shit.

			
That murder, though, was like a gift from God for one other person—Mariano. Maranzano’s loan shark operation had extended extra coin to Mariano, which the boss knew he couldn’t pay back. Maranzano had a plan, though. After the mob boss scared the living daylights out of Mariano with several beatings, Maranzano offered Mariano a deal: He’d cut the interest on the loans by half, and in return, Mariano would rat on his own people.

			
Out of respect, Don Vito had let Mariano keep a seat at the table, even though he had no say in any decisions. He didn’t have a say within his own family anymore, either, but he had plenty to say to the Maranzano gang—trading information in return for shaving his debts and keeping them from beating the hell out of him. 

			
He was saving his own skin with the Maranzanos, but if the Colorado connection ever discovered Mariano had been sleeping with the enemy, that would have been the end of him. So, with Maranzano out of the way, Mariano was once again able to slip the noose. 

			
“Papa’s slipperier than a can of Red Rose lard,” Austin often said.

			
“The little lady,” as Maria came to be called, was the one that people trusted and relied on for favors, for food, for…well, whatever they needed.

			
Joe and Peter meantime collected the bootleg money in burlap sacks that had to be “washed” through the family’s grocery store. 

			
One March day in 1927, when the family had extra cash because Mariano had mysteriously gone missing for two weeks, they went out and bought a piano and placed it in Ferdinando’s Focacceria. It was high time for Austin to make his way, and since he was a helluva piano man, he could earn money playing for tips. Which he did. Yes, there were the occasional times when one of his spells would come on, but Ferdinando and his wife knew what to do, and the customers were all like—or actually were—family, so they knew enough to just wait until it passed. They never made Austin feel ashamed, or as Mariano would say, like a freak. 

			
Nothing much changed—until the unthinkable happened. Black Tuesday. That was the day, on October 29, 1929, when the Wall Street Stock Market crashed—not that the family knew what that was. But they knew that, thank God, even though rich people were jumping out of windows and poor people were on bread lines, somehow it hadn’t much affected the bootleg business and thus the money laundering business. 

			
Others weren’t as lucky. Men lost jobs and families were evicted, and not just in Red Hook; the newspapers proclaimed it was this way all over the world. Her neighbors were starving, and even more women were out in the streets with their children.

			
Maria had a sit-down with the bosses and got their permissions to take a percentage off the top each week to pay rents of the poorest widows with children in the neighborhood. 

			
Mariano wasn’t around very much any longer, which was a relief to everyone. What he did for money during these times, they could only guess. When he did show up, though, he was either angry or drunk, or angry and drunk. 

			
On Union Street, Mariano was loathed, Maria was loved. He was feared, she was fearless. He took, she gave.

			
 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 46 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 46 –

			
Daughters of the Italian American Revolution

			
Red Hook, Brooklyn, New York 

			
1929–1930

			
Laura, the beauty with the dimples, the flowing mane of black curls, and the good fashion sense, never got to buy those pretty dresses she so admired. One day in the eleventh grade, she declared that her education was over and refused to go back to school. She took a job sewing mattresses at the O’Mara Mattress factory and promptly took up with a small-time crook named Frank Furiano. 

			
Maria was raving mad. “He no good!” she warned Laura when Frank first started coming around. “He can’t even steal without getting caught. A real stunad. Stay away from him, or I kill you!” 

			
Laura eloped with Frank two months later and moved into his parents’ one-bedroom apartment where he began to beat her regularly. Each time she’d hide from her family until the bruises faded because makeup just wouldn’t cover it. Her co-workers knew she was being beaten up, but sometimes it was just a woman’s fate, and there was nothing anybody could say or do about it.

			
Laura knew she had married a man who was worse than her father.

			
If her brothers ever knew that the two-bit hoodlum with no affiliation to any of the “families” in the neighborhood had beaten their sister to within an inch of her life, they would have had no problem doing the same to him and not leaving even that inch of life in him. But, like her mother, Laura couldn’t live with her no-account husband, but neither could she live with his murder on her conscience. 

			
But knowing this marriage had forever trapped her in a prison of her own making, she did the only thing she could: She took a lover. 

			
No, not the kind of lover like the fancy puttanas had—those men in silk suits with flowers in their lapels who could keep a girl in style—but a decent, blue-eyed man named Lorenzo Sant’Angelo. Lorenzo drove an O’Mara mattress truck and, despite his thin frame, hefted mattresses single-handedly up five flights of stairs. 

			
When Laura got pregnant with the second of the four sons she’d eventually have, the new baby was named for her brother Giuseppe. He was, by the grace of God, the spitting image of the beautiful Laura. He was all dimples, with a tiny nose, black eyes, and masses of black ringlets. Frank was so stupid, he believed that gorgeous baby boy was his spitting image, and maybe it was—even though Frank himself had no dimples, a Roman nose, and hair as straight as a stick. It was the kind of look that a young girl might at first glance think of as thrilling and dangerous.

			
But there is a difference between dangerous and cruel. The meaner he got, the less attractive he got. It was like his soul was showing in his face. In fact, one day when Flo, Clara, and Maria were crossing the street, Flo, who had become fascinated by cars, said, “Mama, lookit that Buick roadster over there. Doesn’t the front with those buggy headlights and that ugly front grill look just like Frank’s mug?” Maria looked at the Roadster, saw that yes, it sure did look like Frank, and all three of them burst out laughing until tears rolled down their faces. 

			
“Good thing the baby don’t look like Frank,” Clara added, and they all started laughing out loud again. 

			
Maria was just grateful to God that the baby looked nothing like the front grill of roadster, and even more grateful that he didn’t have Lorenzo the truck driver’s blue eyes either. 

			
Lorenzo had fallen deep as a bottomless well for Laura and she for him, but the idea that she lived not just with another man, but with the brutal Frank, drove Lorenzo crazy. This was especially true since he believed the baby was his, because, for starters, little Giuseppe looked like a miniature version of Lorenzo’s little brother, Tommy Boy. But then again, Tommy Boy looked a bit like Frank, which was traditional Sicilian.

			
Maria always knew everything, even though her kids often thought she didn’t know anything about what they had gotten up to. She understood when she could step in and stop whatever trouble they were getting into and when they had to learn those lessons for themselves—just as she had learned them: the hard way.

			
Flo and Clara in those same years had done well in school and eventually even adjusted to Red Hook. But they never stopped yearning for Colorado, for little Bella, and for Blue. No matter how many years passed, every time Flo thought of her mama wolf running after the train, she’d burst into tears. The apartment was too small even for a dog, which left the animal-loving girls bereft. 

			
“Mama!” Flo would wail. “What am I supposed to do—have a big, fat rat for a pet?” Sometimes Blue would come to her in her dreams, but when she woke and realized Blue wasn’t real, she’d get to sobbing all over again. 

			
Maria relented and one day brought home a parrot, which she had gotten from a customer who was moving upstate. His name was Omar, and he unfortunately only spoke Portuguese and wasn’t very friendly. Every time the girls would go near him, he’d spit out, “caralho!”—fuck!—at them in Portuguese. Omar had one other talent as far as anyone could tell and that was imitating a doorbell, which he “rang” very loudly several times a day. 

			
Omar was no substitute for Blue or Bella, but when the girls somehow learned what caralho meant, they’d whisper it to each other when they saw someone unsavory and then laugh themselves silly. 

			
Eventually, Maria managed to get the peroxide blonde moved out of the apartment next door by phoning Mr. Lansky, who’d sent the man in the fedora to “collect the rent.” Conveniently, that just so happened to be about the same time Joe married a girl named Irene, so the new couple moved into the former apartment of the woman Laura had nicknamed, “Puttana Blondie.” At first Flo and Clara were excited to have a new “sister” to visit, but then they saw what was on Irene and Joe’s bed and froze in fear. Covering their maroon, quilted, satin spread was Irene’s collection of scary porcelain dolls with real hair and gowns also made of satin. 

			
Maria was also able to take over the empty storefront across the street and open a second shop. She called it, “Maria Latteria,” where she sold fresh milk, mozzarella made by a local wife, imported cheeses such as Parmigiano Reggiano, and fresh-baked bread. After she closed up for business each night, she’d open the soup kitchen for women and children in need in the back. Maria, Flo, Clara, and other volunteer ladies (and even Sister Joseph-Mildred on occasion) would pass out hot soup, bread, cheese, and when possible, as many panelli as Flo could coax out of Ferdinando. Not that the big-hearted ristorante owner would refuse Flo anything. 

			
One stormy evening, six months after the stock market crash, as Maria was about to close up the dairy store, a young woman showed up outside pushing a baby carriage. Maria ran to open the door as the young woman, her back to Maria, lifted the baby out of the carriage. She turned around and said, “Buonasera, Mama.” Callie. Without giving the family notice, not only had Callie showed up out of nowhere, she had showed up with a baby.

			
A big man in a fancy coat came rushing up right after them and took the baby from Callie so she could throw her arms around her mother.

			
As they stood in the rain, Maria, half in shock, half enraged, and completely disgusted, stiffened against the hug and said, “I suppose you have come to take Carlotta away from me?”

			
“Oh no, Mama, I would never do that,” Callie said.

			
Maria pretended Callie wasn’t even there and walked back inside the store, but before she could close the door, Callie and the big man holding the baby followed her. Maria stood at the open door, blocking their way.

			
“Then what do you want, showing up here after seven years? Never once contacting us, never once asking if your daughter was alive or dead,” Maria said, cold as a delivery from the ice man.

			
“I’m—we’re—back, Mama,” Callie sputtered. Then, “This is my husband, Orazio, and this is our baby, Katerina, but we call her ‘Kat.’”

			
When Maria stepped back to let them enter, more to protect the baby from the rain than as a welcome back to the family, Callie took the blanket from the baby’s head. Kat was an angel baby—the baby Callie would have accepted if it had been Charlie’s—even though she was a girl. Her head was covered in bright, blonde fuzz, her huge eyes were the sweetest hazel, and her nose was more of a button than an appendage. Baby Kat looked like the Gerber baby before there was a Gerber baby: the perfect white, American infant.

			
“And what about Carlotta?” Maria spit out. 

			
Looking up at her new husband with the same loving stare she had once reserved for Charlie, Callie answered, “Oh, don’t worry. We aren’t here to take her away from you. In fact, Orazio, he doesn’t even want to think I ever had another husband, let alone a child!”

			
Orazio looked confused, but said nothing.

			
“And you think this is normale? To reject your child not once but twice?”

			
“Oh Mama, I want to be in her life. I love her.”

			
Maria snorted and went about putting the day-old bread into baskets to bring to the needy families, not looking up at them. 

			
“Mama, after all this time, she’s more your child than mine—” Callie said as Maria cut her off at the pass, putting her finger right in her daughter’s face.

			
“Carlotta! Her name is Carlotta!”

			
“I know her name is Carlotta. Carlotta Maria, in honor of you.”

			
“In honor of Carlo you mean. Anyway, what do you want?” Maria repeated. 

			
“I—we—want to come back to the family,” Callie said a big grin spreading over her face. “Orazio—Raz—has a business opportunity here. His father died and left him the shoemaker shop around the corner. Raz is trained to fix shoes, but he is too educated for that kind of work. He’s a writer. He’s building a little theater in the back where he can put on his plays.”

			
“His plays,” Maria sneered, making it as clear as a plate glass window that she didn’t care to hear anything else.

			
Callie continued anyway. “Yes, Raz writes plays, you see—that’s how we met. I was working at the little theater in Schenectady when Raz came to town to put on a play.”

			
Maria shook her head. “A shoemaker-play writer. Perfetto. Now get out.”

			
“But Mama!” Callie cried, just like she’d sounded when she had been Charlie’s spoiled brat wife.

			
Maria gestured towards the door. As the couple turned to leave, Callie turned back and vowed, “We will come back. Raz wants to meet my brothers and sisters too.” 

			
They won’t want to meet him or see you, Maria thought.

			
A month later, a letter arrived at 151 Union Street inviting them to Raz’s first play in Brooklyn: Falling Leaves of Sorrento.

			
Peter, Flo, Clara, Carlotta, and Austin—against Maria’s wishes—decided they just had to go, if for no other reason than to see what the hell Callie was up to this time around. They were shown to the best seats in the rickety theater behind “Orazio’s Shoe and Saddle Repair Shop.” It was, in reality, a pretty terrible place, but the girls had never been in a theater before or seen a real play so to them it was amazing.

			
The play, an awful melodramatic hour of hand-wringing and bad acting, concerned a man whose beautiful fiancée had been kidnapped by a bandit, so he kills himself. It made no sense, but the family was dazzled nonetheless.

			
The only one who didn’t enjoy it was Carlotta. First, she became uncomfortable, and then downright disturbed, because the skinny lady holding the beautiful golden baby (who had been introduced as “Aunt Callie”) kept turning around to stare at her.

			
“Is she really our aunt? Why does she keep looking at me?” Carlotta whispered to Flo, who knew the truth but didn’t answer. 

			
Carlotta understood the silence meant this wasn’t a question she should have asked. Or a question she might ever get answered.

			
 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 47 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 47 –

			
Boys Will Be Men

			
Red Hook, Brooklyn, New York

			
1930

			
Peter grew, if not more handsome—impossible—then even more appealing. The ladies were struck dumb by his unintended swagger, his natural confidence, crooked smile, and ability to charm the pants off anybody. Literally.

			
Men, too, loved being around Peter, not just because he was funny and trustworthy, but also because where Peter went so followed the ladies. 

			
Then, without warning, Peter eloped with a neighborhood gal named Jeannette Giarratano, leaving half the women in Red Hook bereft.

			
Not that they had to wait too long after the wedding to get, as they called it, “into the sheets with Pete.” No, Peter was not a faithful husband, but he was a good provider. His extra money came not from his backbreaking job on the docks, but from his leg-breaking job collecting for the local numbers runners. Although, in all fairness, Peter was more of a heartbreaker than a leg-breaker. Half the time, he took the money out of his own pocket and told the runner he’d collected it.

			
By the time Peter and Jeannette had been married less than three years, Jeannette had already popped out two gorgeous baby girls. First there was Marie, named in honor of her mother-in-law. When the second baby was born, they named her Beatrice in honor of Jeannette’s grandmother but gave her the fancy French middle name of Claudette in honor of her favorite movie star, Claudette Colbert. That was as wild as Jeannette ever got. 

			
She kept her home spotless and contented herself with her duties as a mother and proud wife to the catch-of-the neighborhood. She knew Peter was a man many women wanted—and she suspected many women still had. 

			
In fact, Jeannette once heard a woman say to her friend as they were passing the pushcart where she was perusing kitchen items, “Ooo, slow down, here he comes.”

			
The other one answered, “I’d keep the light burnin’ on the porch for that boy anytime.”

			
They turned around and laughed out loud, just like, Jeannette thought, a couple of cheap dancehall girls.

			
She didn’t know who these loudmouths were talking about, so she turned around and saw Peter walking towards them. When he saw Jeannette, though, he stopped heading in their direction, turned, and walked instead towards the pushcart where she had been standing.

			
Jeanette put down the eggbeater she’d been eyeing and took her husband by the arm, glared at the women, and strolled away, holding onto him with one hand and pushing the baby carriage with the other. Before they got to the corner, Jeannette turned and sneered at them. A few seconds later, Peter turned and winked at them. If Jeannette had seen it, she said nothing. Not to him anyway. She never would. Her mother had taught her to always pretend to look the other way when it concerned a husband.

			
The only one Jeannette ever talked to about Peter’s flirtatious ways was her sister, Caterina, but it was only to let her know that she was no dummy and knew what he was up to. The conversation she had always wanted to avoid, but would nonetheless have to happen, took place one crisp fall afternoon when the sisters were out walking their babies. Jeannette, who was dressed in her new fur coat even though it wasn’t fur-coat weather, brought the subject up herself.

			
She admitted that, yes, she knew her husband sometimes had experiences with other women, “But I’m the one he bought this expensive mouton coat for, and I’m the one he comes home to every night.” 

			
Caterina stopped herself from saying, “We both know your husband does not come home to you every night, and that fur coat probably fell off a truck.” 

			
She buttoned her lips but thought: Better to have a man who doesn’t look like a matinee idol and is faithful to his wife, than un uomo bellissimo—a beautiful man—who can’t keep it in his pants.

			
Maria’s oldest son, meanwhile, was the opposite of Peter. Joe was tall, slim, and muscular but looked like, well, a regular Joe—not like anybody’s idea of bell’uomo. Joe was as faithful a husband as any girl could wish for. No one who knew Joe would have expected anything less.

			
Joe’s wife was a neighborhood girl, Irene Impastato, who happened to be the niece of Paul La Dona. In addition to the fact that Joe loved his bride, the marriage helped bring the feuding families together. Moving away from Colorado had put enough distance between the Barberas and the La Donas that, if absence didn’t make their hearts grow fonder, it did make those hearts grow a bit softer. Capone had been right about the Barberas moving to New York.

			
Not that any of them—with the exception of Peter—loved Brooklyn as much as they loved Colorado, but at least the family wasn’t being killed any longer. 

			
Irene was raised in the city, so she was a citified gal who didn’t know any other life, but she would have been OK wherever they landed. She was just one of those kind-hearted souls who was as good as bread, even though she was plain in the face and big in the belly. Irene was what they called a “big gal.” 

			
Didn’t matter. Helen Keller herself could have seen that Joe and his wife were very much in love.

			
Joe told his brothers, “If I was any happier, I’d think it was a setup.”

			
They were a very happy couple, yes, but then again, not. The disappointment in their lives was that Irene’s big belly never held a baby, which the couple had yearned for. People began to wonder about it, and they’d even ask her, “So when are you two going to start a family?” 

			
Or worse, mistaking her for being pregnant, they would blurt out, “Yowsa! When’s the baby due? Looks like any day now, right?” 

			
Two years into the marriage, Irene—fearing that Maria, too, would think her a failure for not producing a child—threw herself into Maria’s arms and cried, the tears landing on her big bosom and wetting down the bodice of her flowered house dress. 

			
“Mama Maria! I just can’t get pregnant,” she wailed, reminiscent of Callie all those many years ago. It was so long ago, in fact, that “Baby” Carlotta was already seven years old. 

			
“No matter how I pray, no matter how many novenas I make,” she continued, “I just can’t get with child.”

			
Maria tried practical advice. “Praying is good, cara, but fari l’amuri is better.”

			
“But we do make love,” Irene sobbed. “I try to keep my Joe as happy as I can. I never refuse and I’m always ready for him, whenever he wants me.”

			
Too bad there is no Chepeta here in this Red Hook, Maria thought. She was half temped to send Irene to Colorado to try and find the medicine woman, but she knew that the tribe would have moved on by now.

			
Worse, the more Irene failed to get pregnant, the bigger her collection of scary porcelain dolls grew, until they covered every inch of the bed. There was the satin doll in a faded pink gown with frightening brown curls and a giant bow atop her head; there was the one in the blue gown with blonde hair and eyelashes and glass eyes that opened and closed. The worst one was the one in the giant brown gown and matching hat with lace that covered its face. They all wore Mary Jane shoes with white stockings, and all had painted faces with red lips. Some had scary little doll teeth too. 

			
Joe loved Irene so much it never occurred to him that the dolls scared the hell out of everybody else.

		
	

OceanofPDF.com
