– 48 –

			
Only The Good Die Young

			
Red Hook, Brooklyn, New York 

			
1930

			
Flo and Clara, now thirteen and fourteen (both fourteen as far as school records went), and Carlotta, seven, all swore that Auntie Irene’s dolls came alive at night and killed people.

			
“You know that man they wheeled into old man Pirro’s funeral parlor today?” Flo asked Clara one night as they snuggled together in the tiny single bed. “I think that terrible doll with the brown gown came alive last night and killed him dead!” That caused Carlotta, who slept on a cot next to them, to break into a million tears and run to the safety of her mother’s bed—or the only woman she had ever known as her mother.

			
Maria snuggled Carlotta in her arms under her big quilt and sang her to sleep with their favorite Sicilian lullaby.

			
Vò e la rivò, ora viene il padre tuo

			
e ti porta la semenza, la rosa marina e il basilico…

			
It was the last song Carlotta ever heard her “mama” sing.

			
A few days later—it was May by then—Maria went to visit Irene next door, toting along her constant companion, Carlotta, as well as her “medicine bag” of holy water, oil, and herbs. Maybe the old remedy would help even though it had failed to do the job with Callie. When Maria walked into the apartment, she began rubbing her stomach. 

			
“Mama, what’s wrong—do you feel sick?” Irene asked.

			
“She said she’s fine,” Carlotta piped up. “But look, Auntie Irene. Mama’s belly is swole out big as yours.” 

			
Good natured Irene laughed. “Oh honey, nobody’s belly is big as mine!” Then turning to Maria, “Mama, maybe you want some Bromo-Seltzer?”

			
“No, no, I’m fine,” Maria answered, even though it was clear that she was uncomfortable. 

			
“Pass some gas,” Irene offered. “It always helps.”

			
“No, no Bromo, no pìditu.” In her best English, Maria continued, “We here because I want to pray for you have a baby, not to pray rotten belly.”

			
Irene nodded, grateful for any help in the baby department, but she was nonetheless mostly concerned about her mother-in-law. 

			
Rubbing the side of her stomach before they started praying, Maria brought up a subject she knew would be about as disturbing to her daughter-in-law as any, but it had to be said. 

			
The dolls, she delicately explained, which were posed on the bed could be the problem. Since so many in the family found them scary, was it possible that they were, ah, ah, “bambole maledette?”—Cursed dolls? 

			
Irene looked stricken, but Maria continued, nonetheless. “Could maybe,” Maria suggested timidly, “they shouldn’t be on the bed?”

			
Irene, like a mother protecting her real-life children cried, “No, Mama Maria! No! These are my sweet bambini.” 

			
Maria made that face, the one she made when she found something to be ridiculous—the one where she’d open her eyes real wide, lift her eyebrows, and look sideways. 

			
“Maybe we pray anyway,” she said, looking up at the gigantic crucifix hanging dangerously over Irene and Joe’s bed. Maria poured some oil into a tiny dish and added a few drops of holy water and some smelly herbs. Irene knelt down next to the doll-covered bed, bowed her head, and put her hands together in prayer. 

			
Carlotta couldn’t bear to be so close to the killer dolls, so Maria told her she could go find her sisters who were playing in the schoolyard. 

			
Carlotta was more than happy to get out of there, so she jumped up, ran out the door, found her sisters in the schoolyard, and told them, “Mama says Auntie Irene’s dolls is cursed. I was up there, and it’s true. Soons Mama walked in, her stomach got to hurtin’.”

			
“Oh that’s ’cause Mama’s got ‘her friend,’ silly,” Clara said like a know-it-all.

			
“No, she ain’t got no friend up there,” Carlotta answered. “Just Auntie and them dolls.”

			
Clara looked up at the sky as though she was exasperated with this child who didn’t understand grown-up stuff. “You’re such a baby, Carlotta,” she said. “Mama’s having her ‘lady business,’ that’s all. You know what ‘lady business’ is, right?”

			
When Carlotta still looked confused, Flo weighed in, “She means she’s bleeding.”

			
“No, no, she ain’t bleeding, she just got a bellyache,” Carlotta answered, shaking her head like she was talking to two real-life dummy dolls. 

			
Conversation done, Carlotta begged the older girls to play Potsy with her even though they tried to act too old to play a kiddie game. They would never admit that they still loved to play, so Flo, pretending to be above it all, nonetheless threw a pebble into the first box on the potsy square which had been chalked in. She hopped on one foot to the box, totally unaware of what was transpiring upstairs at 151 Union Street. 

			
Back at 151, before the ritual had even begun, Maria bent over. Irene, assuming it was a special posture for the pregnancy ceremony, bent over too. When Maria didn’t make any gesture or sound to get the prayers started—she hadn’t even reached for her Rosary—Irene glanced at her sideways. 

			
Should I be doing something, she wondered but was afraid of asking for fear of looking dumb in Maria’s eyes.

			
Nothing. 

			
Finally, after a few minutes had passed, Irene whispered, “Mama?” 

			
A low and painful-sounding groan erupted from Maria. She looked at Irene with fear in her eyes, clutched her stomach, and slumped to the floor. 

			
“Jesu Christa! Mama! Mama!” Irene screamed and lifted her mother-in-law up to a sitting position with the older woman’s back pressed up against the bed rails, her legs splayed out in front. 

			
Maria skidded back down to the floor, slipping in and out of consciousness. Irene ran to the window, leaned out, and screamed for help. People outside stopped, looked up, and ran towards the building. Ferdinando and Anselmo, the ice man, also heard the cry, and the two rushed up the stairs. They found Maria unconscious on the floor with Irene hovering, begging Maria to keep breathing. 

			
Together, the two men carried Maria downstairs to the ice truck. Irene climbed up into the front seat and Ferdinando lifted Maria onto her lap. Ferdinando hopped in the back with the ice, and Anselmo took off at top speed, swerving and honking his way to the Brooklyn Hospital.

			
All three of them screamed for help as they carried Maria into the foreboding stone building.

			
The nurses inside were overwhelmed trying to deal with scores of patients groaning, moaning, coughing up blood, and bleeding from accidents, beatings, and gunshot wounds, so they just ignored the family. 

			
Somehow, Joe, Peter, and Austin had found out through the neighborhood grapevine that their mama had been rushed to the hospital, so all three brothers showed up within minutes. They threw open the front doors and saw their mother lying on a lobby bench in Irene’s arms, moaning. This was a sound they had never heard come out of her—except maybe during childbirth. No matter how terrible things were, nor how much of a brute Mariano had turned into, not even the loss of her babies or the near loss of Peter had ever caused Maria to moan so helplessly. She was Mama who could always fix whatever was wrong.

			
But now tiny Maria cradled in Irene’s big arms looked as lifeless as one of her daughter-in-law’s porcelain dolls. 

			
“Where are the damned doctors? Why aren’t they taking care of her?” the brothers asked Irene and Ferdinando, who answered feebly, “We try, they just go away and not care!”

			
Joe cried out to his mother, “Mama! We’re getting help. Please, please hold on, Mama!” All three rushed off in different directions trying to find a doctor. Austin grabbed the first man he saw wearing a white gown, white cap, and black rubber gloves and begged him, “Please! Please help my mother!” 

			
The doctor brushed him off and turned to walk away, but Joe and Peter, suddenly at his side, surrounded the doctor, never having to say a word to one another. 

			
The doctor’s name, embroidered on the pocket of his white coat, read, “James Morgan III, MD.” 

			
“Peter Barbera the First, Doctor Morgan Three,” he said at first quietly. Then with increasing volume and fury, “Let’s help you get right over there where our mama is suffering since you’re not doing a Goddamned thing about it!” 

			
A little annoyed and a lot frightened, the doctor was pushed along by the brothers like a prisoner surrounded by cops. When they reached the bench where Maria now lay unconscious, Dr. Morgan bent down and made a signal with his hand, so Irene got up to let Ferdinando and Joe lay her head down on the bench. Dr. Morgan felt Maria’s stomach, looked up, and said, “Oh, for God’s sake. It’s just everyday appendicitis. She needs bedrest and only liquids. If she doesn’t get better in a few days, bring her back for surgery. Meantime, if you get on the line over there, right outside by the Rexall sign, the druggist will give you some tablets to give her.”

			
Only appendicitis, they thought, not knowing what the hell appendicitis was. “Does that mean it’s just a bad bellyache?” Joe asked. 

			
“Basically, but it could get worse,” the doctor answered as he walked off, leaving her laid out on the wooden bench like a drunk. 

			
The last thing Dr. Morgan the Third would have ever guessed was that the little immigrant woman he had just dismissed out of hand had probably saved more lives than he ever had and had lived a life more extraordinary than he ever would. 

			
That little woman had survived starvation, servitude, and the slaughter of her family and friends. She had lost six children, some before they took their first breath. She had managed to keep ten of her living children safe in the wild, without resources, through blizzards, a flood, and the threat of savage beasts on a remote mountain. Unable to read, write, or speak much English, she had nonetheless made her way in this new world. She’d been a bootlegger, a landowner, and run a cattle ranch full of ornery hands. She had worked like a man and had become the only woman to earn a place at the table with America’s newest rich and most dangerous men. 

			
Would Dr. Morgan have believed it? For that matter, would any of the respectable, fancy Fifth Avenue families who sent their sons to Harvard and Yale to become doctors and bankers ever acknowledge that their ancestors had once also been immigrants—folks who’d also done the unspeakable to make it big in America? 

			
No, because the stories of those rich, white tycoons had been bleached cleaner than hospital sheets. 

			
Not that it mattered anyway, because Dr. Morgan never turned around to see this newest family tragedy play out. He’d seen that same scene before—probably a thousand times—among the poor immigrants who flooded the hospital day after day. Just another ordinary day in the life of Dr. James Morgan III. 

			
But no matter how many times the Barberas had lived through heartbreaking scenes, this day would never be ordinary for them. 

			
Irene knew that the doctor was wrong. This? This was much more than a bad bellyache. She managed to squeeze onto the bench where Maria was stretched out and once again put her mother-in-law’s head on her lap and stroked her hair. 

			
Before Joe took off sprinting to the Rexall window, he bent down and kissed his mother who was now gasping for air. 

			
He also felt that something was not right. People didn’t gasp for air if their bellies hurt.

			
Peter? He just paced back and forth, helpless, his hands in his pockets, head bent to hide tears. Ferdinando and Anselmo, hands clasped behind their backs, followed him like twin shadows.

			
Austin? He knelt on the floor, held his mother’s hand, and whispered in Sicilian, “Don’t worry, Mama. Joe went for the tablets. They’ll fix you up.” 

			
When she didn’t respond, Austin squeezed her hand. Nothing. 

			
“Mama!” Austin cried, “Mama! Per favore svegliati. Respirare!”—Please wake up. Breathe!

			
But that didn’t happen. Couldn’t happen. Maria was thirty-nine years old.

			
 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 49 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 49 –

			
Nobody Is Better Than You. Nobody.

			
Red Hook, Brooklyn, New York

			
Later that day

			
Clara and Carlotta stopped their game of Potsy and frowned when they saw Joe, Peter, and Austin approaching. They were sure their brothers were going to make them quit playing and go home for supper. 

			
“Ugh. Baccala night,” Clara told Carlotta who stuck her tongue out.

			
“Why can’t we eat meat on Friday instead of—blecchk—baccala?”

			
“Because God died on the cross on Friday, and he liked fish,” Clara answered. 

			
Clara, holding her pebble, looked up at her brothers and begged, “Come on, it’s my turn. I’m winning. Just five more minutes, pleeeeease?” 

			
Instead, Joe knelt down to embrace Clara, and Austin picked Carlotta up in his arms.

			
Peter reached down to Flo, but she shrugged him off. She wasn’t thinking about fish or potsy or any of it. In fact, she didn’t even question why her brothers were there. Instead, her face took on a kind of glow, and she reached out her hands to touch something that none of the others could see. 

			
Right there in front of her, as real as a playground rat, stood Blue. Her beloved Blue.

			
“Blue?” Flo asked and reached for her wolf. “Where have you been, Blue? How did you find me?”

			
Tears streamed down Peter’s face, which caused his normally strong brothers to fight back tears themselves. They were here to tell the children the worst news a child could ever be told, but somehow, they also understood there was a miracle transpiring before them that only Flo could see. 

			
They let the news go unsaid because they felt that, for a moment, and maybe the last one she’d have in a long time, Flo was happy. 

			
They let her have her time with Blue, who seemed to have returned, not to save Flo, but to comfort her, just as she always had done. 

			
As Blue’s image began to fade and Peter came into focus, Flo looked confused, then alarmed. 

			
“Where’s Blue? Why are i miei fratelli all here? What’s wrong?”

			
Joe only said, “We need to go home, girls.” 

			
Flo screamed, “Blue is here! I’m not leaving!”

			
“Blue is with you, always, wherever you are,” Peter said. That’s when Flo knew without anyone saying the words, that both of her mamas were now gone to heaven.

			
It slowly dawned on Clara that something was terribly wrong, so she just dropped her pebble, her eyes big as saucers. Carlotta was still a little girl, so she was just confused. Flo nodded her head at her sisters to stop fussing, and let the brothers take their hands and lead them home. 

			
When they reached 151 Union Street, the girls heard the sound of people crying inside the focacceria. Flo charged up the stairs, knowing, but refusing to believe what Blue had come to tell her. “Mama! Mama!” she cried as she whipped open the apartment door to a scene she would never forget. What seemed like dozens of people—family, friends, people she’d never seen before—were crowded inside the small space, sobbing; some were on their knees—women pulling at their hair, men standing and rocking back and forth. 

			
Everyone turned to stare at the girls, who were afraid to step inside. They somehow knew that once they walked in, life would never be the same again.

			
Laura, Irene, and even the mysterious Callie rushed to the door and tried to usher the kids inside.

			
“Mama!” the children screamed. “Where is Mama?” But they knew she wasn’t there and that she might never be there again.

			
“Where is Mama?” Carlotta screamed again anyway. 

			
Callie tried to take her in her arms, but Carlotta pushed her away. “Go away! I want my mama!”

			
Laura tried to tell them the truth. “Mama got very sick. Sicker than anybody thought….”

			
All three girls started wailing for their mama, wrenching themselves out of the arms holding them, and then, led by Flo, ran out and down the stairs. 

			
Callie, Laura, and Irene chased after them, but the girls were too fast. The older sisters ran to the schoolyard. Not there. Ran to the church. Not there.

			
Frantic, they stopped everyone on the streets to ask if they’d seen the children. 

			
“We need to go home and get the men,” Irene said. “We need to find the girls before it gets dark. Then she added, loud enough for everyone to hear, “Please Saint Anthony, finder of everything lost, please help us find the girls,” she begged. “Please. I vow to give the church one dollar every week of my life if you find them for us.”

			
“We need to find Papa, too,” Laura added, looking more terrified of finding him than of not finding her sisters.

			
Callie, every bit the outsider, suggested, “My husband is very smart, maybe he can find them.”

			
Irene, normally gentle as a falling snowflake finally snapped. She had about had it with this spoiled woman who hadn’t seen her own precious daughter since she was an infant.

			
“Go! Go find your husband and let us find your missing daughter. Just get out of my sight.”

			
Callie and Laura stopped dead in their tracks. Laura, who knew her sister-in-law better than anyone else (and had often been hidden by her when Frank was on a tear) couldn’t believe what she had just heard coming from sweet, quiet Irene. 

			
The tirade wasn’t over. 

			
“You don’t deserve that sweet baby girl you tossed away like garbage. Go!” Irene screamed. It was as though all the pent-up anguish she’d suffered from being unable to conceive, plus the terrible heartbreak of having her beloved mother-in-law die in her arms, came rushing out of her like lava out of Etna. “Go!”

			
Callie began to cry and turned to Laura for support. After all, Irene wasn’t even blood, yet she thought she could talk to her like that. 

			
What nerve.

			
But Laura turned her back on her sister and walked away with her sister-in-law, leaving Callie alone on the street not knowing what to do.

			
When Laura and Irene reached 151 Union Street to get more help, Ferdinando waved them in. They saw the three girls sitting at the counter, tears streaming down their faces, plates of panelli in front of them. 

			
But this was something even panelli couldn’t make better.

			
Trying to ease their pain, the normally standoffish Signora Buffa, who was herself tearing up, brought out a tray of fresh-made cannoli and said, “Mangiate, ragazze dolci, mangiate.”—Eat, sweet girls, eat.

			
Mama was dead and wouldn’t be coming back. That was a fact. Life was bitter despite the sweets laid out in front of them to ease the pain.

			
They had yet to come to grips with the reality that their papa would be their only parent. Who would protect them from him now? 

			
What would become of sweet Austin—the brother who still lived at home and the one their whiskey-soaked, raging father despised for his helplessness and his fits?

			
What would become of them all? 

			
Flo put her hands on her head and sobbed. Was she strong enough to take her mama’s place? It was then that she felt Maria’s arms wrap around her and heard her say, clear as day. “Nobody is better than you. Nobody.” 

			
No one else had heard that, but everybody in Ferdinando’s Focacceria heard the sound that followed: 

			
Ooooowwwooo.

– 50 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 50 –

			
Days as Black as Night

			
151 Union Street

			
May 1930

			
After Signor Pirro finished preparing Maria’s body for viewing, Laura did her mother’s hair in an up-do with the same sparkly hairpins she’d used when Signor Capone and his men had come to the ranch.

			
They dressed her in her best blue taffeta dress with her tiny black pumps on her feet. On the fourth finger of her left hand, Laura put the small diamond wedding band that Maria’s sons had chipped in to buy Maria for her twentieth anniversary. 

			
Joe, Austin, Peter, Mariano, and some men from the funeral parlor carried Maria’s body in a coffin to 151 Union Street and lifted it up the stairs to the family apartment where she would be waked.

			
Signor Pirro and his staff placed the coffin atop a stand in the living room. The funeral had been postponed for a few days until sisters Helen, Mickey, and stepsisters Lucia and Antonella could get there by train. They came without their husbands, who had to stay back to guard the ranch and keep it running. Yellow Fly and Walter had made a circlet of feathers to put around Maria’s head, which the sisters hid for the time being from Mariano.

			
Helen and Mickey slept with Flo in Irene and Joe’s apartment, while Clara and Carlotta reluctantly slept at Callie and Raz’s house with Lucia and Antonella for the duration of the wake. None of them had ever been in a whole house in the city before, which to them looked like a palace in England—not that they wanted to be there. All four slept in the nursery with baby Kat. The only bright spot in those dark days was that the baby took to Carlotta, gurgling and laughing every time she came into view and crying when she went into another room like she knew they were sisters.

			
On day one of the viewing, the family had the first hour of the three-day wake to themselves in order to view Maria in private. Carlotta fainted, Clara threw up, and Flo stood stunned, not crying and not moving. They all thought the same thing: Mama looked like one of Auntie Irene’s porcelain dolls in her long, blue dress and fancy hairdo. She was even in a box just like the ones that the dolls had come in.

			
Austin somehow did not have a fit and just sat alone. He did not speak to anyone and wandered in and out of the apartment.

			
For the next three days people came and went. The older sisters used Ferdinando’s kitchen to prepare food, and Ferdinando himself cooked for them when things got too rough. 

			
Some of the folks who came to pay their respects were friends, some family. All of the La Donas who had moved to New York came, as did the Carlinos—all the way from Colorado.

			
When the Carlinos walked in, Callie got so weak in the knees she nearly fainted and was made to lay down on the couch next to where her mother’s body was laid out.

			
“Always has to steal the show,” someone said, too loud, as she was being revived. 

			
“Can’t face Charlie’s family after what she did,” said someone else. “And now with the new husband and all…” 

			
Her husband, Orazio, meantime, glared at his wife’s late husband’s family and left the wake in order to avoid shaking their hands. 

			
On the day of the funeral itself, which dawned ugly, the family alone gathered around Maria’s body in the coffin. They each bent down to kiss her ice-cold lips. As Mariano did so, he yanked the diamond ring off her finger.

			
Joe shoved his father and demanded that he put the ring back on their mother. “We gave her that ring, and you have no right to take it from her.”

			
Mariano answered, “It’s against the law of America to bury someone with jewelry. Believe me, I will give it to the next daughter who gets married.”

			
No one believed him, but it was not the time nor the place to start a family brawl, so they let it pass until a more appropriate time. 

			
As the scuffle was ensuing and just before Signor Pirro closed the coffin lid, Lucia and Antonella slipped the feather wreath on Maria’s head and made the sign of the cross. 

			
Antonella whispered, “Like Mama always said, ‘Miracles come from more than one God and bad times from more than one devil.’ I think we have both here today.”

			
The coffin lid was closed, and Mariano was never the wiser about the Indian crown of feathers on Maria’s head.

			
The funeral mass at Saint Mary Star of the Sea was presided over by none other than Monsignor Preston himself, with two of the parish’s Sicilian priests in attendance, as well as an organist and full choir. 

			
After the funeral mass, Maria’s coffin was lifted out of the church and into the hearse by six pall bearers: Joe, Austin, Peter, Mariano, Don Vito, and Ferdinando. Maria’s sons all wore black cowboy hats in tribute to the ranching life their mother had loved so well in Colorado. 

			
Maria’s body was taken to the local cemetery, which was crowded with hundreds of mourners, from the powerful to the powerless—everyone whose lives she had touched. 

			
Mariano had always failed to grasp the fact that, at the end of life, a person’s true legacy was how much they were loved and cherished by those they in turn had loved and cherished. He imagined all the big shots there had come to pay their respects to him. 

			
Austin, who had remained calm throughout the ordeal of the last few days, lost his composure when the coffin was lowered into the ground. First, he let out a wail, then dropped to the ground; his eyes rolled back in his head, and his body went into full seizure.

			
Flo, who had taken her mother’s place taking care of Austin, had carried a spoon in Maria’s handbag in case her brother went into one of his spells. She handed it to Joe who put it into Austin’s mouth to keep him from biting off his tongue. 

			
Mariano was furious. Once again, Austin had disgraced him in front of the Carlinos, and now, as he watched him seizing, his only thought was that he would be stuck with this freak forever. No more Maria to cater to him. 

			
Mariano’s face told the story of his anger and disgust, causing every mourner to have the same thought: What would become of poor Austin?

			
That night, Mariano left his grieving family in the apartment and went to his favorite tavern. It was the same one where he’d gambled away Maria’s money within the first month of moving to Brooklyn. But this night, he skipped the poker game in the back and made a big show in the crowded front room by kissing his latest mistress, the one he called Cora-Lee Due, although God knows what her name really was. 

			
Then, like a carnival magician, he waved his hands around, reached into his pocket, and pulled out Maria’s little diamond ring, stuck it on Cora-Lee Due’s pinky finger, raised her hand, and announced, “Drinks on me you bums!” 

			
Three days later Austin disappeared. 

			
 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 51 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 51 –

			
Unloved, Unwanted, Unhoused

			
Red Hook, Brooklyn, New York 

			
May 1930

			
Two days after the funeral when everyone had gone home, Flo and Clara picked Carlotta up from school and took her home to the 151 Union Street apartment. It was the first time they were alone without Mama or their big sisters.

			
Austin was still at home, but he was weeping and trying to fry up some meat to make gravy for the pasta. He’d never made gravy before, and it wasn’t going well.

			
Flo and Clara had helped Mama make gravy before, and it looked like Austin had gone and burnt all the meat. He’d tried to make braciole, too, but forgot to stuff it with breadcrumbs or to even make breadcrumbs. Instead, he rolled up a piece of beef, tied it up with some string he found on a box of cannoli, which a mourner had brought, and fried it to death.

			
The three girls had a big laugh with Austin about the mess he’d made, which was good because it was the first laugh they’d had since Mama had passed.

			
As they were trying to clean it all up, Mariano walked in, saw what was going on, and smacked Austin across the face, “Tu sì ritardatu e nun apparteni â famigghia!” Mariano screamed, saying he was “retarded” and no longer belonged with the family.

			
When Austin wept harder, Mariano pushed him down, which caused the girls to cry. Mariano reached for Austin again, but Flo stepped in. 

			
“Papa! Please, just go out. We’ll take care of this. Please.”

			
Mariano grabbed his hat off the table where he’d flung it and slammed out the door.

			
The four siblings looked at one another. What now?

			
Austin had some money left over from the last time he’d played piano and said he’d take them all downstairs for a bowl of pasta con sarde, which is what they did. Mariano didn’t return home that night, so they were all able to sleep a little better than if he’d been there and raging.

			
The following morning, Flo, Clara, and Carlotta went off to school, kissing Austin goodbye. He promised them he’d do better making supper that night.

			
When the girls arrived home from school, Austin was nowhere to be found, and supper hadn’t been started.

			
Carlotta went next door to see if Auntie Irene had seen him, and she said she hadn’t since the night before. When Joe came home from the docks, Irene and the girls asked him if he’d spotted Austin, but he, too, had not.

			
Irene prepared enough food for all of them, so they ate supper in silence, each worried in their own way about where Austin could be.

			
Flo finally piped up. “If Austin is lost and he starts to fit, what will happen to him?” That got them all to crying again, so Joe told the girls to clean up the supper dishes, and he and Irene would go look for him.

			
They enlisted Peter, and the three of them scoured the neighborhood, but no one had seen him. 

			
At about eleven, Mariano sauntered in. When they told him that Austin was missing, he looked neither surprised nor concerned. Peter and Joe exchanged glances, and Peter grabbed his father by the shirt.

			
“If you done anything to Austin you better ’fess up right now, or you’ll be sorry you were ever born, you lousy piece of shit.”

			
When Mariano pulled back his arm to land a punch, Joe grabbed it and twisted it around his father’s back. Mariano’s sons were no longer boys he could beat at will. They were grown, strong men, and Mariano was a drunk. 

			
“I don’t know,” Mariano slurred. “For all I know, he had a fit and fell in the river.” 

			
After days of searching and going from police station to hospital to police station searching for Austin, Maria’s children had to face the fact that he was gone.

			
After much discussion, it was decided that Irene and Joe should pay a visit to Callie in her big stone house in Bensonhurst. Callie, busy with baby Kat, was shocked when she answered the knock on her front door to discover Irene, Joe, Jeannette, and Peter standing there.

			
She invited them in, sat them on the velvet sofa and chair in the living room, and brought out a tray with demitasse cups, a pot of espresso, and a bottle of Anisette. 

			
Thinking perhaps they’d brought Irene over to apologize, she sat down with them and put baby Kat on the floor on a blanket.

			
They all stared at each other for a bit until it got downright uncomfortable. Joe finally spoke up.

			
“Thing’s ‘r bad over at the apartment,” he said.

			
Callie just blinked, totally unaware of what her responsibility in this whole tragedy was supposed to be.

			
Figuring whatever was coming was not going to be good for her, she poured an extra big shot of Anisette into her espresso, sat up straighter, and folded her hands in her lap like the Queen of England meeting her subjects.

			
“Yes, I figured. Papa can be very strict.”

			
Peter jumped up. “Strict? Strict? Che cazzo dici? He’s a shit!”

			
Callie smoothed her skirt and said, “Well, yes, I know that too. I lived with him long enough, remember?”

			
“So you agree,” Irene managed to say, “that living with him is no place for your daughter. She won’t last ‘till she’s ten with that man!”

			
Callie, trying not to bring the conversation where she didn’t want it to go while trying to sound outraged at the same time, said, “You’re not thinking of putting her in an orphanage are you?”

			
Peter smacked his head. “Va fa Napoli,” he said, which kind of made no sense, but everybody knew he was trying not to get vulgar again. Not that it was easy to keep his temper with this sister of his. 

			
“We are here to say that Carlotta needs a mother—”

			
“And she’s got a mother,” Irene cut in. 

			
Callie picked Kat up and smoothed the blonde fuzz on her head. “But I have a baby to care for, and well, Carlotta doesn’t even like me. She sure don’t like Raz.”

			
They all stared at her, surprised that anything she said, thought, or did could still shock them. 

			
It took a lot to get Joe hot under the collar, but his collar was practically shooting flames at that point. “That’s because she don’t even know you! And it’s just about time that girl learnt the truth!” 

			
“The truth?” Callie sputtered. “But Raz—”

			
Irene stood up. “You mean to tell us that Raz don’t know little Carlotta is your daughter?”

			
“I didn’t say that!” Callie shot back like a fast ball pitcher. “It’s just that he don’t like the idea that…well, that, I, you know, had another man in my bed and that anybody might know it.”

			
They all looked at each other. Peter stood up and over Callie. “Anybody might know it? The whole of friggin’ Pueblo knew it. You were louder than a bunch of cats makin’ kittens. Every night.”

			
Joe jumped in, “And every mornin’. You was the only one who wasn’t embarrassed by your bed sounds, so don’t give me that shit.”

			
“What exactly do you expect me to do at this point, anyway?” Callie asked, clearly annoyed.

			
Jeannette finally spoke. “We expect you to do the right thing. That’s what. And don’t act like you don’t know what that is.”

			
“I have to talk to Raz about it,” Callie answered.

			
“If you don’t I will,” Joe snapped. “And if you don’t do it tonight, I’ll be back tomorrow to talk to that husband of yours—if he can stop writin’ plays long enough to listen.” 

			
“Oh, I think you’ll make him listen,” Irene said.

			
The four of them got up to leave, Peter turning over his full cup of espresso onto Callie’s fake Turkish carpet. 

			
“Oops.”

			
Laura was the one chosen to deliver the news to Carlotta. She gave her the truth but nothing like the truth.

			
She quietly explained to Carlotta, Flo, and Clara that “Auntie” Callie wanted very much for Carlotta to live with her for the time being.

			
“She feels that because you are still a little girl, it wouldn’t be good for you to live here with Papa.”

			
“Why can’t I live with you then?” Carlotta begged Laura.

			
“I would love nothing better, but you know Frank. He’s meaner than Papa and twice as drunk.”

			
They all agreed that was true.

			
“How long do I have to stay?” Carlotta asked.

			
Laura couldn’t answer this, which caused all three kids to become hysterical again.

			
“Mama died, Austin’s gone missin’, and now Carlotta has to go live with Callie and whazzisname, Mister Big Shot?” Flo sobbed.

			
Three days later, Callie, Orazio, and baby Kat showed up to take Carlotta away. Once again, Carlotta knew, as she must have known as an infant, that she was not wanted and would never really belong anywhere with anyone ever again.

– 52 –

			
Ruby’s Lips

			
All Around New York

			
December 31, 1930

			
It was the eve of Flo’s fifteenth birthday, the first one without her mama. Maria had always made a big fuss for each of her kids’ birthdays, bringing out a homemade cake flaming with candles, the family singing, “Tanti Auguri a Te”—“Many Wishes to You.” Flo would never forget how they’d all applaud wildly when she blew out the candles as though she’d completed an impossible task.

			
Last year, even though she’d gotten a pair of patent leather pumps from the Sears and Roebuck catalog for Christmas, Mama had presented her a week later on her birthday with a new store-bought dress. It had roses on it and a real silk sash around the waist.

			
So what? she thought. If there wasn’t going to be a cake or a birthday celebration, she’d put that fancy dress on and take a few trolley cars to Callie’s house to visit Carlotta, whom she missed like crazy. 

			
When Flo got there, her little “sister” was so excited to see her that she turned somersaults in the living room and then clung to her happy as a boardinghouse pup. She jumped up, twirled Flo around, and sang, “Happy Birthday to you, Happy Birthday to Firenze…” using her given Italian name which she thought was about the cleverest thing she’d ever done in her whole life.

			
Flo was shocked that not only had Carlotta remembered it was her birthday, but also that she’d made her a card with hearts and real lace, even though the little girl had no idea she’d see her that day.

			
Callie had forgotten that it was Flo’s birthday, but she tried to make up for it by telling her that she would ask Raz to take them all out to a local restaurant. “It’s not just your birthday. It’s New Year’s Eve,” Callie said. “Time this family had a celebration.”

			
Callie knew she wasn’t supposed to, but she put some lipstick on Flo anyway, because as she said, “You’re practically a grown-up lady now.”

			
Flo couldn’t believe how pretty she looked with her lips painted bright ruby red. She’d never thought of herself as pretty before, because she was too busy being, as Laura called her, “a Tom boy.” 

			
“This must be what my lips looked like when I was a baby,” she said.

			
Callie told Flo that that they’d been so bright red some folks thought that Mama had gone and painted those baby lips with rouge.

			
“Really?” Flo answered, turning her face this way and that as she stared at herself in the mirror atop Callie’s giant mahogany bureau. “I kinda ’member when they used to be red. But then when Blue got runned over by the train, they stopped bein’ ruby red and turned pale. Clara told me true, that when we almost got run down ourselves that first day in Brooklyn, my lips went all red again. But just for a minute.”

			
Callie didn’t think her husband would cotton to such spooky nonsense, so she dropped the subject and told Flo not to talk about it in front of him.

			
When Raz got home from work, Callie told him—not asked him, mind you—to take them all to a pizzeria to celebrate. Flo and Carlotta had never tasted anything as good in their whole lives as that pizza pie topped with pepperoni, they said. 

			
“I feel like I died and went to res-runt heaven,” Carlotta exclaimed taking a giant bite. 

			
Callie, Flo thought, was not at all like the mean lady her older sisters and mother had said she was. And Uncle Raz seems a pretty good feller too.

			
Then, when she thought the night couldn’t get any better, Raz drove her home in his Buick car. 

			
Now this is livin’.

			
The only bad part was that when they reached 151 Union Street, Carlotta got to crying and begging to come back upstairs to live with her and Clara again. But as they all knew, that could never be. 

			
Living with Callie and Raz wasn’t the best situation for Carlotta, but it sure beat living with Mariano and the puttanas he’d bring home. And besides, it was the only situation possible.

			
Flo tried to hop out of the car, but Carlotta clung to her like a monkey to a tree. She gently pried the little girl’s arms off, promising to come back the next week to visit.

			
She felt awful about leaving Carlotta, but truth be told, she also had a bit of a spring in her step because Callie had made her feel very special on her first birthday without her mama. Flo was also anxious to get upstairs to Clara so they could ring in the new year together. They’d get out the pots, pans, and spoons and make a world of racket at midnight—just like Mama used to do.

			
But as Flo climbed the stairs, she heard yelling and furniture being tossed around. 

			
Oh God, what now? she thought, dreading what was waiting for her inside the apartment when she opened the door.

			
Mariano was drunk and Clara was whimpering, trying to hide herself under the table. From the way their father was lunging at her sister, it was clear the bastard had filth on his mind.

			
The sight brought Flo an unexpected flash of memory that she’d kept hidden in her brain since she was a little girl.

			
Mickey. Colorado. Her mother. A gun. 

			
So, not thinking—or maybe not caring—about the consequences, she jumped on her father’s back, punching and kicking. 

			
He tossed her off and saw that she had painted lips. The shock of seeing his daughter with those same red lips she’d had as a devil baby made him stop thinking about groping Clara and start wailing on Flo instead.

			
“Whore!” he screamed, reaching for her and ripping the dress her mama had given her one year ago to the day. 

			
“Ruby! Painted lips like a puttana! Ruby the whore.”

			
With that, “Ruby” grabbed a kitchen knife, which he easily snatched out of her hand, yelling, “Strega! Mavara! La strega deve morire!”—Witch! The witch must die—over and over.

			
Clara scooted out from under the table and tried to stop him, but she was helpless as a little bug against the big man.

			
“You try to touch my sister again, and I will kill you. I swear!” Flo screamed.

			
Mariano laughed and lunged at Flo again, kicking her in her groin. No matter the pain, Flo refused to fall.

			
As she lunged back at him, the apartment filled with a sound so loud it was nearly deafening. Mariano’s hand dropped to his side, paralyzed, the knife falling and sticking into the linoleum. 

			
No, the sound wasn’t coming from New Year’s Eve whistles outside, it was coming from inside. 

			
Ooowwwooo.

			
It couldn’t have been more than a few seconds later that they heard footsteps running up the stairs. The door flung open, and Joe and Irene rushed in, done up in their finest celebratin’ clothes. 

			
Joe dug the knife out of the floor, and Irene grabbed hold of the girls who were scratched up and beaten, Flo’s beautiful dress all torn up.

			
Irene managed to get the hysterical sisters back to her own apartment and cleaned their wounds as Clara sobbed and clung to her. Flo would not allow herself to shed even one tear.

			
Joe was furious—not a thing that often happened to the level-headed cowpoke. He threw his father down to the floor, spit at him, and made sure he aimed it so that the glob of phlegm landed on the older man’s lips. 

			
“I’ll deal with you tomorrow,” he warned. Then he grabbed the house keys and locked the door from the outside trapping Mariano inside to rage alone.

			
The next morning, the girls, Joe, and Irene woke to a gentle snowfall blanketing the whole town. It made everything seem so calm and peaceful that it was hard to imagine what had happened a few hours earlier had really happened. 

			
That afternoon, with Mariano still locked in the apartment and snoring it off, they left to join some of the rest of the family for the holiday meal at Peter and Jeannette’s apartment. 

			
The girls couldn’t go back into their own home, and with all the stores closed for the holiday, they had to keep on the same torn and blood-spattered clothes they’d had on the night before.

			
When they walked into their brother and sister-in-law’s apartment to the delicious smells of roasting pork and fresh baked bread, the family festivities were already underway. But the shock of seeing those two teenage kids battered so bad turned the celebration into a meeting instead.

			
Laura was there with her kids, but not her husband. Callie and her family hadn’t been invited. 

			
“We won’t go back to Union Street and Papa ever again,” Flo announced. 

			
It didn’t surprise anyone, but the question remained: Where would they live?

			
The girls sat silent as they listened to everyone else debate their fate: They couldn’t move in with Irene and Joe as long as the brute still lived next door, that was for sure. Maybe they could move in with Callie and that new husband of hers. But, as Jeannette pointed out, Orazio was already annoyed that they’d taken in Carlotta. He’d go mad with two more kids. The girls were certainly welcome to move in with Peter, Jeannette, and their babies, but the apartment was already overcrowded to overflowing. The final suggestion was that they could stay with Laura and her family in Frank’s family’s apartment. No way. That was the worst of all possible solutions. 

			
The only thing that was clear was that the girls couldn’t and wouldn’t stay with Mariano.

			
It was about then that Flo noticed that in addition to her bruises, she had developed big, scaly red patches overnight on her arms and the tops of her hands. 

			
This city, this father, this way of life was destroying her.

			
She heard someone come up with the bright idea that Flo and Clara could each live with different members of the family.

			
Flo could move in with Peter, while Clara could stay with Callie. Not perfect but it was safe. 

			
Flo was only fifteen, but she knew what she had to do. There was no way on earth that she’d be separated from Clara.

			
She stood up, trying as best as she could to cover up the ugly, scaly patches on her skin.

			
“Everyone be quiet!” she yelled over the din. The room for the first time fell silent. 

			
“I have something to say. Me and Clara—we will go back to Colorado,” she announced. “We can live with Mickey, Antonella, and Jack, Yellow Fly, Black Bill, Helen, and Silas. We want to be with our animals and ride our horses. We can be cowgirls just like Mickey. That’s what we want to do. That’s what we will do.”

			
When the family got over the shock of Flo taking charge of her life over the plans of her family, or their attempt at plans, they all started talking at once.

			
So, Clara stood up and yelled, “Everybody! Shut up! Yup. That’s what we’re gonna do.”

			
Flo put her arm around her sister and spat out, “We won’t be separated, and we won’t live in this terrible city one more minute.” 

			
Then she grabbed a few struffoli—honey balls—from the platter of sweets on the table. “If you don’t send us back, I swear we’ll hop a freight train.”

			
She popped one of the tiny fried treats into her mouth and declared, “OK, it’s settled then? We’re goin’ home.”

			
Clara grabbed her sister’s hand for courage and repeated, “We’re goin’ home.” 

			
Then she stopped and added, “Maybe that’s where Austin went too. Home. To Colorado.”

– 53 –

			
Loss and Found

			
New York to Colorado

			
January 1931

			
So, it was decided. Flo and Clara would go back to Pueblo. 

			
Until they could leave, though, the girls crowded in with Irene and Joe, everyone on Mariano high alert. It would be at least two weeks before they could finish their travel arrangements and get it set up with Mickey and the rest. 

			
Then out of the clear blue sky, three days before they were scheduled to leave, Flo handed Joe and Peter a cable. 

			
“What is this?” Peter wanted to know.

			
“It’s where Austin is. We want to take him with us to Colorado.”

			
The cable read: “Alive. Stop. Pilgrim State insane asylum.”

			
The brothers looked confused. “Huh?”

			
“I wrote a letter to Don Vito when Austin disappeared. I figgered if anyone could find him, it would be him. I hoped he might send someone to look for him, and sure enough, they found him. That’s what this cable means,” she answered.

			
When they all stared at her, stunned, she finished up by saying, “I didn’t think he’d run away, and I for sure knew he weren’t dead. Don’t ask me how I knew. I just knew.”

			
The brothers turned the cable over in their hands. Joe asked, more to himself than to the others, “Papa? The sumnabitch had him committed?”

			
“It sure looks that way,” Peter said, shaking his head and slamming his fist into his other hand. “Now we just gotta find this place.”

			
“I know where it is,” Flo piped up. “I went to the library, and the lady there helped me. It’s in a town called Brentwood in a place called Long Island.”

			
Early the next morning, Flo, Joe, and Peter were heading out to Long Island with a bag of Austin’s clothes in a big Cadillac sedan that Joe had borrowed.

			
The drive was long—almost fifty miles—and cold. The car was supposed to have heat, but it was a lotta show and no go. 

			
They thought the same thing about Pilgrim State when they pulled up. It looked like it was built for show and no go, with its massive brick walls and hundreds of barred and caged windows. This was not a hospital made to comfort the mad, but to incarcerate them. The gigantic structure had a center entrance with two wings jutting out on each side like the arms of a giant. It looked frightening—at least from the outside.

			
“Why would they build a place that looks so awful to hold people who are sick?” Flo asked. There was no answer.

			
Inside was worse. Folks in every state of crazy were wandering the halls screaming, mumbling to themselves, walking like zombies, crying, and fighting. To call it terrifying would be to make it seem more like a carnival fright house than the scary asylum it really was.

			
They found the administrator’s office and asked for Austin Barbera. After a few hours of waiting, a man in a dark suit came around to tell them which ward he was in.

			
“We’re his family and we’re here to take him home,” Peter said.

			
“Home?” the man asked pointing to the paper in his hand. “It says right here: ‘Austin Barbera was admitted suffering from insanity. He doesn’t have a home or a family.’” 

			
“I’m here to tell you different,” Joe said.

			
“And I’m here to make sure you understand,” Peter added.

			
“Hmmm. Perhaps we can start with a short visit,” the man offered, more as an order than a suggestion. 

			
“Let’s start with ripping up that form,” Peter answered. “Then you can go ahead and get another form that gets him sprung from this joint.”

			
“Well, it’s more complicated than that,” the man said.

			
“No, I don’t think it is,” Joe snapped. “We’re family, he’s a grown man, and he was confined against his will. The fella who got him committed—our father—is a drunk and had no authority to do it. Austin is not a minor.”

			
The man just stood there, so Flo said, “Mister, I think you should do what my brother said. Right away.”

			
The implied threat did not go over well, but he knew the family knew they were within their rights, and besides, the place might be brand new but was already crowded.

			
They followed him to the ward where they were met by a huge male attendant standing in front of a bolted door. He walked them down another hall to Austin’s “room.” Joe looked through the tiny window of the locked metal door of the cell and saw Austin lying on the floor, facing the brick wall. He was bound in a straitjacket. 

			
“Get him out of that contraption right now,” Joe demanded.

			
The attendant smirked at them and said, “Can’t do that. He’s a violent one, that one.” 

			
“He’s the gent’list gent on earth,” Flo cried. “He’d never hurt no one!”

			
“That just ain’t so, missy,” the attendant said. “He hit me when we tried to put him in the tubs, and he hit the other fellas too.”

			
They didn’t even want to know what “the tubs” were. 

			
Flo stood on tippy toes to look for herself into the window and almost sicked-up at the sight of her beloved brother all bound up on the floor. “Austin!” she screamed. “Austin! We’re here to get you out.”

			
After several more hours of paperwork with Joe signing this and that official document while Peter and Flo stood outside their brother’s cell, Austin was released.

			
An attendant removed the straitjacket and led him, wobbling, out of the cell door to his waiting family and into the bright electric lights of the hallway. He rubbed his eyes because all he could see were spots as they went from the dark cell to the glaring lights. They all prayed that the lights—a few of which were blinking from a power shortage or some damn thing—wouldn’t bring on a seizure. If that happened, they knew he could be committed all over again. 

			
Austin himself thought maybe he was already having a seizure because once the spots cleared up, he couldn’t believe what he saw before him was real. How could his brothers and sister actually be there right in front of him? 

			
“We come to take you home, brother,” Joe said as Austin collapsed in tears into his arms.

			
“Papa…” Austin tried to say, but confinement had confused him so much that he couldn’t continue. He didn’t know what was true, what was a dream, what was a lie.

			
“Flo is the one who found you,” Peter said. This seemed to give Austin a moment of clarity before he went blank again.

			
They changed him into the clothes Peter had brought and walked him to the car. As they were piling in, Austin started to cry. “No! No, Papa, no.”

			
“You will never see that bastard again,” Peter assured him. “Never.”

			
Flo took his hand. “Austin, we’re all going to live in Colorado. You, me, and Clara. Not Papa.”

			
He looked confused but understood on some level that he was safe. It would be days or maybe even weeks before the barbiturate, Luminal, which he’d been force-fed in the asylum was out of his system.

			
Austin stayed with Peter, his wife, and children, never speaking much but crying a lot. Would he ever be the same, they all wondered.

			
Two weeks later, although he was hardly himself but at least had started to communicate, the family, who had delayed the girls’ trip home, decided it was finally time for Austin, Flo, and Clara to make the move.

			
Bags packed, they boarded the train to go back to the ranch in the opposite direction from which they’d come seven years earlier.

			
“I’ll write every single day,” declared Clara from the open window of the moving train to the family waving goodbye from the platform. “Every day. I promise.”

			
Laura smiled through wet eyes, but Irene wept uncontrollably. 

			
“Joe, I know it’s for the best, but those girls are like my own children.”

			
Joe hugged his wife and said, “Sweetheart, I promise one day I’ll take you to Colorado. Maybe even to live there.” 

			
She didn’t know that, much to his own peril, Joe had removed the two porcelain dolls Irene had stuffed into the girls’ luggage for good luck. He hid them in the basement of the old building where Irene never ventured. 

			
Them girls have enough to deal with without getting scared out of their wits when they open their bags, he’d thought. He figured he’d deal with Irene when or if they went back to the ranch one day and Irene went looking for her bambini. 

			
As the train pulled clear of the station—porcelain doll-less—Austin was frightened. Clara was excited. Flo held off on having an opinion for the time being.

			
They settled in and right off dug into the tomato and Provolone sandwiches Irene had made for them, each thinking the same thing: Will it be the same? What will become of us?

– 54 –

			
Return of the Ruby Rover

			
Barbera Ranch, Colorado 

			
January 1931

			
“The hell with waiting for them in Pueblo!” Mickey declared. “We’re going to Denver to grab our kin right when they get off that train in the city.” 

			
“But their tickets are paid for all the way to Pueblo,” cranky Helen protested. “I love them and all, but that’s no cheap ticket.”

			
Mickey shot her a look. “Jesus Christ, Helen. You were born a cheapskate, and you’ll die a cheapskate.” Cranky Helen looked even more cranky and walked away in a huff with Mickey calling after her like a bird, “Cheap, cheap, cheap.”

			
When their train pulled into the Denver station, Flo, Clara, and Austin reached for their suitcases and climbed off and onto the platform looking for the track where the train to Pueblo was scheduled to arrive in several hours. There were more tracks than they’d expected. They had forgotten just how big the Denver station was.

			
“Just one more train ride, and we’ll be home again,” Flo assured her brother and sister, hoping she sounded brave as all get out and not scared to death like she really was. The new plan, she announced, was for them to go inside the station and look at the big board to see which track and at what time the Pueblo train would pull in.

			
Before they got more than a few feet, they heard screaming and yelling. 

			
“Oh my God!” Clara said, practically losing her breath. “It’s them. It’s them!”

			
Running towards them, arms outstretched, were a group of characters as colorful and cowpoke-looking as you could imagine. Mickey, Helen, Yellow Fly, and Walnut Jack. 

			
The three travelers dropped their bags and ran to them, eager to be taken into their big arms—especially Mickey’s, which were now especially big.

			
Yellow Fly, his braids flying out from the bottom of a top hat, a giant medallion flopping over his coat, ran right past, shouting, “Valises!” In their excitement they had dropped their bags, leaving them free for thieves to make off with.

			
Mickey, who was garbed in wide overalls, a big, banged-up, flat-brimmed hat, and a jacket made of pelts, grabbed Clara, hugging her so tight the girl thought she’d turn blue and faint. 

			
Austin recognized Walnut Jack, who rushed up, engulfing him in a bear hug. The giant would have been hard to mistake for anyone else anyway, seeing how he was dressed pretty much the same as Mickey. But he was now missing the familiar eye patch over the mess that was once a working eye. He had long ago declared that he wasn’t going to wear it anymore because he thought the patch made him look “too citified.”

			
Helen, in a deerskin coat and cowboy boots, her hair done up high and fancy, reached for Flo and threw her arms around her kid sister, who immediately burst into tears. 

			
It was the first time since Maria’s funeral that Flo had allowed herself to cry.

			
Safe. They were safe.

			
They piled into the bed of a pickup truck, bundled under big, thick, wool blankets for the drive all the way back to the ranch. Never had freezing cold and falling snow felt so wonderful.

			
When they saw the huge Circle Double Bar A sign piled high with snow like a hat, Flo and Clara got up, threw off their blankets, and did a crazy dance. They jumped out of the moving truck, running the rest of the way home, arms raised, reaching for the endless sky.

			
They could see right off that things were different from what they remembered. The barn was bigger and so was the ranch house itself. Cattle were grazing on what wheat they could forage as well as the giant bales of hay that had been collected and stored by the ranch hands for winter. 

			
Cowpokes they’d never seen before were herding on horses that they’d also never seen before. As they approached the house they passed a new corral too. They were met on the new, wide front steps by Antonella, Lucia, and Flip, who’d grown almost as tall as the two tiny women. There was also a little girl standing with them who might have been the baby Lucia had been carrying when they left. Antonella, who’d been holding a newborn in her arms, passed the infant off to Flip. 

			
Are they glad to see us or angry we’ve returned, Flo wondered, as the two stepsisters met their big hugs with stiff arms and half smiles.

			
Austin, Clara, and Flo walked into the house, pointing out this new room and that new contraption. Antonella then led the way to their bedroom as though it was her house and not theirs.

			
When Flo took off her coat and lifted her suitcase onto the bed, the sleeves of her blouse rode up, revealing the scaly patches on her elbows and arms.

			
Antonella screamed and pointed at them, as though the newly returned girl had become a monster. 

			
Flo quickly pulled down her sleeves to cover up her shame. I think I got happy too soon, she thought.

			
Mickey noticed her sister’s sores but also her “wife’s” reaction, and without even asking what had happened to Flo’s arms, she spun Antonella around and said, “Here’s the way it is, dearie. You don’t be lookin’ at my sister like that or actin’ like you just done, or you might find yourself right back where you come from.” 

			
Antonella started to protest that Flo could be carrying a disease that would get them all sick. Mickey stopped her cold. “Va eccati!” she snapped, which basically means, “go jump in a lake,” “get lost,” “calm the hell down,” or whatever the speaker wants it to mean.

			
Antonella sulked away, at which point Mickey put her arm around Flo and said, “Don’t pay her no mind. You ’member how she always was. She just don’t want me payin’ no attention to nobody who ain’t her.” And then she followed Antonella out the door to have a bigger “Don’t-you-give-my-kin-no-shit” talking-to with Antonella and her sister.

			
It began with, “When you two sickly, misshapen kids was orphans, we took you in. But Flo, Austin, and Clara? They’re blood.”

			
Nobody mentioned that they weren’t yet orphans when Joe had found them.

			
Cranky Helen saw Flo’s scaly patches and said, “Don’t you fret, darlin.’ We’ll take you to the doc who will fix you right up.”

			
“Doc Dawson?” Flo asked.

			
Helen scowled making her dimples flash. “Oh honey, Doc Dawson tied one on last spring and fell off a ridge. They say he was a goner before he hit bottom.”

			
“He was a nice man,” Clara remembered, walking into the room.

			
“Nice and drunk,” Helen added. “But we got a new doc now. He’s not a drunk, but he ain’t as good as Doc Dawson neither.” 

			
Fat chance he’ll be able to fix me ‘right up’ then, Flo realized. 

			
But at least she was home. Or was this still her home? 

			
I want my mama, Flo yearned, fighting back months and months of longing and pain as she looked around at the house that she had imagined would ease the hurt. But home was her mama—not a place. 

			
Now what?

			
While they all stood there uncomfortably looking at one another, from outside came the most beautiful sound in the whole wide world. The only sound that could ease her pain. 

			
Ooowoooo.
