– 1 –

			
The Feral Baby

			
Wet Mountains, Colorado 

			
December 31, 1915 11:59 PM 

			
The moment 1915 turned into 1916, two things happened on Greenhorn Mountain that would change everything everywhere forever: A feral baby girl was born. And the seed for the American Mafia in the West was sewn. 

			
They say that baby girl came barreling out of her mama’s belly howling—not crying mind you, but howling—louder than the blizzard roaring outside, louder than the hungry wolves circling that Godforsaken two-room shack where the fugitive La Barbera family had been hiding out for nearly two years. Another girl was the last thing her brute of a father, Mariano La Barbera, wanted. 

			
She wasn’t the first, and she wouldn’t be the last. 

			
But this one? She had that wild thing about her—like a wolf you rescue as a pup. No matter how you try to domesticate it, it will always have an untamed instinctive part that wants out, that hunger that’s got to be satisfied, that instinct to survive and fight. 

			
Mariano knew that. 

			
The kids knew that with his jug of homemade brew already half-emptied plus the howls of their newborn sister, the bleeding of their mama, and their own whimpering, their papa was being driven into such a frenzy that he might go ahead and kill them all.

			
“Strega!” he bellowed each time the newborn opened her bright, ruby-red lips and howled. Then in Sicilian, “Mavara!”—Witch.

			
But their mama—her name was Maria—knew that wasn’t true, and she wasn’t going to let her new baby be called such names, such filth. Normally a quiet woman who had long ago accepted the harshness of life—and death—in America, she suddenly gave out a cry so loud the children heard it even over the deafening roar of the raging blizzard. Their papa didn’t notice, or maybe he just didn’t give a damn. 

			
Maria’s scream was so piercing, the kids thought maybe she was hemorrhaging and dying. They had seen new mothers die plenty of times back in the camp. They’d just bleed out until there was nothing left. 

			
They were not only scared—they were also desperate. If Mama died, they’d be on their own with no one to protect them. Their snake-headed father would let them die—or beat them to death—as sure as eggs is eggs. 

			
“Mama! Per favore Mama!”—Please Mama!—they begged. But her answer didn’t comfort them either.

			
She only let out a low rumble and rasped, “Firenze!” She took a breath, the frozen air rushing out of her and tried again, louder this time: “Firenze!” 

			
Teenage Pietrantonio, who called himself “Peter” in the American way, didn’t understand and thought she might be calling upon a saint for help. “Mama, stai bonu? Ti nesci sangu?”—Are you alright? Are you bleeding? 

			
Maria shook her head, touched his face, and said, “Nun mi servi nenti,”—I don’t need anything. Then the woman who never made any decision without her husband’s consent, declared in as strong a way as she could, “Lu picciriddu si chiama Firenze.”—The baby is called Firenze. 

			
The kids forgot for a moment how cold and hungry they were and just stood in front of their parents’ makeshift wooden bed, dumbfounded. Then they all stared at their papa, knowing he’d strike, not caring that their mama had just given birth and was holding a brand-new baby. But no, that crazy bastard didn’t move. He didn’t seem to be among them any longer. He was in another place. 

			
Without blinking or acknowledging anyone, he got up, opened the cabin door, and let the blizzard blow right in. He stepped outside, threw his hands up, and yelled, “Si chiama Firenze? Si chiama Firenze? Mavara!”

			
Then, remembering that the infant had howled, not cried, he realized with horror that she might be worse than a witch, she might be lupa mannaro—a monstrosity. It was a common belief in Sicily that babies conceived during a full moon would become lupo mannaro. He didn’t remember when the newest baby was conceived, of course, because he took Maria every night in the most brutal ways. 

			
This night though, he wasn’t thinking about sex as he stood in the demon blizzard as if it was as easy on him as the air on a warm spring day. He was a broken man and wasn’t able to feel much of anything anymore except anger. But now, for the first time in his life, he felt fear. 

			
He reached down and picked up a splintered and snow-covered log and felt its heft before turning back to the door. The bambina had to be killed before it grew.

			
He went back inside, pounding the log over and over into his palm as he moved towards Maria and the baby. This is what it had come to. No food, no money, no escape, and now his wife had given birth to a girl—and a wild one at that. 

			
End this before it begins. 

			
When he reached the bed, the kids screamed—but not at their papa. A massive grey wolf—a lupa—with unnatural blue eyes had followed him into the room. Covered in ice and snow, she bared her giant fangs and growled. Then she leapt towards the bed. 

			
There was no place to hide. It was so quick, even Mariano snapped out of his trance long enough to get his wits about him and quickly retreated outside, never turning his back on the animal—only on his freezing children.

			
Once, he had been a man so brave he’d fought all the power in the world in order to make a better life for his family. Now he was a man who would abandon them inside a cabin with a wild beast. Not that he’d ever admit to such a thing. 

			
Perhaps he hoped the beast would save him the trouble of killing the crazy, howling infant and the smaller kids, too, since they’d put up the least fight. 

			
Inside the shack, the children watched, horrified, as the animal, mouth open, salivating, and with her big canines gleaming, focused on their precious new baby sister. The wolf, they all believed, had come to devour her…and maybe them too. Maria knew better.

			
Peter spotted Mariano’s six-shooter on the table. He managed to grab it without being mauled. Angelina, the oldest sister, cried, “Ammazza u lupa! Ammazzulu Petrantoniu!”—Kill the wolf, kill him Peter! 

			
The wolf was too close to his mother and baby sister for Peter to shoot. If only their older brother, Giuseppe, were there. He’d know what to do. But Giuseppe had left in the spring on a cattle drive, swearing he’d bring back money. He still hadn’t returned. Maybe he was dead. Nobody knew. 

			
So, right then, Peter assumed the oldest brother role and said, as quietly as he could, “Come on kids,” forcing his sisters outside into the storm. He fired a shot to drive the lupa’s pack away, saving not just the kids but also his papa. 

			
Not thinking beyond the moment, young Peter shut the door, trapping himself inside the cabin with his mother, his new sister, and the wolf.

			
If it were just him against the animal in the tiny shack, he might stand a chance. But that wolf would sure as hell rip his mama and the baby apart before he ever had a chance to shoot. He had to get off a shot first. 

			
Peter yelled to his father, who couldn’t have gone more than a few steps in the blizzard with the wolf pack gathered behind him. “Papà! Pi favuri, non struppiari a picciridda! Pi favuri Papà!”—Papa please! Help! Please take care of the children. Please! 

			
Mariano may have heard him or maybe not. But either way, he didn’t budge. 

			
So, Peter, not yet old enough to sport whiskers, tried to lure the wolf away from Maria and the baby so he could shoot. It was impossible. The wolf did not even turn his way. 

			
With his small hands shaking, he aimed the gun, but instead of pulling the trigger, he beheld a sight so crazy, he knew he should never tell anyone about it. 

			
That wolf, well, she just ignored him, shook the ice and snow from her fur, and didn’t even budge when he screamed. Instead, she leapt onto the bed where Firenze lay in Maria’s arms. 

			
Maria tried to cover the baby with her own body, but it was no protection. 

			
Peter cocked the gun, but he was shaking too hard and was too scared of killing his mama or sister to pull the trigger. 

			
At the sound of the hammer being cocked, la lupa turned to him, bared her teeth, shining dangerous in the little bit of candlelight, and growled a warning. 

			
Had the smell of blood been so strong that the wolf went against her instincts and left her pack behind to enter into the domain of humans? 

			
It had been a tough winter, and food had been scarce even for these rulers of the mountain. You’d have to be just plain loco to not figure that the she-wolf was going to devour that baby girl right in front of her. It was the way of the wild up there on that muntagna orribile e spietata—the horrible, ruthless mountain.

			
But the little one? She opened her eyes and—God’s truth—gurgled up at the wolf, happy as a rich man’s newborn swaddled in the finest linen. 

			
The beast opened its giant mouth, dripping slime, her tongue lolling, and growled deep and low before ripping the ragged birthing blanket off of Firenze. Maria finally screamed and clutched the newborn even closer. 

			
The wolf paid no mind to the terrified mother and instead raised her massive head, howled, and shoved the baby out of Maria’s arms with her snout. Maria could do nothing but watch, helpless, as the she-wolf opened its jaws. But instead of tearing Firenze to pieces as easily as she’d torn the birthing blanket, la lupa began to lick her. She licked her matted black ringlets, her face, her tiny hands and feet, her legs, and chest, cleaning away any extra birthing fluid clinging to her. 

			
At some point, Maria began to understand the extraordinary thing she was witnessing—mother-to-mother. La lupa was treating that baby girl as her own. She’d heard about things like this back in the old country.

			
When Firenze’s tiny body had been licked clean, damned if that beast didn’t lick her crazy ruby lips before jumping back down to sit watch at the foot of the bed. Peter was paralyzed, no longer just with fear, but with awe.

			
Many minutes passed—which seemed like many hours—before the animal threw its giant head back and howled again, this time so loud that the freezing kids and their papa could surely hear it in spite of the deafening winds.

			
Outside, the smaller children—whose lips were turning blue, the snot and tears freezing on their faces—wailed, believing their mama was dead and that their baby sister had been eaten by the wolf. They knew it because Mama, baby Firenze, and Peter had all gotten spooky-quiet inside that damned shack.

			
But back inside, with the snow rushing through the gaps between the log walls, none of that had happened, even as the wolf’s unholy howling continued. 

			
Peter swore that—damn!—la lupa kept on howling until the tiny newborn pursed her lips and howled back: “Blooooo,” was the sound that came from her bright-red baby lips. 

			
Only then did the grey wolf put her head down and stalk back to the door, brushing Peter with her massive body as she passed. He carefully swung it open, jumped back, and the wolf trotted back out into the blizzard, bypassing the terrified children outside. 

			
As they rushed back in, gathering their blankets around them, Peter spotted his father, stumbling like a specter trying to make his way back in the blinding snow. He also could make out the form of the wolf. She charged towards Mariano, knocking him off his feet and ripping at his pant leg. Just as suddenly, she turned and ran back to her pack, leaving Papa bleeding but alive.

			
When the kids were all back inside, it was so dark and so quiet that they were sure their mama and the baby were dead.

			
Then they heard it. A tiny baby howl: “Blooooo…”

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 55 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 55 –

			
Home on the Range

			
Barbera Ranch, Colorado

			
January 1931

			
Was that Blue? She had seen it for herself. Blue had collapsed and died trying to catch Flo’s train. 

			
Nonetheless, she ran out of the room, through the kitchen, and out the front door. Yes! There she was: her own Blue, staring at her with those magical blue eyes and a litter of little ones.

			
Could it be that Blue didn’t remember her? She wasn’t running to her or even excited to see her. So, Flo ran to her beloved other mother. The wolf remained cautious, taking a few steps towards her but more to protect her pups than to greet Flo. 

			
“Blue!” she cried. “It’s me—Flo.” The wolf let out a low growl and trotted back to her pups.

			
“Blue. It’s me,” she sobbed.

			
Mickey approached her younger sister, bent down, and put her hands on Flo’s shoulders. “Sweetheart, that ain’t Blue.” 

			
“I know Blue went and died when she was chasing me on the train. I saw her collapse,” Flo said, “but I also know that it’s her all right.”

			
“Blue didn’t die that day,” Mickey explained. “We didn’t see no wolf body on the ground next to the tracks or even on the tracks, so we looked for her that day. And the next, and the next. Somehow, she survived, though. We were sure of it. Reckon, she went off into the wild. Then one day, don’t know how much longer, but it was a while, Blue came back to the ranch with a belly full o’ babies.”

			
Flo looked confused, so cranky Helen picked up the story.

			
“That mama-wolf there? She isn’t Blue. She’s her offspring. We call her Bella Blue.”

			
Flo didn’t know how to process this information. “But why didn’t you tell me?” she demanded.

			
“We didn’t know you thought she died, so how could we tell you?” 

			
Clara perked up. “Is there a new Bella too?” 

			
“Well, we got a few donkeys and a coupla mules,” Walnut Jack said, his bad eye squinting against the sun even though there was no eye to speak of. “But this here wolf is named in honor of the an’mals what saved you in the flood.”

			
“Your Blue,” Mickey explained as best as she could without crying herself, “she passed on two years ago now. Right here.” Then, pointing to the west, she added, “She’s buried on the first rise over. She’ll spend eternity overlooking the whole spread. We can walk over later. OK?”

			
“OK.” Flo blinked back tears, trying to take it all in. “Bella Blue?” she called without expecting any response, but the female grey padded over. When Flo held out her hand, Bella Blue licked it and rubbed up against her and then did the impossible—for a wolf anyway. She rolled over for a belly scratch.

			
“BB,” Flo said. “I’ll call you BB.”

			
Yeah, maybe they were home again. 

			
BB would never be as domesticated as Blue, but Flo and Clara were no longer tiny girls who needed to be protected in the wild—or from the wild man called Papa either. Austin was now the challenge. And as miraculous as Blue had been to Flo, so was Bella Blue to Austin. She became his, and she, like Blue, was able to sense when Austin was about to fit and would warn him. 

			
Mickey made it very clear that there was to be no prejudice against her sisters or Austin on this ranch. They were to be as loved and welcomed as if they’d never left. And in turn, they, too, would be given the same chores as any of the other siblings. 

			
She was still worried about the stepsisters though. “Mama took you in and my sisters accepted you, and you just Goddamned better—” 

			
Helen, riding shotgun for her sister, cut Mickey off before she said something she’d regret. “Love them like we love you both. If you are part of this family, then you love them like we do—”

			
“Or you get the hell out,” Mickey finished, perfectly willing to say something she might regret. But then again, Mickey never regretted anything she ever said.

			
Instead, she stuck her hands in her belt, looked at Antonella and Lucia and said, “Got it? Good.”

			
“Let’s walk up the rise so I can visit Blue,” Flo said. “I will put my things away later. First my Blue.”

			
When they were nearing the top, they saw two tombstones. “Who else died?” Clara asked, panicked. 

			
That’s when they found out that Silas had passed shortly after Mama, leaving young Helen a widow. He was buried next to Blue.

			
“We didn’t tell you because you was already so aggrieved,” Mickey said. “We wanted you to try to find some peace. But we knowed how terrible Papa would be. Peace was never somethin’ anybody could have around him.”

			
“Walter carved the tombstones himself,” Helen said, wiping away tears. Flo, Clara, and Austin hugged Helen, and the three cried, not just over their deaths but over the wonder of the lives lived by Silas and Blue. 

			
Silas’s marker read: 

			
Here Lies Our Brother Silas Smith

			
Best CowMan in Colorado

			
Blue’s read: 

			
Here Lies Our Beloved Blue

			
Mother Hero Friend

			
Would you—could you—go home again when those who kept you from harm and loved you even when you didn’t deserve it, no longer existed? 

			
Those were questions that, at that moment, didn’t have answers. What they did know was that they were back in Colorado without Mama, Blue, or Silas. 

			
But they didn’t know if it could ever again be home. 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 56 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 56 –

			
Skin in the Game 

			
Pueblo, Colorado 

			
1932–1935

			
It took time, but in time, Antonella and Lucia even came around. That ridge became Flo’s place of solace. She’d attend school and do her chores—herding cattle some days, tending to the pigs and goats on others—and then ride up there on her favorite horse, Stella. She’d sit until suppertime talking to Blue about everything: the good, the bad, the confusing.

			
Helen, as promised, had taken her to the new doc in town, a man named Dr. Neary, to find out how to get rid of the scaly rash that was taking over Flo’s skin. It was a nasty thing and caused her to constantly itch day and night. Doc said it wasn’t a rash but something called “psoriasis.” And it was permanent. He said it was brought on by “nerves.” 

			
“Nerves?” Flo asked. “What do you mean, and how do I get rid of it?”

			
“Well, darlin’,” the doc said, “I’m afraid that once you get it, you can’t get rid of it. Sometimes it comes on bad when you are highly emotional or frightened or have a shock. Like I said, it’s permanent, but we can treat it.”

			
Flo tried not to cry. She knew this was bad. She hated her skin, and she figured she might hate her future because of it, too. For one thing, she could never get married. What man would have a woman covered in red, scaly, itchy patches? For that matter, what man would she want to see that?

			
“I guess this means I’ll never have a boyfriend or anything…” she managed to get out. 

			
The doc looked surprised. “Of course you will,” he said like he really meant it. “Don’t you know that you’re a beautiful young lady?”

			
Beautiful? Nobody’s ever said that to me, she thought. He must be saying it because he feels bad for me. Yes, that’s it.

			
But Flo was beautiful. She’d turned into a five-foot-five, raven-haired stunner with a slim waist and strong muscular legs that went on forever. Every once in a while, she’d carefully apply lipstick to give her back those ruby lips she had been born with. She loved the way they made her mouth look, even though Papa had tried to ruin it by calling her “Ruby.” A whore named Ruby.

			
Doc made Flo a compound of tar that she was to smear on her sores wherever they were, which sometimes meant the tops of her hands, her elbows, and her legs from the knees down. 

			
Clara felt about as bad for Flo as she would have felt for herself and tried to make her feel better by saying, “Look it ain’t on your face, and you are still gor-gee-oooose.”

			
Smearing the black stuff on, Flo thought, was worse than the psoriasis itself. It was miserably uncomfortable, and it stained the bedsheets so bad that it looked like black blood. But it did relieve the itching.

			
She came to realize for herself that being out in the sun in the summer with her sleeves cut off and shorter pants on did help clear her skin up, and the tanner she got the better. 

			
She also came to understand that Antonella had assumed the full position as Mickey’s second spouse, taking on the traditional duties of wife and mother: bedmate at night, cook, housekeeper, and mother during the day. Lucia and Yellow Fly’s little girl, who was as wild and adventurous as Flo had been, was called Elu. And Mickey and Walnut Jack had just had another baby before the group returned home—that was the infant Antonella had passed off to Flip on the porch. Her name was Juicy. It was really Josephine, but no one ever called her anything but Juicy.

			
Not happy that Mickey had kept her bargain to bed down once a week with “that one-eyed freak,” as Antonella called Mickey’s husband, she was nonetheless happy to have another baby to care for. 

			
After a respectful period of mourning, cranky Helen took a job in town teaching English to the newly arrived children of the Italian miners. After Silas died—and Mama had been right, Helen had become a nursemaid during his waning years—she would often stay at the boarding house in town during the week. It wasn’t considered a racy thing to do because Pueblo was pretty far from the ranch, and if she didn’t have a ride home, it was easier to stay at the boarding house. Anyway, she was “the widow Smith” and “the teacher” and therefore a very respectable lady.

			
One fine spring eve, Helen was having supper with a teacher friend at the new café in town, when in walked the new fella that people were talking about, Michael Ambrosio. 

			
Michael was a tailor by trade, but he fancied himself a lecturer and intellectual. His main themes, when he could draw a crowd after mass on Sundays or town meetings on weekdays, were the joys of Communism and the wonders of vitamins. Ambrosio was blond, unimposing, slight, and smart. 

			
Helen attended one of his lectures on an evening when she had nothing better to do. After the lecture she remained undecided about whether he was brilliant or a blowhard. 

			
When she walked out the door he was standing outside shaking hands like he was a priest or something, and when he shook Helen’s hand it took long a time before she could pull it away. The next day she received a card at the boarding house asking if he could call.

			
What the hell, she thought. He’s not my type, but I do want to learn about vitamins.

			
In spite of her reservations about him, they began to see one another. He fell in love with her, and she eventually fell in like with him. What she admired was that Michael stood for something—anything. She thought his views about communism were right. After all, her mama had always said that all people were equal. 

			
She remembered Maria always saying, “Nobody is better than you. Nobody.” That meant that nobody was better than anybody else, right? Right. (Well, except for the mine owners and managers who were scum.)

			
But Helen wasn’t sold on Mike’s ideas about vitamins. She figured they got all the so-called vitamins they needed from their own beef, vegetables, and fruit. Why would she need some powder compound?

			
At one of Mike’s Wednesday night sparsely attended lectures, he tried making the case for vegetarianism and declared, “Beef is not just unhealthy but un-communist because not everybody can afford to buy a steak.” He was immediately pelted with two tomatoes and a head of lettuce.

			
This wasn’t, of course, the brightest cause to take up in cattle country. In fact, it was dumb as school in summer. Worse, it was a step too far for Helen the rancher’s daughter. She told him to take a short walk off a high mountain.

			
Thing is, cranky Helen got even crankier after the break-up, so it was probably good that their split didn’t last long. It took no time for Mike to figure out that life without beef was not worth life without Helen. So, he made the ultimate gesture to win back the woman of his dreams. He showed up after work at the boarding house bearing a bloody steak wrapped in butcher paper. 

			
A year later they married and moved to Brooklyn, Mike’s hometown. The locals were happy to get rid of him, the family was unhappy that he took her away from Colorado, but the Brooklyn contingent was thrilled to have her back—even if she was a cranky pain in the ass. Nothing made her happy, so it didn’t matter where she lived.

			
To prove his sincerity to his new bride, Mike opened a butcher shop.

			
“Love does crazy things to a man,” Mike would often say to justify his change in meat philosophy. He didn’t personally eat meat, but he sold enough of it to keep a roof over their heads. 

			
Meanwhile, during those same years, Austin found his calling as a cattleman and herded and drove with the best of them. On weekends he’d play piano at the Cock and Bull Saloon.

			
He also increased the goat herd until it, too, became a going enterprise. He still had seizures, but BB would warn him when one was coming on, and by that time, everyone around him was used to it. “Looks like Austin’s gonna go fittin’ again,” somebody would say, and someone else would grab the spoon he always kept in his pocket and stick it under his tongue.

			
Flo and Clara had attended school and graduated with honors and then tried to figure out what they would do with their lives.

			
Ranching seemed to be the only choice. It was what they knew, but they’d also lived in the big city. Life was still going on outside the cattle world, and they secretly told each other that they wanted a piece of that life too.

			
Clara, who always loved to shake it up, hadn’t missed a town hall dance since Jesus was a baby, and she learned every new step that came along. Maybe she would be a dancer she decided. But she had to get better at it.

			
Problem was, cowpokes weren’t very good at (or interested in) ballroom dancing. Square dancing sure, ballroom, not so much.

			
By the age of twenty, Clara, not five feet tall, decided to change her style from cowgirl to movie star. She bobbed, permed, and bleached her hair bright blonde to match her dancing idol, Ginger Rogers. 

			
With her chiffon dresses and yellow/white hair, she looked as smooth as new silk on the dance floor. She would have to find different partners, though, because the local fellas’ bowed legs were better for driving cattle than dancing foxtrots. 

			
It wasn’t until she learned to Peabody that she came into her own as a dancer. The Peabody was a dance in which you’d glide not step or jump around, and it seemed like it was invented just for her.

			
She practiced it with Flo every day and then taught a local boy, Jerry the blacksmith, to do it. He agreed to learn just so he could get close to Clara and hold her around the waist. Even though he wasn’t nearly as good a lead as she was a follower, they entered a dance contest and won twenty-five dollars.

			
Flo was happy that Clara and Austin had found something they wanted to do with their lives, but she still had no idea what she wanted for herself. 

			
She tended the animals and thought maybe she could become an animal doctor, but the chances of a young lady becoming a doctor, even an animal doctor, were not good. 

			
She volunteered at the veterinarian’s office, and eventually he helped her get a certificate of some kind to be a veterinary assistant. 

			
Still, Flo was restless. She had never lost that wild, untamed thing she’d been born with. There was always a part of her that was different, a part that wanted out of normal existence, a part that wanted to be free like…well, like a wolf.

			
Flo dated many young men who came calling. Maybe one would be different, she hoped. 

			
There wasn’t a single cowpoke who didn’t follow her around like a lovesick fool, but they didn’t know her secret shame. She always wore long-sleeved blouses and pants or dresses that covered her arms and half her hands, and thick stockings that covered the marks on her legs when she wore a shorter, more stylish skirt. 

			
So, she’d only go so far with each young fella before dropping him. When they wanted to hold her hand and she wasn’t in a flareup, she’d let them, but when her skin was erupting, she’d break up with them—and break their hearts. They never knew why she broke up with them, but they always landed as hard as being knocked off a bucking bronco.

			
As she’d say to Clara, “None of their damned business what my skin looks like. They don’t know, and I’ll be Goddamned if they ever will.”

			
“But some man will come along, and you’ll want him to know,” Clara would protest. Flo’d shut her up with a look. She had perfected their mama’s glare—the one that was so final and so definite it could shut up a howling bobcat.

			
But Clara knew her sister was not really a hard girl. Flo had a soft side. Prickly on the outside, soft on the underbelly, just like a porcupine. 

			
Life had put Flo in charge and made her tough, and, like their mama, she didn’t take no shit from nobody. In Flo’s case though, her skin had become both her shield and her enemy. She didn’t want anybody to know because if they did, she feared they’d scream like Antonella had done that first day. Folks would think she had leprosy. But on the other hand, it kept her safe from men like Papa too. 

			
She almost got engaged to one fella, Johnny Smith, a nephew of Silas’s. He was the only one she ever told about her skin, and they’d joke that they each had skin that stupid white folks didn’t like. 

			
He was different from other men. He was sweet and kind and easy on the eyes. He thought the same about her. But, when he took her home to meet his family, Johnny’s mother made them break it off. 

			
“We Black folk have enough troubles without you marrying a white girl—even if she is as dark as us when she’s been in the sun,” she said. 

			
He protested, but he knew his mother spoke the truth. It was one thing for Helen and Silas to have gotten married because they lived on the ranch and their whole lives were spent there. But Johnny was the first in his family to go to college—two years at Howard University—and his mother wasn’t going to let him jeopardize it for some fool crush he had going on. 

			
“You gonna be a lawyer and that girl probably didn’t even finish high school.” It wasn’t true, but it didn’t matter.

			
No way. No how. No more talk.

			
The awful end to their romance only made Flo harden her heart against hurt even more than it already was. 

			
Nobody is better than you. Nobody.

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 57 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 57 –

			
The Peabody Body

			
Around the USA 

			
1936–1940

			
With relentless practicing in the barn, Clara and Jerry the blacksmith got so good that they entered and won a contest in Trinidad, one in Chicosa, and a big one in Pueblo. They were racking up the cash, but all that traveling around plus the cost of dancing shoes was eating up the profits.

			
Jerry, who wasn’t the brightest bulb in the ceiling, had finally come around to the notion that maybe Clara was just never going to say “yes” and take their partnership from the dancefloor to the marriage bed. Hell, she wouldn’t even kiss him. 

			
“You’re just not smooth enough,” Clara would say. He thought she meant his dancing, but she meant his way with the ladies.

			
But they enjoyed each other’s company, even though Jerry’s father had started accusing him of being a sissy man who liked dancing better than hammering metal all day. But Jerry didn’t let his father discourage him. He’d keep dancing until Clara gave in. Or that was the plan.

			
The biggest dance contest in the state was coming up soon in Denver, and if they won, he was sure he’d win her heart. She wasn’t interested in winning hearts, just cash—like Denver’s seventy-five-dollar cash prize. Clara made up a “dance team” name for the contest, and she and Jerry became “The Ranch Dancers.” 

			
On the day of the contest, they stood in the arena watching the other fifty or so couples practicing on the massive floor. “They don’t stand a chance against us, Clara,” Jerry told her. 

			
“I know. We got this made in the shade,” she answered.

			
Then onto the dance floor in the middle of all the others came Big Al Venturo and his big gal of a partner. She was all red hair, red lips, red dancing pumps, and a red satin dress. 

			
“If she ain’t the reddest thing since apples, I don’t know what is,” Jerry observed.

			
Clara didn’t give two damns about the redhead, but she couldn’t take her eyes off the big man with his thick, black wavy hair and pencil-thin mustache. They called themselves, “Dream Dancers.” When the dancing duo got into position, Clara whispered to Jerry, “Jesus. I think they are going to do the Peabody. He sure is a fine-looking fella, but he’s way too big to be graceful. Nothing to worry about.”

			
Then the Dream Dancers stepped out and way above every other couple on the floor. In fact, people stopped practicing themselves to watch them glide.

			
Clara’s heart sank. The contest came down at the end to the Ranch Dancers versus the Dream Dancers in a tiebreaker. Finally, the Dream Dancers pulled it out and won first place. Clara, carrying her second-place cup, went over to congratulate the winners and decided instead to say, “Big Al, you’re about the most graceful Peabody dancer in the whole wide world.”

			
Big Al was even bigger up close—maybe six-foot-two—but his size didn’t intimidate four-foot-eleven-inch Clara, and neither did his gal, Big Red. Towering over her, Al started to thank Clara, and instead she cut him off and said, “If you ever want to win the national title, look me up. Circle Double Bar A Ranch out near Pueblo.”

			
Jerry scowled and Big Red sneered.

			
A week later, Big Al made his first appearance at the ranch gate where one of the cowpokes directed him to the corral. When Big Al spotted Clara, she and Flo happened to be practicing their trick riding routines for the upcoming rodeo. Clara was standing up on top of a speeding horse going round and round the corral. Right there and then the big man lost his big heart. 

			
What Big Al knew about horses was not what you’d call extensive. He just knew they still pulled the manure wagons in New York City, and if you got too close, they would bite you. But the sight of Clara looking like a rodeo star from Life Magazine made Al wish he owned cowboy boots instead of patent leather dance shoes. 

			
He didn’t know that the last thing Clara wanted was another man in cowboy boots and was just yearning for a fella who owned his own pair of patent leather dance shoes.

			
Flo knew, just knew, as soon as her sister jumped from her horse and began sashaying towards Al that their days as inseparable “twins” were numbered. Annie Oakley was about to lose Calamity Jane to the Peabody. At least temporarily.

			
Flo called after her sister who was butt-swaying herself towards Al, “Hey, Clara. It must be jelly ’cause jam don’t shake like that!” Clara turned around and gave her the high sign.

			
It was love at second sight. Within weeks, Clara and Al dropped their dance partners, teamed up, and never looked back. The way they moved so naturally together on the dance floor was magical, even though Al was a good fifteen inches taller than Clara. They practically became a national sensation and were even photographed for that new Look Magazine. People lined up to see them perform the Peabody at theaters and dance halls around the country. 

			
Clara confessed to Flo in a letter that, yes, she had lost her virginity to Big Al, and that making love was more fun—almost—than doing the Peabody. Flo was shocked. She’d never known a girl who’d lost her virginity before marriage, let alone her own sister, and wondered if she ever would.

			
No, it’ll never happen. I will never show my skin to any man. 

			
But since hope springs eternal, Flo became more vigilant about applying the tar ointment, until one day, young Flip sniffed her and said, “Aunt Flo, you smell like one of them tar roads they got up in Denver.”

			
Mickey yelled that he smelled like half a horse’s ass, and he ran away laughing. Flo tried to laugh it off, too, but in her heart, she knew he was right. That was the end of the tar treatment and the start of even worse psoriasis flareups.

			
A few months later, Clara phoned up the ranch house with big news. Flo answered the telephone, and Clara told her to gather everybody ’round for an announcement. She and Big Al were getting married. They planned a May wedding in Brooklyn. 

			
“Everyone has to come,” she declared. 

			
Somehow Flo had always thought Clara would, sooner or later, tire of dancing, return home, and they’d go back to being Annie and Calamity forever.

			
“Married?” Flo croaked as though she didn’t understand the word.

			
“Yes, married,” Clara gushed. “And you are going to be my maid of honor, so buy yourself a beautiful gown.” 

			
“Gown? Are you never coming home again?” Flo asked, happy for her sister but sad for herself.

			
“Of course we’ll be back,” Clara answered. 

			
Flo knew what “we” meant. It meant that once Clara got hitched, she and her sister would never share time alone together in their own world. One of them was a grown woman now, and that would be the one who was younger than she was.

			
In late May, Flo boarded the train to New York. She wanted to have a few days with Clara before Mickey and Austin came in for the wedding. Before Clara became a wife. 

			
In Flo’s luggage was the beautiful, long-sleeved, green satin gown and dyed-to-match high heeled pumps that the salesman in Denver had called “gen-u-ine Poe Der Swahr, French material.” 

			
She knew she would be a knockout, but she couldn’t help but be worried about other things. Would it be too hot in New York to get away with a gown that covered her limbs? Would Papa be at the wedding? Would she remember her way around Brooklyn, would, would, would…

			
But no, she realized, it really wasn’t any of those things giving her this sense of uneasiness, this unsettling in her soul. It was a kind of knowing without knowing. Same as how, on the night of the wolf, her mama had known without knowing: 

			
Something is about to happen that will change everything everywhere forever.

– 58 –

			
His Heart Over Flo-eth

			
Brooklyn, New York

			
May 1940

			
The wedding morning broke hot for a day in May. 

			
“Hell,” Clara sighed, sitting miserably in her silk wedding gown in Callie’s living room. “This would be a scorcher even if it was August.” 

			
Flo, sweating even worse even than Clara in her big, hot, satin gown, with a cold rag to her forehead, added, “Hot as Miss Cora-Lee’s on nickel night.” 

			
With their sisters fanning them with fans and newspapers, which did as much good as waving flaming torches at them, Clara tried lifting her arms to dry off the embarrassing wet stains under her armpits.

			
Laura tried wrapping a bunch of netting around her sister’s shoulders to help cover the sweat stains on the silk gown.

			
Flo, whose dressmaker in Denver had added extra-long pointed sleeves to disguise the sores on her hands, thought she might pass out. 

			
“If only Mama was here,” cried Clara. “She’d know what to do.”

			
If Mama were here, I wouldn’t have broken out in these giant skin sores that are itching like a bitch in this heat, Flo thought.

			
Never one to feel sorry for herself, this day, though, was pushing Flo to the limit. Her partner in crime since birth was getting hitched, her mama was long gone, she was covered in psoriasis, she would never be loved by a man, it was nearing 95 degrees, and she was swathed head to toe in a stupid, giant gown. 

			
Big Al’s best man, a mystery fella named Tony, had arrived late last night. He had been at a friend’s father’s wake in upstate New York. Even Big Al was worried he wouldn’t arrive on time.

			
Flo already had a chip on her shoulder against this man because he’d kept everyone on pins and needles about making it to the wedding. The last thing she wanted to do was walk arm-in-arm with him out of the church after the wedding ceremony. 

			
“Some best friend,” she complained to Clara, waving a big newspaper in an attempt to cool off. “Making everybody worry that he wouldn’t show up on time.”

			
“Aww c’mon, Flo,” Clara protested. “He’s the nicest fella on earth. He was at a funeral.”

			
“Sure, sure,” Flo harumphed. 

			
They finally left and rode in a limousine the few blocks to Our Lady of Sorrows where their family and friends were already packing the church waiting for the big event. When the organ sounded, Flo took a deep breath and walked down the aisle, sweat soaking everything: armpits, hands, and even her newly pin-curled-hair had turned into a stringy mess. Clara followed, a beautiful, long veil covering her face and hair and trailing six feet behind her. She was accompanied by her brother Joe, since Mariano had disappeared a few months earlier, which was a blessing as far as everyone was concerned.

			
Flo had grown up a lot since the last time she’d laid eyes on her papa, and she wasn’t sure that she wouldn’t have shot him dead if he had shown up.

			
When Clara’s veil was lifted and she was handed off to Al at the altar, Flo took her sister’s bouquet to hold during the ceremony. As she tried to maneuver both her own huge lily and white roses bouquet and her sister’s even bigger one, she glanced up and got her first look at the best man. He was staring at her sweaty self, but not like she was a clumsy, sweaty mess. He was staring at her the way Clark Gable stared at Vivien Leigh on the movie screen.

			
No wonder, Flo thought. I look like I’m wearing the damned drapes from Tara in this big, hot gown. 

			
She really wanted to say—but of course didn’t, seeing how they were standing at the altar—“Whatcha lookin’ at Shorty?” Tony was hardly what you’d call a tall man.

			
In fact, she thought, He’s more Clara’s size, and Big Al is more my size, although I’d never look twice at either one of ’em. She then gave herself a mind slap for thinking bad thoughts on her sister’s wedding day. But she knew it was because she felt lost and alone.

			
When the ceremony was over and Big Al lifted little Clara clear off her feet for the big kiss, everyone applauded. Flo did, too, and realized how wet her hands were in the extra-long sleeves. 

			
So, when Tony grabbed her hand so they could walk together back up the aisle, she took his arm instead. 

			
“I’m Anthony Stasi,” he said, ducking the rice being tossed their way as they stood on the hot stone steps of the church with the bride and groom. “But everybody calls me Tony.”

			
Flo just looked at him. She was a good two inches taller in her “gen-u-ine Poe Der Swahr, French material” high heels than he was. 

			
Not in the least intimidated by Clara’s taller sister/maid of honor, Tony continued, “And I know your name is Flo. Nice to meet you, Flo.”

			
She gave him a weak smile in return and turned back to look at the newly wedded couple. All she wanted to do was get out of that gown and into a bathtub of ice.

			
The party after the ceremony at the local Italian Sons Hall was what they called “a football wedding.” That means it made the barn weddings at the ranch look like a royal wedding, and if you didn’t duck, you could get hit with a flying prosciutto sandwich.

			
Sandwiches were tossed around tables, whiskey and beer flowed like it was legal—which it was by then—and Mickey, Austin, and Peter provided the music on fiddle, guitar, and piano. Every male relative over the age of sixty got up to sing, everyone danced the tarantella, and even the die-hard Sicilians tried square dancing, although they made it look more like the tarantella than a quadrille. When Tony asked Flo to dance, she refused but invited him later to square dance because she knew he would never in a million years know how to do it. He surprised her by jumping right in and leading her around in a serious do-si-do. 

			
What? she thought.

			
For a few minutes, she forgot how hot she was and started enjoying her sister’s day when the worst thing in the world—at least as far as Flo was concerned—happened. It was the thing Flo dreaded at every family party and avoided like influenza: Peter called her up to sing. Flo always loved to sing, but mostly to herself or at a little family party, but never, ever in public.

			
Against her will, and with the entire party calling her name even though they’d never even heard her sing, she was dragged up to the small stage. She leaned into the microphone and said, “I’m no singer. Really!”

			
Someone in the crowd yelled out, “Compared to Uncle Ralph, you’ll be like Helen O’Connell,” which caused everyone to laugh as she stood up there panicked. More sweat. If possible.

			
She was about to jump off when Tony jumped on stage, said something to the band, and started singing until she laughed out loud and joined in: “You must have been a beautiful baby, ’cause baby lookit you now!”

			
To wild (drunk) applause, Tony lifted Flo down from the stage, and she found herself laughing until tears were running down her face. 

			
“Now that’s more like it,” he said and led her onto the floor in a wild Lindy Hop. He, like his best pal Big Al, was a helluva dancer.

			
When the night was coming to a close, Clara called all the single ladies up to the stage. She turned her back and tossed her bouquet yelling, “Whoever catches it gets married next.”

			
Somehow, the bouquet landed on Flo who instinctively caught it even though she’d tried keeping her arms at her side. What? She looked around like a mad woman for some other gal to give it to.

			
Instead, Tony grabbed her hand and led her to the dance floor for the last dance, a slow ballad. “Well, I guess I’m going to have to marry you now.”

			
“Like hell you will,” she answered, half-charmed, half-annoyed at the nerve of him. 

			
“Exactly!” he answered going for a full-out swirl and dip. “Like hell I will marry you. You’ll see.” 

			
“No. Just no,” she answered on the way up from the deep dip.

			
“So, you’re saying definitely ‘no’?”

			
“Definitely ‘no,’” she answered, joining the crowd heading out the door.

			
He grabbed her hand. “OK! So now we have a place to start negotiating.”

			
She laughed, shook her head, and made her way outside to see the newlyweds off in their open-top car with dozens of cans dragging off the back.

			
The next day, as Flo was packing, with her cotton gloves on to keep some new miracle cream in place, Callie came into the room. It was the room that Flo and Clara had been bunking in together before the wedding.

			
Callie was carrying the biggest vase of flowers she’d ever seen except for the ones piled on hearses at mobster funerals. “What the….”

			
“You’ve got a not-secret admirer,” Callie said. “They’re from Anthony.”

			
“Oh Jesus,” Flo said. “I hope you like them because I’m hopping on the 12:35 outta here.”

			
“Charlie used to bring me flowers,” Callie sighed. “It’s a sign of true love.”

			
“It’s a sign of a man who thinks he can get what he wants,” Flo laughed, shaking her head.

			
“Don’t be so mean,” Callie said. “He’s a man in love.”

			
Flo stopped packing, lifted her sleeves, and showed Callie the fiery red, itchy and scaly patches on her elbows. 

			
“Marriage is not in the cards for me. I’m not you. I don’t want a man, and I sure as hell don’t need a man.”

			
Callie hugged Flo. “I think that man has other plans.”

			
With that, Flo kissed her sister goodbye and headed for the station to board the train with Mickey and the rest of the gang heading back to Home Sweet Home, Colorado. 

			
During the trip, Mickey got up the nerve to ask snippy Flo, “So…you like the best man from the wedding? You sure had fun together—”

			
Flo cut her off, saying, “Non-starter. Not interested.” 

			
Mickey shook her head. It was clear to everyone but Flo, though, that this Tony fella had other plans.

– 59 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 59 –

			
Mickey, Should I Marry?

			
Barbera Ranch, Colorado

			
1941

			
Tony sure did have other plans. 

			
He wrote letters to Flo which she read and tossed, never bothering to answer. Yes, she liked him, thought about him a lot, and even sometimes imagined what it would be like to be married to him. But she knew in her shutdown-heart that it could never be. 

			
Most of the time, especially when she was upset or nervous, her body broke out in huge, scaly patches, some as big as her whole calf. She told herself, It’s best to just stick to my horses, my animals, and those cute-as-a-button, brand-new wolf pups. 

			
She was a rancher’s daughter and planned to live and die as one.

			
As beautiful, funny, and as much of a firecracker as Flo was, she was bound by her secret shame. It wasn’t just her skin that caused her such humiliation; it was also what had caused her condition in the first place. No one could know she had pulled her own father off her tiny sister when she caught him trying to commit an unspeakable act.

			
After several visits to Dr. Neary, Flo felt comfortable enough with him to confess most of the truth. She told him that her father had jumped on her sister—but didn’t say why—and that she fought him off. She told him that the next morning, she had her first breakout. The doc figured out the part she didn’t say and tried to explain that sometimes things like her skin breakouts were “psychological.”

			
“Flo,” he said, as gently as possible, “I think it’s possible you got the condition to keep yourself safe.”

			
“What?” she asked, disgusted that he’d suggest she did it to herself.

			
“Sometimes, when a person, say a young woman, witnesses or is the victim of a horrible attack, their mind can cause them to get a disease.”

			
“Absolutely not,” she snapped at him. Leave it to a man to think it’s the woman’s fault, she thought. “You aren’t a woman, so you don’t understand.”

			
He continued anyway, even more gently. “It’s a protection mechanism, Flo. Your brain might have triggered your immune system as a way to keep what happened to your sister from happening to you.” Then, he took a deep breath and said, “To keep you safe from brutal men who rape women.”

			
She was shocked he used the word “rape,” but he was a straight shooter. She had to give him that. Was it possible?

			
After Johnny, she had promised herself that the world would only see the outside, never what was going on under her work clothes. Those duds, however, which were great for covering up her condition, did exactly what she was trying to avoid. They made her look like a star in a Western movie, and it drove the drovers crazy. 

			
Let them dream, she’d think, even though she refused to allow herself to do the same.

			
In fact, in one of the letters Tony sent her, he even remarked that he pictured her on the ranch and he bet she looked like she belonged in a Western movie. She never answered that letter either. But she thought it was dumb, seeing as how those actresses were fake, and she was real.

			
After a dozen more letters, Tony decided to try phoning her up. If he’d been born with the gene for shame, he would have given up long before, and for sure would have dropped the whole thing when she did answer the phone.

			
“Who do you want to speak to?” she asked as though she hardly knew who he was. 

			
But Tony didn’t have that shame gene, and he did believe that eventually Flo would come around. He could tell she liked him and didn’t care that Big Al and Clara told him he had no chance. 

			
“I know my sister better’n anybody in the world, and if she said ‘no’ you better know she means it. Besides,” she added to soften the blow with a joke, “we’re Sicilian and you’re Calabrese. It would never work.”

			
“So, you mean she’d kill me and still be mad?” he said, and they all laughed like hell, even though what he said probably held a grain of old-time Italian stereotype.

			
Tony ignored Clara’s advice and decided to go to Colorado and do whatever it took to get Flo to admit she liked him back. 

			
He graduated with his certificate in accounting on June 5th, and on June 6th, he was on a train headed to Colorado. 

			
He showed up at the Circle Double Bar A ranch house on an especially hot, dusty day and was greeted at the door by a tiny, crooked lady and her tiny, crooked sister holding a squalling infant. Behind them stood two men: an Indian with braids down his back and a giant white man with one eye.

			
A relentless reader of fiction and history, poor Tony from Brooklyn felt like he’d taken a trip and ended up in some crazy, scary Grimm’s fairy tale. “Whatever you’re selling, we ain’t buyin’,” Antonella said, nearly closing the door on him.

			
He stuck his foot in the door—a trick he’d learned earning his way thru school as a door-to-door Fuller Brush salesman. Yellow Fly and Walnut Jack moved Antonella out of the way and stared Tony down.

			
They didn’t scare him. He’d come all this way, and if he had to fight a savage Indian, a one-eyed giant, and two bent little women to rescue his beloved, that’s what he’d do.

			
Instead, he didn’t have to say a word because Lucia scurried up next to Yellow Fly and said, “Wait a second. Ain’t you that city slicker feller from Brook-a-land who calls up here on the telyphone?” 

			
Tony nodded his head, and the whole bunch moved into the doorway to get a better look. Antonella wiped her hands on her apron, pushed the stray hairs off her forehead, and said more than asked, “You lookin’ for Flo, right? She’s out on the range.”

			
Tony stuck his hand out to introduce himself, which no one took, but Yellow Fly did look him over and nodded, if not with approval, then, well, with something.

			
“I’ll go find her. If she’s of a mind to come, she’ll come. If not, you can have a meal and be on your way. Lucia, give the man some grub.” With that, Yellow Fly walked out the door, mounted a horse, and took off. 

			
An uncomfortable hour later, during which Tony tried to make conversation that nobody wanted to hear, Flo came riding up. She walked in, let the screen door slam behind her, and used her wide brimmed hat to dust off her well-worn chaps. Then she looked at Tony and said, “You sure are persistent.”

			
“The greasy wheel and all that,” he said. No one had any idea what the hell he was talking about.

			
He told her he was staying at the hotel in town. He didn’t tell her that he intended to come by each night when her chores were done to visit. He was always polite but also a big flirt. He had a return ticket and time was running out, so after church on the Sunday before he had to leave, they were down by the creek having a picnic. Flo had a fried chicken leg in her hand when Tony leaned in, took her face in his big hands, looked deep into her eyes, and kissed her. 

			
A shock ran through her whole body, and she thought she’d burst into flames. What just happened?

			
He asked her right then and there to be his wife. She wouldn’t answer him. 

			
She couldn’t marry anyone—not with her skin. How could she? How could he want a woman like that?

			
“I love you, and I want to be with you for the rest of my life. Even with your crazy family,” he laughed. Flo couldn’t help but laugh too.

			
The next day when he left, she didn’t see him off at the train, but he got something better. He had her promise that she’d at least think about it. 

			
She tried to tell him whenever he called about her psoriasis. She said sometimes when it flared up, it looked like leprosy. He said she was being crazy. “You’ve never seen it,” she told him. He said he didn’t care about that, and besides, he added, how could he see anything when she was always covered from head-to-toe even in ninety-degree heat?

			
She hung up on him. This disruption of her spirited, unmarried, free-to-be-herself-life was not something she needed. She had chosen to remain unmarried, so why was she also wishing things could be different? 

			
Sure, he was exciting in ways the cowpokes who had come calling would never be. Tony dreamed big, he wanted to see the world, and he wanted to see the world with her.

			
Do I love him? Does that matter? she’d think over and over.

			
He didn’t stop calling and writing. Sometimes, she’d even get on the phone, and once in a while, when she couldn’t help herself, she’d write him a letter. Nothing flowery, almost like a report of the state of the ranch, the animals, and her work with the veterinarian. 

			
But she couldn’t stop thinking about him and wondering what it would be like to be his wife.

			
One night, overcome with confusion, she fell into Mickey’s lap. “Help me, please,” she cried. “What can I do? He just won’t take ‘no’ for an answer. And I just can’t ever say ‘yes.’”

			
Mickey, always the practical one, picked up her sister’s face, wiped her tears with the cuffs of her rolled up shirt, looked her right in the eyes, and said, “Oh for Christ’s sake, Flo. What if I’da thought ‘Poor woe is me. I love a woman but I gotta get married to a man. I can never have what I want, wah, wah, wah.’ Huh?”

			
Flo had no answer other than, “But that’s different.”

			
“You bet your ass it’s dif’rent,” Mickey said. “Dif’rent from everybody in the whole world practically. Sure dif’rent from anybody we know. A woman living with a woman and a man?”

			
“Well, that’s for sure,” Flo admitted, allowing herself to giggle, wiping her tears.

			
“Did I give a good Goddamned if the whole-a Colorado thought I’d gone to the bad? No,” Mickey reminded her.

			
“But that was your choice. I have no choice,” Flo shot back, pulling up her pants’ leg to show her sister the latest flareup.

			
“Bullshit. He loves all of you, no matter what,” Mickey said, then asked, “Tell me sumthin.’ What if Mama had got to feelin’ sorry for herself with a shack full-a starving, half-froze kids and not a nickel to her name, huh? Instead, she did what was near-on impossible. She made booze, she ran bootleg, she ran a buncha men on a ranch. Us Barbera gals don’t cotton to that ‘I cain’t do’ crap.” She stopped to take a breath and finally asked, “Tell me true, do you love the feller, Flo?” 

			
Flo just looked at her sister, her eyes as wide as supper plates. 

			
“I’ma take that as a yes,” Mickey said. “Just don’t be a Coward Howard.” Then she added about Flo’s persistent suitor, “Me? I sure admire a person who don’t give up no matter what.”

			
And he didn’t. Tony came back to Colorado, and first thing he did was come to the ranch when he knew Flo would be out on the range and asked Austin and Mickey for Flo’s hand.

			
Austin said, “Sure ’nuf. But I don’t know if my sister loves you back. She might turn you down flatter’n a copper’s feet.”

			
Mickey said, “Balderdash! She likes ’im alright. She just too stubborn to admit it.”

			
Tony’s face lit up. “That’s what I think too. I love her like crazy.”

			
Mickey smiled. “Sure, I give you my blessing to take her hand. Hell, take the whole thing, if she’ll have you.” Then she laughed like she was funnier than Buster Keaton. 

			
They told Tony to come for supper that night. 

			
Nothing on that ranch was ever done in private and certainly not something as big as a proposal. So, Austin invited everyone to witness the whole thing because, for starters, a proposal was sure more exciting than repairing fences. And there was going to be cake and wine after. 

			
“Well,” Austin thought for a second and said, “If she says ‘yes,’ that is. No ‘yes,’ no cake.”

			
“Can we still have the wine?” Walter wanted to know.

			
When Flo came in for supper and saw Tony standing there among the crowd, she couldn’t help but smile. 

			
To her shock, Tony went down on one knee as everybody hovered over them. He took out a ring and asked her to be his wife, adding, “I will cherish you every day I live on this earth. Just like I do now.”

			
She didn’t say yes, but she also didn’t say no. When Tony took that for a “yes” and tried to place the ring on her finger, the red, scaly skin on the top of her hand showed. She tried to pull her sleeve down to cover it, but he pushed it back up, kissed the sores, and pushed that ring all the way on. 

			
Then he said something that knocked everybody for a loop. “Nobody’s better than you,” he said and kissed Flo on the mouth.

			
She pulled away shocked. Tony didn’t understand why everybody had gasped and were now holding their breath

			
Finally, Flo threw her arms around Tony, buried her face in his neck so they couldn’t see she was crying, and said, “OK.” 

			
Before she could change her mind, they got married by Father Angeletto, who was really getting on in years. Clara and Big Al came out to stand as matron of honor and best man. A small party for the family and the ranch hands followed. Flo insisted on keeping it on the down-low.

			
After the party, the groom took his bride to the hotel in town. He booked the bridal suite, which was slightly bigger than the not-bridal-suite rooms.

			
When Flo emerged in her cotton head-to-toe nightgown, she slipped into bed and turned the bedside light off. He turned it back on, slowly removed the nightgown from her shoulders, looked at what she’d hidden from the world, and said, “You are perfect.”

			
They made love that night, all night and all the next morning. For the first time since she had first broken out in the psoriasis, Flo didn’t care about her skin.

			
When she went to the sink to wash up, she saw she still had that ruby red lipstick on from the night before. Only this time it didn’t come off.

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 60 –

			
Days That Will Live in Infamy

			
Brooklyn, New York

			
1941–1942

			
Tony had a job waiting for him in Brooklyn—accountant at the Spinner’s food market. Flo didn’t want to leave Colorado and go back to the bad memories of Brooklyn, but Tony promised her that if, after meeting his family, she still didn’t want to live in Brooklyn, he’d find a job in Pueblo or even Crowley, and move back to the ranch.

			
Flo was nervous in the cab taking them from Grand Central Terminal to his family’s home at 200 Franklin Avenue in Brooklyn. 

			
What would his family think of her? What would she think of them?

			
When they pulled up to his parents’ house, his father, big strapping Domenico, and mother, itty-bitty Domenica, were waiting for them right inside the front gate in what looked to Flo like a mighty big house on a mighty small piece of property.

			
“You didn’t tell me you were rich,” she said, turning from him to the house. 

			
“Not rich. Hard working.”

			
Domenica rushed out and did the right thing: She hugged her new daughter-in-law before hugging her own son. She was tiny like Maria, and her hug was as big as Maria’s had been. Domenico did the same.

			
Before they knew what was happening, out rushed five sisters of all shapes, sizes, and ages from thirteen to early twenties, as well as one tall, handsome brother. 

			
Before Flo got swept up in the hurricane that was the Stasi family, Tony held out his arms and said, “Flo meet Anna, Rosaria—Rosie, Francesca—Fran, Philomena—Philly, and last but not least, Maria.” 

			
“They’re almost the same as my own family’s names,” Flo cried. 

			
“That’s ’cause we are your family,” Philly said, hugging her, which ended in a free-for-all hugging fest. 

			
“Oh, and here’s my brother, Louie,” Tony said, but she didn’t get to greet him as she was rushed along by the sisters and up the stairs into the house.

			
The next few months were easy for Flo. This was the home life she had always wanted. A kind father who took care of his family and a mother who could be a mother without having to protect her family from both wild animals and a wild animal of a husband.

			
Flo called Domenica Mama Tri, not mentioning that Mama Due had been a wolf. 

			
Domenica taught her to cook Calabrese, which included the warning that Sunday gravy needed plenty of sugar, as though anything other than that was a mortal sin. 

			
Domenico was simply “Papa” because her long-lost and never seen again father had never been a father to her, which she realized when she saw what a real papa could be. Strange as it must have seemed to their city neighbors, Flo’s new father-in-law also had a way with animals, same as she had. She even told him about Blue which seemed as normal to him as rain in spring.

			
In fact, he thought it was also normal to have his own small menagerie in Brooklyn. There was a string of ducks that followed him wherever he went, a Chow Chow dog named Jack Dempsey, and a goat that he kept in the tiny backyard called Shoeless Joe. 

			
“And you say I have a weird family?” Flo would laugh.

			
Four months after they were married, Flo missed a period. By the next month it was certain. She was pregnant. Mama Tri and Papa got up and danced around the table as the sisters all came up with names for the baby. Anna was sure it would be a girl and suggested…well, “Anna.” The rest thought it would be a boy and that he should have an all-American name. 

			
“Dominick,” they decided was about as American as apple pie or maybe as Italian American as pizza pie. 

			
Her own sister, cranky Helen, said he should be named Karl Marx, or if it was a girl, Karla Marx. Flo told her to name her own kids after a bunch of communists. “How about changing your Joey’s name to Benito,” she joked. Even Cranky cracked a smile.

			
Tony and Flo—with Flo’s once-again ruby red lips in full bloom—were so happy together that they prayed this baby would be the first of many; they wanted a big family just like the ones they’d grown up in. At night, in bed, on the third floor of the Stasi brownstone where they had their own apartment, Flo would snuggle with Tony after making love and fall asleep wrapped up in his arms. 

			
While falling asleep on the night of December 6, 1941, Flo was so content with her new life, new husband, and their new baby on the way, she thought it couldn’t get better. Then it did. She felt the first flutter of life and put her husband’s hands on her belly. 

			
“Honey,” she said, as they held hands over her fluttery stomach, “We are so blessed. And I am one happy cowgirl.” 

			
Then, the next day, Sunday, December 7, something happened that would again change everything, everywhere forever. 

			
Tony and Flo were having breakfast, and he had her in stitches reading aloud the Sunday funny papers when they heard heavy footsteps rushing up the stairs. 

			
Rosie ripped open the door without knocking and screamed, “Hurry up downstairs! It’s all over the radio! The Japanese bombed Pearl Harbor!” 

			
“What’s Pearl Harbor?” they both asked, confused.

			
“I don’t know, but they bombed it,” Rosie cried pulling them up out of their chairs. They rushed down to where the family was gathered around the radio, listening to the news. Within hours, all the papers had put out special editions describing the horrific attacks. The Japanese had bombed not just the American naval base but several islands in the Pacific and had torpedoed eight US naval ships, sinking four of them. 

			
“Unprovoked!” the papers screamed. The Japanese had wreaked havoc against their American “friends.”

			
The next day, the Stasis, along with millions of other American families, once again gathered around their radios and listened to President Roosevelt declare that December 7, 1941, was a day that would live in infamy. A state of war, he said, now existed between the United States and Japan. 

			
Flo touched her pregnant belly. “Will you be called up?” she asked, every bit as terrified as Domenica, who reached out to grasp the hands of both of her sons.

			
“I guess we all will be,” Tony answered.

			
“Japan,” his mother said. “It’s so far away. Isn’t it?”

			
The youngest sister, Frances, piped up, trying to make the best of a horrible situation. “Thank God Tony has an accounting certificate. If they call you, and they probably won’t what with Flo being pregnant and all, they’d only put you in an Army office doing numbers.”

			
Anna added, “And Louie’s just a teenager. He’s too young.”

			
Four days later, on December 11, the US declared war against Italy and Germany after they declared war on the US.

			
On December 31, Flo’s birthday eve, Anthony J. Stasi, first generation Italian American, whose family had immigrated to America from Italy for a better life, received notice that he had been selected “for training and service in the United States Army Air Corps.” 

			
He was ordered to report to Fort Hamilton in five days.

			
“Ma mio figlio! L’Italia è la nostra patria,” Domenico said, panicked. Questo Corpo Aereo lancerà bombe sulla nostra stessa gente?” He wanted to know if Tony would fight against his own people in Italy, if the Air Corps would drop bombs on their homeland, Italy.

			
“Papa, please. America is our homeland now, not Italy. You ran from Italy, remember? And I will do whatever my country asks me to do. And if they send me to Italy, well—” 

			
Louie stepped in. “Pops,” he said using the American hep cat expression, “that Mussolini fella is a rotten dictator. I’ll volunteer to kill him myself,” he said, pretending to shoot a machine gun.

			
Domenico didn’t agree. “He was a good man. He made the trains run on time.” 

			
“Train schedules! That’s a great reason for him to keep all the Italians down,” Tony said, shaking his head.

			
“So, it be gooder to bomb-fight them?” Domenico asked, using his best English. 

			
As upset as he was that his sons would go to war, Domenico was doubly disturbed that his sons might end up fighting and killing their own kin in Italy.

			
Domenica put her arms around her boys, weeping, and asked in Italian. “They can’t take two sons from one family, can they?”

			
Four days later, Louie, underage, walked to the local recruiting office and voluntarily joined the Army with an altered birth certificate.

			
The Stasi boys were quick to become 110 percent American soldiers, anxious to get into the action—not knowing, of course, what the action would really be like.

			
Tony sent letters from basic training when he could, writing that it was tougher than he expected. “I never knew I could run, jump, climb, and do drills for twelve hours without dying,” he kidded. “But wait until you see my new muscles. I’ve got muscles on top of muscles.”

			
He was right. Three months later, Tony walked through the gate at 200 Franklin Avenue for his short leave before shipping out. He was handsome in his US Army Air Corps uniform with, yes, new muscles on top of muscles. 

			
Tony was thrilled with what Flo had grown too—a big baby belly. They spent what little time they had alone but shared supper every night with his parents and sisters. 

			
He had, he boasted, scored so high on his exams that he was chosen for flight school. 

			
“Does that mean you’ll be in school for most of the war?” his sister Philly asked, hoping against hope.

			
Tony ruffled her upswept hairdo and said, “No, it means I’ll be flying in a bomber.”

			
All the joking stopped, and silence took over. 

			
“You’ll be flying a fighter plane?” Flo asked, panicked.

			
“Well, I don’t know if I’ll be a pilot or not, but for sure I will be flying in one.”

			
Flo gasped and held her belly, Domenica asked what it all meant, and the sisters broke out crying, all except for his oldest sister, Maria. Married to an older man with two young sons of her own, she hugged Tony and said, “We’re proud of you, Tone. Really proud of you.”

			
Domenico lit his pipe and just stared, too afraid to say anything for fear of breaking down. They all knew Tony may never return. There was a very good chance he would be shot down as he was dropping bombs on the land of his ancestors—the foreign, yet not foreign country where both he and Flo still had aunts, uncles, and little cousins they’d never met.

			
On the day he was set to leave, Tony and Flo told the family that he wanted his wife to see him off at the station alone. The sisters became hysterical at the very thought of it, but their mama told them it was only right for Flo to have this time alone with her husband. 

			
Saying goodbye to his parents and sisters was tough, knowing he may never see them again, but leaving his pregnant wife was even harder. What if he died in combat and never met his child? Flo would be left a widow with a baby. What would happen to them?

			
She knew what he was thinking and said, “You’ll be safe. I know it. I promise you.”

			
As Flo and Tony approached Grand Central Terminal—the place that had changed Flo’s life in so many ways so many times—she steeled herself against the biggest change of all. 

			
This time the station was in a different kind of chaos with thousands of soldiers, sailors, and their families rushing every which way, crying, saying goodbye, looking for the tracks where their troop trains were boarding.

			
They found the right track and Tony kissed Flo like he’d never kiss her again. He hefted his duffle bag onto his shoulder and climbed onto his assigned packed-to-the-rafters troop train to the raucous sounds of laughter, joking, and singing. Young men going off to adventure in foreign lands who were still green enough to believe they were invincible, that the USA would defeat “the Gerrys,” “the Japs,” and “the Eye-ties” in a month.

			
As he boarded, Tony found an open window so he could lean out and wave to Flo on the platform. 

			
As the train pulled out, she ran alongside it, holding her belly. He called out “Nobody’s better than you. Nobody!” hoping she heard him.

			
“I’ll see you real soon! I love you!” she called back, running until there was no more platform. 

			
Just like Blue followed my train once upon a time, she thought, crying until she could hardly see. 

			
Then she caught her breath. Were the tears blurring her vision? 

			
Or was she really there? 

			
On the platform standing in front of Flo of the ruby lips, stood a blue-eyed wolf.

			
Ooowwooo.

EPILOGUE
		

	
	
		

			
EPILOGUE

			
All-American Family

			
Anthony J. Stasi fought the Nazis in Italy and France in the US Army Air Corps, (US Air Force). He crewed on a long-range, B24 aircraft nicknamed “Strictly Social” in the invasion of Anzio, Italy, as well as on a P47D Thunderbolt single-seat fighter known as “Stinky” in the invasion of Utah Beach, Normandy, France.

			
When the war ended, he returned home to his wife, Flo, to meet his son, four-year old Dominick Stasi for the first time. 

			
The Barbera Magic Continues

			
After graduating cum laude from Wellesley College, Linda’s daughter, Jessica Stasi Rovello’s first job was on a film shoot in Brooklyn where she was drawn to a tiny restaurant. Becoming friendly with the owner, she asked about renting the apartment upstairs. The address? 151 Union Street, Red Hook, Brooklyn, New York. The restaurant? Ferdinando’s Focacceria. The apartment? The same one in which her great grandmother had lived and died. Two families united one hundred years later.

			
Dominick Stasi came of age, and he, too, joined the US Air Force, became a nationally recognized engineer known for his innovations in network and cable TV, including the satellites that launches MTV onto the air.

			
Flo and Tony had one more child, Linda Stasi, columnist, TV host, novelist, and a scrapper in the mode of her mother.  

			
Flo’s Grandchildren

			
Jessica Stasi Rovello, Linda’s daughter, became one of the first and only female CEOs of a major digital games company—Arkadium. She was named to Crains NY 40 Under 40 and was on the cover of INC. Magazine as the CEO of the “Best Workplace in America.” She is now CEO of @Woods. 

			
Christopher Stasi, Dominick’s son, became a nationally honored engineer who founded and is CEO of several tech and management companies, winning national praise and awards for his innovations.

			
Jackie Stasi, Dominick’s daughter, is a nationally known and honored media marketing expert who has won “Agency Person of the Year,” as well as Adweek’s coveted Media Plan of the Year.

			
Flo’s Great Grandchildren

			
Marco Rovello, Jessica’s teenage son, at just seventeen, has two works of art on exhibition at the Metropolitan Museum of Art in NYC. He is attending the prestigious Rhode Island School of Design.  

			
Dean Stasi Rovello is in high school and is following in his grandmother’s footsteps as a journalist and writer.  He covers the stories of committed high school athletes on his website and Instagram @HSCommitted.  

			
Reed Rovello is a middle schooler who loves basketball and living in Brooklyn, just a few blocks from his ancestors.

			
Olivia Stasi, Christopher’s daughter, is a kindergartener who lives in Hermosa Beach, California and loves ice skating, music, and playing at the beach.

			
Barbera’s Children and Grandchildren Whose Military Service Has Been Honored

			
Frank Candiano Jr., Laura’s son, a Lieutenant Colonel in the US Army, was the first US Army officer to lead troops into and secure Ground Zero on 9/11, where they remained at the smoking pit for months.

			
Mario “Bobby” Candiano, Frank’s brother, served with honor in Vietnam and died at the age of twenty-eight from exposure to Agent Orange, leaving a wife and two toddlers behind.

			
John Venturo, Clara’s grandson, became a decorated helicopter pilot serving in Desert Storm as well as a decorated police officer and pilot who is, at the time of this writing, running for US Congress.

			
Engraving on Florence Stasi’s Gravestone

			
1916–2001

			
Nobody’s Better Than You
