Acknowledgments

			
Thank you forever to my late mom, Florence Barbera Stasi and her siblings who loved me like crazy even though I was a brat: My Godmother Aunt Clara, Aunt Carrie, Uncle Pete, Aunt Angie, Aunt Mary, Aunt Laura, Aunt Helen, Uncle Austin, and Uncle Joe. 

			
A huge, big thanks to my agent Liza Fleissig, of the Liza Royce Associates literary agency who never stopped advocating tenaciously for this book despite pushbacks from those who just didn’t want this true and yet untold story of the other Sicilian immigration story to be made public. Thank you from the bottom of my heart. You did it!

			
To all the great folks at Regalo Press: Publisher Gretchen Young who loved and understood this book right off and made me an offer I couldn’t—and never wanted to—refuse. Thank you for getting it and getting me. Ditto to Post Hill Press Publisher Anthony Ziccardi, my editor Adriana Senior, and Managing Editor Caitlin Burdette who worked incredibly hard to make this book exactly as it should be and exactly as I dreamed it could be. Xxoo

			
To my husband, Sid Davidoff for his advice, suggestions, and patience and to my daughter Jessica Rovello for coming up with so many great ideas that helped me get through when I was ready to “absquatulate” as the cowpokes used to say. (Look it up!)

			
Thank you to the gorgeous Marie Yervasi, daughter of Peter, the last living kickass Barbera gal, who helped me in so many ways that I can’t begin to count them. Marie—you are the best and I love you.

			
Thank you to all my cousins for allowing me to use their parents’ and grandparents’ names to imagine and reimagine what it was like to be them at the turn of the century: theirs loves, their losses, and their bravery.

			
Grazie mille to my writing pals from the Art Workshop International which meets each summer in Assisi, Italy. This is where I have taken two weeks a year to formulate and begin all my novels. I especially need to thank my 2023 writing group led by the great bestselling author, SJ Rozan, my mentor and my friend who has guided me through three novels there, as well as Dr. Mia Talmor, Nancy Ennis, and Cynthia Fagen—great writers all. We all met with SJ every morning in that glorious space to write, critique, and write some more. Oh, and to eat great meals and drink too much wine.

			
Many many thanks to my three pals, Damien Miano, Rosemary Rogers, and Antonia Ka for reading, re-reading, and for always being ready, willing, and able to offer invaluable advice and suggestions, which helped turn this book into a living, breathing work. Xo.

			
Grazzi Assai to the wonderful Roberto Scarcella Perino of NYU and music fame for translating both English and standard Italian into Sicilian—and making sure even that was accurate for the time period. You are a genius, a terrific person, a gentleman and scholar (as they say). 

			
Thank you to my long lost cousin, Sam Carlino, the wondrous BBQ king of San Jose, California, for all his friendship and help. His fantastic book Colorado’s Carlino Brothers, a history of his family, knocked my socks off. His great book is a must read for every fan of The Godfather because I found his real life descendants, their family name, as well as events and the horrific murders they experienced (which were reported in newspapers of the time), so similar to the names and events in Mario Puzo’s classic that it makes me wonder if they may even have been his inspiration. 

			
Our connected families sure were mine!

– 2 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 2 –

			
Witch’s Brew

			
Greenhorn Mountain, Colorado

			
January 1, 1916 

			
Mariano made his way back inside, grumbling about his bloody leg. Maria watched him carefully as she tried to get Firenze to nurse with whatever her starving body could produce. Her husband was cogitating over this development, sure enough, but he was still looking mean and surly as a rattler.

			
Why couldn’t it be a boy—a normal kid? Mariano thought. He could put a boy to work by four or five years old. He needed sons more than ever now that they were living wild. 

			
Girls were just a man’s burden. They had to be married off, sold off, or carried off as soon as they reached puberty so they could produce sons for some other poor bastard. And this one? This unclean thing, this strega that his wife had birthed? Who’d ever marry that? 

			
More bad luck. His whole life had been cursed since he’d come to this God-forsaken country. Now he was a man running from the law and hiding out like un fallimento—a failure. With another daughter. One that had already been visited by the devil. 

			
Killing la picciridda might even be the solution to the curse hanging over his head. Kill the witch, kill the curse. 

			
Maria was scared. She knew what Mariano was thinking. She’d lived with him too long to not know. So, she prayed as hard as she had ever prayed to her Lord Jesus. She begged Jesus for a miracle to save them, a miracle to protect her children not just from the wolf, or the grinding poverty, but from her husband. She prayed for the Lord to help Mariano find God again. He had once believed in Him. He hadn’t always been a beast. 

			
She hugged Firenze closer. 

			
Then, just like that, after he finished off his jug of homemade brew, Mariano did find God. Seems God had been staring at him all along, right there at the bottom of his now empty jug.

			
It wasn’t exactly what you’d call a “coming to Jesus” moment though. It was more like a “demon rum” moment. 

			
The flash of inspiration came to him from a recent bit of news. His goombah, Gaetano Passentino, had recently met him at a secret spot partway up the mountain to bring the family a hunk of Parmigiano, half a sopressat’, some flour, and the latest rumors from down below. Gaetano wasn’t much of a talker, but that day he talked to Mariano—a lot. He claimed the miners had told him that on January 1, the Colorado governor was going to ban liquor in the whole damned state. Gaetano swore on his mother’s life that it was true. He even pulled out a news article to show Mariano. But since neither of them could read, it didn’t matter anyhow. 

			
“Nobody in America can drink alcohol after the new year?” Mariano asked, shocked.

			
“No, no,” Gaetano answered. “Just in Colorado. A man could go to another state to drink,” but nobody they knew could afford to do that, anyway. They couldn’t even leave town. 

			
What? How could men live without wine, without beer, without whiskey? 

			
Gaetano claimed that Protestant do-gooders, who had the right to vote, had voted to ban liquor in the whole state. 

			
Mariano began to seriously contemplate what this meant. So now a fella couldn’t knock back a few in public anymore? Couldn’t stop by Mrs. John Bianchi’s saloon ever again on a Saturday night, get drunk, and play Scopa until he was out of money or out on his ass? 

			
Trying to keep a man from his drink was about as dumb as trying to keep a man from the puttanas hanging around saloon tables willing to relieve a miner, cowhand, or drifter of his money in exchange for a good time upstairs. A law like that could make him a rich man because if there was one thing he knew, it was how to make good brew. His wine and hooch had been in such demand down at the mines—before the murders—that he always had a dollar or two in his pocket.

			
Ban liquor? Any dang fool knew that to live, a man needs cooch and hooch. 

			
Mariano suddenly whooped with joy, which scared the bejesus out of the kids, who were already shaking in their boots. If they had any boots to shake in, that is. 

			
The idea came to him as easily as the forbidden apple had come to Eve. Except in this case, the forbidden fruit was…sugar beet. 

			
All Italian peasants knew that sugar beets were so easily turned into booze it was almost a sin. And now it actually was. Or illegal at least. The Sicilians called this homemade brew “Sugar Moon,” and they’d been making it as long as anybody could remember. 

			
Now that it was officially illegal to buy, make, move, or sell liquor in that cursed state of Colorado, any man with knowledge of making Sugar Moon or vino could make a fortune. Mariano had that knowledge. He only needed a cart. And a mule. 

			
Those rich ’Merigaans would be sorry they made booze illegal. These fat-cats had worked the immigrants and brown folk in the coal mines until they were dead or crippled, and now they’d gone and taken away the one thing that helped those poor working stiffs forget their miserable existences: Booze. 

			
No matter how bad things got, they always had their Zuccuru Luna, their Sugar Moon. They had cooked up that brew in the old country, and the Siggies (Sicilians) had been cooking it up here in the New World since settling in this hellish place, this United States of America. This Wild West. That was the only skill most of them had left that was of any use: growing grapes and beets and turning them into potent-as-hell wine and moonshine.

			
For Crissakes, knowing how to make wine and Zuccuru Luna was as much a part of Mariano and Maria’s lives as their dark skin, black eyes, and curly hair. 

			
Mariano began to concoct a plan, completely unconcerned with the family chaos swirling around the shack. Goddamn! Why couldn’t he supply the hooch and let Mrs. Bianchi supply the cooch? He’d sell his vino and ‘shine, not just to the miners, but to the prominent big bugs who ran the mines. He’d charge those rich sumnabitches double. It was the Siggie form of enterprise. He didn’t need an education to grow, make, and sell. He couldn’t read or write in any language, but he sure could count. 

			
Mariano had forgotten all about the wolf, the family’s fright, their plight, even his bleeding leg. Instead, he began planning his escape from hell via the Sugar Moon Express. 

			
His bootleg brew would make those brutal mine managers, the pezzu ‘i novanta—big shots—so dependent on him that they’d forget he was a wanted man on the run, forget that they considered Sicilians an inferior race of filthy, impoverished, degenerate criminals. 

			
He’d be the big man. Those pigs with their fine white houses and their respectable womenfolk who dressed in silks and had real ribbons in their hair and wore fancy button shoes—they would need him for the bootleg booze they would want so bad. 

			
He began to imagine making so much money bootlegging to the mining camp’s managers and the miners themselves that he’d become an important man, omu di panza, a “man of the belly” as they said in the old country. He’d buy himself a pair of fancy shoes, a suit, maybe even a silk hat.

			
No more living off the land, hoping to bag an elk or a big buck with just a six-shooter, which was about as likely as lassoing an eagle in flight. 

			
All this hiding out without a pot to piss in? That was about to be over—but not forgotten. Never forgotten. 

			
Finally, he’d have respect. Rispettu. 

			
As his plan was coming together in his fevered brain, baby Firenze started howling again. He turned to look at her. Maybe he wouldn’t kill her—not just yet anyway. Maybe the witch cast a spell and that’s how he came to this important new thinking. 

			
If she was really una mavara, (and how could she not be—this child who could summon the wolves), maybe he could turn it to his advantage. When she got old enough, he could force her to use her powers against his enemies. He’d make her use u malocchiu—the evil eye—on anyone who got in his way. And if she refused, well…. 

			
Since living wild, Mariano came to know that brute strength and brutality were the only way a man could control anything—or everything—in a brutal country. It had always worked with the others, including his family. 

			
He didn’t know it yet, but such brutality would never work with this one—the baby that howled with ruby lips.

			
Firenze was the fifteenth of sixteen children that pint-sized Maria Ragusa La Barbera would conceive in both the old country and the new world. Since marrying Mariano, Maria had either been pregnant, nursing, or mourning nearly every day of her life. She had lost almost as many babies as had survived, Yet, each infant she lost was as painful and unbearable as the ones she’d lost before. And each one who’d lived brought her as much joy—even if most of them had been girls. 

			
Girls were a terrible disappointment to her husband, and he took each one out on her. Though she’d given him three boys, the last four had been girls. Mariano would say in disgust that she just wasn’t built for boys.

			
But Maria knew that she had been born to breed, especially this one, this new magical wild child, the one her husband didn’t want. 

			
The one he already feared.

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 3 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 3 –

			
Big Love in a Small World 

			
Lucca Sicula, Sicily

			
1905 

			
Mariano had forgotten that he hadn’t been born to bully and tyrannize. In fact, before coming to this new country where everything had gone so badly and become so deadly, he’d been the sort of man that women either wanted for themselves or for their daughters. 

			
By the time he reached the age of thirty, in fact, Mariano had made it kind of big in a very small-town way. He was a tall, powerfully built, but slender man—not a showstopper but handsome enough. More importantly, he commanded respect. He had managed to acquire enough land just south of Lucca Sicula in Sicily to start his own small vineyard. They said he had a way with the grape. 

			
To support his budding enterprise, he also raised goats and chickens. 

			
Odd that he didn’t have a wife, perhaps, but if anybody dared to ask, he’d just say he never had time for a family. But fate was about to change all that. One day, he walked into the general store in Lucca Sicula and stopped dead when he laid eyes on the proprietor’s fourteen-year-old daughter, Maria. He opened his mouth to speak, but all that came out was, “Matri e Sarvaturi!”—Mother and Savior! 

			
Mariano was a goner. He knew right off that this was the woman—or girl anyway—that he’d waited for his whole life. 

			
Mariano thought Maria was about the prettiest thing he’d ever seen—a tiny girl with wide, dark eyes and long black curls that went clear down her back. She smiled at him when he handed her the money for his goods. Her smile and those tiny feet—the only things peeking out from under her long cotton dress—put him into a tizzy. Love, lust…whatever it was, he had to have her. 

			
But Mariano didn’t exactly come a-courtin’, as they say. That same day, he simply approached her father, Pietro, as he was loading goods onto a customer’s wagon. Mariano picked up a fifty-pound bag of flour like it was a toy, lifted it into her wagon, and tipped his hat to the customer as she drove off. Then he turned to Pietro and said he wanted to marry his daughter. Mariano told Pietro he owned a small farm with goats and planned to buy more land. He said he would take care of “the girl,” since he didn’t yet even know her name. 

			
Neither Maria’s mother, Beatrice, nor young Maria had a say in the matter. Beatrice wrung her hands, put her arms around Maria, and cried, “No, pi favuri Petru! Idda sulu na picciridda, iè!”—No! Please! She’s just a girl!

			
But the thing is, as soon as Mariano La Barbera jumped off his horse, Maria felt a strange fire inside. So, she took her mother’s hand and told her it was alright, and that Papa knew best. Her mother was shocked. She dragged the screaming Maria back inside the store by her ear. 

			
But it was near a done deal. Maria had been preparing for this day all her young life, and her dowry included a trunkful of pillowcases and sheets, which she and her mama had been embroidering since she was a little girl—a little girl dreaming of the handsome man who would one day ask for her hand and sweep her away. 

			
Mariano, the sort-of handsome man who asked for her hand didn’t exactly sweep her away; he just sort of took her away after a week of negotiating with Pietro. Her mother wept; Maria smiled. 

			
But the deal almost fell apart. Through a friend, Pietro found out one small detail that Mariano had forgotten to mention: his wife. 

			
When confronted, Mariano came clean—or so he claimed. He told Pietro she had died and hung his head. That was good enough for Pietro and for Maria. Dead was dead. 

			
A month later, they were married with the blessing of Padre Paolo, who didn’t question whether Mariano’s first wife was really dead or not. Everyone they knew—and some they didn’t—turned out for the festivities. 

			
Big pots of pasta were served along with roasted meats prepared by all the ladies. Pietro’s homemade vino flowed, and music by Mariano’s brothers filled the town square. 

			
As expected, Pietro beamed. Beatrice wept some more. Mariano’s father, Rafaelo, got stinking drunk, and Mariano’s mother, Giulia, kept her arms folded across her big bosom and scowled. 

			
Maria? She was every inch the blushing bride in a white dress and hand-embroidered veil covering first her face, then flowing down her back after she became a wife. She savored every second of the biggest day of her life. Everyone danced in the streets, but even the best dancers stopped to watch when Mariano took tiny Maria in his big, strong arms and twirled her around. She followed his every step as though she’d danced with a partner all her life, when in fact, he was the first man she’d ever danced with other than her father, whose shoes she would stand on as a little girl so she could follow his steps. Maria gazed up at Mariano, and he stared down at her, their lust so clear that people stood with their mouths open. 

			
It was not just unusual for people to marry for love back then/back there, but when love mixed with lust, there was no stopping it. 

			
This was a girl who was fit and ready to fulfill her destiny as lover, breeder, mother, and helpmate to her husband. 

			
Everyone agreed: this was what you call a love match. Maria burned for Mariano like he did for her. Their lovemaking was epic—and loud—so they became the talk of the village. That was OK. They were married. She wasn’t the least embarrassed, even though they were, as custom dictated, living with his parents. After a few months, little Maria was proudly sporting a baby belly and a sly smile. 

			
She didn’t care that her mother-in-law wagged her finger in Maria’s face and said that only filthy girls and puttani engaged in marital relations when they were expecting. That night the couple made louder love than ever. 

			
Mariano was so in love with his bride that he picked wildflowers for her every day and fresh vegetables during harvest and brought it all home to her like a child bringing an apple to his teacher. He tended his vineyard with a whole new vigor. He was now a family man.

			
Mariano, unlike most Sicilian men, was not middatu a so matri—a mama’s boy. He stood up for Maria to his mother whenever the older woman tried to talk trash to her or about her. They walked hand-in-hand everywhere and exchanged kisses during their evening passiata through the tiny town. Mariano’s friends and even his brothers teased that he’d become pussidutu—possessed. Mariano couldn’t even look at Maria without taking her face in his hands and kissing it all over while proclaiming, “Bedda!”—beauty. 

			
Maria counted herself very lucky to have been found by such a man. Such a lover. Such a husband. 

		
	

OceanofPDF.com

– 4 –
		

	
	
		

			
– 4 –

			
Promises 

			
Lucca Sicula, Sicily 

			
1909 

			
So, how in the hell did the Barbera family end up like this, with Maria married to a mean, ornery varmint if ever there was one? Why did they leave the only village and home they and their ancestors had ever known? And what had turned, if not a God-fearing man, at least a good, loving man into one so bitter and angry? 

			
Maria had already birthed three babies—including a set of twins—when the hard times hit Sicily. Bandits roamed, drought was threatening, work was drying up faster than the land, taxes were crushing, and a long spell of bad weather had hurt not just their village of Lucca Sicula but also the whole province of Agrigento. Still, Mariano refused to give up, eking out what he could from the land. The vines withered and died no matter how he tried. Despite this, he still brought his Maria what few flowers he could find that were left in the field. He’d help her on and off with her shoes and even did the unthinkable: He helped her care for the babies as they came along. 

			
Then in December of 1908, bad became unbearable, and the world they knew came to an end. First, Mount Etna erupted for one whole day and night, which caused small, local earthquakes. Those small quakes caused a whole lot of shaking but not much damage. Even so, it was terrifying. 

			
A few days after that—Christmas week in fact—the earth stopped teasing and shook hard across the whole of Southern Italy. It was the biggest quake to ever hit Europe. It was so bad that the port cities of Messina and Reggio di Calabria were almost completely toppled, killing thousands upon thousands.

			
Then, even though it couldn’t get worse, it did. 

			
Ten minutes after the quake, the sea started to recede and sucked itself back in so far it was hard to tell where it was. Where did the sea go? Everyone was scared as hell. And rightfully so.

			
It didn’t take long to find out. It began with a roar so mighty it could have come from the mouth of God himself. And before anybody could run, the sea rushed back covering the land like Noah’s flood itself. Three forty-foot tidal waves hit one after the other, drowning everything and everyone. Nearly 100,000 souls perished and those that did survive were left starving and homeless. 

			
The people who had relatives in the mountains and inland began heading to towns like Maria and Mariano’s village, seeking shelter with family. The starving survivors who made their way there brought the barely-hanging-on towns to the breaking point. Babies died, mothers and fathers roamed the tiny village streets begging for scraps. Maria’s parents’ store was out of food—even the hundreds of jars of peaches they had canned were sold or stolen—and they had to close. Maria went to help close up the store, and she and Beatrice hung onto each other and cried. Gone. Everything they’d worked for was gone, dried up like the dead earth under their feet. 

			
It was as though the wrath of the Almighty had come down to punish Sicily. Mariano’s family survived on sugar beets and whatever olives and grapes hadn’t dried up and died before they came to full fruit. They had one goat left, and even it wasn’t doing too good. Kill it for meat or keep it for whatever milk and cheese it could produce? How could they continue? 

			
The only way out was out. 

			
Mariano had never wanted to go to America; he had made terrible fun of the young men who had left Sicily to go to the New World thousands of miles away. Paradise was here—right here—in Sicily. Until it wasn’t. 

			
Whenever he could, Mariano hired out as a day laborer in the farms that were still hanging on and got some work cutting down trees for a company that shipped them to Naples, but that only paid what amounted to twenty-five centesimi for a twelve-hour day. It wasn’t even enough to buy food for one person. He would be gone looking for work for weeks at a time, leaving Maria and the children with his parents, who didn’t want them. 

			
There was very little work and too many men willing to work for even less than those measly wages. When Mariano would come home to Maria and the little ones with little or nothing, Maria—who was very understanding for lady so young—would tell him, “Non è colpa soi. A scarsizza fa lu prezzu.”—It’s not your fault. Scarcity sets the price. 

			
In his head, Mariano knew her words to be true, but his heart said something different. His family missed meals. While Maria didn’t complain, his mother did. She never tired of reminding Maria that if they weren’t burdened with her and her children, her own belly would be full. Maria only smiled and skipped meals, feeding her children as little as it took to keep them alive. 

			
When Mariano was gone, he couldn’t know what was happening back home, but he knew his mother well enough to know she wasn’t making Maria’s life pleasant. He worried that Maria felt marrying him had been a mistake. She was a beauty and could have done better—not living on scraps in her husband’s father’s house. 

			
A rich man could have come along and seen that beautiful young girl at her parent’s store. She could have become the wife of a wealthy man living in a big house in Roma. 

			
Maria didn’t think that way, though. To her, Mariano was the greatest gift God could have ever given her. He was kind, he was gentle with her and, well, he set her on fire. No life, she believed, was without pain—often great pain—but she at least she had a man who would always be her rock. Until he wasn’t.

			
Even so, Mariano felt ashamed—as though the natural disasters that brought them so low had been his fault. A man who couldn’t feed his family wasn’t a man. He was more helpless than a woman. Mariano was un nu fallimentu—a failure in the midst of greater failure. 

			
So, the next time the traveling padrone—Enzio Saicchitano—came to their village, Mariano and his brother Arcangelo listened to what he had to say. Saicchitano was what they called a middleman, and it was his job to travel to the poorest villages looking for strong Sicilian bucks who were broke and desperate for a break. He’d offer the biggest and strongest of them free passage and well-paying jobs in the land of opportunity.

			
On this day he stood in Lucca Sicula’s piazza, resplendent in a silk derby hat and cutaway jacket, and called for the men to gather ’round. 

			
When he had a bit of a crowd, he began his pitch. “The land is dried up,” Saicchitano said, sweeping his arms all around. “There is no work for you men.”

			
“But we—my brother Ange and me—hear that there is work in Naples rebuilding the port. We plan to go there,” Mariano said, turning around to go back home. 

			
“Yes, you could do that,” the padrone answered, grabbing both of the brothers’ attention. “Travel eight hundred kilometers and leave your wife and family to find work. If there is any.”

			
The brothers turned back around. It couldn’t hurt to just listen to the man, could it? “Thousands of desperate men are already there begging for work. It’s like slave work.” He took a pause to gauge the mood of his audience. He nodded his head and continued. “Or—or—if you are wise and brave enough, you could make a new life. In America. It’s a country where men are free. You would be free from this cursed place and its crooked government.” He spat in disgust. 

			
Mariano and Ange exchanged looks. 

			
“Do you know two million Italians have already left and gone to America?” the padrone continued. “They all found the good life in a good country. Plenty of work, and better, plenty of land.”

			
“Maybe that is so, but we can’t leave our home,” Ange shot back. “You want us to make a home in America? We know nothing there.” 

			
The padrone answered with a common phrase, using Sicilian to show he was one of them—but a rich version now: “Casa quantu stai e tirrinu quantu viri.”—Home for as long as you need to be, and land as far as the eye can see. 

			
Mariano put his fingers together in a pinecone hand gesture, shook them at the padrone, and answered with a saying of his own: “Asini, donni e sciccariaddunun t’alluntanari di li toi.”—Don’t go far away from your donkeys, women, and oxen.

			
The game was on.

			
“No. No,” Saicchitano protested with a twinkle in his eye, “Sulu a fimmina e a iaddinasi perdi si troppu cammina.”—Only women and hens get lost if they wander too far. 

			
Ange jumped in, shaking his finger at them both and grinning. “Tra il dire e il fare c’è di mezzo il mare.”—An ocean lies between what is said and what is done.

			
The old men got up from their chairs around the dried-up stone fountain where they gathered every day, to watch the show. This was going to be good. 

			
Mariano, playing to the crowd, nodded his head and added, “Cu lassa a vecchia pa nova sapi chiddu chi lassa ma un sapi chiddu ca trova.”—He who leaves the old road for the new, knows what he leaves, but he doesn’t know what he will find.

			
The padrone shot back, “Tutti i nodi vengono al pettine.”—All tangles come to the comb. In other words, if he wasn’t honest, it would have come out already. 

			
Fat Lorenzo, the village skeptic, stuck his very large head into the crowd and bellowed, “Quello ha gli ami da pesca in tasca.”—That one has fishhooks in his pocket. 

			
The whole crowd, including the padrone roared with laughter, so Fat Lorenzo, loving the attention, added, “In bocca al lupo”—In the mouth of the wolf. 

			
The padrone again laughed but, realizing the whole thing was running off the rails, got serious, put his arms around the strapping La Barbera brothers, and said with all sincerity, “Amici e vaddati.” He meant it was good that their friends were looking out for them, but he added, “Trust me. I won’t steer you wrong.” 

			
Ange laughed, did a little dance, and spat out, “Scecchi Minchia! Fidari è bonu, nun fidari è megghiu.”—Donkey dick. Trusting is good. Not trusting is better. 

			
The padrone, understanding that the contest wasn’t over until the brothers said it was over, said, “Amici miei. A mali estremi, estremi rimedi.”—My friends. Desperate times call for desperate measures. 

			
That could not be denied. Even the crowd stopped most of its caterwauling. 

			
The padrone had come up with the truest, saddest, toughest phrase of the day, and he had just won them over. 

			
As Fat Lorenzo had predicted, as soon as Saicchitano knew he’d hooked them, he reeled them in like fish. “I arrange everything,” he said, “As soon as you arrive in America, I will be there to personally escort you to the place where jobs will be waiting for you. You will never be on your own or lost.” 

			
“What kind of jobs?” Mariano asked. 

			
“Farm laboring jobs in Colorado, America,” Saicchitano said. “And the bosses there? They are crazy. They pay foreign workers one dollar and fifty cents in American money a day. And after a few years, maybe one or two at the most, the government gives you your own land to farm.” 

			
The brothers looked at each other. How could that be? Those were rich man’s wages. And free land? 

			
“That’s six times more than you could earn in the same job here,” the padrone added. “If you could find the work.” Which they knew they couldn’t. 

			
“But this place, this Colorado? Che cos’è?” Ange asked. 

			
“Che cos’è?” Saicchitano laughed, taking a piece of newspaper out of his satchel and reading it to them: “‘Colorado,’ it says right here in this newspaper, ‘is the Italy of Western America.’ So many Italians there, it’s like you were still home. And most are right from this area.” 

			
Mariano knew it sounded too good to be true, but as the padrone had said, “Desperate times call for desperate measures.” Besides, Mariano figured since he was a smart man, he’d just beat them (whoever “they” were) at their own game. 

			
“What about our wives and children,” Mariano asked. “Do they come with us?”

			
Saicchitano said, “Of course. As soon as you are settled, I will arrange for them to join you.”

			
For the first time, Mariano allowed himself to imagine what it could be like. He’d get that Colorado land; he’d farm it with Maria by his side. He would become what they called an American cowboy. They would be rich. They would be free. They would be happy. 

			
But there had to be more to it, Mariano knew, so he asked, “Saicchitano how do you earn money from this?” 

			
Saicchitano said his job was to find good, honest Italian men who were willing to work hard and make homes in the American West. He said he was an “agent.” It sounded like he meant he worked for the American government, but he never said that. Not really. 

			
He was no damned agent for the government. He was just a modern-day slaver for John D. Rockefeller Jr.’s Colorado Fuel and Iron Company. These padroni made a lot of money by betraying their own kind, promising the moon but sending them to hell. 

			
The US government knew about this filthy practice but looked the other way. The one thing that was true was that the US of A was in desperate need of workers—especially on the rails and in the mines. Thing is, they just didn’t give two damns about how the workers got there or what happened to them once they were handed pickaxes, hammers, and shovels. 

			
Saicchitano always promised the men at these gatherings that America needed workers so badly that he was authorized to provide them with free passage to the towns where good jobs waiting for them. He even took care of the paperwork. He said he’d already arranged for hundreds of wives and families to follow the men he’d recruited. The small fees for their passage would be taken out of their husband’s salaries. It was not anything to worry about. 

			
The La Barbera boys were strong like bulls and willing to work until they dropped—if they could see there was a light at the end of the tunnel. And they could see light, so they signed their marks on the dotted line and put an X next to their chosen work, which Saicchitano told them was “farmhand.” To assure the padrone that he would not be sorry for taking them on, Ange said they knew how to grow grapes, sugar beets, and other vegetables, so they’d be great farmhands. They even knew how to raise and slaughter goats. 

			
Saicchitano signed his name underneath their Xs. Fat Lorenzo, who didn’t trust the stranger no matter how good he was at proverbs and for sure didn’t trust any document, called out a warning as he left the group, “Chi non ha intenzione di pagare, firma qualsiasi contratto, Mariano.”—Who doesn’t intend to pay, signs any contract, Mariano. Then, the big one: “Orva di l’occhi.”—Blind to the truth.

			
Mariano laughed, but it wasn’t genuine. The brothers were both worried, but Mariano only joked back that the proverb contest was over. 

			
Fat Lorenzo couldn’t help himself and called out a final warning, “Mariano, Arcangelo: Nun vi fati schiacciari da ‘sti bastardi.”—Don’t let the bastards get you down. 

			
They wouldn’t, they promised one another. They wouldn’t let anybody get them down. There was no choice now, anyway. They just had to trust that the padrone was a man of honor and not a pezzo di merda—a piece of shit—and that they hadn’t signed their lives away. 

			
Honor? That son of a bitch made P.T. Barnum look like the pope himself.

Advance Praise for The Descendant
		

	
	
		

			
Advance Praise for The Descendant

			
“The Descendant is a riveting family saga that carries readers from a small Sicilian village to the mines of Colorado and the streets of Brooklyn. Inspired by real events, it reveals the resilience and daring of the Barbera family—especially the fierce women whose courage and defiance helped them survive poverty, violence, and the pull of the Mafia. Epic in scope yet intimate in its storytelling, this is a powerful portrait of love, loss, and the fight to carve out a future against impossible odds.” 

			
—Jacqueline Friedland, USA Today bestselling author of He Gets That From Me and Counting Backwards

			
“Vivid as folklore and true as history, The Descendant brings early twentieth-century Colorado to life through women whose strength and defiance reshape their families—and the world around them.”

			
—Shelley Blanton-Stroud, author of An Unlikely Prospect

			
“Told through the eyes of one family, The Descendant is the story of how thousands of Italian immigrants helped settle the American West fighting unimaginable hardship, massacre, flood, blizzards, and bigotry to emerge triumphant as cattle ranchers, cowboys, bootleggers, and heroic WWII fighter plane crew members. Written with heartbreak and humor, Stasi’s novel brings 
needed change to the accepted, but biased, untold Italian American immigrant narrative.”

			
—Andrew Cuomo, former New York State governor and New York Times bestselling author

			
“Captivating fiction often requires an author putting the reader in a situation they have never envisioned possible. In The Descendant, Linda Stasi uses her family’s incredible true journey to take us to a time and a place few know anything about.

			
“I guarantee your imagination will put you there with them and, as I did, you will wonder what you would do to survive. This is a story that will resonate in your thoughts for a long time.”

			
—David Fisher, author of twenty-four New York Times bestselling fiction and non-fiction books
